# A2.1 Executive summary

This Multi-country Project (MCP) proposal is part of a global initiative to advance Early Warnings for All (EW4All) as announced by the United Nations Secretary General in March 2022 and articulated in the EW4All Executive Action Plan (EAP). The project will be implemented through the Multi-Donor Trust Fund for Early Warning Systems (MTEWS), aiming to reach the most vulnerable communities in the targeted seven countries, namely Antigua and Barbuda, Belize, Dominica, Grenada, St. Kitts and Nevis, St. Lucia, and St. Vincent and the Grenadines. The project’s needs will establish Early Warning Systems (EWS) infrastructure, set up systems for its long-term operation, support local capacities in the generation and dissemination of early warnings products and help introduce relevant policies and mechanisms to ensure sustainability. The project will also contribute to the achievement of SDG 13 by stimulating the design and funding of multiple regional delivery mechanisms to support the financial and technical needs of the targeted countries.

To summarize, we will follow the approach of the ADP CRPP proposal. An EWS Global Funding Proposal (FIP) serves to provide details on the global program and financing architecture, its theory of change, alignment with the GCF investment criteria, and a description of the project’s expected results. Eligibility criteria for the selection of interventions, activities, sites and beneficiaries are provided in Appendix 3.

## Climate change problem

Rising global near-surface temperatures associated with anthropogenic climate change have already exceeded 1.5°C above pre-industrial levels, exacerbating disaster risk triggered by different types of hazardous events. Societies and the natural systems on which they depend are unable to cope with and adapt to the effects of climate change, some of which are irreversible. These include increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as heat waves, increased, exacerbated by changes in the weather and climate, including increases in temperature (leading to heat- waves), damages to agriculture and human health and the intensity of storms, cyclones and storms causing flooding, landslides, droughts, wildfires, and sea-level rise.

These changes in climate increase the risks faced by communities and the livelihoods on which they depend. They create and exacerbate food insecurity, water scarcity, and other environmental stresses that can lead to conflict and disrupting ecosystem services. These impacts are reflected within National Determined Contributions (NDCs) which identify freshwater resources, food production and nutritional security and terrestrial and wetland ecosystems as the top priority areas for adaptation in many countries. The effects of climate change on these systems include increased temperatures, sea level rise, land and forest degradation as critical impacts of climate change, with systems such as water resources, agriculture, forests, coastal zones, and human settlements being most affected. These include reduced crop yield, soil degradation, animal diseases, loss of livestock, reduction of water supplies, salinization of water resources, human migration, ecosystem and biodiversity loss, increased coastal erosion, damage to infrastructure

## Key climate change induced risks in participating countries

A summary of key climate change risks in each of the seven participating countries in the MCPS is provided below: Antigua and Barbuda.

### Antigua and Barbuda

Ranked as the 5th highest on the World Risk Index (2023), Antigua and Barbuda is highly exposed to an array of baseline hazards, including hurricanes, extreme rainfall, droughts, and rising sea levels. Hurricanes are considered the country's main climate hazard, with a high likelihood of occurrence in the future. The 2017 hurricane season was particularly severe, with building stock in Antigua and Barbuda, using the Representative Concentration Pathway (RCP) 8.5, will experience direct losses from storm surges and wind-in the amount of USD 435M for a historic event, USD 4.9Billion for a 100-year event, and USD 27.6Billion for a 500-year event. The country has experienced 8 drought episodes between 2000 and 2020, resulting in drought being considered the "2nd major climate threat."

### Rising sea levels and increasing land and sea surface temperatures are expected in the future.

The low-lying geography of Antigua and Barbuda makes it particularly vulnerable to rising sea levels. The current and projected warming is increasing evaporation rates, which will lead to increased water stress on the islands. This will have a negative impact on agricultural productivity with less rainfall and more frequent droughts. The food production and livelihood systems in Chad are highly dependent on climate factors, particularly precipitation patterns and temperature. These factors influence crop yields, livestock production, and access to clean drinking water, which is essential for food security and farm workers' productivity. Since 1995, Chad has been ranked at the bottom of the ND-GAIN Country Index (the index ranks countries based on their ability to adapt to climate change). This is due to a combination of high poverty, frequent conflicts, and the risk of both droughts and floods. This underlines the country's great need for investment and innovations to improve readiness, and a great urgency for action.

### Chad faces severe environmental degradation, and climate change is aggravating already challenging climatic conditions by increasing the frequency and magnitude of extreme events.

The current and projected warming is increasing evaporation rates, which will lead to increased water stress on the islands. This will have a negative impact on agricultural productivity with less rainfall and more frequent droughts are very likely.

### The food production and livelihood systems in Chad are highly dependent on climate factors

particularly precipitation patterns and temperature. These factors influence crop yields, livestock production, and access to clean drinking water, which is essential for food security and farm workers' productivity. Since 1995, Chad has been ranked at the bottom of the ND-GAIN Country Index (the index ranks countries based on their ability to adapt to climate change). This is due to a combination of high poverty, frequent conflicts, and the risk of both droughts and floods. This underlines the country's great need for investment and innovations to improve readiness, and a great urgency for action.

### Ethiopia

Daily maximum temperatures in FiJi have increased at the rate of about 2°C per decade. There has been a clear shift towards more frequent and intense heatwaves, with longer periods of extreme heat becoming hotter, while more intense rainstorms occurring more frequently. An increase in the frequency and intensity of extreme rainfall events is also projected. The 30-year daily rainfall total is projected to increase by approximately 5–7°C by 2080 compared to current levels.

### Ethiopia

Projections indicate that temperatures in Ethiopia will rise by 1.5-2.5°C by mid-century under RCP4.5 and up to 3-4°C under RCP8.5. Rainfall will become more erratic, with prolonged dry spells worsening drought conditions, wild land and forest fires becoming more frequent and intense, and the risk of flooding increasing.

### Ethiopia

The impacts of climate change in Ethiopia include reduced water availability, frequent crop failures, and heightened food insecurity. South-West Ethiopia is facing devastating floods causing widespread destruction of homes, public infrastructure and affecting essential services such as health care facilities.

### Ethiopia

In July 2014, torrential rains in the Gambella region triggered floods and landslides severely affecting 143,000 people and causing 57 deaths. Waterborne diseases, water-borne diseases, water-borne diseases, and water-borne diseases affected large areas.

### Cambodia

Cambodia faces a high level of disaster risk, with 88% out of 131 communes in the 2016 INFORM Risk Index. The country is particularly vulnerable to floods and droughts due to its location on the border of Vietnam and Thailand. Underlying vulnerabilities such as economic and environmental challenges further heighten the risk from climate hazards for disadvantaged populations.

### Cambodia

The impacts of climate change on flooding and drought risks also decrease Cambodia's GDP by up to 10% by 2050 compared to projected GDP in 2050 in the absence of climate change. Agriculture, infrastructure, forestry, human health, and coastal areas are most affected by climate change. The observed impacts of climate change include

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND BUDGET PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 8

## Critical climate change impacts in Somalia are drought and flood. Drought affects nomadic and agro-pastoral communities who are more vulnerable to prolonged dry seasons and consequent water scarcity and reduced pasture. Droughts in Somalia result in reduced livestock production and agricultural productivity, livelihood loss, food insecurity, and ultimately migration. Floods in Somalia are characterized by high intensity rainfall events that cause flooding in human casualties and major impacts on the economy and damage to infrastructure. Flooding frequently occurs along the Juba and Shabelle Rivers in southern Somalia and flash floods, in the north along intermittent watercourses. Intense rainfall events and subsequent flood events have become more frequent, washing away fertile soil and causing widespread devastation.

### Proposed Interventions

This project is a follow-on, delivered through two Outcomes, intended to assist vulnerable countries in making continuous strides towards meeting the EWWAI vision. This will be achieved by supporting concrete investments in the seven countries in Outcome 2, and by establishing a sustainable architecture at the global and regional levels to continuously support implementation of the EWWAI.

The project addresses critical barriers that prevent the effective generation, dissemination, communication, and use of early warnings for climate change adaptation, preparedness and response to warnings, through two broad outputs listed below:

Output 1 aims to accelerate and scale up national access to global and regional technical and operational support for implementation of EWWAI. This will be achieved by strengthening the capacity of countries in Outcome 2 and making significant efforts to achieve the goal on universal access to early warnings, enabling conditions will be enhanced globally and regionally to promote country-level actions toward a global coordination and support architecture for EWWAI that will ensure universal access to early warning services. The project will work closely with the World Meteorological Organization (WMO) and United Nations Office for Disaster Risk Reduction (UNISDR).

Output 2 addresses barriers that prevent the implementation of a comprehensive and evergreen set of WMO and UNISDR, the seven countries in Outcome 2, have established National Hydro-meteorological Services (NHMSs) and/or their equivalents; 2) Drought and flood monitoring systems; 3) Drought and flood early warning systems; 4) Drought and flood early warning dissemination systems; 5) Early Warning Information Systems (EWIS); 6) Early Warning Communication Systems (EWCS); 7) Early Warning Response Plans (EWRPs). The project will support the implementation through the national government with support from UNDP, the WFP Pillar leads (UNMO, UNDRR, IFRC) and their in-country national offices and other partner agencies where relevant.

The two outputs are interdependent. Output 1 is a necessary condition for Output 2 to be successful as it will ensure that NHMSs have the resources they need to provide long-term and uninterrupted support to critical baseline capacities on which MHWFs are built. These are: robust scientific information and analysis on weather, water and climate hazards and vulnerabilities within National Meteorological and Hydrological Services (NMHSs); a strong understanding of the inter-linkages between drought and flood; an improved ability to provide more diverse understanding of disaster risk and its underlying factors, with greater Impact Based Forecasting (IBF) to inform decision-making at all levels. Output 2 is necessary for Output 1 as it will ensure that NHMSs are better equipped responsible for different elements of managing a national EWS, and it has a long-term financing plan to maintain the necessary hydrometeorological infrastructure, and community-level capacities to receive, understand and respond to EWWAI warnings affecting countries at all levels to protect lives and livelihoods.

# Summary of climate interventions in participating countries

## To be able to address the climate change induced and exacerbated actions highlighted above through MREWS and associated early action/responses ahead of an anticipated weather/climate event, each country will need to develop and implement a national climate risk management strategy. This project will support the development of such strategies.

### Project components

#### Component 1: Climate Risk Management Strategy Development
- The project will update and align disaster management legislation and policies with international standards.
- Modernizing the Disaster Management Act, integrating disaster risk reduction (DRR) into sectoral policies, and strengthening institutional capacity for climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction.
- Developing a national climate risk management strategy that includes a comprehensive disaster risk assessment, national strategies for emergency management agencies, and promoting data sharing collaborations. Enhancing public awareness and education on climate risks will be addressed through the development of a comprehensive communication strategy.

#### Component 2: National Early Warning System (NEWS) Development
- An early warning system will be developed to provide timely information about extreme weather events.
- The project will incorporate indigenous knowledge and practices into the NEWS and improve disaster risk knowledge in communities.

#### Component 3: Strengthening of National Meteorological Services
- Improving infrastructure for real-time monitoring of hydro-meteorological data, including hydrological and marine monitoring.
- Enhancing routine operations and forecasting services to support climate change adaptation efforts.
- Building capacity on network infrastructure resilience and setting up communication campaigns to raise awareness about EWS.

#### Component 4: National Emergency Telecommunication Plan (NETP)
- Developing a NETP for the country.
- Adopting the Common Alert Protocol (CAP) will allow for the dissemination of alerts in multiple languages, including sign language, and for people with disabilities. The project will also support the adoption of the Caribbean Disaster Emergency Management Agency (CDEMA) Model to ensure interoperability between national and regional emergency management systems.

#### Component 5: Community Early Warning System
- Enhancing community disaster response teams.
- Providing training and equipment for first responders, supporting the government in establishing dedicated financing mechanisms and structured early action measures in communities.

### Cambodia

The project proposes to improve and expand platforms for data integration by enhancing data management protocols and strengthening and generating risk knowledge at the national level. It will develop a comprehensive approach to disaster management, focusing on human resource development in EWS, particularly for women and those with disabilities.
- Administrative tools, communication channels, and platforms will be developed to ensure timely and accurate information reaches all stakeholders.

### Chad

At the sub-national level, the project will develop DRR capacity at the local community-based Early Warning Systems (EWS) and flood warning systems. The project will also provide training for EWS operators in emergency preparedness and response, focusing on the most vulnerable populations using the government's Shock Resistant Social Protection framework.
- Strengthening local capacities and integrating community-focused strategies, the project will support the development of a national climate risk management strategy.

The intervention in Chad falls under the four pillars of CDEMA, addressing underlying institutional and regulatory barriers through a cross-pillar approach to governance and coordination issues.

## The project will address gaps in the implementation of each of the four EHPilot pillars, identified in the Ethiopia Impact-Based Multi-Hazard Early Warning Action National Roadmap 2013-2030, complementing existing programs and projects and contributing to the EHWP objectives. The project will support and ensure coordination with national Pillar lead and anchor organizations (PLOs) and other relevant stakeholders (NGOs, CSOs), including international civil society organizations (CSO) supporting the EHWI initiative in Ethiopia.

### A national disaster risk database will be created, coordinated by the Ethiopian Disaster Risk Management Authority (EDRMA). The project will support EDRMA to develop multi-hazard data and risk analysis and strengthen the vulnerability profiles in priority areas with comparison and analysis of hazard, exposure and vulnerability. National and sub-national development actors (SDA) will be supported developing insurance and coverage in East and South-East areas of the country, improve data sharing, and develop specific gender-based VSL focusing on women, elderly and children.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 11

## Media communication channels will be developed according to types of audience, including vulnerability and gender-based issues. The project will build capacities of communications specialists to address media, and of journalists to communicate about disaster risk, provide media content about disaster risk, identify mitigation measures and anticipatory actions, and promote early warning systems. The project will also support the development by the Ethiopian Red Cross Society (ERCS) and EDRCM who will develop multi-hazard Anticipatory Action plans linked to a national early warning system. These plans will be developed in line with the National Early Warning System agreement on multi-hazard alert thresholds, development of community-based early-warnings systems in priority areas, stockpiling and prepositioning of essential emergency stock at national level and in identified priority areas (food and non-food items), including the identification of key actors for anticipatory action as part of emergency preparedness and response planning, integrated into social protection mechanisms.

### The project will strengthen governance and coordination through development and implementation of protocols and relevant mechanisms, including protocols and SOPs for data sharing among MWEWS agencies to enhance collaboration and regular exchange of information. This will be achieved by establishing a multi-stakeholder platform for data exchange, establish Memoranda of Understanding (MoUs) between authorities for early warnings, and create protocols for data sharing. The project will additionally enhance coordination among stakeholders in developing MWEWS plans and establishing national-level emergency preparedness and response planning. Coordination at the sub-national and sub-tranational levels will be undertaken along with strengthening of community disaster management committees.

#### A robust tracking system for assessing hazard impacts and future risks will be established. It will expand the necessary location-specific and targeted forecasts to all areas are affected by hazards. The project will address challenges in accessing data, including lack of harmonized data collection systems, limited availability of data, inconsistent methodologies and parameters in Fiji, including disaster data; creating a centralized database system to store risk information across sectors and disaggregated by gender, age, and disability; and improving infrastructure to collect and analyze the data. The project will also establish an early warning system for floods and droughts at national level locations. The project will upgrade observation networks, including Automatic Weather Station (AWS) installations, satellite imagery, and remote sensing systems in Somalia. It will also develop a flood forecasting model and expand location-specific predictions and develop a scientifically based drought risk map for Fiji. A drought monitoring system for health and food security will be established. It will strengthen the use of AI and a centralized database for forecasting.

#### The project will support the implementation of IoT broadcasts and help establish a regulatory framework for mobile manufacturers to support, specifically focusing on automated systems and mobile coverage in rural areas and for fishermen. The project will also develop an early warning system for floods and droughts at national level locations, automate EWS communication dissemination and improve warnings through mass messaging, utilize traditional knowledge and local languages, and provide training for disaster risk managers and community members to respond to early warnings based on previous successful implementations, focusing on how they access and act on received messages. Traditional and local knowledge will be incorporated into the local EWS, as well as reviewing and integrating it with other existing systems in Somalia.

#### Somalia's E-WALL project activities contribute directly to the realization of the proposed activities under Somalia’s National Roadmap for Implementation of EWS4R 2014-2027. The project will develop a comprehensive Risk Assessment Framework including hazard risk assessment methodology harmonised with regional approaches and international frameworks, as well as an early warning system that is tailored to national needs and priorities at the national level and applied/interpreted and translated for critical consideration of key vulnerability factors. A database of disaster impact data loss and damage tracking to also be established. The project will develop disaster risk knowledge management systems and tools to support decision-making processes, including a dashboard that will identify specific geographic areas, sectors and groups, tailored for EWS and DRM plans and local risk management plans. Climate risk summaries of key vulnerability factors will be developed which are accessible for national and local levels.

**Page 15**

The project will support building capacity and coordination for applying improved disaster risk knowledge at national level through long-term training and enhancing public awareness and education through a national campaign to empower communities.

* Improve data collection, analysis and exchange between federal line ministries, sectoral offices and regional bodies.
* Strengthen the harmonization of early warning data collection, analysis and exchange between federal line ministries, sectoral offices and regional bodies as well as improving data automation, modeling and forecasting of early warning. It will undertake activities to improve the interoperability of early warning information exchanged with neighboring countries and other relevant stakeholders.

This includes early warning communication strategies and SOPs being established for early warning information, communication and dissemination services (EWS), including broadcasting services (BWS) and radio; and BWS and CAP will establish comprehensive infrastructure networks and early warning dissemination services and equipment which cover simulation exercises, CAPI adoption, telecommunication service provider agreements and other related activities.

The project will emphasize the building of local preparedness capacities. The project will support inclusive, people-centered EWS by bolstering local networks to receive and disseminate information and last mile connectivity. Finally, the project will enhance preparedness for timely responses and early actions by improving response and recovery disaster management systems and mechanisms, including strengthening community-based disaster risk reduction (DRR) preparedness measures and plans for district levels, as well as engaging policy, legislation and strategies to integrate Anticipatory Action and preparedness, forecast based plans and protocols/EAPs and SDGs.

Climate change adaptation

The MCF will establish and strengthen the role of MHIWs in informed decision-making as a foundation for risk-informed development programs. It is aligned with the Agenda for Sustainable Development 25 specifically SDG 1 and SDGs 1, 2, 9, 13, 17 and 20. The project will support the implementation of national disaster risk reduction strategies and frameworks towards achievement of the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015-2030, particularly in line with the priorities of the Sendai call for action. It contributes specifically to target G of the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction." The project contributes to the priorities identified in Article B of the Paris Agreement" by supporting the following: a) reducing exposure and vulnerability to climate change; b) avoiding or reversing irreversible permanent loss and damage; c) comprehensive risk assessment and management; and d) resilience of communities, households and individuals.

In sum, the project supports achieving global targets in reducing exposure to climate changes impacts, with a strong focus on those most vulnerable. It will lay the foundations for further projects and the supporting mechanisms that are needed to achieve these goals. The project will also contribute to the achievement of the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015-2030 and global facilities and networks for technical and financial support will be strengthened. Implementation strategies and approaches from past projects and initiatives will feed into effective and pragmatic project design, which address the unique environmental, social and economic conditions in each country. The project will also contribute to addressing challenges and concerns of SDGs and ULOCs, those with limited infrastructure on fragile and conflict affected states.

# B. PROJECT PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1. Project Description

### 4. Climate Hazards and Associated Risks (Impacts, Exposures and Vulnerabilities)

#### 4.1. Global Context

- Approximately half the global population of 3.3 to 3.6 billion people live in countries or regions that are highly sensitive to climate hazards.
- These areas are already experiencing multiple climate hazards concurrently and are unable to undertake anticipatory action and risk reduction measures at scale.
- The acceleration of climate risks is outpacing the financial flows towards adaptation, the requirements of which are estimated to be 5 to 10 times higher and will increase to US$360–340 billion by 2030 and US$780–900 billion by 2050.

#### 4.2. Regional Context

- The Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) region has seen the strongest warming trend of about 0.7°C per decade on record, warming faster than global averages with temperatures projected to rise by 3.5 to 6.5°C by 2100.
- Prolonged droughts, increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as hurricanes, heavy rainfall, heatwaves, and floods are common in the region.
- The Caribbean islands have been particularly affected with higher temperatures and lower humidity which have triggered wildfires in the region. Sea levels have risen at a higher rate than global average, leading to saltwater intrusion into freshwater supplies on the island of Cuba, and landslides triggered by heavy rains which also caused human displacement.

#### 4.3. Country Context

- The Caribbean islands are particularly vulnerable as they are small islands with limited resources.
- Small islands have limited capacity for adaptation due to their size and lack of resources.
- The impacts of climate change on the Caribbean region include increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, sea-level rise, saltwater intrusion into freshwater supplies, and damage to coastal infrastructure.

#### 4.4. Project Specifics

- In Antigua and Barbuda, there are several changes leading to (see Appendix A for further details):
    - Increase in rainfall and temperature extremes which increase the risk of flooding, landslides, and mudflows.
    - Intensification of droughts reduce water quantity and quality.
    - Increased temperatures expand the range of disease-transmitting vectors (dengue and malaria).

- In Ecuador climate risks include (see Appendix A for further details):
    - Increase in rainfall and temperature extremes which increase the risk of flooding, landslides, and mudflows.
    - Intensification of droughts reduce water quantity and quality.
    - Increased temperatures expand the range of disease-transmitting vectors (dengue and malaria).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 14

## In the South-West Pacific, TCs, floods, droughts, sea level rise and ocean acidification have had significant impacts on people, economies and ecosystems. The region is influenced by both the El Niño-Southern Oscillation (ENSO) and the Indian Ocean Dipole (IOD)[20]. Most of the South-West Pacific shows upper-ocean (-0.70°C) warming since 1993, with rates exceeding -0.5°C per decade in some areas[21]. The region is also experiencing a general increase in tropical cyclone frequency and intensity. The western and central tropical Pacific, and south and north mid-latitude are also higher than the global mean. Observed sea surface temperatures (SST) have increased by 0.35°C since 1982[22]. This warming is associated with a general increase in TCs frequency and intensity over the South-West Pacific. This drying trend is expected to continue in the coming decades, except in parts of western and equatorial Pacific. Heavy rainfall events will increase in the western tropical Pacific and the higher evapotranspiration is expected to amplify or partially offset this effect[23]. The region is also experiencing a general decrease in sea level since 1993 with rates exceeding -0.5 cm per decade in some areas, particularly in the South-West Pacific under the 2°C global warming and above scenario. The intensity and frequency of TCs will increase and, together with sea level rise will contribute to storm surges leading to extreme coastal water levels in most regions in the Pacific Islands[24].

## In these regional changes lead to (see Appendix I for further details):

- Current warming of 0.2°C per decade is expected to accelerate, reaching 1.3–3.5°C above pre-industrial temperatures by mid-century.
- Greatest impacts are due to high tides ($500 million in annual exposed assets – 5% GDP), the most intense of which will be experienced in Fiji and Vanuatu[25].
- Increases in rainfall intensity will increase the likelihood of 1/300-year floods with expected losses of $2 billion[26].
- Drought and coastal salination due to S&R land surge affects crop production, leading to food imports.

## Climate change is also expected to have significant impacts on water resources. The region has experienced a decline in rainfall over the last 50 years, which has led to infrastructure damage and crops and has contributed to waterborne diseases. Droughts have caused rice failures leading to food and water insecurity while storms have damaged infrastructure and crops and lead to loss of lives. Landslides are also expected to increase due to heavy rainfall events.

## Most African countries will face increasingly high temperature climates, with increased mean and extreme temperatures, including heat waves[27]. In the Horn of Africa, there has been a decrease in precipitation since 1980s and in southern Africa, decreased precipitation in North Africa under 2°C global warming. The intensity of heavy rainfall is also expected to increase, increasing exposure to pluvial and fluvial flooding across all but north and southwestern Africa[28].

## In Chad drought has been a major problem for the last two decades.

- Successive droughts have affected the food security and livelihoods of households across all large swathes of the country, with 1.5 million people affected in 1981–85, and 2.4 million people affected in 2009–2010. The most recent drought has affected over 3.7 million people[29].
- Floods (inland and perennial) resulted in 123,088 homeless persons. These disasters led to a total of 10,556 recorded deaths, with only 67% were a result of viral or bacterial disease diseases such as cholera, meningitis and dengue fever[30].
- Climate change is expected to increase temperatures [2°C to 4.8°C (very likely range) by 2080], with more very hot days leading to greater incidences of heat stress and reduced agricultural labour production.
- More frequent rainfall will lead to great extremes of drought and flood, with reductions in yields of maize and other crops.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 15

## B

### Somalia: Climate Change and Extreme Weather Events

#### Economic Impacts of Extreme Weather Events in Somalia

- **Droughts:** Substantial economic damage, with a 1/9 return period leading to losses exceeding US$1 billion.
- **Floods:** A 1-in-100 year flood event resulting in losses over US$531 million.

#### Climate Change and Extreme Weather Events

- **Flooding:** Has a significant impact on GDP, with a flood event with a 1/27 return period causing a 3.3% productivity loss.
- **Heat Stress:** Results in significant losses due to its impact on labor productivity and livestock yields.
- **Infrastructural Damage:** Exceeds US$50 million annually as a result of extreme weather events.

#### Climate Change

- **Increased Frequency, Intensity, and Duration of Droughts:** Likely to lead to increased flash flooding. In 2013 alone, over 248 million people were affected, with over 1.2 million displaced.
- **Dry Spells:** Expected to become more frequent in the coming decades.
- **Increased Frequency, Persistence, and Intensity of Droughts:** Will increase by the end of the 21st century at all spatial scales under Representative Concentration Pathways (RCP) 2.6, 4.5, and 8.5.

#### Climate Change Impacts

- **Prolonged Dry Seasons:** Result in water scarcity, reduced agricultural productivity, food insecurity, and ultimately, famine.
- **Heatwaves:** Daily temperature maximums have increased over the last two decades and are projected to continue rising by up to 2°C per decade. Parts of Somalia will experience extreme heat, and the southern and some parts of the northern areas are projected to experience very high heat.

## In Asia, annual temperature has increased in the past century with large increases and extremes in West and Central Asia, resulting in an increasing trend of growing season length across the continent and causing strong, more frequent and longer heat-waves in South and East Asia. There is a decreasing trend of the South Asian summer monsoon precipitation over the last 50 years, which has been linked to increased evaporation rates and decreased precipitation, and increased evapotranspiration in West and Central Asia can have contributed to drought conditions and decreased surface runoff.

### Climate projections suggest rising temperatures increase the likelihood of the threat of heat-waves across Asia, droughts and an increase in extreme rainfall events. In South Asia, this is likely to lead to more frequent and longer-lasting heat waves and glacier melting in the Hindu Kush Himalaya region. A greater warming in North Asia, including the Tibetan Plateau, Central Asia and East Siberia, is expected to cause a significant decrease of snow cover over the mountainous areas in West and Central Asia with increased dryness of land. Elevation-dependent warming is expected to continue and lead to glaciers shrinking at varying rates among regions. Temperature rise will be strongest in winter in most regions, while it will be weakest during summer in South Asia.

### The wet bulb temperature, a measure of heat stress, is likely to approach critical health thresholds in West and Central Asia by the end of this century. In East Asia, the frequency and intensity of extreme heat-waves is likely to increase in Asia with a possible spread of part of South Asia experiencing heat-stress conditions in the future.

### A large percentage increase in annual precipitation is expected in South and North Asia, but decrease over the northwest part of India, Pakistan, Afghanistan, Central Asia and East Siberia. The frequency and intensity of heavy rainfall events will intensify and become more frequent in South, Southeast and East Asia. Monsoon land precipitation is likely to increase in East, Southeast and South Asia with a possible spread into parts of North Asia. There may be an overall decrease in flood frequency in monsoon regions in Southwest Asia, Peninsular India, eastern Africa and the northern half of the Andes® which without further mitigation efforts, will lead to continued loss of lives and infrastructure® On the other hand, there is a high likelihood that climate change will increase the frequency of extreme rainfall events, increasing the likelihood of glacial lake outbursts and significantly affecting water resources over time.

### In Cambodia these three changes have resulted in the following (see Appendix 1 for further details):

- The past 25 years saw more floods caused by heavy rains, droughts and lightning causing fewer deaths but higher damages.
- Rising temperatures and decreases in average rainfall during the dry season are expected to increase the number of heatwaves and extreme heat events.
- Increases in extreme rainfall during the monsoon is likely to increase riverine and flash flood risks;
- Which will be exacerbated by climate change, including increased evaporation, moisture and a deeper convective monsoon under climate change may potentially increase the risk.

To climate change and the need for humanitarian and development assistance. Furthermore, the expansion, shifts and intensification of climatic hazards due to climate change, leads to the exposure of rural communities and rapidly growing informal urban settlements, to both higher existing risks and novel risks they have never previously experienced. The case of droughts in East Africa is a good example of this. These communities are often characterized by poverty, interconnected and are likely to increase exposure and vulnerability to the impact of climate change induced sea level rise, increasing temperatures and more frequent extreme weather events.

Climate change has a disproportional impact on the poor and vulnerable who lack assets and access to information and resources and are therefore less able to anticipate, adapt or mitigate these impacts. Often these communities are reliant on natural resources for their livelihoods and are highly exposed to climate risks, including droughts, floods, extreme climate risks, including on small islands, along coastlines and in flood and land slide prone regions. Many live in un-regularized settlements and shanty towns and lack basic facilities and infrastructure. Furthermore, vulnerable groups within these communities often include women, children, the elderly, people with disabilities and those living with chronic conditions or disabilities. Often this leads to less access to receive information and respond to climate related disasters.

With functional communication networks allow people to communicate and provide for preparedness and response to multiple hazards (see Table 1), including early warning systems and information on projected weather patterns, and their potential interrelated impacts.

Discussions of adaptation in high risk and high vulnerability can negatively affect well-being and major setbacks in human and economic development, with long-term consequences to resilience building and well-being. MHEWS are a proven, effective and flexible climate change adaptation measure that saves lives and provides at least a tenfold return on investment. The cost per life saved is estimated to be US$100 million. Economic assessments in several countries have shown positive benefit-cost ratios for investments in weather and climate services between 1:4 to 1:36 from reducing extreme weather related economic losses, reduced humanitarian relief costs and maladaptation risk coping strategies.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V2.0 PAGE 18

## Table B: Integrating Hydro-meteorological Observations and MHEWS can help in adapting, mitigating and responding to climate change induced hazards

| Hazard | Type of Early Warning | Adaptation, Disaster Preparedness |
|---|---|---|
| Drought | Standardized Precipitation Index (SPI), Soil Moisture Content Index (SMCI), soil moisture content | Water conservation, drought emergency response plans. |
| Heat Wave | e.g., heat index, heat Bulb Globe Temperature (HGT), heat load intensity | Health warning systems, public awareness campaigns. |
| Shortened Growing Season | Growing Degree Days | Crop rotation, planting date adjustments, agricultural insurance programs. |
| Flood | River stage, discharge, rainfall-runoff, floodplain mapping | Flood zoning, evacuation plans. |
| Flash Flood | Real-time rainfall intensity, radar-estimated rainfall, rainwater runoff | Emergency response plans, public education, land use planning, emergency shelters. |
| Extreme Precipitation Event | Rainfall intensity, duration, return period | Storm water management, urban drainage systems, infrastructure resilience. |
| Variability | Precipitation time series, trend analysis | Diversified cropping systems, crop insurance programs. |
| Thunderstorm | Radar reflectivity, Doppler velocity, wind speed | Lightning protection, public awareness campaigns, emergency response plans, building codes. |
| Tropical Cyclone | Satellite imagery, wind speed, atmospheric pressure | Coastal storm surge forecasting, evacuation plans, building codes. |
| Storm Surge | Tide gauge data, wind speed, atmospheric pressure | Coastal storm surge forecasting, evacuation plans, building codes. |
| Sea Level Rise | Tide-gauge data, water level, coastal inundation models | Sea level rise projections, coastal protection measures, infrastructure assessments |

### Scenarios in the absence of proposed mitigation

Global mean surface temperature is 1.5°C unchanged, even if greenhouse gas emissions are substantially reduced in the near term.26 This can have unprecedented and potentially catastrophic outcomes on societal well-being.27 It will lead to regional changes in the intensity and frequency of weather and climate extremes generally scale with global warming and new modeling studies show that extreme events will become more intense and frequent, leading to significant changes in extremes on the global scale and for large regions.28

About 13 million people lose their lives annually due to climate-related impacts on health through pollution, extreme heat, floods, storms, droughts, and other hazards.29 In 2050, the world economy may lose up to 14% (USD 23 trillion) compared to what world GDP is projected to be in 2050, on account of climate change.

## Changes in climate and action on reducing greenhouse emissions is taken. The IPCC report highlights the acute food insecurity, malnutrition, displacement and mortality faced by communities in LDCs who are disproportionately affected due to poverty, governance challenges, limited access to basic resources and high levels of climate-sensitive livelihoods.

### 5th Assessment Report (AR5) of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC)

- Widespread across temperature increases and extremes, heavy precipitation events, significant increases in runoff and flood hazard.
- There is an increased probability of extreme droughts, precipitation deficits, and risks associated with water availability and access to drinking water.
- The global average surface temperature has increased by 1.0°C since the pre-industrial period (1850–1900).
- There is a 66% likelihood that the annual mean surface global temperature between 2021 and 2030 will exceed the 1.5°C “Level”.
- This highlights the need for global coverage of MRV and response systems which allow preparedness actions and preventative and anticipatory action to attenuate the impacts of climate-induced shocks.

### Goal: Adaptation

The objective of this MCF is to accelerate the design and implementation of the EWI4 initiative through coordinated global support involving all the key actors across the early warning value chain. This project, through its country interventions, will contribute to reducing the vulnerability of communities in LDCs who are disproportionately exposed to climate hazards, with a clear emphasis on women, poor and vulnerable groups within these communities.

### Key Messages

- The EWI4 initiative is designed to support countries in building their capacity for early warning systems.
- It will strengthen communication channels to ensure timely and effective dissemination of warnings and information to the last mile. This project will also prepare disaster management authorities to effectively use these early warnings and advisories for early and anticipatory actions which allow them to adapt, avoid, and facilitate recovery from climate-induced hazards.

### Underlying Assumptions

- The theory of change for this project is: Generation, delivery and adoption of multi-hazard early warning systems will lead to increased preparedness and response capacity in LDCs.
- The underlying assumption for the theory of change is: Improved, effective and timely disaster mitigation and response by national authorities will be achieved through the implementation of climate services supported by multilateral agencies and private entities.

### Establishing a coherent and effective early warning system

The EWI4 initiative will support countries in building their capacity for early warning systems. It will strengthen communication channels to ensure timely and effective dissemination of warnings and information to the last mile. This project will also prepare disaster management authorities to effectively use these early warnings and advisories for early and anticipatory actions which allow them to adapt, avoid, and facilitate recovery from climate-induced hazards.

### Key Messages

- The EWI4 initiative is designed to support countries in building their capacity for early warning systems.
- It will strengthen communication channels to ensure timely and effective dissemination of warnings and information to the last mile. This project will also prepare disaster management authorities to effectively use these early warnings and advisories for early and anticipatory actions which allow them to adapt, avoid, and facilitate recovery from climate-induced hazards.

### Underlying Assumptions

- The theory of change for this project is: Generation, delivery and adoption of multi-hazard early warning systems will lead to increased preparedness and response capacity in LDCs.
- The underlying assumption for the theory of change is: Improved, effective and timely disaster mitigation and response by national authorities will be achieved through the implementation of climate services supported by multilateral agencies and private entities.

This report is important to strengthen the internal mechanisms and capacities by which institutions at the national level develop and communicate early warning messages, and how these messages are received, understood and utilized by local responders, communities and populations. Some of the root causes and barriers associated with these aspects of MHEWs and that need to be addressed are described below:

Poor strategic planning for long-term financial support to MHEWS

67 There is insufficient capital at the global, country and community levels to establish and maintain the observation networks needed to provide early warning services. The costs of establishing new systems or upgrading existing ones with basic economic observations are also difficult to gather in real time because suitable cross-shoring and other technologies are understood. These observations are needed to improve our understanding of multi-hazard exposure and vulnerability, as well as to support climate change adaptation planning.

68 At the global level, there is a lack of coordination between countries on how to address these issues. Some countries have limited financial resources and human/institutional capacity to establish and maintain observation networks that are needed for early warning services. The SOF has been established to address this resource constraint in SSS and LDCs, and this project will ensure that the SOF is able to provide support to countries in these regions.

69 At the country level, there are a number of challenges associated with establishing and maintaining observation networks. Some countries face similar challenges in operating and maintaining their hydro-meteorological and communication networks. These include limited institutional capacity, lack of funding for maintenance, and inadequate training. Increasing climate risk constraints opportunities to raise necessary public budgets. This often leads to the deterioration of existing institutions or the use of equipment that fails to meet required standards, creating challenges in quality control and interoperation.

70 Efficiency and effectiveness of early response efforts are constrained by lack of forecast-based planning and financial resources as the grassroots and municipal levels. This is critical as local governments are often mandated with maintaining and operating these systems. The costs associated with establishing new observation networks or other resources can be rapidly delayed before and during emergencies is a significant barrier to early response and anticipatory action. These challenges need to be addressed by strengthening institutional capacity, including the mechanisms, systems and capacities needed for anticipatory action. Funding is also needed for the actual implementation of anticipatory action, including within national systems.

71 We strongly recommend that countries should consider establishing, rehabilitating and/or maintaining their soft and hard infrastructure for early warning services. In some countries, it has become challenging to effectively coordinate and manage these networks due to a lack of institutional capacity and limited financial frameworks to allow the funding of EWS, particularly those that are maintained by local actors. Such strategies can enhance the capacities and resources to develop comprehensive and compelling proposals that help to address the challenges associated with establishing and maintaining observation networks in different contexts.

The absence of a comprehensive gap analysis at the national level prevents these countries from benefiting from the global and regional support architecture and facilities, such as GCF or the Global Environment Facility (GEF), or from global financing campaigns which target donors across different sectors.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 21

Limited political, financial and national support for country-level actions for a comprehensive MHEWS

Technical, scientific and institutional barriers prevent the generation and development of reliable, meaningful and actionable weather and climate information, as well as its effective communication and use by people, government, communities and institutions. These barriers are compounded by limited access to data and information on the value of MHEWS and its associated components, including among policy makers. Many LDCs and SIDS fail to leverage regional facilities and partnerships for necessary and sustained technical assistance due to limited human capital and capacity constraints. The lack of a comprehensive MHEWS strategy at country level is also reflected in the absence of collaboration with key initiatives and frameworks which provide contextual operational support across a wide range of hazards. This includes the World Meteorological Organization (WMO), the Global Framework for Climate Services, regional centres of excellence including national and regional academic institutions, initiatives and projects.

Inadequate policies and strategies for inter-agency coordination, data sharing and governance for preparedness and response.

Inconsistencies in policies, institutional structure and mechanisms that govern the collection and sharing of meteorological/hydrological vulnerability and exposure data can pose a barrier to implementation of international agreements and frameworks. These inconsistencies are compounded by limited access to climate information from public or private financing and fail to provide suitable structures to strengthen inclusive, people-centered approaches to early warnings. Capacity constraints in many countries also limit the ability to effectively implement these policies, especially as “paradigm shift” needs. There are barriers within regions and countries which prevent the effective use of available climate information and EWS (CWE) for line agencies for different sectors, and those responsible for national disaster response.

In most countries, ministries or departments with independent administrative structures and processes are involved in the generation, analysis and use of climate information. Meteorological and hydrological services often reside in different ministerial portfolios, which can lead to a lack of coordination between these services and other line agencies for expertise, for example health and agriculture. Inadequate and inefficient mechanisms for data sharing and coordinated action limit the ability to effectively implement international agreements and frameworks such as the Sendai Framework and Anticipatory Action* or Forecast based Financing* for resilient sectors and areas**. Furthermore, the Information and Communication Technology Ministry and telecommunication regulatory authority and private telecommunications companies are often responsible for the development of national early warning systems (EWS) and the transmission from observation stations, dissemination of early warnings and alerts and obtaining feedback from targeted communities. This can lead to a lack of coordination between these services and other line agencies for expertise, especially when there is limited institutionalization in national EWS. Their exclusions result in missed opportunities to take advantage of the growth in networks and services.

The inability to access data or facilities below the provincial or district level, de-linking the generation and use of CWEs. The inadequacies and deficiencies in SoFs is a barrier to the dissemination of CWE using public and private communications infrastructure. SoFs are often linked to the development of easily understood, targeted, tailored and

**Inadequate technical and scientific capacity**

NNMS in developing countries face a number of technical and scientific challenges and constraints in the generation and use of early warning information. These include limited access to data, lack of infrastructure for data collection and transmission, and insufficient human resources with the necessary skills and expertise.

NNMS often have insufficient training and capacities required to develop and run models locally. There is therefore a need to develop in-house skills in these areas, but also, to have the parallel development of CWS through regional and global networks. This would allow NNMS to access more advanced tools and technologies for data collection and analysis.

Institutions to support weather and seasonal forecasts as much as possible through provision of data to NMSs and other agencies, including those in developing countries, are also required. These institutions should be able to provide other forecasts available through global producing centres, for e.g., the UK Met Office (UKMO), the European Centre for Medium-Range Weather Forecast (ECMWF), and the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA).

The primary challenge is the lack of capacity in developing countries to generate and use data. The generation of data to inform anticipatory and early action. In many countries the subject course for hydrometeorology is not existed, resulting in a systemic barrier in the development of skilled professionals to support met services, much less at a sub-national level where they are better access to local information and contexts. Agencies need to be in a position to conduct local level monitoring and analysis of data to inform early action. This is particularly important for the identification of hazard exposure, social vulnerabilities and disaggregated distribution of assets, access to services and infrastructure at risk. While regional centres have a role in making first order estimates, it is national institutions that must undertake more detailed assessments of the risks posed by climate change.

The lack of capacity in developing countries to generate and use data also limits their ability to provide early warning information. This enables outtake and foresight on the possible impacts of climate change. The number of countries that have accessible, understandable and usable data is very low. In many cases, the level of data access is limited by the fact that the levels represent the lowest of all Sendai Framework Target 5 indicators. In large part this is a result of a lack of field capacities within hydrometeorological systems as well as those related to data processing through new technologies. For example, in many countries, there are no hydrometeorological services or institutions that can provide information on soil and hydrologic variables such as those pertaining to evaporation (land use and cover), soil profiles (illustrated conductivity), land use/cover, etc.

The collection of this data requires programming skills and use of programming languages and tools. Skills are also required in analyzing spatial data and hydrological modeling using tools/software and cloud based tools which leverage large data infrastructure without the costs associated with traditional computing. The development of Artificial Intelligence (AI) tools are also lacking. It is these innovative technologies that paradoxically would allow met services to move into a more cost-efficient manner existing gaps in EWS coverage and in some cases can provide solutions to data gaps.

**Gaps in observational data are particularly severe and persistent in SDS and LGs and even in middle income economies. This is a bottleneck at the start of the EWS value chain about which the international community has been concerned**

The lack of technical and financial capacities at a national level has acted as a barrier to adoption of innovative digital technologies for early warning systems (EWS). The use of mobile networks can provide the means for the development of consistent and reliable field monitoring such as the use of IoT-based sensors to autonomously measure and transmit data using mobile networks. Generation of timely early warnings, particularly in remote areas, requires use of sensors and communication infrastructure that are not always available or affordable.

Mobile networks have the potential to overcome many of these challenges by providing a cost-effective means for real-time warning to local communities as well as transmission of data to NMWS for further analysis and interpretation. Nonetheless, there remain several technical and financial barriers to the adoption of mobile networks for EWSs that have a shortage of ground personnel and for communities without the necessary technical skills.

Effective local actions based on MNWIS are provided by a number of barriers:

* Lack of capacity at national level
* Inadequate infrastructure
* Limited access to information

Many countries have significant gaps in telecommunication networks which exclude some of the most climate vulnerable communities in rural areas, especially nomadic and migrating pastoral groups and forest dwelling communities. Access to early warning systems is often limited by lack of electricity or access to power radios. Inadequate capacities for translation, contextual rephrasing and packaging of early warning information and advisories for multiple channels can prevent end-users from receiving such information. Most mobile networks are not designed with the needs of vulnerable groups in mind. Messages are often too complex, use technical content and tailor risk communications to diverse audiences, and tend to use technical terms in major national languages. In addition, messages are often sent without consideration of the local context or audience. Messages are often not timely, actionable or understood by local actors. Warning messages and advisories usually lack the local context and are not tailored to the different needs of target communities, including the elderly, persons with disabilities and health impairments. The limited access to information is a major barrier for effective early warning systems. The provision of early warnings often fails to address the different needs of end users arising from differential access to information, resources and infrastructure. This leaves the most vulnerable members of communities to access early warnings or take appropriate action.

Inefficient national and local coordination in the development and implementation of anticipatory actions:

* There is a lack of national and/or sub-national level plans outlining cost-effective action and development use of triggers which determine when to act, roles of different actors in the early warning value chain, to define the relevant and most appropriate and effective types of anticipatory actions for these actors. Under one third of countries globally have recognized the need for anticipatory actions and have developed national or sub-national level plans such as the Monitor, Target G indicator 64 suggesting they may not have these plans. Furthermore, the percentage of local governments that have integrated anticipatory action into their national system is low.

Equitable and meaningful decision-making opportunities and roles for women in the design and management of MNWISs related to climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction (DRR) are limited in many countries and regions services. Inadequate resources at the local institution level to provide adequate training for community members on how to use MNWISs and to develop follow-up warning messages with local action. B (2). Theory of change narrative and diagram

Addressing the barriers to effective end-to-end MHEWS requires engagements at different levels of organizations, governments, communities, and individuals. The project will support countries in developing their own national strategies for new and innovative technologies while ensuring the interventions engage meaningfully with local communities, their own knowledge, understanding and coping strategies for climate related disasters.

This project has two broad outputs and associated activities which address the barriers described above. Output 1 is designed to build individual tools to set up and accelerate technical and operational support to countries with the ambition to enhance their MHEWS coverage and capacities. This output will catalyse regional and global collaboration among actors active in EWS support, including through thematic workshops and other events. The outputs of this project are expected to be scalable and replicable, leveraging the comparative advantage of diverse actors to deliver shared value to countries aligned to the EWMAS call to action. The project will also seek to strengthen the institutional mechanisms for MHEWS implementation and infrastructure to avoid fragmented, discontinuous, and unsupported support to countries in improving end-to-end MHEWS.

Output 2 ties together the four key components of the EWMAS EAP by addressing barriers in observations, risk management, early warning dissemination, and response. The outputs are designed to address the gaps at the national level; these are designed to ensure effective use of MHEWS for disaster preparedness and response. Hence the investments will be made in a way that ensures the development of impact based and actionable early warnings for early and anticipatory action by national actors on the ground. Engagements with the governments will also seek strengthening of institutional mechanisms and governance for more efficient and effective use of MHEWS, including through capacity building and knowledge sharing. The project is designed to improve ensured usable multi-hazard risk information is transferred to end-users, including women and vulnerable groups within communities, along with guidance on effective mitigation actions to reduce impacts (losses and damage). Finally, the work will focus on supporting countries in developing their own national strategies for new and innovative technologies that hybridize new technologies and local knowledge.

The project will seek to mobilize technical expertise and financial flows to meet the needs of countries (particularly LDCs and SIDS) to establish, operate and maintain their national end-to-end MHEWS. To do so, it will establish regional and national collaborations, multiple funding instruments and complementary mechanisms that maximize country ownership and sustainability. The project will support countries in developing their own national strategies for the development of national roadmaps, investment pipelines, and approaches to sustain national EWS through state and private sector partnerships. It will also seek to ensure that the technical and financial support provided is aligned with the technical and financial support needs for end-to-end MHEWS, translating country visions into concrete investments.

The project has two broad outputs and associated activities which address the barriers described above. These outputs and activities are mutually supportive and reinforcing (Figure 1).

## Outputs:

### Output 1: Enhancing enabling conditions, both globally and regionally, to promote country-level actions towards the EWB4R goals.
This output will address barriers pertaining to both support for country level actions as well as gaps in global and regional policies, standards and inter-agency coordination to scale up coherent national strategies for end-to-end climate risk management. This will be achieved through enhanced technical assistance, operational support, collaboration and knowledge sharing and the existing global support architecture and will be led by WMO and UNDRR with strong support from other regional partners.

### Output 2: Accelerating actions towards EWB4R goals.
This will implement national level projects and priority investments listed under the four pillars in the EAP based on the context of each participating country. This will be led by UNDP, WMO, UNDRR, ITU, IFC, in collaboration with other key partners.

### OBJECTIVES

1. Enhanced coordination and technical support for EWS
2. Strengthened NMS to support EWS implementation
3. Strengthened knowledge management and lessons learned

### PROJECT OUTPUTS

- Output 1: London global, regional and national support for the development of EWS
- Output 2: Improved disaster risk governance and preparedness for communities
- Output 3: Prior strategic planning for long-term financial sustainability

### BARRIERS AND RISKS

Barrier 1.1: Insufficient technical and scientific capacities in the participating countries to develop, implement, and sustain EWS
Barrier 2.1: Insufficient disaster risk knowledge for affected communities
Barrier 2.2: Insufficient disaster risk knowledge for affected communities
Barrier 3.1: Inadequate or absence of dissemination at the sub-national level
Barrier 3.2: Inadequate local-level disaster preparedness and planning

### ASSUMPTIONS

Assumption 1: The EWS will be fully integrated into the national disaster management system.
Assumption 2: National governments, including government, multilateral, non-government, private sector actors are committed to continuous improvement of the EWS in their country.

B.3. Project/programme description

This project addresses the four key elements of MHEWS (1) disaster risk knowledge; (2) detection, observation, monitoring, analysis and forecasting of hazards; (3) warning dissemination mechanisms and communication; and (4) preparedness and response and recovery.

Effective MHEWS depend on coordination with and across different sectors at all multiple levels and require feedback mechanisms which allow for their improvement. The involvement of both national and local governments and communities is essential to the successful implementation and sustainability of coordinated MHEWS.

Addressing persistent gaps in establishing and managing an end-to-end MHEWS at the country level is a key requirement. The project proposes to address these gaps by building on existing initiatives, including those related to early warning systems, climate information and EWS. These programmes and initiatives in the participating countries are summarized in Table 2 in the appendices.

The proposal also recognizes that an effective end-to-end MHEWS can only be built on time-lag and time-step integrated approaches which require a long-term perspective. The project will support the development of a national hydro-meteorological strategy, including the management of different elements of a national EWS value chain; and a 10-year long-range planning to maintain the necessary hydrometeorological infrastructure, and community level capacities to receive, understand and respond to EWS through risk information systems. The project will also support the development of an integrated approach for DRR and SDGs, can be built only through long-term support; the project will therefore strengthen the existing architecture and create the environment in which countries can better utilize timely, long-term, uninterrupted support.

Component/Output 1: Enhancing enabling conditions globally and regionally to promote country-level actions towards the targets

Output 1.1 aims to scale up accelerated global and regional support for national efforts to increase Multi-Hazard EWS (MHEWS) capacity, coverage and performance.

Three key activities are emphasized: Activity 1.1 focuses on enhancing global and regional enabling conditions; Activity 1.2 is a multi-year initiative that will build upon the success of the GEF-6 Early Warning Initiative; Activity 1.2 emphasizes the strengthening of monitoring and evaluation (M&E) processes to support EWF4R implementation, including the development of an M&E framework for EWS in the context of climate change adaptation. The dissemination of lessons learned to inform national progress on EWS. Together, these outputs are designed to improve the effectiveness and reach of EWS ensuring that more communities are better prepared for multiple hazards.

these global and regional activities aim to establish robust, sustainable, and effective national EWS globally, ensuring that countries are well-prepared to respond to and mitigate the impacts of disasters.

### Activity 1.1: Coordination and Technical Support for EWSA Implementation

Activity 1.1 focuses on enhancing coordination and providing comprehensive joint technical support to ensure the effective implementation of the EWSA initiative. This involves the development and application of normative tools, guidance materials, and training modules. The four Pillar lead agencies will collaborate to create detailed and standardized guidelines, training materials, and strategic orientation documents for the EWSA initiative.

### Activity 1.2: Capacity Building

This sub-activity will result in the development of pillar-specific and joint inter-pillar guidelines, tools, training curricula, and methodologies, building on what exists and accounting for variations in regional and national contexts. Strategic Orientation Documents (SODs) will be developed to provide a framework for EWSA implementation.

### Activity 1.3: Early Warning System Analysis

This sub-activity will also focus on advancing the application of AI-based tools within the framework of EWS, offering practical examples and identifying opportunities to enhance the initiative's impact. This sub-activity will include the development of an EWSAI Toolkit, which will include tools, models, applications, and methodologies for early warning system analysis.

B

understanding that they need to invest in accessible risk communication, tailored for the specific needs of persons with disabilities. The global normative guidance will provide a comprehensive framework to ensure that all individuals, including persons with disabilities, have equal access to vital information to protect themselves from disasters. The guidance will be developed through an inclusive process involving key stakeholders such as WMO Members, civil society and persons with disabilities. It will cover the development of accessible risk communication strategies, including the use of appropriate formats for disaster warning messages, accessible formats and inclusion of persons with disabilities in the development of risk communication strategies. The guidance will also include a set of tools to support implementation, such as a checklist to ensure full operational readiness through SOPs and emergency broadcasting plans; implement GCF (in partnership with ITU); strengthen editorial capacity to contribute to early warning chain ahead of disasters and cover DRR topics in terms of peace in post-disaster situations. The guidance will be developed by WMO and partners, and will be validated and suitably tailored to the context of the seven participating countries to inform the activities under Activity 11.2.

In addition to this, WMO will undertake a review of its guidance on hazard types and update as needed to enable the use of multi-modal ensemble and probabilistic forecast to issue warnings and communicate uncertainty. As part of the review and update process, WMO will also develop a set of tools to support implementation of the guidance. The guidance is packaged and disseminated with WMO Members to enable their uptake and use.

Sub-Activity 11.2: Accelerate Technical Support to Countries for EWS implementation

Building on the work under Sub-Activities 10.3, 10.4 and 10.5 of Activity 10, this sub-activity focuses on accelerating technical support for countries implementing EWS, focusing on providing operational and technical assistance for the development and implementation of EWR national roadmaps, supporting countries to test the capacities developed under Sub-Activities 10.3, 10.4 and 10.5, and ensuring that these are sustained. The focus will be focused on scaling up and enhancing the work of the Tropical Cyclone Programme (TCP), the Severe Weather Forecasting Programme (SWFP) and the Early Warning System for Droughts and Floods (EWSDF). Regional expert groups and expert teams will be engaged to reach more countries with the above mentioned capacity development programs to address a broader range of hazards, and provide better support to WMO Members.

Use these tools to ensure that EWS are developed in alignment with international standards and best practices outlined in sub-Activity 11.3. Pillar leads, WMO Regional Climate Centres (RCCs) together with other global, regional and national agencies will co-design and implement analytical frameworks and models for the development of early warning systems for specific hazards. The tools will also include guidance on damage, and effective mitigation action to provide the basis of impact-based forecast warning services (IWSWs). These tools will be developed in close collaboration with WMO Members and partners. The tools will include providing and helping to design systems for climate and multi-hazard risk data sharing, archiving and access using open-source software and scripts for data cleaning, analysis, visualisation and modelling. Regional centres will also be responsible for the development of IWSWs for specific hazards in their regions. These tools will also provide guidance on building and triggers to inform early/anticipatory actions. Wherever feasible, the regional centres will explore co-production with local communities and other stakeholders including civil society organizations, private sector organisations. The capacity of WMO, UNDRR (IFRC) will be augmented to enhance the quality of support to countries via Output 5. In particular, the existing EWSW supportive frameworks such as SWFP, TCP, SWF and RIs will be reviewed and updated in line with the new guidance on hazard types and update as needed.

The sub-activity will support the integration of EWS into national priorities, roadmaps, and financing strategies. Technical support and coordination across all pillars of the EAP which relate to governance and legal frameworks for EWS and early warning governance and implementation will be provided by WMO. The technical architecture will be developed through implementing global financing campaigns and consultations with various partners and donors that will take place throughout the implementation of the project. The sub-activity will also develop methods to facilitate the development of national roadmaps, including the identification of key stakeholders and actors at all levels to DMEs to fast-track access to GCF financing for EWS strengthening.
