### Activities 3.2 - Strengthening ESWAI to support GEF/GEF II

The environmental sustainability global M&E framework for ESWAI will be implemented and rolled out, including updates and enhanced metrics available on the dedicated dashboard to track progress of the initiative globally and at country levels across all pillars. The initiative will also develop a reporting framework for reporting EWS performance based on a global reporting framework. The framework will include annual reporting on progress on the ESWAI targets at the UNFCCC COP, Work will also be supported on the development of an index capturing the inter-pillar elements and other key indicators to track progress towards the ESWAI targets. A particular target G. Efforts will focus on: development and implementation of comprehensive tools to evaluate, communicate and disseminate results, including a global environmental sustainability (GES) composite indicator and index system for benchmarking global progress; developing and consulting the ESWAI M&E framework across pillars, considerate of inter-pillar elements, updating and validating pillar assessment and score cards to regularly track country coverage and quality of country data into the ESWAIDashboardand maintain the dashboard, including the creation of a database.

#### Sub-Activity 3.2.1 - Operationalizing the GES M&E maturity index

This sub-activity will develop an environmental sustainability index system for benchmarking global progress across all pillars and at the inter-pillar level. It will update and validate assessments and score cards to track country progress across the EWS value chain, and provide diagnostics support to countries on monitoring EWS effectiveness. This sub-activity will also develop a GES M&E maturity index to assess the capacity of countries to implement the ESWAI. The initiative will also track progress on completed assessments and ensure that country score cards are updated regularly. Moreover, WEF will continue to monitor and report on GES M&E progress in its annual Global Risks Report. These activities will strengthen global information and reporting systems on GEF’s M&E practices for informed action.

#### Sub-Activity 3.2.1 - Strengthen global ESWAI M&E and reporting to enable regular program updates

This sub-activity will develop a comprehensive framework for the GES M&E system across pillars, considerate of inter-pillar elements, it will conduct data collection and related monitoring actions on field and develop and publish studies, reports, other knowledge materials to showcase outcomes of framework monitoring, including annual Global Risks Report.

## Sub-Activity 1.3: Development of Knowledge Management Framework that guides Learning and Exchange

### Sub-Activity 1.3.1: Development of a repository for early warning knowledge products, guidance, and disseminate lessons learned.

This sub-activity will develop processes and protocols for the creation, management, and dissemination of lessons learned. It will establish processes and protocols for knowledge sharing among stakeholders and develop a repository that is open access and online for the dissemination of knowledge products. The sub-activity will contribute to the development of an early warning knowledge base by developing guidelines on how to collect, store, share, disseminate, guidance, peer-learning, and reliable technical guidance from multiple UK and non-UN sources on themes cutting across EWS.

### Sub-Activity 1.3.2: Documentation and dissemination of good practices and lessons learned

The identification and documentation of good practices, challenges, innovations and solutions encountered in early warning programming will be done under this activity. Post-disaster forums will be conducted to learn lessons related to the implementation of EWSs and to identify areas for improvement. The sub-activity will also document and the refinement of functional EWSs will be included in Resource Frameworks. The sub-activity will also disseminate early warning knowledge products, is subject to peer-to-peer learning and continuous improvement and informed decision-making.

### Sub-Activity 1.3.3: Development of global and regional technical expert working groups

This sub-activity will develop a global and regional technical expert working group composed of national representatives to advance collective action in core thematic areas such as open data as a digital public good for EWS, early warning, early and anticipatory action and finance, preparedness and response planning. It will facilitate the sharing of lessons learned from the implementation of EWSs with other countries and regions, including through collaboration with Activity 1.1.2, and augment opportunities for national experts in LDCs and SIDS to influence global dialogue on EWS.

### Sub-Activity 1.3.4: Capacity building

The call for proposals will include a component that will provide capacity-building support to countries on topics such as public warning systems architecture and regulation, cell broadcast implementation, multi-channel deployment (satellite adaptation, and standardization for hand-held and chip manufacturers), to assist countries in their deployment; as well as cross to resource mobilization, technical assistance, training, and workshops.

## Component/Doput2: Accelerating actions in重点outcomes towards EWS

### Sub-Activity 1.3.5: National level investment activities

This component will roll out national level investment activities in seven countries to address common and local-level challenges that are barriers to the implementation of EWSs, including the identification of key gaps and bottlenecks identified under the global EWS initiative for 2023*. Concrete investments will focus on EWS hardware and software, risk knowledge, disseminating and communicating early warnings and preparedness for local-level actions that reduce vulnerability and increase resilience to climate-related hazards. The sub-activity will also support the development of national level action plans.

### Sub-Activity 1.3.6: Accelerating actions in key areas

Concerted efforts will be made to close gender gaps in access to, control over, and use of EWS technology, communications and services. This includes considerations of needs and challenges facing rural women, elderly people, children, youth, persons with disabilities, and other vulnerable groups such as pregnant women and children. It will support women’s equitable decision-making opportunities, including in design and management of EWS areas, laws, policies, and institutional arrangements that ensure the full participation of women and girls in ensuring women play meaningful roles in NMSs and early action. In a country like Somalia, for example, women and girls have low levels of understanding about early warning messages and generally have lower access to information than men. The

These partners have extensive experience in assessing presence and capacities of these agencies. The UNDP and its respective Country Offices will provide technical and administrative support, coordination and oversight to the implementation of the Output. This Output is designed to contribute to the achievement of the following SDGs: 13.

The implementation of the seven country projects will be supported by a global team of experts and a global Project Management Unit (PMU). The international expertise will be drawn from the Pillar Leads and other partner agencies and expertise in the areas of climate change adaptation, disaster risk reduction, gender equality, human rights, disability inclusion, and disability experts who guide and participate to the implementation of project activities in the seven countries. Some of the international experts will be hired on a part-time basis and will support activities identified and approved by the PMU. The PMU will also provide technical assistance and capacity building for national focal points for all countries to a wide range of highly qualified and experienced experts on cost-sharing and therefore, cost-effective basis.

### Strengthened Governance and Coordination

The activity will focus on legislation and policies governing the exchange of information to facilitate coordinated emergency response. The PMU will also support the development of coordination, clear functions, roles and responsibilities of agencies in the national warning dissemination process with associated updating of the WMO Register of Alerting Authorities (RAA). Alert distribution and feedback channels will be expanded to reach and users with targeted and actionable information. This will include the establishment of a national early warning system for vulnerable groups. The provision of differential access to information and resources by women and vulnerable subgroups will be included in the dissemination strategy. The PMU will also support the development of guidelines for the use of mobile phones and other devices by agencies for interpretation and dissemination of alerts to ensure delivery of clear and actionable warnings to end users. This will include extension officers and other government staff in line departments and journalists.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 24

This activity will attempt to establish or strengthen existing local Early Warning Committees (EWC) or equivalent coordination organisations with representatives from elected/traditional leaders who are linked to food, development or DDR structures whenever feasible given the country context. Meaningful involvement and representation of women and vulnerable groups in these committees will be ensured. Each EWC will seek to be a registered entity under the relevant national provisions and will seek to have a formal mandate to coordinate disaster risk management (DRM) activities at the local level, including through intersectoral arrangements. A clear coordination and reporting structure will be instituted between sub-national agencies from the lowest level of governance up to the district level. In all countries except Chad and Somalia, each EWC will have direct access to the national DRM focal point. The EWCs will be supported by a network of local disaster risk management (DRM) organisations fulfilling these roles will be engaged. Capabilities of these local institutions, from district down to village level will be enhanced through regular engagements and training programmes that strengthen institutional structures and procedures.

## Sub-Activity 2.1: Strengthened coordination mechanisms

The sub-activity will develop and implement strategies for effective early warning, early action and preparedness. It will support the development of a strategy and action plan and associated legislation to mainstream early warning and anticipatory action, while aligning existing best practices and a supportive framework and legislative environment with international standards. The strategy and action plan will be developed in close collaboration with relevant stakeholders and will include a comprehensive review of existing disaster risk management (DRM) policies, laws and strategies as well as climate plans. The resulting strategy and action plan will be endorsed by the national government and will be communicated to all levels of governance. The sub-activity will also develop and implement a mechanism for strengthening legal incentives for accountability. The sub-activity will include disability-inclusive communication Organisations of Persons with Disabilities (OPDs) in the development, implementation and monitoring of emergency preparedness plans. This will ensure meaningful participation of persons with disabilities to comprehensively understand their needs in humanitarian emergencies.

The lead government agency in each country in coordination with the NDRRMC will continue to use existing interinstitutional platforms and mechanisms for information sharing and will help coordinate efforts to inform the public about available options, including financial incentives, for maintaining the hydrometeorological network.

Data sharing between and within agencies at the national and sub-national levels will be strengthened and a formal definition of data sharing agreements will be developed. This includes access to equipment, infrastructure, equipment, and other support to overcome the barrier that is preventing effective data sharing between national and local authorities, first responders, humanitarian actors, and communities. The sub-activity will also develop and implement a strategy for respective response plans to reduce the impact of the hazard (Sub-Activity 2.3). Targeted groups include national and local authorities, businesses, communities, National Red Cross Crescent Societies, NGOs, the UN, and community groups. This will be achieved by developing and implementing a framework for data sharing and establishing procedures for user access to data hosted on servers. Data integration frameworks and systems which utilize application programming interfaces (APIs) that allow for interoperability between services may additionally be required in countries and agencies where such systems are not currently operational.

This activity will additionally ensure policy and financial support for mainstreaming of early warning, early action and preparedness into national DRM strategies and plans. This will include the development of a framework to share best knowledge. Commitment financing will be critical to anticipatory action and response in the event of a crisis. The success of the work in this activity hinges on committed financing for disaster preparedness and anticipatory actions ahead of a crisis. The sub-activity will also ensure that early warning information is integrated into social assistance through enhancing shock-responsive social protection systems at the grassroots level.

## Sub-Activity 2.1. Enhanced governance capacities

This will include: (i) a clear assignment of roles and responsibilities at the national and subnational levels for services; (ii) strategic plans and organizational structure; (iii) periodic processes to assess and evaluate its services; (iv) coordination platforms for service delivery; (v) institutional arrangements for ensuring effective collaboration, information sharing, and interoperability of systems, co-design of services; (vi) comprehensive plans are in place for communication, competency framework, cooperation, studies, quality system and financial resources. Development and updating of national plans, strategies and action plans for climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction, including the development of a national strategy for all priority hazards and meeting Sendai Target 6 Goal Indicator 4. All countries' local governments have a plan to act on early warning systems.

### Strengthened governance, collaboration and inclusion

Government arrangements, particularly for disaster preparedness and response, will be strengthened to include clear provisions, protocols, roles and responsibilities for early warning and early action in the context of climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction. This includes strengthening coordination with local communities. Collaboration will be strengthened between grassroots organizations including government agencies, traditional and religious leaders, local schools and health centers and civil society organizations.

- Capacity building on gender equality and inclusion: Strengthening collaboration among national governments and entities concerned with gender and disability, with the work of NDMAs and NMIs in order to more effectively use existing gender and disability data.
- Raising awareness on crucial intersections of gender inequality, disability social exclusion with success of MHIWS uptake and use by ensuring the inclusion of gender and inclusion topics in every capacity development activity for all stakeholders.
- Strengthened collaboration between key ministers, academia and the private sector, including at the community level by supporting countries in defining their roles, responsibilities and identify ways of working which will ensure that they are able to effectively implement MHIWS.

The sub-activity will strengthen risk knowledge capability in these institutions by combining local, traditional, Indigenous, generational and scientific knowledge that can enable resilience under a range of future risk scenarios. Building on existing approaches and lessons learned from the implementation of MHIWS, the sub-activity will identify new innovative approaches to leverage new and existing technologies to expand risk knowledge capability at all scales, including national, regional and local. The redeveloping of overarching vulnerability metrics as a basis for responding to and anticipating emerging crises and risks.

### Sub-Activity 2.1.2: Monitoring, Evaluation and Learning

Sub-Activity 2.1.3 ensures continuous monitoring of other project activities and sub-activities based on quantitative and qualitative data, including the use of an independent verification to track project progress and support adaptive management decisions. This is expected to result in improved monitoring, evaluation and learning systems integrated within project implementation processes. The sub-activity will also ensure that lessons learned are captured and used as adaptive approaches to the implementation of MHIWS. It will institutionalizing arrangements to ensure capacity and resources (e.g., staff, budget, etc.) to implement, monitor, report on gender responsive actions and will enhance capacities for monitoring and evaluation at all levels. The sub-activity will also ensure that project partners are able to report better on Sendai Framework Targets 4 and custom indicators. Specific activities will be taken to set up a formal mechanism for the collection of data and information from communities (e.g., focus group discussions, participatory technologies (surveys), forms) where available combined with regular consultations for feedback from focal persons in communities, specifically including representatives of women and vulnerable groups. Specific actions to be taken for monitoring and evaluation will include the development of a monitoring plan, which will be developed at the inception phase administered in the initial phase of the project with additional surveys at the mid-point and at the end of the project.

## Activity 2.1: Climate risk assessments and data collection for which digital technologies will be leveraged where possible.

The activity will also undertake disability inclusive surveys to assess the efficacy of critical project interventions, for example, measuring the socio-economic benefits from the use of climate products and advisors at the level of institutions and communities. The survey will include a focus on the impact of these products and services at national and regional level for collaborating on the impact assessments and also for reviews and evaluations of the projects themselves.

### Sub-Activity 2.1.1: M&E survey design, implementation and analysis of results for feedback into project reporting, management and assessing project impacts.
This will be split into monitoring visits undertaken at quarterly intervals and disability inclusive assessment surveys conducted in collaboration with national and regional institutions and communities. The survey will include:
(i) M&E survey design, implementation and analysis of results for feedback into project reporting, management and assessing project impacts. This will be split into monitoring visits undertaken at quarterly intervals and disability inclusive assessment surveys conducted in collaboration with national and regional institutions and communities.
(ii) Data collection on the impact of climate products and services on people with disabilities.
(iii) M&E and assessing the reach and effect of communication and dissemination strategies.

This will cover:
a) Monitoring of data sharing and integration protocols at the institutional level, specifically between agencies involved in the project.
b) Effectiveness monitoring of the project’s activities to ensure that they are reaching their intended audience.
c) Effectiveness across different channels of dissemination and across both institutions and communities. The latter includes the use of digital technologies for communication and dissemination.

d) Reporting on a quarterly basis with updating the strategy on an annual basis, and M&E of environmental and social safeguards as per the country specific safeguard plans and strategies on a quarterly basis.
This will specifically include any safeguarding measures that are in place to protect the rights of marginalized and disadvantaged groups.

### Activity 2.1.2: Disaster risk knowledge and observatory
This activity will result in the systematic collection of risk data, information and analysis leading to improved awareness about disaster-prone populations, services, infrastructure, assets, types of vulnerabilities, exposure loss and damage accounting.
Existing data collection and reporting systems in countries will be strengthened for reliable data production and dissemination of relevant risk information. This will include maintaining disability inclusive data collection tools, including the Washington Group Short Set of Disability Questions and the UNICEF-Washington Group Child Functioning Questionnaire. Other tools that will be maintained or developed with the support of partners such as NCDM, NMH and other stakeholders, include sector ministries, and sub-national governments, will benefit from capacity building.
The countries will strengthen their disaster loss accounting systems and monitoring of Sendai Target 1.b. The activity will also develop a national observatory for climate risk information and analysis to provide access available to people at national and local levels. The activity will also establish collaborations with relevant academic institutions and research organizations to develop methodologies and algorithms for contractual and actionable data end-users.

### Sub-Activity 2.1.1: Develop disability risk knowledge system
The sub-activity will increase production and access to risk information and effective application of this information at national, regional and local levels. It will also ensure that each country has quality, timely and contextually relevant risk information for EWS which integrates community-based early warning systems into the stakeholder engagement plan.
This will result in improved generation of national, sub-national and local risk information for a comprehensive understanding of climate risks and vulnerabilities at all levels. The sub-activity will build on relevant agencies in data access, metrics and analysis to support decision-making will be built to facilitate access to updated risk information in standardized and user-friendly formats.
Finally, the sub-activity will build institutional capacities to incorporate climate risk into EWS. This will include capacity building for national focal points in each participating country and high resolution, forecast linked models for specific hazards (floods, dry-spells, heatwaves, drought, thunderstorms, landslides, glacial lake outburst floods).

* Mobile ICT based Kobo/SDK, Interactive Voice Response, interactive mobile quizzes using key entry and UMTS.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 37

## Sub-Activity A.2.2: Strengthen observation and monitoring networks

This sub-activity will focus on the strengthening and/or establishment of meteorological, hydrological and climate forecast and monitoring systems for each participating country. This activity will leverage SDTF investments which impact current operations and capacity development in these areas. The objective is to strengthen national capacities internationally in order to improve NWP products which improve the skill of these forecasts. Capacities of NMHSs will be further enhanced to address gaps in hydrometeorological data acquisition beyond GSDN, through institutional and staff capacity development, as well as by strengthening the use of SDTF investments for the implementation of new technologies that feed into the generation of accurate and timely early warnings. Additionally, the NMHS will adopt a quality management system (QMS) to ensure the continuous improvement in the quality of its services and products, with full compliance with G80. The activities will build on implementation plans of existing programmes such as the Flash Flood Guidance System (FFGS) or the Hydrological Status and Outlook Systems (HydroSOS initiative).

The sub-activity will also focus on strengthening early warning systems for floods by enhancing data sharing and information exchange infrastructure to support EWS. Implementation of the WMO Information System Version 2.0 (WIS v2.0) is a critical component in this regard, as it provides a common framework for the development of an interoperable EWS. This is not duplication of investments of SDTF in WIS v2.0 implementation in some countries. This will be coupled with policy advocacy and development to enable sharing of critical data required for early warnings, based on the WMO Unified Data Policy. Early warning systems will also be strengthened by ensuring that all relevant stakeholders and agencies are well informed through data sharing and sharing of forecasting products.

The sub-activity will enhance data sharing between actors involved in the generation of EWS. It will implement both a gap analysis on early warning needs to be conducted at national and regional levels to expand the WMO cataloguing of hazardous events initiative (MIMO CAT) for all weather and climate driven hazards and their monitoring requirements; the catalyzing of investments in new technologies such as satellite imagery, remote sensing, ground-based observations, satellite observation, radar, lightning detection and other advanced technologies that will be used to build detection and forecasting capacity. This will also include the development of a national early warning system (NEWS) for mountainous areas and will accelerate the implementation of the WMO action plan on hydrology by establishing regional hydrological status systems.

The identification of different EWS will be done to identify and address gaps in communication and data exchange. This will ensure seamless data integration and communication between the different components of the EWS in their respective areas as well as with other stakeholders.

## Sub-Activity A.2.3: Strengthen NMHSs' forecasting capacity

The sub-activity will strengthen capacities for data processing, forecasting and analysis. The network of Regional Specialised Meteorological Centres (RSMCs) and associated National Meteorological and Hydrological Centres will be supported to improve their capabilities in the areas of numerical weather prediction, high resolution storm forecasting, marine weather predictions, high resolution TCS and IFD. Additionally, WMO's World Information System for Prediction and Forecasting (WISPF) will be expanded to provide real-time operational support to monitor a wide range of hazards.

## Action 2.3: Warning dissemination, preparedness and response

This sub-activity will focus on the development of warning messages in line with the Sendai Targeted G-4, Indicator G-3.9*, and Indicator G-4**. This will be achieved by developing last mile connectivity and by establishing multi-

#### At the community level, the activity will promote and support inclusive, people-centered co-development of gender-responsive climate change adaptation plans and strategies using and extending approaches such as the Participatory Integrated Climate Services for Agriculture (PICSA). Existing community-based systems including Indigenous Knowledge Systems (IKS) and flood and drought warning systems will be strengthened. Sub-Activity 2.3.2: Strengthen disaster preparedness and response capacity

Actions under this sub-activity lead to comprehensive protocols and protocols for climate change adaptation and preparedness, with emphasis on supporting communities most at risk in each country. Its action will build on the work of Sub-Activity 2.1.4 (described above) and Sub-Activity 2.3.1 (discussed below). The following actions are included under this sub-activity:

(i) Communication protocols and identification of focal persons for each community to ensure that communities can access information in a timely manner.

(ii) Protocols for graduated warning levels so that communities have heightened awareness during periods of increased risk exposure.

(iii) Protocols for evacuation during disasters, and

(iv) Protocols for relief, extension and health services to deliver appropriate services to communities.

Sub-Activity 2.3.3: Strengthen disaster adaptation and response capacity

This activity will result in the development of disability inclusive disaster preparedness/contingency plans to ensure that communities with disabilities are not left behind during climate change adaptation action. Capacities for preparedness based on risk-informed and impact-based forecasts will be ensured at the local level to enable local authorities, first responders, and community members to respond effectively in a timely manner. The activities of this sub-activity will be done by governments and first responders including agencies such as National Red Cross or Red Crescent Societies and will do their preparedness capacities, systems and procedures will be strengthened based on the integrated community and national disaster risk reduction plans. This will include: (i) Strengthening the capacity of local authorities and first responder capability; develop a sustainable knowledge base, and ensure the training programme is disability inclusive in line with the UN Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities in Humanitarian Action.

Institutional and operational level capacities will be built by scaling up investments in preparedness cash stocks, supply chains and logistics, as well as enhancing accountability of local responders. Communities will be supported to develop disaster risk reduction plans, climate change adaptation plans based on feasibility studies, climate information and local knowledge. These will include evacuation routes and plans for future construction and retrofitting of small infrastructure for emergencies. Special emphasis will be given to the needs of persons with disabilities in these activities.

Awareness building of the increased risk of gender-based violence during emergencies and need for accessibility of facilities such as shelters will also be ensured. Community members, including women and persons with disabilities, will be trained on CB-EWS of early warning devices such as water level recorders, rain gauges and anemometers. Support and oversight may also be provided for small scale infrastructure for emergencies such as installing water storage tanks, more improved sanitation facilities (e.g., latrines), or other basic needs (e.g., providing solar lights to communities that are not installing toilets). ## B

### National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and cross-sectoral actions

The NAP will focus on the following areas:

- **Interventions in Antigua & Barbuda**: Developed through an extensive multi-stakeholder engagement process, culminating in a roadmap for reinforcing a climate-centric MHWIS in the country. The roadmap includes: i) strengthening the institutional framework; ii) developing a national governance framework; iii) data sharing collaborations and consolidation; iv) bridging the gap between technical information and community comprehension; and v) enhancing capacity for emergency management. Moreover, several key activities will be implemented to address climate change impacts on the coastal environment – including ocean conditions and environmental impacts – and the introduction of an EWS for Barbuda that is not included in the NAP.

- **Interventions from a governance perspective**: Involvement in A4N will focus on modernizing the disaster management policy and legislative framework, including through integration of disability issues, and integrating DRR considerations into sectoral policies. The interventions are aligned with existing plans on a national and regional level and reinforced by a national strategic plan for natural resource management agencies, together providing a solid foundation for the effective functioning and sustainability of the NAP. A key element in this regard is the establishment of a Disaster Preparedness and Response Fund. This fund will provide a sustainable mechanism to channel resources towards disaster risk reduction.

- **Interventions from an implementation perspective**: The interventions will be underpinned by improved disaster risk knowledge in the country (Phase I), focused on creating a seamless information flow between relevant government agencies, supporting organizations and the public to improve coordination of disaster response. This will be achieved through the establishment of a national platform for stakeholders. This will include increasing the availability of sector-specific data; disability-inclusive disaster risk management; knowledge sharing among key stakeholders in the areas of climate change adaptation, natural resource management, health, as well as adding focus the marine environment and social risks. At this high level is the effort to develop an online risk data platform, designed to centralize and manage critical risk information across all relevant sectors, as well as underpinning disaster and disability inclusive hazard and risk assessments, addressing significant knowledge gaps, particularly in Barbuda.

### Pillar 1: Actions on climate change adaptation

- **Meteorological service (AMS)**: Including support actions to direct additional budgetary aid to the services, hiring and training of technical staff, and improving the resilience hierarchy of the ARMS offices through structural enhancements and the introduction of a new building. The ARMS will also be supported in developing a national meteorological observation infrastructure and monitoring systems to enhance flash flood risk forecast system for urban and rural areas, as well as enhancing the capacity of the ARMS to provide early warning services on extreme environmental changes. The enhancements to these networks will include provisions for real-time monitoring and analysis of hydrometeorological data, ensuring interoperability with risk data in place at ARMS through the introduction of a platform that allows for the sharing of information between ARMS and other relevant agencies.

- **Pillar 2: Work on similar warning messages that are easily understandable by the public, including persons with disabilities**: Ensuring that warning messages are clear, concise, and actionable in accessible formats during emergencies. This will be achieved through the development of a national emergency communication plan for all levels of society and their associated anticipatory actions. A National emergency telecommunication plan will be developed alongside a common alerting protocol to support simple dissemination platforms, including text-to-speech, voice and vibration broadcasting, sirens, among other traditional forms of mass warning, as well as accessible formats to reach persons with a variety of disabilities.

## Final Implementation under Pillar 4 of the project will support the scaling up of community-based EWS (CB-EWS) to empower local communities, including persons with disabilities, to take an active role in monitoring, disseminating, and responding to potential threats – developing specialized risk informed responses/contingency plans for extreme events based on the needs of vulnerable groups, including persons with disabilities. The project will support the development of CB-EWS and disaster response teams, providing training and equipment for first responders, as well as providing resources for rapid evacuation and sheltering in case of emergency. It will also support the establishment of a national early warning system and establishing dedicated financing mechanisms and structured early action measures for pre-impact actions based on the needs of vulnerable groups, including persons with disabilities.

The anticipatory action will be closely linked with the government’s disaster risk management – ensuring that relevant legislation is updated to accommodate the emerging functions of key response institutions.

### The intervention in Chad aims to ensure that EWS are impact-based by building capacity to carry out disability-inclusive post-disaster needs assessments and loss and damage reporting, promoting longer-term adaptation planning and knowledge sharing. It will also support the development of a national early warning system for extreme events, including in public financial management in the context of fragile and Conflict-affected States (FCS). The project will promote the development of a pipeline of supporting projects, ensuring data collection is transmitted in a timely way, ensuring data governance and accountability, and ensuring that CB-EWS are inclusive. It will also support the capacity building for persons with disabilities through consultation, and developing rights and protocols for issuing and disseminating warning messages. The project will also support the development of an early warning system for food security, towards a multi-hazard system, considering wider adaptation challenges.

It will be prepared based on the right to information on risks, advice on preparation measures, and warnings/advisories that are locally relevant and respectful to the needs and expectations of people living with disabilities, the elderly, persons with illnesses, religious and minorities.

The project will develop an offer of financial incentives for farmers through multi-phase surveys (pre-need surveys and post-disaster assessments) and developing national capacity for cell phone pilots. It will be preferred for communication, including accessible formats for persons with disabilities, and monitor trust in existing and new communication channels. The project will also support the development of a pipeline of supporting projects ensuring anticipatory action protocols are tested and adapted, providing capacity to carry out simulations, lessons learned and post-disaster needs assessments, supporting crisis response capacity at devolved levels, while bringing together stakeholders for joint planning and decision-making on CB-EWS. It will also support the development of early warning alerts. The project will respond appropriately to the needs of persons concerning transparency, accountability and the management of information.

The preparatory work on institutional and regulatory frameworks, will ensure that coordination and governance approaches are in set motion to support these activities and addresses, and address critical institutional, financial and regulatory barriers.

The project aims to utilize risk knowledge systems as it figs, focusing on historical data, climate models and end-user needs.

The proposed interventions will be implemented through a multi-phase approach. For EWS: Investments will be made to improve monitoring and warning for severe weather, and localized flash flooding, and droughts. A new drought monitoring system for food security will be established along with Drought EWS.

### The EWBK intervention for Cambodia provides a comprehensive plan for governance and coordination, disaster risk reduction and warning dissemination and preparedness in Cambodia. The plan includes developing a disability inclusive data sharing protocol, upgrading the Cambodia Disaster Damage & Loss Information System (CDDIS), and implementing a national early warning system. It also includes the development of a gender, age, disability and inclusion considerations, as well as the establishment of river gauge and drought gauges in target provinces to address weather information needs. Furthermore, the plan focuses on raising awareness of emergency alert systems through community-based approaches through various activities such as training workshops, seminars, and accessible formats for persons with disabilities.

### The governance and coordination aspect of the plan involves establishing a comprehensive early warning secretariat, coordinating disaster risk reduction efforts in Cambodia, implementing a cross-pillar approach to gender, disability and inclusion. The disaster risk awareness and coordination section emphasizes upgrading the CDDIS, preparing preparedness and contingency plans, developing an accessible format for persons with disabilities, raising awareness of emergency alert systems among persons with disabilities, and including river gauge and drought gauges in target provinces to address weather information needs. Additionally, it highlights the development of a comprehensive human resource plan to meet early warning requirements.

### The communication section focuses on establishing a national early warning system, coordinating mobile network providers, and upgrading online presence and dissemination channels. It entails equipping local authorities with early action capacity, implementing community-based inclusive EWS, and providing accessible formats for persons with disabilities. The project also includes the development of river gauge and drought gauges in target provinces to address weather information needs.

### The EWBK project proposed in Ecuador is designed to significantly enhance the country's ability to anticipate, prepare and respond to various climate-related hazards such as floods, landslides, droughts and extreme temperatures. The impact of these hazards is increasing in Ecuador in the last decades due to climate change. The project aims to establish a robust and integrated multi-hazard EWS that leverages advanced forecasting techniques, standardized climate data management systems, and community-based preparedness strategies. Key components include capacity building activities such as the development of a functional legislative framework, the development of a National Framework for Climate Change Adaptation (NFCCA), the establishment of an early warning system, the implementation of risk assessments for the relevant hazards, the improvement of the hazard forecasting, the integration of Indigenous Knowledge Systems (IKS) into early warning practices, the integration of rights of persons with disabilities into early

**B**

warning lines, systems, data collection, communication strategies, risk management strategies, and protocols, the improvement of the communication strategies and the creation of gender-sensitive disaster preparedness protocols.

These activities are aimed strictly at addressing the pressing climate challenges in Ecuador. The country is highly vulnerable to a wide range of natural hazards including droughts, floods, landslides, earthquakes, volcanic eruptions, meteorological events, droughts, floods, and landslides. The project’s focus on strengthening governance structures and enhancing institutional capacities ensures that EWS are effective and sustainable. By improving disaster risk knowledge, enhancing the capacity to respond to climate-related hazards, and ensuring that early warning systems (EWS) in place will mitigate the adverse impacts of climate-related hazards on vulnerable communities.

Key activities of the project include:

- Strengthening governance structures and the enhancement of data collection systems.
- Address specific gaps identified in Ecuador’s national climate strategy. These include the need for more robust and sustainable financing mechanisms for climate adaptation, as well as improved coordination among Ecuadorean institutions to ensure that disaster risk reduction (DRR) and climate change adaptation (CCA) are integrated into all sectors of government.
- Implementing gender-sensitive and disability inclusive approaches and the inclusion of Indigenous Knowledge Systems (IKS) aligns with Ecuador’s national strategy. The project will also focus on ensuring that EWS are accessible and responsive to the needs of women, persons with disabilities, indigenous peoples, and other vulnerable groups.

In summary, by addressing the specific gaps identified in Ecuador's national climate strategies, including gaps in institutional capacity, financing, and community preparedness, its activities are specifically designed to strengthen the country’s preparedness and resilience in the face of increasing climate variability and extreme weather events, ultimately contributing to a more resilient society.

### Ethiopia

The project interventions address critical barriers that prevent the effective generation, dissemination and use of early warnings for climate change adaptation, preparedness and response to warnings, through three broad outputs tailored to specific country needs, which are aligned with the five core activities under Output 2 of the Global FP. The project will support the development of a National Risk Data Hub (NRD-Hub) in Ethiopia, which will be managed by EMDAC and supported by UNICEF. This will enable the development of a central National Risk Data Hub managed by EMDAC and accessible to all. This will enable the development and sharing of impact-based disaster risk profiles focusing on highly vulnerable areas especially looking at floods risks in the country. The project will also support the development of an early warning system (EWS) for flood risks under covered-areas. An analysis on most relevant communication channels to address early warning to vulnerable populations, including women, persons with disabilities and indigenous peoples, will be conducted. This will enable the identification of community level priority high-risk locations, linking with Anticipatory Action. Early warning systems will be operationalized at community level in priority high-risk locations, linking with Anticipatory Action. An emergency call number and service will be developed.

Multi-sector line ministries will be engaged to agree on sector-specific early warning and Emergency Thresholds, and multi-hazard Anticipatory Action Plans will be integrated into Preparedness Planning in highly vulnerable areas, linking with the development of an EWS.

A National Risk Data Hub coordinated by EMDAC with contributions from all sector Agencies/Ministries will be formed to collect and develop multi-hazard disaster inclusive data and risk analysis and undertake capacity development for expert human resources. The project will also support the development of a national early warning system (EWS) at the level of high-risk areas in Ethiopia. Gender-based, disability-inclusive EWS focusing on elderly and children will be developed as part of this exercise which will also lead to the establishment of community-based disaster inclusion EWS in priority areas.

The project will support the development of a gender-sensitive and disability inclusive early warning system for all forms of disasters including vulnerability, disability inclusion and gender-based issues. This will be used to build capacities of communications specialists to address media and to journalists to communicate about disaster risk.

### B.A Implementation arrangements

UNDP will be the Executing Entity of the EWAMI-MCP and will be responsible for the overall project execution. It will collaborate with the EWMAR pillar leads in the provision of technical assistance and support.

The UNDP will enter into a Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) with the GCF, on approval of the project. This will be in accordance with the terms and conditions set out in the GCF’s General Conditions for Contractual Agreements. The Executed Entity is to provide oversight and quality assurance through its Headquarters and Country Office units. This role includes: [project preparation oversight], [project implementation oversight] and [post-project evaluation oversight]. It also includes oversight roles in relation to reporting and knowledge management. The project assurance function of UNDP is to support the Project Board in carrying out objective and independent assessments of the project’s progress, performance, and results.

The Executed Entity will be responsible for ensuring that all project activities are managed and completed. Project assurance has no role to be independent of the Project Manager; therefore, the Project Board cannot delegate any of its responsibilities to the Executed Entity, UNDP Headquarters or M&E Office. Instead, the Executed Entity provides Assurance role on behalf of UNDP. The “senior role” of UNDP is to represent the interests of the parties which provide funding and/or technical expertise in the project (designing, developing, facilitating and implementing) and to ensure that all project activities are carried out in accordance with the terms regarding the technical feasibility of the project. The senior role must have the authority to commit or acquire supplier resources required. If necessary, more than one person may be required for this role. Typically, the implementation of the Funded Activity is considered a Subsidiary Agreement.

### Global implementation arrangements

This multi-country project will be implemented by UNDP acting as an Executing Entity and Funding Entity in terms of direct procurement of goods, works and services and production for direct implementation. The Accredited Entity shall authorize seven implementing UNDP Country Offices to implement the project in each of the seven countries in accordance with the MAM. This arrangement is referred to as a Subsidiary Agreement. If the GCF does not consider the Subsidiary Agreement to be sufficient for the implementation of the Funded Activity, the Project Document shall not be considered a Subsidiary Agreement. This EWAMI global project will be implemented in multiple countries and therefore will use the multi-country project type.

## Pillar Focus Points (WMO, UNDRR and ITU) will support the Project Board through a Coordination Group and will work with UNDP to ensure that the project is aligned with the priorities of the UNDP.

### Funding
Funds will be fed from the GCF to the UNDP and then the UNDP to the respective Pillar Leads as per the activity budget. Implementation of the global proposal will be undertaken by UNDP at global and country levels in partnership with WMO, UNDRR and ITU. At the global level, UNDP and its partners are responsible for the global level implementation. Regional UNDP Programmes (e.g., UNEP, UNFCCC) will also be involved as appropriate. The following process will be followed, whereas WMO, UNDRR and ITU will follow their agency processes: (i) At the Global and country levels, UNDP will partner with WMO, ITU, UNDRR and SDC, or an Agency to Agency Termination Agreement will be signed if the project is implemented by another agency. (ii) The UNDP Project Document (PJD) will be developed in consultation with the Pillar Leads and other relevant stakeholders. (iii) A PJD – MOU will be signed for the project duration, following UNDP Programme and Operations Policy and Procedures. (iv) UNDP HQ will review and approve the PJD and MOU. (v) At the country level, UNDP will engage with Government through Standard LOA between UNDP and a Government Ministry/Institution/SDG on the implementation of a project when UNDP serves as an implementing partner | United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) Global Policy Guidelines (PDD guidelines). The PJD will be formalized through Responsible Party Agreements. (vi) Beneficiaries will be selected through established criteria as per country context and in accordance with the relevant national laws and regulations. (vii) UNDP will engage with WMO, Geneva, with respective regional offices covering each of the 7 countries to deliver technical assistance, training, and capacity building based on country priorities.

### Implementation
As part of the UNDP Global Procurement Policy Procedure (PDD guidelines), UNDP will implement the planned activities of the programme document, following a Direct Implementation (DIM) modality. In line with the programme document, UNDP will develop a Project Management Plan (PMP). UNDP HQ will develop and approve a Multi-Year Work Plan by following a UNDP standard work plan format. Highlighting the expected outputs, outcomes and the relevant outputs (with their respective baselines, targets and indicators), Responsible Party Agreements, Periodic/Yearly Implementation Reports, Final Evaluation Report and other relevant documents will be developed as per the PDD guidelines. The PJD is approved by the Project Board; and the funds are available. UNDP HQ and/or participating COs will follow a common digital transparent competitive procurement process at their respective levels, for the selection of the national entity implementing the project activities in accordance with the relevant national laws and regulations from inception to completion of the project.

### Selection of Contractors
The procurement process is based on contractors’ status with the development of a Standard Terms of Reference (TOR) for each activity (in which relevant eligibility criteria or for selection of beneficiaries and/or interventions, amongst others, will be highlighted), advertising them in the UNDP global procurement website, invitation of receiving applications from interested parties, evaluation of received applications by the UNDP Procurement Committee (if available), formalization of the contracts by the UNDP procurement committee (to be formed), awarding by the activities by signing of the legal agreements with the contractors. The PJD will specify the terms and conditions for the contractor to use the available funds of the activity to the contractor in installment basis (per the agreement), based on the satisfactory implementation of the assigned tasks. In case where other UN agency implementing activities (STU, WMO, UNDRR), UNDP will sign a Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) with the relevant agency and the project activities will follow UN/UPN procurement processes. The list of responsible parties for implementing the project activities for the global proposal are provided below, which is reflected in figure 2.

The Project Board is responsible for making, by consensus, management decisions when a quorum is required by the Project Management Unit (PMU). The Project Board shall be responsible for ensuring that project activities are aligned with UNDP’s strategic objectives, best value for development results, best value money, fairness, integrity, transparency and effective international competition. In case a consensus cannot be reached within the Board, final decision shall rest with the UNDP Executive Coordinator. As the main governing body of the project, the Project Board will be responsible for ensuring that all project activities are in line with guidelines and the latest policies regarding the establishment and operation of Project Board Following (UNDP) Programme Management Unit (PMU). The Project Board will consist of representatives from each of the countries projects, including at least one representative from the relevant UN Country Team.

The Project Board will be compliant as following:

a. UNDP will hold the role of Executive and will be represented by the UNDP’s Director for the Bureau of Policy Support Programming (BPS) or his/her directly designated representative.
b. A pillar lead representative that will hold the role of Senior Supplier on a rotation basis. Pillar lead rotation should be determined in consultation with the Project Board.

Project Board meetings will be held biennially, twice a year. In addition, the members of the committee will look to meet physically every other year (three times in the five years) for a knowledge sharing meeting. Such meetings will be hosted by one of the countries projects and will include representatives from each country project. Pillar Leads, GCI, UNDP and two representatives from each of the country projects, including at least one from the relevant UN Country Team.

A global PMU will be constituted within UNDP for the overall project coordination, oversight and monitoring. The PMU will also provide technical support to help the country projects in accessing regional and international resources through networking, knowledge sharing and capacity building activities. The PMU will also provide technical assistance for progress for their inputs and support. The structure of the global PMU is provided in Figure 2.1 comprises of four full-time staff - a Project Manager, a Finance Analyst, a Programme Operations Associate and a Procurement Analyst. The PMU will be responsible for providing project management services to each country project specific for country’s needs, in an effective manner that also responds to national capacities. It will also look into developing a capacity building strategy for the country projects. The PMU will provide technical assistance and support provided part-time technical support through a team of advisors who specialize in climate change adaptation, climate information and EWS, a Gender, Disability, Social and Environmental Safeguards expert, a Communications Expert and other subject experts insight from as an need-based basis.

## Country level implementation arrangements

The 15 country projects will be implemented as part of the global multi-country project following the established Direct Implementation Model (DIM) under which UNDP acts as the implementing entity. The specific country-level implementation arrangements will be vetted and agreed by the government via the relevant Nationally Designated Authority to the GCF and other Government agencies and relevant ministers. UNDP has a country presence through established fully operational offices in each of the 15 countries, with the exception of Afghanistan where UNDP is represented at the country level by a Resident Representative. UNDP will be responsible for overall project implementation and a Government Ministry/Institution/IGO implementation of specified project activities defined by the project. UNDP will follow the GCF’s Direct Implementation Model (DIM) as described in the GCF’s Guidelines on DIM. Each country government, as described within each existing signed country SAA/R in the case where specific government agencies will implement certain project activities, these Agencies will act as Responsible Parties for those specific activities and will be responsible for ensuring that they are implemented in accordance with the terms of the UNDP and the Responsible Party. The Project Board at the national level will be comprised of UNDP as Executive. The Senior Beneficiaries are the most vulnerable communities and will be represented by the NDMA or Disaster Risk Agency as appropriate, designated by the NDA. The Project Board will be responsible for making, in consensus, management decisions when guidance is required by the National Project Manager Unit (NPMU). The NDA will provide technical advice to the Project Board on risk management for development results, best value, fairness, integrity, transparency and effective international cooperation. At least one representative of the NDMA or Disaster Risk Agency will be a member; final decision rest with the UNDP’s Resident Representative. The Project Board will meet at least once a year.

The project will be developed by UNDP under DM modality with the support of a National Project Management Unit (NPMU) and in close collaboration with the NDA. The NPMU will be responsible for providing technical advice to the UNDP country office on the implementation of specific actions and/or activities for the delivery of project outputs. RPs will be accountable to the respective UNDP country office for the implementation of specified actions and outputs of the project to achieve intended development results. The NPMU will provide support in the selection of the most appropriate UNDP, NDMA and UNDP based on their comparative advantages and core competencies and will support the respective UNDP country office in the preparation of the Project Document (PD) for submission to the UNDP country office as per the terms of P&P agreement that they will enter into with the UNDP country office.

The structure and unit of the National PMU will be decided by each country. The National Project Unit or National Country Office (NCO) will be responsible for the overall management, coordination and implementation of the project. The Project Manager function will end when the final project terminal evaluation report and other documentation required by UNDP are submitted to UNDP.

The NPMU will provide technical advice to the Board through relevant subject experts, reflecting their role in each country.

Sub-national coordination of project activities will be done through relevant district agencies, and where needed, through sub-district level institutions. The community level institution at the village level will be informed by village level institutions for social and civil protection. Community members will play a critical role in the implementation of the project. The NPMU will provide technical advice to the Board on the roles and responsibilities at the community level in all countries in the formulation of their respective Stakeholder Engagement Plans. These plans inform the roles, functions and responsibilities by which communities can be fully engaged in the project.

The planning process for each country will be initiated with a baseline assessment that will be conducted during the first quarter and annual plan will be updated annually, based on stakeholder engagement. The stakeholders will also be engaged during the mid-year review to assess progress of the project and evaluate adaptive plans required as well as response to the needs and priorities identified by communities. Community participation in the project will be tracked through tools developed by the project management, leveraging where possible the existing decentralised government structures to implement the project activities. The community level institution at the village level will be fully represented on the Board will be through the decentralised structure of the Implementation Partner in each country.

Responsible Parties will be selected by UNDP based on their technical and institutional capacities, comparative advantage, cost-effectiveness, and ability to deliver results within the agreed time frame. The selection process may also refer to other potential organizations. UNDP standard rules and regulations will be followed at all times, as per POPP (https://www.popp.un.org/). The Responsible Parties will be selected based on their participation in the Hazard Assessment for Transfers (HACT) assessments. Exhaustive stakeholder engagements and feasibility studies which included analysis of vulnerabilities and key priorities of stakeholders have guided the selection of sites and beneficiaries for the different project activities. The responsible parties will be responsible for the implementation, coordination, monitoring and reporting of project activities, manage resources, monitor and report on the results as outlined in the Responsible Party Agreement.

B

This GCF program focuses on establishing a global support architecture that intends to be long-lasting, built on the principle of multi-partners to address a wide range of needs which have to date been addressed, if at all, in a fragmented manner and with limited coherence across different initiatives.

The quantum of GCF financing leverages a program of global/multi-country coverage to establish a pathway towards EWAII not just for the public sector but also for the private sector. This will be achieved through partnerships with governments but also CREWS, SOFFI and other initiatives, come together to overcome the fragmentation. The multi-partners approach particularly the involvement of the likes of FFI, allows exploring the possibility of meaningful public-private sector collaboration in addressing climate change.

### Key characteristics and dynamics of the private sector

Considering that EWS also affect various economic sectors, project interactions with the private sector are expected to be significant. The level and nature of private sector engagement in this project will show significant variations depending on country contexts. However, the following commonalities can be drawn to guide private sector engagement within the project.

The private sector, especially in the telecommunication industry, plays a critical role both in the collection/transmission of basic observational data and dissemination of early warnings. This project will, with the help of ITU and GMA seek collaboration with private sector partners to ensure that the existing mobile cellular networks are used for the purpose of coverage of early warnings will not be met unless the existing mobile cellular networks, managed by private operators, are utilized to assist in automatic collection of observations and to complement radio, TV and other networks in dissemination of early warning information. The project will also seek collaboration with private sector partners managed private operators in the dissemination of early warning information.

Telecommunication infrastructure is vulnerable to extreme weather events and therefore, complementing such infrastructure with satellite-based communication systems. Telecommunication infrastructure can also be leveraged to host observation systems such as lightning sensors and generic weather observations, particularly in remote areas with limited power supply.

Limited public-private partnerships with providers of digital networks and services within the reach of early warnings and weather information through multiple communication channels including radio, TV, internet, mobile phones, satellites and social media will also be sought. The project will seek to ensure that such public-private sector partnership and dissemination mechanisms may offer the only effective means of communication and therefore also need to be considered.

Private sector engagement in the project will also take place at a different scale involving multi-national corporations which are expected to supply hydrometeorological equipment to target countries. National governments will be supported to adopt innovative technologies for early warning systems, including those that can be provided by public sector entities and private businesses. This will facilitate alignment with corporate social responsibility and help leverage mechanisms such as the ITU Global Compact which offer businesses the opportunity to adapt sustainability principles and targets into their business strategies and operations.

The project will also seek collaboration with other initiatives such as the World Meteorological Organization (WMO) EWAII Advisory Panel e.g. GMA, for the telecom industry, DfR for Microsoft and Amazon and Planet Labs for digital platforms.

This will also facilitate the adoption of emerging technologies such as artificial intelligence and provide access to new and innovative digital platforms, social media and technological platforms such as remote sensing systems for high-resolution hydrometeorological observations. The project will seek collaboration with other initiatives such as WMO EWAII Advisory Panel e.g. GMA, for the telecom industry, DfR for Microsoft and Amazon and Planet Labs for digital platforms.


---
**Page 56**
---

At the other end of the spectrum, private companies and investors can help design low cost, autonomous EWSs and alarms that are managed by communities, for example for floods10 and landslides11.

The insurance sector plays an important role in using improved weather and climate data. This project will explore how insurance mechanisms can be used to reduce disaster risk and increase resilience. Many developing countries can benefit from many developing countries, risk transfer mechanisms using insurance are significantly underdeveloped due to the absence of weather/climate and other socioeconomic data needed for product development, lack of awareness about insurance products, and limited access to financial services.

Global economic losses from natural hazard-related disasters in 2011 amounted to USD 270 billion and 385K of the losses were uninsured. This represents a loss of 40% of their total assets over time.

The absence of insurance as a mechanism to mitigate the impact when climate-induced extreme events strike continues to be a major impediment for resilient development in many developing countries.


### B.2.1.3 Climate Information Services

#### The global support structure

The speed and scale of interventions required to deliver on the EWSA objectives can only be achieved through coordinated global support involving all key actors across the EWSA value chain. The needs of LDCs and SIDS to establish, implement, and maintain effective national early warning systems require multiple funding instruments. These instruments need to bring together a wide range of stakeholders.

There is a need for facilitative national investment planning to achieve EWSAs around which finance can coalesce to achieve shared value and results. This includes the development of national roadmaps and investment pipelines and the communication of these with donors, investors, and other stakeholders. The roadmap should be developed in close collaboration with the relevant authorities and stakeholders.

The need for facilitative national investment planning is also reflected in the need to recognize the respective business practices, funding cycles, and operational practices of various financial instruments to accommodate the different needs of LDCs and SIDS. This includes the development of a comprehensive suite of specific technical and financial support needs for end-to-end EWSAs, translating country vision into concrete investment.

The absence of a consistent, reliable and robust source of holistic technical and financial support to countries is the main reason why many countries are not able to implement their national early warning systems. This tends to be driven through project financing. Global initiatives such as WMO and mechanisms like CREDIS are therefore necessary for countries to receive consistent, continuous support that meets the global standards, allows them to exchange best practices, and enables them to access a wide range of funding instruments.

Recognizing that responding adequately to the SDG 1 call for EWSAs will require concerted efforts at the global level for a sustained period of time, developing a strong exit strategy for this project will become one of the critical deliverables of this project. The exit strategy should be designed in such a way as to ensure that the momentum generated through various channels including bilateral or multilateral public financing, philanthropies, private sector financing, or other innovative funding mechanisms is maintained and sustained over time.

The exit strategy for this project will be developed by the project team with input from key partners and through key partnerships and it aligns with key Frameworks and strategies at the global and regional level. The project is aligned with the WMO Global Framework for Climate Services (GFCS), designed to facilitate delivery of best practice climate services.

This project is designed to address existing root causes and barriers to sustainable climate information services, MHEWS and Anticipatory Action in the most vulnerable countries among the LDCs and SIDS. The following strategy is proposed to ensure sustainability beyond the project's lifespan.

#### Institutional strengthening

The project will strengthen the existing institutions and governance at the global, regional and national levels, rather than creating new parallel structures. Sustainability will be supported by the establishment of holistic national and regional frameworks for climate information services that are based on a common set of standards, protocols, guidelines, coordination and management of national climate services. The project will support integration of climate services in key policies, strategies, plans and budgets which will provide a foundation for uptake of climate information in decision-making processes at all levels. National and regional frameworks will be supported by the development of climate information products and anticipatory action including by integration in the national/regional budget/loans will also contribute to sustainability.

State-of-the-art hydrometeorological monitoring equipment and tools for observations, monitoring, modeling and prediction will be integrated in the existing hydrological and meteorological institutions, based on available data management systems and infrastructure. The project will support the development of a National Information and Communication System. Special attention will be given to building capacities for GEM of both equipment and data analysis and interpretation skills. This will ensure that NIMRs are able to provide climate information services in support of strategies for QBM through financial arrangements such as SOFF, for which it will provide support. NIMRs will be supported in designing and disseminating a suite of tailored climate information products for national ministries, departments and agencies. The project will also support the development of a National Climate Information Service. Support purchased through GCF Processes will be transferred to the governments per standard processes as detailed in Article III of the GCF Agreement. This will ensure that the NIMRs and other relevant government is mutually agreed upon manner that places a priority on the sustainability of project results and objectives.

In order to ensure long-term technical and capacity support, the project will support NIMRs and NDINAs to participate in multi-stakeholder platforms for climate information services. These platforms will be established at national level with governments and policy makers. It will allow network actors to apply to a wide variety of other funding sources, including utilizing academic grants to support research as part of MEL. This national and regional technical capacity will furthermore ensure that NIMRs and NDINAs are able to provide climate information services in support of strategies for QBM and support their capacity to sustain transformation processes in the future. By including DAEs and NDINAs in the development of proposals, this programme will also expose them to new ideas and enhance their proposed development capacity.

#### Community level capacity strengthening

The project will adopt a broad multi-stakeholder approach and bring on board government agencies, multilateral and international organizations, civil society actors, Organisations of Persons with Disabilities (OPDs), Red Cross and Red Crescent network, private sector actors and communities as users of climate information, early warning and anticipatory actions as well as from the perspective of their role in implementing strategies for QBM. The project will support the strengthening of government led coordination mechanisms. Institutional linkages will be strengthened by enacting enabling policies and procedures that improve effectiveness of delivery and use of EWSs. Local, community-based institutions, in particular, will be strengthened to ensure that they are able to provide climate information services in support of strategies and plans. These activities will be implemented through government agencies and institutions with a mandate for disaster risk reduction, early warning and response. Lessons learned from past time and scope these strategies and plans and successes will be scaled up through the relevant agencies to other vulnerable regions in the country.

#### Programmatic alignment

This multi-country project will unlock opportunities for coordinated action and serve as an accelerator and amplifier for EWSs support on-financed through complementary instruments. It will work closely with other important programmes such as CREWS, GCF and SRF. The project will also contribute to the achievement of the climate change goals achieved by building on multilateral mechanisms such as CREWS and the GCF, which mobilizes a significant number of diverse external sources ranging from Ministries to national NGOs operating through a collaborative and partnership-based business models. This will ensure scaling up of projects supported by these agencies, particularly under SOFF and CREWS.
