## Financing

Establishing a financing strategy in each participating country is one of the deliverables of the project to address the critical challenge of financial sustainability. The strategy will assess the feasibility of various possible sources of finance, including public and private funding mechanisms, and identify the most appropriate mix for each country based on their specific needs. This includes the national hydrometeorological monitoring network including public and private financing sources. The financing strategy will include financial mechanisms that ensure the sustenance of: i) the national climate change adaptation framework and scenario and trigger development; ii) support to build institutional capacities, capabilities down to the level of communities. These mechanisms will be operated at multiple levels and tailored to each country's fiscal policies and capacities for effective and efficient fund transfer for DRM. Contingency funds will thereby be retained by countries to address unforeseen events or emergencies in a timely manner. The strategy will also ensure that through effective action built on CIEWS, this arrangement applies to countries where contingency funds are already available. This will be achieved through the harmonization of these mechanisms based on agreed approaches built by consensus between governments and donors. Where this coordination is lacking the project will help coordination mechanisms between relevant agencies and the government.

### Public-Private Partnership

The financing plan will explore public-private partnership on data usage, analysis and dissemination. It will develop and implement campaigns and consultations with various development partners, seek synergy among donors and coordinate funding strategies to ensure that all parties are aligned in their efforts towards achieving the project goals. Direct Access Entities to fast track access to GCF financing on early warnings. Countries will be supported in sourcing financing from national financial institutions, international organizations, vertical funds and corporate donors seeking to support climate adaptation.

### Policy Strengthening

Policy actions will fit into policies and governance arrangements to strengthen national level FMS coordination among governing actors at the national and local level across sectors. Relevant national strategies and plans will be updated or developed for NMHS and NOA/MA entities in each country contributing to Sendai Target 7 Indicator A-All countries, B-Developing countries, C-Least Developed Countries, D-Small Island Developing States. Strengthening policy/governance coordination, exchanging of information, coordinated dissemination of warnings and establishing DRM operational procedures will be supported by the project. The strategy will also ensure that there is a common supportive framework and legislation within the respective countries. The strategy will be linked to social protection and DRM based on local priorities and will address assignment of roles and responsibilities at the national and subnational level for governance and implementation. A common platform will be established and inter-institutional arrangements will be specified for data sharing, inter-operability of systems, co-design of services.

## The project is expected to support capacity building, technology development and technology transfer through multiple mechanisms.

Under Activity 1.3 knowledge exchange between countries and across initiatives with a focus on countries serving as hubs for early warning systems (EWS) and/or disaster risk management (DRM), the project will support capacity building through development of analytic frameworks and models for BfA and scenarios to inform early and anticipatory actions.

Under Activity 1.3 the project will support capacity development towards meeting the needs of Sendai Framework Target 4, which includes strengthening national capacities in the areas of EWS and DRM. This will be achieved by providing information to support BfA, better integration of NDMAs and NMMS for more efficient use of this information and improved monitoring, evaluation and learning systems.

Use a hybrid approach to improve early warning services and disaster risk management (DRM) by addressing technical gaps leading to better accuracy and timeliness of these forecasts leading to GABON compliance. This will also lead to the installation of EWS and forecasting services for all priority services leading to meeting Sendai Target 4 Indicator 4.

Activity 2.3 will support the development, deployment, and maintenance of early warning systems (EWS) and broadcasting alerts, communications and dissemination of early warnings across multiple channels, but specifically focusing on remote areas and areas with poor reach of the NMMS and most dissemination channels. The Common Alerting Platform in all countries backed with multiple channels of dissemination by working with network operators. The activity will also build capacities of extension services in co-development of early warning which integrate community-based early warning systems (CB-EWS) into their disaster risk management activities. Data for autonomous community based EWS will be transferred for relevant hazards to adapt to local EWS needs, particularly in remote, 'off-grid' locations and areas with poor reach of the NMMS and most dissemination channels.

# EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s initial Investment Framework.

## GLOBAL CONTEXT

- **3.5 to 3.6 billion people are highly vulnerable to climate change** (1).
- Between 2012 and 2019, 1.5 billion people were affected by disasters.
- $133 billion USD in economic losses of 145 countries reporting to the Sendai Framework.

### IMPACT OF DISASTERS

- Only 1% of disaster-related development assistance was allocated to ex-ante prevention and preparedness, while the majority went towards post-disaster response (2).
- As of 2021, half the countries in the world, less than one-third of LDCs and less than a third of SIDS had a MAPP.

### COST-BENEFIT ANALYSIS

Numerous studies have assessed the costs and benefits of EWS. EWS save lives and assets worth at least ten times their cost (3). In 2019, the World Bank estimated that $8 million USD in such systems in developing countries would avoid losses of $5–16 billion per year. Such investments benefit from high-benefit-cost ratios. For each of the 7 countries included in this project, an economic analysis will be conducted to assess the cost-effectiveness and financial viability of the proposed EWS system against a baseline scenario analysis for the entire proposed project.

### PROJECT IMPACTS

The project is expected to have the following impacts of GCF strategic level impacts through increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the most vulnerable people and communities globally through a programmatic approach which accelerates access to EWS for the most critical climate hazards. It does so by investing in technologies and finance for generation, dissemination, and use of early warning information.

### IMPLEMENTATION

Seven countries will receive holistic investment and technical assistance to establish an effective end-to-end EWS system across all relevant sectors. This includes strengthening national capacities through training, capacity building, and ensuring last-mile communication and community-level capacities to receive, understand, and act on climate information and early warnings by ensuring each country prepares and updates disaster response plans and ensures communities have access to the necessary tools and resources for preparedness.

### IMPACT ON PEOPLE

The project will directly contribute to improving the resilience of people potentially at risk from climate-related extreme events and hazards. In 2019, an estimated 3 billion people were highly vulnerable to climate-induced disasters on account of socio-economic vulnerabilities and physical exposure.

### ACTIONABLE CLIMATE INFORMATION

Actionable climate information products for agriculture, energy and water management will increase resilience across all sectors in the seven countries. This includes early warning systems (EWS) that can be used to protect lives, livelihoods, crops, infrastructure and other assets. By the end of the project, it is expected that these target countries are well on track to achieve the targeted overall early warning coverage and hence the total population of the countries will be receiving actionable climate information products.

### GOVERNANCE AND IMPLEMENTATION

The project will support existing EWS frameworks such as WMO’s, WFP’s, and WFP’s aligned with existing initiatives including the Global Early Warning Initiative (GEWI), the UNDRR’s Climate Resilience Strategy, and the World Bank’s plans to make one billion people safer from disaster by 2030. The project will facilitate knowledge exchange, coordination and cooperation.

### REFERENCES

1. **UNFCCC**. *Global Green Climate Fund Proposal D*. Page 59.
2. **World Meteorological Organization (WMO)**. 2021.
3. **UNDRR and AKNL**. *Status of Action on Hazard Early Warning Systems: Target G* United Nations Office for Disaster Risk Reduction, 2022.


[1] UNFCCC Global Early Warning Initiative for the Implementation of Climate Adaptation. Early Warning for All – Executive Action Plan 2017-2037.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 60

The project will contribute to the achievement of the GCF Indicators of reduction in the number of people affected by climate-related disasters and extreme events.

1. Improving the resilience of people potentially at risk from climate-related extreme events and hazards in LDCs and SIDS where communities are highly vulnerable to climate-induced disasters on account of socio-economic vulnerabilities, such as poverty, lack of access to basic services, limited infrastructure, exposure to natural disasters (e.g., hurricanes, cyclones, coastal flooding and sea-level rise), floods, landslides, and drought.
2. Strengthening NMHSs and NMSIs in participating countries through regional and global collaborations, which enhance the capacity of these institutions for early warning systems.
3. Enhancing regional and national NMHSs with targeted and timely data which help communities take effective responses to climate-related hazards such as hurricanes, cyclones, floods, landslides, droughts, heatwaves, and anticipatory actions, including forecast-based financing so that resources are made available to at-risk communities in advance.
4. Increasing the safeguarding of assets and infrastructure from climate-related hazards through accurate, timely and actionable early warning services. Actionable climate information products for agriculture, energy and water management can help reduce the impact of climate-related disasters on livelihoods, food security, and other climate-related hazards and thus reduce losses and damage to crops, infrastructure and other assets.
5. Strengthening national DRM legal frameworks and financing mechanisms as well as coordination, collaboration, knowledge sharing and information exchange among stakeholders in order to enhance disaster risk management and increased opportunities for regional cooperation and up scaling of climate resilient sustainable development.

# D2: Paradigm shift potential

This project will help shift the emphasis of DRM in countries from response to preparation and anticipatory action by strengthening national capacities for disaster preparedness and response. It will do so through a comprehensive approach to the design and implementation of evidence-informed transformed policies and plans which reduce the exposure and strengthen the resilience of communities, critical infrastructure, and vulnerable sectors to climate-related hazards and their impacts on climate change. It will ensure the needs of most vulnerable populations are considered in all phases of DRM activities and that they have access to relevant information, tools, and relevant policies, procedures and processes.

## Global support architecture

The project establishes a sustainable mechanism to support countries both through direct investments and by leveraging a comprehensive MWEHS value chain. It will catalyse learning and impact beyond one project-based investments, which characteristics investments in CEMs in many developing countries. Elements that constitute the global support architecture include: (i) establishment of an enabling environment for DRM; (ii) assessing the gaps and needs and fostering synergies with other initiatives with complementary objectives. These include the Alliance for Hydrometeorological Development, CREWS and SOF, which will lead to support towards knowledge generation and sharing among countries through ongoing development of a strategic framework for CEMs and for the global support architecture.

## Financial investments

Finally, these investments and commitments between countries will be based on EVM&E standards and standardized assessments. This represents an unprecedented level of cooperation and collaboration across many key actors in the MWEHS value chain, from global to regional, national and even sub-national levels. This will leverage multi-institutional partnerships for disaster preparedness and response. The project will also support countries in establishing multi-sectorial collaborations within government ministries and at-risk-level implementation mechanisms for disaster preparedness and response. The mechanism will enhance partnerships between government and non-government agencies, including the participation of civil society actors, Pillar Leads, NGOs and national entities and mechanisms such as CREWS and SOF.

## Scaling up and replication

The scaling up and replication to other countries is flexible given the global support structure, should further funding be made available. Substantively, the establishment of selected EBM schemes and Anticipatory Action Initiatives have a high potential for scaling up and replication in other countries. The implementation of these initiatives at scale will also contribute to scaling up and replication. For example: (i) The implementation of the CAP and use of multiple channels for dissemination, but those specifically leveraging on the cellular networks will facilitate uptake by different telecommunications companies; (ii) The development of a national early warning system and its integration into vulnerability assessments; (iii) Development of agricultural advisories using PICA and E-PICAS; and iv) Community-based autonomous water supply and alarm systems.

## Potential for knowledge sharing

The enhancement of MWEHS and production of IHP which utilise and combine traditional indigenous knowledge with modern and scientific techniques will facilitate adoption of adaptation and responses to early warnings, facilitation anticipatory action and the development of a culture of preparedness. The implementation of these initiatives will also contribute to scaling up and replication. For example: (i) The implementation of the CAP and use of multiple channels for dissemination, but those specifically leveraging on the cellular networks will facilitate uptake by different telecommunications companies; (ii) The development of agricultural advisories using PICA and E-PICAS; and iv) Community-based autonomous water supply and alarm systems.

## Enhancement of MWEHS

The enhancement of MWEHS and production of IHP which utilise and combine traditional indigenous knowledge with modern and scientific techniques will facilitate adoption of adaptation and responses to early warnings, facilitation anticipatory action and the development of a culture of preparedness. The implementation of these initiatives will also contribute to scaling up and replication. For example: (i) The implementation of the CAP and use of multiple channels for dissemination, but those specifically leveraging on the cellular networks will facilitate uptake by different telecommunications companies; (ii) The development of agricultural advisories using PICA and E-PICAS; and iv) Community-based autonomous water supply and alarm systems.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 - PAGE 62

## Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment:

The project will contribute to the creation of a conducive global level, building on the significant investments of SDRF in human and institutional capacity development and thereby promoting country-level actions towards the EWRM baseline.

### Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment:

It will streamline national and local protocols to improve institutional effectiveness of coordinating with multiple stakeholders. It will also provide a platform for countries to share best practices, lessons learned, and information for anticipatory action. It will strengthen national DRM legal frameworks as well as coordination, collaboration, and knowledge sharing from the local to the international level leading to an effective DRM cycle and increased opportunities for regional cooperation.

### Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment:

The project will catalyze a systems change in the operational procedures and processes of participating countries in the generation and use of early warnings. It will facilitate the integration of climate information services in key decision-making processes, including disaster risk management (DRM) planning, budgeting, and programming.

### Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment:

It will also provide a platform for coordination between meteorological and hydrological organizations with other governmental institutions by embedding early warnings, DRR and anticipatory action into government contingency plans and DRM budgets. It is advocating for and providing links to international climate services providers such as SOTC, WMO, and others to ensure that participating countries have access to tailored climate information services on climate change threats; its link with initiatives such as SOFF to ensure the flow of climate information from these organizations to national level actors as well as human and institutional capacity of NAMs as per WMO standards; and it will deliver fine-grained and updated vulnerability assessments to assist in long-term disaster mitigation and adaptation planning.

### Overall development and dissemination of timely, tailored and targeted climate information products:

The development and dissemination of timely, tailored, and targeted climate information products will be transformational in building the long-term resilience of key economic sectors such as agriculture, tourism and health, to climate change impacts. The use of these climate information products will increase efficiency and provide more detailed and better-informed approaches and response actions to climate change.

### Overall development and dissemination of timely, tailored and targeted climate information products:

A summary of the paradigm shift potential for each participating country is presented below.

#### Antigua and Barbuda

Overall contribution to climate resilient development pathways consistent with relevant national climate change adaptation strategies; and also,

The proposed approach for Antigua and Barbuda will create a paradigm shift in the end-to-end MHEWS process, enabling effective anticipatory action and planning across multiple sectors. In particular, the project will be transformative in the near term by improving the ability of the country to monitor and forecast climate-related variables such as rainfall, winds and sea temperature and other parameters. This will have a significant impact in terms of building resilience of fisheries ecosystems and coastal communities against extreme weather events, including hurricanes, flooding, and marine activities – including shopping and tourism - against the impacts of swells, coastal flooding and erosion. The marine monitoring will also enable tracking and forecasting of environmental impacts in coastal ecosystems - including coral reefs and mangroves - as well as providing early warning for tsunamis and other natural disasters.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 63

## Contribution to regulatory framework and policies, and the creation of an enabling environment

The approach for Antigua and Barbuda is focused on a strong cross-cutting governance and coordination component, mainstreaming disaster preparedness and response into national sectoral policies and strategies, as well as ensuring effective implementation through capacity development. The effectiveness of this approach can be enhanced by the development of a national strategic plan for national emergency management agencies, that together with the policy updates will provide a solid foundation for the effective functioning and sustainability of MHEWS and ensure its long-term success.

### In addition to strengthening the regulatory framework for MHEWS, the project will build capacity among key agency representatives in disaster risk management and climate change adaptation through training and awareness programs. Together with the strategic action plan, such capacity development will create an enabling environment for collaborative actions, ensuring smooth and efficient implementation of preparedness and response strategies.

#### Potential for Knowledge Sharing and Learning

The establishment of a National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) and a comprehensive climate data management strategy provides a model that can be replicated across other sectors or regions within Ecuador and even in other countries facing similar climates. The use of innovative technologies for data collection and forecasting, the inclusion of communication strategies to disseminate information on climate change and disaster risk reduction, as well as the development of an enabling environment through strengthening governance structures and establishing inter-ministerial and multi-stakeholder coordination platforms, will provide support for replication and scaling up in other regions of Ecuador and countries in the region.

#### Potential for Knowledge Sharing and Learning

The project contributes to the creation of a harmonized MEWS strategy which will systematically capture lessons learned and best practices. This creates a strong foundation for knowledge sharing at both national and international levels. The development of standard data frameworks and the establishment of a climate data centre will also foster an environment where climate data is readily available, enhancing decision-making and planning across sectors.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE D4

## Contribution to the Regulatory Framework and Policies

The MWEWS project will directly contribute to the enhancement of Ecuador's regulatory framework by creating a functional regulatory framework for MWEWS. The project's focus on developing and strengthening governance capacities, including the development of an enabling environment for climate change adaptation, will lead to more coherent and effective climate-related policies. Additionally, the creation of binding documents for warning dissemination and the development of anticipatory action protocols will contribute to more robust and enforceable policies.

### Overall Contribution to Climate-Resilient Development Pathways Consistent with Relevant National Climate Change Adaptation Strategies

The project aligns with Ecuador's national climate change adaptation strategies by enhancing the country's ability to anticipate, prepare for, respond to, and recover from climate-related hazards. By improving disaster risk knowledge, enhancing forecasting capabilities, and strengthening governance capacities, the project contributes meaningfully to the broader goals of sustainable development in Ecuador.

### Chad

#### Contribution to Climate-resilient Development Pathways Consistent with Relevant National Climate Change Adaptation Strategies and Aligns with Relevant International Agreements like the Paris Agreement

This initiative will strengthen climate information systems by supporting the development of EWS, the monitoring of climate variables and observation facilities, and building the capacity of the stakeholders involved. It will contribute to strengthening governance capacities for disaster risk management in Chad.

#### Contribution to the Regulatory Framework and Policies, and the Creation of an Enabling Environment

The project will address the disconnect between climate information systems and the end users in Chad by investing across the value chain from data collection to dissemination. This will ensure that climate information is communicated to people most at risk of climate disasters and builds two-way communication as a way of promoting a culture of risk and an understanding of local capacities, and local knowledge systems for disaster preparedness and disaster response. The project will also contribute to strengthening governance capacities by supporting the development of EWS, and in EWS, to ensure a coordinated approach that contributes to a longer-term vision in Chad.

#### Potential for Scaling up and Replication

Qualitative research and dialogue conducted to inform the design and delivery of inclusive and people-centered EWS will address the gap between climate information systems and end users. This will also contribute to addressing the gap between climate information systems and end users, which is a major challenge in many countries.

#### Lessons from this Process and its Results Will be Relevant for Comparable Contexts

The project will invest substantially in knowledge management, M&E, accountability and learning. Research and dialogue conducted to inform design and delivery will also shape and influence global and regional policy discussions on early warning systems and climate change adaptation. The lessons learned through the process of research and development of findings, programs and tools through targeted regional engagement, ensuring community views and voices are included in target groups, and the sharing of knowledge with stakeholders and partners. Local and learning will be shared at the global level through the Anticipation Hub and the RAP. Community members and local organizations will be engaged in advocacy efforts at both regional and global levels.

# Potential for scaling up and replication

## Project interventions in the four target provinces will act as a proof of concept for what a comprehensive approach to EWWAI at a local level can achieve. For this reason, the project will monitor progress carefully and leverage for good practices and lessons learned and replication as part of the overall EWWAI initiative as well as potential for scaling up in other provinces around the country. Additionally, the project will advocate and build capacity for improved monitoring and learning across the EWWAI initiative.

### Potential for knowledge sharing and learning

The Cambodia EWWAI project will form a central part of the already ongoing EWWAI initiative started in October 2021. A key focus of this project is to scale up the work that has been done under the framework of the EWWAI development, developed in conjunction with three government ministries and 34 other agencies engaged with the initiative.

The EWWAI initiative is planning regular semi-annual meetings where facilitators will ensure a focus on cross-pillar knowledge sharing and learning.

### Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

A key area of the project will be on capacity development for the National Committee for Disaster Management (NCDM) and other relevant government agencies. The National Meteorological Authority (NMA) will be both the central as well as the subnational levels. Training interventions will emphasize making the best use of regional centres of excellence and investing in skills in newly emerging technologies such as AI and remote sensing capacities. In addition, limited local focus will also be given to capacity development for the National Committee on Climate Change (NC3C) related to digital transformation.

In the target provinces, for the first time, the project will operationalize the newly developed government disaster risk management framework.

### Contribution to the regulatory landscape and policies

In line with existing laws and decrees on disaster management[1], in 2021 UNDP developed and advocated for the implementation of a national disaster preparedness and response law within the NCDM. This project will review these SOPs as well as develop protocols for shared data management collaboratively with government ministries through the EWWAI initiative. In addition, as part of the planned NDM capacity development initiatives at national level, this project will also support the implementation of a National Disaster Risk Management Action Plan.

### Overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways consistent with relevant national climate change adaptation strategies and plans

Studies suggest that implementing these systems could prevent annual well-being losses of around US$50 million and avoid harm to up to 1.6M people, potentially decreasing losses by up to a 24-38% response.

This project will also directly address the needs and priorities outlined in the National Action Plan on DRM (NAP-DRR) 2014–2028.

## Potential for scaling up and replication

### Integration of AI-powered systems in EWS will provide more accurate forecasts, which will also enhance people's faith in the system. By integrating historical weather data into AI, one can predict future events effectively, especially for specific regions like agriculture and emergency services. It can optimize resource allocation and assess risks of extreme weather conditions.

### Although Cell Broadcast (CB) is not a new technology for public warning systems, it has not been implemented in Fiji due to the lack of infrastructure requirements for mobile service providers. However, the current infrastructure can be upgraded to support CB. The proposed project will integrate CB into the existing EWS system and provide training to farmers in Fiji and will enhance these mechanisms by integrating additional systems to disseminate early warning messages utilizing the Common Alert Protocol system.

### This initiative will also provide a reference point for farmers as reference points to monitor crop status, and provide farmers with guidance on necessary actions is a simple but effective way to implement an impact-based early warning system. Although CB is not yet available in Fiji at present, it will be relevant to farmers. Furthermore, this system has the potential to be extended to other crops in Fiji.

## Potential for knowledge sharing and learning

### Most importantly, people often make inappropriate actions in response to early warnings. Therefore, delays can be caused by a lack of awareness or insufficient knowledge about what they should do. This lack of action may be due to a lack of faith in the messages or insufficient knowledge of the actions they could take to reduce impacts. It can also be attributed to resource or financial constraints, in the cases of emergency services and disaster management agencies. Therefore, it is important for people to have access to information that will enable them to take such actions. Therefore, to have an effective EWS/EAM system in Fiji, there is a need to address people's mindsets and attitudes towards early warning messages, so they are willing to take responsive actions, knowing that if not everybody takes action, the impact of the disaster can be reduced.

### It is important for people to understand what constitutes an early warning message and what influences their decision-making and actions. This understanding can inform the development and implementation of innovative approaches and actions. Technological advancements that enhance community capacity and inclusivity for people with disabilities and the elderly, empowerment of women, and youth are also essential in ensuring that people have access to information that will enable them to take action when an early warning message is received.

### The project will facilitate a stakeholder and harmonized multi-hazard risk assessment methodologies and tools across different agencies. This will provide a common understanding of the risks associated with hazards, exposure, capacity, and impacts. This will necessitate the development of a centralised database or repository system for disaster-related data and information on community-specific risks, incorporating traditional, local, and indigenous knowledge. Once in a mature state, this system can be used to inform the development monitoring of Future VDR or DRM initiatives in Fiji and other countries in the region.

Furthermore, it will aim to enhance technical support for continuously collecting risk data, analyzing it, and applying

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal V3.0 - Page 70

## Environmental benefits

Climate information and M&E will inform the management of natural resources and adaptation to climate impacts such as floods and droughts, particularly in the agricultural sector. The project will scale up the co-development and use of climate information for decision-making by farmers and other stakeholders through the adoption of resilient agricultural practices which improve productivity and reduce agricultural losses.

## Economic benefits

Improved access and coverage of M&E and more effective use of early and anticipatory action will have significant economic benefits. These economic benefits will be in the form of reduced losses and damages across multiple sectors, including agriculture, transport and energy. Better design, planning and management of private and public infrastructure will yield cost savings by reducing damage to assets and infrastructure. The project will also help reduce the impacts of climate change on morbidity and mortality of exposed and vulnerable populations.

## Social benefits

The humanitarian sector is likely to benefit significantly from disaster preparedness and anticipatory action to prevent the spread of diseases in the wake of disasters. The project will directly support livelihoods of agricultural communities, including those engaged in raising livestock and fisheries. Advisories and warnings will reduce the loss of live and livestock through disease outbreaks and other climate-related events such as floods and droughts. The project will also help prepare relevant agencies and communities to respond effectively over resources and access that emerge from climate-mediated hazards. This is most relevant to the northernmost eastern African context where migratory pastoral communities can come into conflict with settled farmers.

# D.4 Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## Least Developed Countries, LDCs and most African countries are particularly vulnerable to a range of climate change impacts and climate-related hazards, which affect people, livelihoods, assets and economy. Affected countries have highlighted the urgent need for better climate information and early warning to help adapt to, mitigate and recover from increasing climate risks. The project will address these needs by strengthening the capacity of national meteorological services of their population to natural hazards and to strengthen and scale up early warning action.

It is noted that vulnerable groups within affected countries are often socially and economically more vulnerable and spend a large part of their income on subsistence, which limits their ability to adapt. Climate change impacts often undermine progress that has been made in poverty reduction efforts and can have significant negative effects on food security and economic growth, particularly in rural areas.

The project will help these rural populations reduce the vulnerability of their livelihoods and assets to climate-related hazards by:

- Strengthening climate information services through enhancing institutions, coordination mechanisms, policies and financial mechanisms and frameworks. This will sustainably address the needs and priorities of the NDMIs and NMDAs in each country.
- Improving the capacity of NDMIs and NMDAs to provide timely and accurate climate information and enhanced capacity of these agencies will meet the needs for enhanced RF, access to data and effective and anticipatory action. The project will support the development of a national strategy for NDMIs and NMDAs will be key responsible parties in the respective projects, which directly addresses their needs and priorities.
- Through this project, systems building of national and local actors will be addressed. This can include strengthening the capacity of NDMIs and NMDAs to provide climate information services at the national emergency operations centres, improving logistics and supply chains for disaster preparedness and response along with relevant procurement processes, establishing partnerships/MoUs between NDMIs, NMDAs, ministries and other organizations responsible for critical climate adaptation measures, National Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies and others.
- The project will address the needs of vulnerable groups within which are often neglected in the project design. These include women, children, older people with disabilities, elderly, youth, cultural minorities, urban and rural communities. In addition, special attention will be given to women-headed households, households with infants, elderly, children and youth.
- Needs of communities particularly exposed to climate-related hazard for longer periods and anticipatory early warning that will enable them to anticipate and prepare for hazards long in advance, in particular those living in coastal areas are particularly vulnerable to climate-related hazards. The specific climate information needs of women, elderly and disabled people require particular attention as they often face increased socio-economic vulnerabilities and relative lack of access to resources and more limited access to resources and services.
- Communities dependent on natural resources, especially those reliant on rain-fed agriculture, pastoralism, forest products and fisheries are often socially and economically more vulnerable and spend a high proportion of their income on subsistence which limits their ability to adapt. Climate change impacts often undermine progress that has been made in poverty reduction efforts and can have significant negative effects on food security and economic growth, particularly in rural areas. This document addresses conflicts. People moved by climate-related events are often treated differently from those displaced by conflict, mostly because climate shocks, such as floods, are seen as temporary and affected people are expected to move back to their original areas. However, the occurrence of climate-related displacements have been increasing in recent years, with significant impacts on host communities' access to land, water, food, and natural resources. Early warnings about the onset of adverse climatic conditions and disasters can help avert conflict by providing timely information to agencies involved in humanitarian work, peace building, and development.

The project will address needs of sectors such as agriculture, tourism, health and water infrastructure for Antigua and Barbuda. It will also provide tools and resources to facilitate adaptation planning and action.

## Summary of the Needs of each country is presented below:

### Antigua and Barbuda

**Vulnerability of the country:**

Antigua and Barbuda are particularly vulnerable to extreme climate events such as hurricanes, droughts and coastal flooding, ranking 85th on the ND-GAIN index. The country's NCD identifies five primary reasons for the country’s vulnerability:

- **High exposure:** Antigua and Barbuda is highly exposed to natural hazards; high cost of repairing damage caused by recurrent extreme climate events;
- **The composition of the country’s economy: a high population density in the coastal zone, and low limited availability of freshwater resources.
- **Economic dependence on tourism:** Tourism accounts for 20% of GDP and 45% of export earnings.

**Climate change is increasing the frequency of high-intensity hurricanes, which will have severe impacts on the country, including loss of life, economic losses and damage to infrastructure. These impacts are further exacerbated by the potential increase in sea levels due to climate change.**

Antigua and Barbuda’s economy is heavily dependent on tourism, which accounts for 20% of GDP (87%) and accounts for the highest overall investment, at 40%. Antigua and Barbuda is also heavily reliant on imports of basic supplies, including food, electricity and building materials, increasing vulnerability to disruptions to shipping routes. The country’s economy is also highly dependent on remittances from its diaspora.

Antigua and Barbuda are also particularly vulnerable given the limited availability of risk information and forecasts for ocean conditions or environmental hazards.

### Alternative sources of financing (e.g., fiscal or balance of payments gap) that prevents government from addressing the needs of the country; and level of depth and history in the local capital market

While Antigua and Barbuda has a relatively small economy, it is heavily constrained by the current economic conditions in the country. Although classified as high-income in terms of per capita GDP, the distribution of wealth is largely skewed by a few high-income persons who have moved to the country abroad, with most of the population living below or just above the poverty line (30% and 45%, respectively). This, along with its high public debt – more than 87% in GDP in 2019 – constrain the government’s ability to allocate funding from the national budget for climate change adaptation. The country is also heavily reliant on international aid.

Anticipatory Action measures needed to build climate resilience and preparedness of Antigua and Barbuda's population.

### Needs for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity

The gap analysis indicates that there are significant gaps in several areas where capacity development and institutional strengthening is needed to enable effective MHIWS and Anticipatory Action. This includes the need for:

- The revitalisation of the linkages between national development organisations and each major stakeholder ministries responsible for disaster risk reduction, climate change adaptation, emergency response, and environmental management; strengthening the institutional and human capacity of ACDI.

#### Vulnerability of the Country and/or Specific Vulnerable Groups, Including Gender Aspects (For Adaptation Only)

**Ecuador is highly vulnerable to a range of climate-related hazards, including floods, landslides, and droughts. These events disproportionately affect vulnerable populations, including indigenous communities, low-income households, and women, who are often the primary caregivers for families and most affected by climate change impacts and response. The project's focus on integrating gender-sensitive approaches into EWS directly addresses these vulnerabilities. Through tailoring climate services and early warning messages to the needs of these groups, the project aims to enhance their capacity to anticipate, prepare for, respond to, and recover from climate-related hazards at the Economic and Social Development Level of the Country and the Affected Population**

**Ecuador's economic and social development is uneven, with significant disparities between urban and rural areas. Many rural communities are particularly vulnerable due to poverty and lack of access to services making them particularly susceptible to climate hazards. The MWEWS project will contribute to enhance the resilience of these vulnerable populations by providing information and early warning services which can prevent significant economic losses and support sustainable development.**

**Ecuador is also highly vulnerable, having a high debt burden and limited access to international capital markets, which hinder the government's ability to independently finance large-scale climate adaptation initiatives. The MWEWS project addresses this gap by establishing a National Climate Finance Framework, which will help mobilise and sustain funding for climate change adaptation interventions.**

#### Need for Strengthening Institutions and Implementation Capacity

**Ecuador's institutions, while developing, still require significant strengthening to effectively manage and implement comprehensive climate change adaptation strategies. This includes improving the institutional framework for disaster risk management, improving inter-ministerial and multi-stakeholder coordination, and building technical capacities within key institutions such as the National Meteorological and Hydrological Institute (NMHI). The project also enhances the development of a Multi-Hazard MWEWS system to provide early warning services in a timely manner, ensuring that Ecuador's climate adaptation efforts are both effective and sustainable over the long term.**

#### Vulnerability of the Country and/or Specific Vulnerable Groups, Including Gender Aspects (For Adaptation Only)

**Cambodia, a country still recovering from decades of conflict, faces significant development challenges and is particularly susceptible to compounding and cascading risks. These risks are further exacerbated after extreme weather events such as rural crop failures, livestock losses, and food insecurity. Climate change increases the frequency and severity of floods, droughts, and storms, exacerbating poverty and food insecurity. Economic downturns and health crises further amplify these vulnerabilities by reducing access to essential services and resources. Women in Cambodia are particularly vulnerable to climate-related shocks and stresses. Specific examples in Cambodia include rural communities highly vulnerable to climate-related shocks and stresses, women and girls facing gender-based discrimination and limited economic opportunities, and the urban poor who lack access to social protection measures and other resources experiencing discrimination and increased vulnerability to exploitation.**

**Economic and Social Development Level of the Country and the Affected Population**

**Between 2014 and 2021/2022, Cambodia made significant progress in reducing poverty, dropping the poverty incidence from 36.7% to 18.16%. However, after the 2019 COVID-19 outbreak and economic shock, approximately 4.5 million people were still living below the poverty line as of 2021/2022. The country is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts on economic instabilities, natural hazard-related disasters, and environmental degradation. Cambodia is highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change, which is projected to exacerbate flooding and drought risks and decrease the country's GDP by up to 3% in the long term.**

#### Vulnerability of the Country and/or Specific Vulnerable Groups, Including Gender Factors (Adaptation Only)

- **Chad's extreme vulnerability** to the impacts of climate change as reflected in its position on the ND-GAIN index (8.4), a development index that ranks countries based on their ability to adapt to global warming. Of proposals submitted. Agriculture in Fiji, mainly rain-fed and small-scale, supports over 117,000 people primarily in rural areas. **Fiji’s GDP is around US$48 billion**, with a per capita GDP of US$536.2. The economy is mainly driven by tourism and agriculture with 91% of the population living near the coast. By the end of the century, natural hazards may annually cost over US$70 million in damages to infrastructure and property. A 1-meter sea-level rise would cause a loss of 4,500 jobs and 23,000 people would be displaced by 2050.

A **1,000-year river flood** in Fiji can lead to 12% falling into poverty, with an average annual loss of US$36 million attributed to natural disasters. The majority of the population lives on small islands and low-lying coastal areas, relying heavily on subsistence farming. Climate change worsens their situation, impacting future extreme weather events. Recognized for their vulnerability, these populations will suffer more from tropical cyclones and floods. There is a rising risk of disasters and disaster-related impacts in Fiji’s coastal communities.

**Fiji faces significant climate risks**, including increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, sea-level rise, and changes in precipitation patterns. These developments are impacting human health, food security, water availability, and infrastructure. Climate change also affects the economy, agriculture, and ecosystems, potentially resulting in loss of life and displacement.

Key sectors vulnerable to climate change include communities, the economy, and infrastructure. Areas especially susceptible to changing climate conditions:

- **Agriculture**
- **Water Supply and Sanitation**

Though Fiji has access to many global and regional donors and has been the recipient of numerous projects, either bilateral or multilateral, none have focused on establishing an end-to-end Early Warning System (EWS) in Fiji. Most projects have focused on specific aspects such as weather forecasting, climate change adaptation, and disaster risk reduction.

The **Fiji EWS project** is a 4-year project focusing on the establishment of an EWS to improve preparedness and response. This is the first project on EWS4all in Fiji, and there are a few other multilateral projects in the pipeline or at their early stages that also focus on EWS, which includes Fiji such as Weather Ready and Resilient (WRR) and Drought Early Warning System (DEWS). The project aligns well with the WRR and DEWS initiatives by focusing on strengthening the capacity of national meteorological services systems and capacity building. Consequently, it allocates the entire project budget of US$500k as co-financing.

For strengthening institutions and implementation capacity:

However, despite the progress made in establishing a framework for the development of an Early Warning System (EWS), there are still gaps and challenges across four essential pillars in Fiji. These challenges stem from fragmented resources, limited technical capacity, and a lack of coordination among key stakeholders in the EWS. To address these issues, it is necessary to enhance institutional capacity by strengthening the national meteorological service system, improving the accessibility and inclusivity of early warning messages, establishing community feedback mechanisms, and integrating traditional knowledge into early warning systems.

The fragmented resources include limited funding for the development and maintenance of the WMO Registered Warning Alerting System and lack of cell broadcast implementation. The regulatory framework for mobile manufacturers/imports to support cell broadcast is also missing. There is no automated system in place to mitigate impacts from extreme weather events, such as cyclones or floods. Additionally, there are limited resources available for the dissemination of warnings by fishermen at sea. Furthermore, there is no coordination structure among different actors working in communication and early warning systems. The lack of a coordinated approach can lead to fragmented information sharing between stakeholders, including government agencies, NGOs, and community groups.

The challenges can be attributed to differing priorities, mandates, and approaches. It is necessary to conduct studies to understand how people access and interpret early warnings and the effectiveness of current communication channels in terms of coverage, frequency, and dissemination processes. Additionally, the lack of feedback mechanisms and surveys currently negatively impacts the quality of warnings. Therefore, it would be beneficial to invest further on feedback mechanisms.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 76

## Vulnerability of the Country and/or Specific Vulnerable Groups, including Gender Aspects

### Somalia

Somalia is one of the most climate-vulnerable countries in the world, with a ranking of 178 out of 182 countries on the ND-GAIN Index 2014, and with the least capacity to respond and recover from extreme weather events. **Somalia consistently ranks among the top five countries** that are highly vulnerable to climate change and severe environmental conditions, affecting the depletion, use and access to natural resources and livelihoods. Moreover, Somalia has a large number of GPs and is one of the highest refugee-producing countries.

Economic vulnerability in Somalia is high due to its fragile economy, which relies heavily on agriculture, livestock, remittances, and international assistance. Desertification is reducing the viability of rain-fed agriculture and livestock production. Vulnerability is further exacerbated by a long-term drought that has already seen rice prices rise to historic highs.

### Absence of Alternative Sources of Financing

Somalia has faced recent humanitarian crises, including droughts, famines, and floods due to conflict. These have led to an alarming high price water crisis in the region. The absence of alternative sources of financing is a major challenge for Somalia's ability to respond effectively to climate change.

### Need for Strengthening Institutions and Implementing Capacity

In Somalia, interagency coordination gaps exist between government agencies and non-governmental organizations as well as a duplication of efforts and inefficient resource utilization. There is a need for policy development and policy harmonization, which means creating and enforcing comprehensive policies that are consistent with international best practices. This will require the need for capacity building in training policymakers and stakeholders to implement improvement and compliance.

### Ethiopia

Ethiopia is highly vulnerable to a range of climate-related hazards, including recurring droughts, floods, landslides, and wildfires, which are increasingly exacerbated by climate change. This vulnerability significantly impacts specific populations, particularly women and children who are disproportionately affected by these disasters. Women of reproductive age face substantial challenges due to systemic inequalities, with over 22% suffering from chronic malnutrition, and 10% experiencing severe acute malnutrition. Children under five years old are also affected by malnutrition, illustrating the severe repercussions of climate-induced food insecurity on human health and well-being. Addressing these vulnerabilities is crucial for enabling preparedness through effective EHSs, ensuring that critical information reaches decision-makers in a timely manner.

Economic and social development level of the country and the effect it population

Ethiopia, despite notable economic growth over recent years, remains a low-income country with a significant reliance on agriculture. The majority of its population resides in rural areas where livelihoods are closely tied to rain-fed agriculture. Rural households are often severely impacted by climate shocks leading to food insecurity, displacement, and heightened poverty rates. This economic vulnerability is compounded by the lack of access to social services such as education and healthcare, further exacerbating the challenges faced by these communities. As part of the feasibility preparation for the GCF-funded Multi-Hazard Early Warning System (MH-EWS) project in Ethiopia, it was vital to improve resilience and support sustainable economic development by integrating climate risk data into national and regional planning processes, thereby addressing the specific needs of the most affected populations.

### D.S. Country ownership (max. 500 words approximately | p.2)

This project will equip countries to establish state-of-the-art EWS which increase the resilience of the most vulnerable communities to climatic hazards; it will facilitate a paradigm shift wherein global and regional collaborations and resources are directed towards climate risk management, rather than disaster response. The project will focus on climate-centered early warnings. These will be coupled with mechanisms for sustainable financial and technical support to NDMA and national EWS focal points.

By focusing on multiple delivery mechanisms, enhancing capacities to be able to deliver support and create a knowledge base through national projects, the project will lead to participating countries developing timely, credible, holistic and easily understandable early warning systems. These will provide actionable information for decision-making in advance of extreme weather events and early warning. It will reduce vulnerability of communities to climate risks by supporting countries to reduce their own exposure through timely alerts and protocols designed to reach the grassroots. Furthermore, this project will establish links between EWS and disaster risk management (DRM) actors, enabling a more integrated approach to preparedness and support for adaptation and disaster recovery.

In recent years, the NDCs have provided a clear role of the NDMA in the country to explain what the aim was and would be, and present the initial architecture of the project. There were extensive discussions and consultations with the NDMA in each country during the NDC development. Furthermore, the NDMA convened larger consultations with a wide range of stakeholders including national disaster management agencies, meteorological services, universities, NGOs, etc., where National and regional DEMA were engaged with to establish their willingness to contribute to the design of country-specific MREWS (through the use of existing resources). The NDCs have also provided an opportunity for countries to explore how they can be used to ascertain the potential for GCF grant funding to be used to unlock additional lending/financing through de-risking measures.

National priorities, as expressed through NDAs, MNP, communications to the UNECE and the Sendai framework have been used as a basis for discussions and determining of appropriate action. Further engagement on private sector financing and insurance gap at national and regional MREWS and regional coordination mechanisms were conducted with participating countries.

## Climate Resilience and Applications Centre (CRAC), Pacific Community (SPC), Asia Disaster Preparedness Center (ADPC), Regional Integrated Multi-Hazard Early Warning System for Africa and the SAHEL (RIMES), Antigua and Barbuda

### The project ensures country ownership by closely aligning with Antigua and Barbuda’s national climate strategy and vulnerability assessment, as well as its disaster risk management framework. This project not only supports the country's climate goals but also strengthens its institutional and technical capacity for DRM.

#### Alignment with national climate strategy and frameworks
The project is aligned with the National Adaptation Plan (NAP), which focuses on enhancing climate resilience and reducing vulnerability to climate-induced hazards. By updating the national disaster management legislation and integrating disaster risk reduction into sectoral policies, the project directly supports the NAP's objectives of improving adaptive capacity at all levels.

#### Strengthening institutional and technical capacity
The project also strengthens institutional and technical capacity for DRM through enhanced knowledge, observatory systems, and community-based EMS. These efforts align with the strategic priorities outlined in the NAP, particularly by building adaptive capacity and integrating climate resilience into national and sectoral planning.

### Engagement with stakeholders
The project is being implemented through an extensive multi-stakeholder engagement process, including two stakeholder workshops to identify gaps and implementation actions, culminating in an implementation roadmap for reinforcing a people-centred MWECS in the country. These multi-stakeholder workshops were followed by multiple bi- monthly meetings of the steering committee and other key stakeholders to lead to, and into the specifics of the various gaps and needs identified, shaping them into actionable project.

### Civil Society Organisations
The project engages a wide range of stakeholders, including CSOs, by incorporating their input in the development of the project. This is achieved through the establishment of a steering committee based on EMS and response plans, ensuring that local knowledge and needs are considered. Local communities are also consulted to ensure the inclusion of their knowledge and practices into the disaster management framework, enhancing cultural relevance and effectiveness.

### Women and vulnerable groups
The project prioritizes the inclusion of women and other vulnerable groups by specifically engaging with them in all aspects of the project implementation. This is achieved through tailored communication frameworks for early warning and ensuring that women, persons with disabilities and other vulnerable communities are actively involved in the planning and execution of community-based EMS. Training and capacity-building activities will be designed to ensure their full participation.

### Strong partnerships
By fostering strong partnerships with national institutions, civil society, and local communities, the project ensures that stakeholders have a say in all aspects of its implementation. This participatory approach not only enhances the relevance and sustainability of the interventions but also strengthens overall ownership and commitment to achieving the project's goals.

#### Objectives
This initiative is fully in line with the priorities of the Government of Chad reflected in its development programs and plans, particularly in terms of disaster risk management programs. This project supports the Government’s National Development Plan (PND) for 2018-2023 and the National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) for 2016-2030 as specified under “Pillar II: Improving the quality of life of the Chad population”. The project is also in line with Priority R2 “Climate Risk Management” from the First National Adaptation Plan of Action and in line with the updated National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) 2021-2030.

#### Gender equality
This project will ensure that gender issues are addressed equitably across all its components by being aligned with the national gender policy. The project aims to address gender inequalities and inequities, from all forms of violence, where men and women have equal opportunities to access and control resources and participate equitably in decision-making processes in the interests of achieving sustainable development.

The proposed activities for Somalia directly respond to the needs outlined in the EWBHL Initiative 2014-2027 in Somalia. The National Multi-Hazard Early Warning Network (NMEWNC) was established to provide a comprehensive early warning system of all hazards, including climate change and weather-related infrastructure and an enabling policy and institutional environment for the dissemination of warnings. The Roadmap for EWBHL 2014-2027 in Somalia is aligned with the National Disaster Risk Management Framework (NDRMF) and the National Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction (2015–2030). In this context, the SoDHA has called for the national rollout of the EWBHL initiative, finalizing gap analysis, and agreeing on a roadmap to consolidate and grow coverage of NMEWNC to encompass all hazards. The Roadmap also calls for the establishment of an early warning system in Somalia, with the UNDP Somalia country office, the EWBHL initiative is now operationalized under the leadership of the NMEWNC.

The National Early Warning System (NEMS) was established by a systematic reformative initiative aimed at aligning it to Article 3, para. 2 and Article 7 para. 2 of the establishment Law 17/2015 of the SoDHA. These Articles state the clear responsibility of SoDHA to gather information and report on decisions concerning early warning systems in Somalia. The NEMS is currently being implemented by the country and abroad working on those activities. # D. Effectiveness and efficiency (max: 500 words, approximately | 1 page)

## The Fiji National Climate Change Policy (NCCP) 2018-2030 is to implement Fiji's development priorities from current, future, and intergenerational climate change risks. It also aims to anchor Fiji’s national climate response and adaptation in the broader context of sustainable development.

### National Adaptation Contributions (NDCs) Report to the UNFCCC
The NDCs report provides frameworks for addressing climate change impacts, and the 2018 Initial National Communication (INC) to the UNFCCC vulnerability assessments and adaptation strategies. 

#### Key Components:
- **Policy and implementation**: The policy identifies and utilizes related and interlinked priorities in DRM and climate change adaptation to allow for the advancement of national actions that effectively address both short and slow onset hazard events, as well as long-term climate impacts and risks.
- **Community service organizations**: All community service organizations involved with the EWS, including school groups, women’s groups, and youth, are coordinated through the FCOSS office. The FCOSS office coordinates with the government whenever there is a disaster, and the FCOSS director is a member of the NDCM Council.
- **Early warning systems**: The importance of early warning to these EWS is to assist in planning for response and recovery purposes. Therefore, access to early warning information is critical.

### Ethiopia
Accompanying national climate policy frameworks:
- **The IGES Early Warning for All Project** aligns strategically with Ethiopia’s national climate and disaster management strategies.
- **NDCR and CRGE**: The NDCR and the Climate Resilient Green Economy (CRGE) strategy specifically, the Roadmap for Multi-Hazard Impact-Based Early Warning and Early Action System (2017–2030) provides a framework for early warning systems. The Strategic Programme and Investment Framework for Climate Services, alongside the Strategy Programme and Investment Framework (SPIF), outlines priority actions to support climate services in Ethiopia.
- **NDCs**: As part of the NDC preparation process, the project reinforces Ethiopia’s commitment to sustainable development and poverty reduction through the integration of climate resilience measures into development initiatives.

### Engagement with national stakeholders
The feasibility preparation for the GEF-funded Early Warning for All Project involved extensive engagement with key stakeholders such as the Emergency Coordination Centre (ECC) and the ERMDC. The ECC Coordination Platform serves as an integrated framework to strengthen national EWS by coordinating actions across four key pillars: early warning, emergency response, disaster risk management, and climate change adaptation capability.

### D.E. Effectiveness and efficiency
The project is designed to align stakeholders at all levels—government agencies, civil society organizations, international partners, and local communities—to ensure that EWS are effective, inclusive, and sensitive to the local socio-political context. By addressing issues such as gender equality, social inclusion, environmental risks, and gender quality, the ERMDC platform will adopt a multi-sectoral and context-specific approach, ensuring that early warning mechanisms are not only operationally effective but also conflict-sensitive and gender-responsive.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 81

This proposal outlines a cost-effective alternative to reactive approaches to climate-related hazards that focus on ad-hoc recovery and investment; it will significantly enhance the risk knowledge and early action capacities of sectors and communities and will contribute to the capacity building of relevant stakeholders. The enhanced risk knowledge will enable disaster risk management (DRM) actors to better anticipate, prepare for, respond to, and recover from disasters and provide accurate information, thereby reducing the impacts of disasters on lives, livelihoods and assets. The project will ensure a larger share of climate-related investments in developing countries by strengthening national policies and procedures, for communities to prepare for and take pre-emptive action against disaster.

A high expected benefit-cost ratio (BCR) on investments in strengthening the hydrological and meteorological agencies and their capacity to provide early warning services and other related information. The project will also address gaps in the value cycle and ensures that actionable warnings reach the last mile and those at risk being able to respond. Improvements in MWEWS and preparedness makes it possible to limit losses from hydrometeorological hazard-induced disasters, such as floods, landslides, droughts, and heatwaves. The project will also improve access to hydrological, social, behavioral and related information, which this program will seek to do by leveraging additional investments in MWEWS and other national and sub-national governance by partners that are able to feed the original data into maintaining all elements of the MWEWS cycle.

MWEWS are a public good for under-developed countries and SIDS, the cost and technical barriers have been reduced significantly. The project will provide a framework for the development of MWEWS in developing countries and planning can only be removed by public investment financed by grant resources from international development institutions. The project does not require any new funding to be provided by governments or other donors. It is envisaged that investments from the private sector, which is scarce in many of the largest countries. However, the project does plan to engage the private sector by stimulating demand for climate information based value-added products and services, by engaging with insurance companies and financial institutions to provide insurance against weather-related risks on aviation and financial services such as insurance. The level of engagement and the type of industry will be determined by the nature of the risk and the willingness of the private sector to invest in this area. This could be achieved through co-financing or the public sector can also engage with the private sector.

On the other hand, there is overwhelming evidence of substantial benefits of investing in weather and climate information services for disaster risk management. The cost-benefit analysis (CBA) of investments in MWEWS estimated to be at least US$ 160 billion per year[2] and the net benefit of investing in the resilience of infrastructure in developing countries would be $4.2 trillion over the lifetime of new infrastructure[3]. The return on investment from DRR offer "triple dividends" by avoiding direct impact, enhancing economic potential and generating sustainable development co-benefits.[4]

### Efficiency and effectiveness

All benefits of MWEWS will be ensured by building on existing national capacities and infrastructure across the MWEWS value chain and leveraging investments from other donors, primarily from SDF and CERFs. Existing institutional capacities and linkages will be effectively used at all levels across government, non-government, private sector and civil society organizations. The project will also ensure that the benefits of MWEWS are sustained over time and create conditions to provide a foundation to strengthen and enhance MWEWS to the community level.

**D**

Synergies with ongoing government initiatives and projects, including those supported through vertical funds will be used to co-finance and share costs. Coordination with ongoing initiatives will prevent duplication of effort while optimizing investments and operation and maintenance of observation infrastructure. Technical support from regional facilities, national meteorological services (NMS), and other relevant partners will be provided for capacity building, training, and data acquisition. Costs of hiring technical staff and their training and capacity building within NMHS, NDMA and other relevant partners will also be reduced through collaborations with regional and national technical and research agencies.

The choice of technologies for observation, networking, and dissemination for each country is governed by a scientific gap analysis. Where possible, existing infrastructure will be used or upgraded to reduce costs. Where necessary, new equipment will be used, including gridded and remotely sensed datasets from other agencies to complement and supplement expensive observation infrastructure. Compliance to WMO and international public standards for data and communication and the use of open source software and free and open data will ensure that the project is sustainable in terms of operation and costs. The development of support networks comprising regional and national academic institutions and centres of expertise, including universities, research institutes, and other relevant organizations, as well as technical assistance from WMO and regional coordinating bodies (SPC, SPREP, SADC, ISAO, CCCCC, CAF etc) investments will be able to target the most critical gaps and ensure the full MNEWs cycle is achieved, reaching as many people, businesses and communities as possible.

## Replicability

The establishment of selected pilot schemes and Anticipating Action initiatives has a high potential for replicating innovations at the community level in other countries, with limited institutional learning. There is limited experience or opportunities for replicating lessons learned from these projects to other countries or even within the same country.

## Sustainability

Many developing countries struggle with establishing and maintaining national-level institutions that are capable of responding effectively to climate change. These challenges include weak institutional structures and capacity, lack of access to information technology, and a lack of human resources. Many countries have limited experience in using soft law mechanisms such as SOFIs, which can result in the creation of new legal instruments or strengthening existing ones. This will contribute to an enabling environment where climate change policies are developed, implemented, and disseminated, in a sustainable fashion, to the benefit of all stakeholders.

The project will create increased opportunities for regional cooperation and up-scaling of climate change adaptation initiatives at national level within sectoral and technical government entities, civil society organizations, and other relevant institutions. This will contribute to an enabling environment where climate change policies are developed, implemented, and disseminated, in a sustainable fashion, to the benefit of all stakeholders.

The project will also strengthen existing SOFIs and create new ones as needed for the generation and international sharing of weather and climate information services. The project will support the development of effective use of climate information to trigger and enhance disaster preparedness and planning activities at national level within sectoral institutions, including those in the water sector.

Deliberate and detailed impact assessment will be conducted on the effectiveness of the SOFIs targeted at increased political commitment for climate change adaptation. The project will ensure community involvement in disaster preparedness and planning.
