E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements

Monitoring and evaluation will be at two levels, at the global project level and at the country level in each of the seven countries. The global MEAL system will feed into overall project management and oversight, reporting as well as knowledge sharing across all seven countries. At the country level, monitoring and evaluation activities will be continuous, two-way flow of information. The national MEAL system will support adaptive management, reporting and knowledge sharing at the national level.

Monitoring and evaluation activities at both levels will be undertaken in compliance with the UNDP POPP and the UNDP Evaluation Policy. The primary responsibility for day-to-day project monitoring and implementation rests with the respective Project Managers. The Project Managers will develop annual work plans to ensure that all project implementation activities are completed on time, within budget and of high quality. They will also report to the Bureau and the UNDP Country Offices of any delays or difficulties during implementation, including the implementation of the MILE plan, so that the appropriate corrective actions can be taken in a timely manner.

The Project Manager is responsible for ensuring that the project team maintains a high level of transparency, responsibility and accountability in monitoring and reporting project results. The UNDP Bureau for Policy and Programme Support (BPPS) will support the global Project Manager through the Global Project Management Team (GPMT). The UNDP Country Office will support their respective Project Managers as needed, including through annual supervision missions.

The BPPS and the respective UNDP Country Offices are responsible for complying with UNDP project level M&E requirements as outlined in the UNDP Programme and Operations Policies and Procedures (POPP). Additional M&E implementation guidance is provided by the UNDP Technical Specialist Climate Change Adaptation and Resilience Advisor and the UNDP National Project Manager. The UNDP Country Office will support their respective Project Managers as needed.

The project target groups and stakeholders including the NDA focal Point will be involved as much as possible in national project level M&E.

An annual monitoring workshop will be held globally and at each country after the UNDP project document has been signed by all relevant parties to: a) reorient project stakeholders on the project strategy and discuss any changes in the overall context that influence project implementation; b) discuss the roles and responsibilities of the team, including reporting requirements and timelines for reporting; c) review progress against the baseline and update baselines as needed, and discuss reporting, monitoring and evaluating roles and responsibilities and finalize the MILE plan; d) discuss the annual work plan and any changes to it that may be required in light of the project strategy; e) review the annual audit; f) plan and schedule Project Board meetings and finalize the first year annual work plan. The Project Manager will prepare the reception report no later than one month after the monitoring workshop. The final reception report will be submitted by the respective UNDP Country Office to the UNDP Technical Specialist Climate Change Adaptation and Resilience Advisor and will include the latest version of the Annual Performance Report (APR).

A project implementation report will be prepared for each year of project implementation at both the global and national levels. The respective Project Managers, the BPPS and the respective UNDP Country Office, and the UNDP Technical Specialist Climate Change Adaptation and Resilience Advisor will review and approve the Annual Performance Report (APR).

The global and country Project Managers will be notified when the information included in the global Project Group document is well in advance of the APR submission deadline and will objectively report progress in the Development Objectives tab of the APR. The annual APR will be shared with the respective Project Board and other stakeholders, including the UNDP Country Office, the NDA focal Point and the UNDP Technical Specialist Climate Change Adaptation and Resilience Advisor.

The quality rating of the previous year's APR will be used to inform the preparation of the next APR. The final project APR, along with the terminal evaluation report and corresponding management response, will serve as the final project performance assessment document for the project. The APR will be submitted by the respective UNDP Country Office and shared with the global Project Board and other stakeholders for comments.


---
**Page 98**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 95

An independent midterm evaluation (IE) will be undertaken at the mid-term of the project, and the findings and responses outlined in the management response will be incorporated as recommendations for enhanced implementation during the final half of the project’s duration. The report will be based on an evaluation of both the global project as well as the country projects. The IE report will follow the standard templates and guidance available on the UNDP Evaluation Resource Center. The national level IE report will be cleaned by the UNDP Country Office and then submitted to the UNDP Evaluation Secretariat for review, comments and approval by Project Boards. These approved reports will then be collated and consolidated into a project level IE. The final IE report will be available in English.

An independent terminal evaluation (TE) will take place no later than three months prior to operational closure of the project. The terms of reference, the review process and the Final TE report will follow the standard templates and guidance available on the UNDP Evaluation Resource Center. The national level TE report will be cleaned by the UNDP Country Office with comments from Project Boards and then submitted to the UNDP Evaluation Secretariat for review, comments and approval by Project Boards which will be consolidated into the project level TE report. The TE report will be available in English. Each UNDP Country Office will retain all M&E records for this project for up to seven years after project financial closure in order to support ex-post evaluations as part of the data management and M&E continuous learning and evaluation as project management cycle and beyond. UNDP stores all records up to seven years.

A detailed monitoring plan will be developed by the UNDP Country Office and approved by the Project Board.

UNDP will perform monitoring and reporting throughout the Reporting Period, including semi-annual reporting, in accordance with the A&A and Funded Activity Agreement (FAA). UNDP has country presence and capacity to perform such functions. The UNDP Country Office will report directly to the GCF Secretariat on a quarterly basis and above the FAA, UNDP will discuss and agree these with the GCF Secretariat in the final year of the project and will prepare a post-implementation monitoring plan and budget for approval by the GCF Board as necessary.

# F. Risk Assessment and Management

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigation measures (max. 3 pages)

Please describe financial, technical, operational, governance/political, money laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF), sanctions, corruption, and other risks that could impede the achievement of project objectives from being achieved. Also describe the proposed risk mitigation measures. Insert additional rows if necessary.

For probability: High has high significant potential; Medium has moderate probability; Low has negligible probability

For impact: High has high significant impact; Medium has moderate impact; Low has negligible impact

### Selected Risk Factor 2: Gaps in governance and institutional mechanism at the national level

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | High |

**Description**

Some countries may lack or have gaps in their NAMs and NAMA and in governance and institutional mechanisms necessary for coordinated action. This could result in delays in project implementation, particularly in the areas of stakeholder engagement, regional conflict and mercury, which is a risk to implementing the proposed activities, particularly under Output 2 of the project.

The project will work towards strengthening government structures for delivery of EDS to communities. It will simultaneously engage with stakeholders at national level to ensure that they are aware of the project’s objectives and how it aligns with their own priorities and activities that are aligned with their core areas of expertise and comparative strengths. This implementation will, as far as possible, involve governmental agencies and not only civil society.

### Selected Risk Factor 2: Operational delays due to cumbersome procedures

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Cumbersome procurement and administrative procedures may result in delays in procurement of critical equipment necessary to set up the hydrometeorological observation networks and communications as well as bring in technical expertise from outside the country.

### Mitigation(s)

The UNDP Country Offices will extend support to executing agencies and responsible parties where necessary, including support for procuring and importing equipment and technical expertise during project implementation. This will reduce the risk to low.

### Selected Risk Factor 3: Risk of obsolescence of equipment due to vandalism

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | High |

**Description**

Levels of insecurity in some participating countries may compromise the design and types of investments in observation and communications equipment, and its regular operation and maintenance. This may lead to limits of the gathering of observational data and dissemination of early warnings affecting the quality of climate information and the ability of communities to respond in time.

### Mitigation(s)

The selection of equipment and its siting were done based on a comprehensive analysis of risks of vandalism and damage, keeping in mind considerations such as security, maintenance, and ease of access. The project will also ensure that all equipment is sited away from towers and installations where possible. The project will also utilize in-service IoT based community managed and operated EDS at an early stage to mitigate the risk of vandalism and damage. This will be done by ensuring that the EDS are installed during field missions ahead of the relevant season, so that it is operational at the time when climate hazards are most likely to occur.


---
**Page 101**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0: PAGE 98

## Selected Risk Factor 5 Poor inter-agency coordination and collaboration

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | High |

### Description
Poor coordination and cooperation both within and between government, multilateral and private agencies can reduce the collection and sharing of information on climate change impacts and adaptation. This may result in a lack of preparedness for extreme weather events or other natural disasters that are not responsive. Private telecommunication companies can be reluctant to adopt standards and share data with customers which may be required to respond to extreme weather events, such as floods or droughts that originate in a neighboring country, or where communications networks in one country overlap with another.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The project will strengthen institutional arrangements and mechanisms for inter-agency cooperation both at the national and regional level. This will be done by leveraging the national and regional initiatives, programs and facilities being led by the EWSA pillar leaders and other actors to ensure that they are aligned with the project’s objectives. The project will also support the development of a common coordinating role and ensure compliance and cooperation between agencies, including the private sector such as telecommunication companies.

## Selected Risk Factor 6 Poor community outreach and engagement in disaster preparedness

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | High |

### Description
The reach of government and non-government agencies to communities varies depending on their ability to support communities to prepare for and plan for disasters or to build their capacities to adapt. Community level preparedness and planning is critical for effective early and rapid response in the event of a disaster, especially when it comes to protecting vulnerable groups such as women, people with disabilities, and children with infants and children. This poses a significant risk to the project’s effectiveness in fragile and conflict affected countries as well as in other contexts.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The analysis of priorities, capacities and vulnerabilities will be used to identify the communities where agencies are most at risk. This has informed the design of community engagement strategies, leveraging local actors both from within government and non-government organizations. The project will also support the development of a common framework for disaster preparedness such as extension and health services and the departments to better equip them to support community level disaster planning and preparations. It will also support the development of a common framework for disaster preparedness at the village level, including activities and infrastructure at the village level with special attention to vulnerable and marginalized groups.

## Selected Risk Factor 7 Limited technical operational capacities to sustain the EWS value chain

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

### Description
There is a risk that the gains made by the project are not sustained due to limited resources, both financial and technical, to ensure the GDM of information and infrastructure are in place to sustain production of MWRs, specifically their link decision support systems to inform early and rapid response. This is a risk to project sustainability, particularly in poor and developing countries which have severe fiscal and technical constraints.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The project will address this through a multi-pronged approach. The development strategy will specifically target public institutions, presenting them with compelling evidence of financial returns from investments into the early warning system, demonstrating the value for money to decision makers in terms of cost savings and lives saved. This will be done by engaging with key stakeholders such as MWRs and NDMAs with the latest technologies and techniques. This will help steer these areas away from any dependence on consciousness raising campaigns or other forms of awareness creation which are often not sustainable over time, but rather to focus on long-term support for national governments in sustaining their MWRs. As part of this effort, the project also fosters collaborations with key stakeholders such as private sector and civil society organizations.

This project will be implemented under UNDP's Direct Implementation Modality (DIM) and as such the entire execution of the project will fall under the United Nations Security Council Sanctions List and the UN General Assembly Resolution. As such, the sanctions regime is enforced by the UNDP and any partner entity that fails to comply with the requirements set out in its own framework of policies and procedures with UNDP.

Partners must meet and adhere to the requirements set out in its own framework of policies and procedures. UNDP will be an integral part of its due diligence process for this project, carry out the requisite partner capacity assessments and AML/CFT screening of any proposed partner that may be required for the delivery of some of the intended activities or any vendor prepared for the purpose of delivery of goods or services to the project.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## Q.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### The UNDP Social and Environmental Screening Procedure (SESP) is provided in Annex 6 for the global project. The project will be implemented through a combination of small works activities and larger EWS projects. These two types of activities are designed to focus on cooperation, dialogue, capacity building and training. The main role of these activities is that they are not inclusive of all stakeholders, including indigenous peoples, women, people with disabilities and other marginalized groups which may otherwise be culturally and economically excluded.

### Outcomes 2 and 3 will involve extensive engagement with communities and local authorities to ensure the project’s success. This will include a focus on gender equality and equitable benefits and consideration to the needs of women, indigenous groups, people with disabilities, elderly and others who may otherwise be culturally and economically excluded. The project recognizes the differential access to resources, opportunities and services by different social groups and seeks to address these inequalities through implementation. Gender Analysis and Actions Plans (GAAP), as well as Stakeholder Engagement Plans (SEP) have been prepared, which will integrate considerations relevant for indigenous peoples. These plans will help to ensure that the project’s activities are inclusive of all stakeholders.

### Outcomes 2 also has minor EWS small works activities that are being defined. These will be small scale, such as small investments in infrastructure or services where there is no need to be sited, small footprints, or repairs or small upgrades to buildings, such as waterproofing and installing toilets. The repairs/upgrade/installation of these assets will not require any environmental assessment or mitigation measures.

### The project excludes activities from project financing which require an ESIA (Environmental and Social Impact Assessment) or which are subject to a national EIA process. These include the installation of solar panels, where there is no displacement of people or their livelihoods as a result of project implementation. No installations or works will be done in sites of cultural, historical or environmental importance and so any biodiversity impacts are expected. New installations of solar panels on existing buildings will not require an ESIA.

### In some cases, the populations include indigenous peoples. The project also regularly requires consultations with these groups. The project does not involve any small works on EWS, and as such unlikely impact was identified during project implementation, or any other impact to its identified. Indigenous Peoples Plans would need to be followed following the identification of an impact. These plans will ensure that all stakeholders are involved in ensuring that meaningful consultations are conducted with IP representatives during project implementation, to ensure their needs are considered in EWS and that they are able to receive EWS in a way that is meaningful to them. Guidance for consultations and engagement with indigenous peoples can be found in the GCF’s Indigenous Peoples Policy.

### 64.2 Financial management and procurement (max: 500 words; approximately 1 page)

#### 63.7 The financial management and procurement of this project will be guided by UNDP financial rules and regulations.
Further guidance is outlined in the financial resources management section of the UNDP Programme and Operations Policies and Procedures (PUPPs). UNDP’s Financial Rules and Regulations policies outline formal procurement standards and guidelines for each phase of the procurement process and apply to all procurements in UNDP.

#### 63.8 The project will be implemented under a Management and Implementation Agreement (MIA) by UNDP.
The MIA management and implementation approach takes full responsibility for executing a project, with no further delegation of the implementation to a government entity or other partner.

#### 63.9 All agreements entered into in connection with the project will be engaged through Responsible Party Agreements. Each responsible party who will be engaged to support the implementation of project, following the Framework for Cash Transfers to Implementing Partners (Part of the Harmonized Approach to Cash Transfers - HACT). All projects are subject to UNDP’s Financial Rules and Regulations policies, as well as other relevant rules, regulations, and policies. The HACT framework consists of four processes: [macro assessments], [micro assessments], [macro assessments], [cash transfers]. These processes include the following steps: (1) macro assessment; (2) micro assessment; (3) cash transfer; (4) monitoring and evaluation. Monitoring and evaluation includes regular reporting, performance reviews, spot checks, programmatic monitoring, scheduled audits and special audits.

#### 63.10 The project will be audited in accordance with the audit framework agreed upon by UNDP and the implementing partner as set out in the MIA established between GCF and UNDP, signed on 15 March 2013 and made effective in May 3, 2021.

#### 63.11 As GCF resources will be provided to the responsible party, less any agreed recovery amount. Under UNDP’s direct implementation approach, the implementing partner is required to submit a financial report on the progress of the implementation of agreed and approved programme activities, in accordance with UNDP standard policies.
The responsible party reports back expenditure via a financial report on a quarterly basis to UNDP.

#### 63.12 All additional requirements will be set out in the Management and Implementation Agreement (MIA) which will be further discussed and revised prior to UNDP Project Document signature) is provided in Annex III.

# Appendices

## Appendix 1: Climate hazards and associated risks (impacts, exposures and vulnerabilities) in participating countries Antigua and Barbuda

### Hurricanes
Antigua and Barbuda are on the frontline of climate change, facing a range of intensifying climatic hazards that threaten their very existence. Ranked as the 5th highest in the World Risk Index in 2021, the islands are exposed to an array of baseline hazards, including hurricanes, extreme rainfall, droughts, and rising sea levels. These risks are exacerbated by the islands' small size, low elevation, and high population density, which compound impacts of climate change.

### Droughts
Droughts pose the most significant threat in Antigua and Barbuda, with the country having faced 15 hurricanes and 14 droughts since 2000. The devastation from hurricanes Irma and Maria in 2017 ranking as stark reminders of the destructive power of these storms. These hurricanes not only caused widespread destruction of infrastructure but also led to a significant loss of life, with over 300 people killed across both islands. In Barbuda, nearly 90% of the island's infrastructure was destroyed, rendering the island temporarily uninhabitable.

The increasing intensity of these storms, fueled by climate change, is expected to lead to even more catastrophic events in the future. The frequency and severity of droughts have also increased over time. According to a study published in 2013 on 50 years of General Circulation Models (GCMs) [4], by 2030, the general building stock in Antigua and Barbuda using RCP 8.5 emissions scenarios could be reduced by up to 79% compared with baseline conditions for a billion-year event, USD 4.01 billion for a 1-in-100 year event, and USD 9.4 billion for a maximum credible event (MCE).

### Droughts
Droughts, while less dramatic than hurricanes, pose a persistent and growing threat to the islands. Historically, droughts have been more frequent in Antigua and Barbuda compared with Barbuda. The island of Antigua has experienced 10 droughts since 2000, compared with just one on Barbuda.

### Sea Level Rise
The long-term projection of Antigua and Barbuda makes it particularly susceptible to land loss due to sea-level rise, which presents a long-term existential threat to Antigua and Barbuda. Projections indicate that by 2050, Antigua could lose approximately 20 km² of land, while Barbuda could lose 15 km².

### Climate Hazards
The interplay of these climatic hazards creates a highly vulnerable environment for Antigua and Barbuda, where the combined effects of hurricanes, droughts, sea-level rise, and extreme rainfall can lead to flooding, which disrupts essential services and exacerbates water shortages caused by drought. The cascading effects of these hazards, coupled with the increasing frequency and intensity of climate-related events, pose a significant threat to communities who depend on the sea, particularly in the tourism and fishing sectors.

### Climate Change Adaptation
The interplay of these climatic hazards creates a highly vulnerable environment for Antigua and Barbuda, where the combined effects of hurricanes, droughts, sea-level rise, and extreme rainfall can lead to flooding, which disrupts essential services and exacerbates water shortages caused by drought. The cascading effects of these hazards, coupled with the increasing frequency and intensity of climate-related events, pose a significant threat to communities who depend on the sea, particularly in the tourism and fishing sectors.

### Local Stakeholders
Local stakeholders have identified several key areas that require urgent attention to strengthen the islands' resilience. These include improving early warning systems for hurricanes and other extreme weather events; enhancing infrastructure to withstand increased exposure to ocean-related hazards such as storm surges and coastal erosion; there is a critical need for accurate and locally specific projections to protect vulnerable groups. The built environment is another area of concern, as many buildings are located in high-risk areas due to the cost of building on land and the existence of outdated zoning regulations.

## Infrastructure: An improved understanding of building resilience is crucial for planning emergency responses, including evacuations and sheltering.

### The tourism sector, which employs a large segment of the population and is heavily concentrated in exposed coastal areas, also requires anticipatory actions. Anticipatory actions, notably in nations like Ecuador, who lack full knowledge of climate hazards, are particularly vulnerable, highlighting the need for better integration of the tourism sector into the islands' Multi-Hazard DRM (MHD-RM) and Anticipatory Action systems. Finally, the island of Barbuda, still recovering from the devastation caused by Hurricane Irma in 2017, is an example of how climate change can undermine disaster response capabilities.

### Ecuador

Due to a combination of political, geographic, and social factors, Ecuador is recognized as vulnerable to climate change impacts, ranking 11th out of 135 countries in the 2021 ND-GAIN Index[1] when coupled with a low readiness score, this makes Ecuador one of the most vulnerable countries. The country's vulnerability is exacerbated by its geography and social factors that undermine development and reduce the availability of natural resources, affecting the majority of the population. This vulnerability was evident during the droughts of 2017[2] and 2018[3], as well as in the floods of 2016[4], 2019[5], and 2020[6].

### Ecuador's climate is highly diverse due to its location on the equator and its varied topography, which includes the Amazon rainforest, the Andes mountains, and the Pacific coast.

Over 1800 km of coastline are exposed to coastal erosion as well as spatial and seasonal variations in rainfall across its territory. The observed average temperature increase was 1.4°C with maximum temperatures rising by 1.0°C and minimum temperatures increasing by 2.3°C between 1976-2015 compared to the period of 1961–1980, affecting all regions, leading to impacts such as: (i) approximately 40% loss of glacier cover on the Antisana, Cayambe, Cotopaxi, and Chimborazo volcanoes; (ii) severe droughts affecting 66.7% of agricultural areas and 53.7% of livestock areas (particularly in the Andes); and (iii) increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events.

### Projections foresee changes in the frequency and magnitude of both average and extreme precipitation and temperature values. By the 2090s, average temperatures in Ecuador are projected to rise significantly, potentially increasing by 5.3°C or more compared with current levels. The number of days with temperatures above 40°C is expected to decrease in the Amazon (by 2-6%) but increase along the coast and in the mountain areas under the highest emission scenarios. Projections also suggest an increase in extreme precipitation events, especially in the central mountain region, with increases of up to 50 mm for the average largest day precipitation[7].

### Floods are the most frequent hydro-meteorological disaster in Ecuador and rank second in terms of casualties, with a total of 124 deaths between 2006-2019. The number of floods has increased by approximately 35% since 2000, with an increase in both the number of events and deaths over the past 40 years, although with significant fluctuations. The rise in the intensity and frequency of these extreme climatic events has caused economic damages and losses to goods

and serious annual losses reaching between $917 million and $3.5 billion[12]. Associated non-economic issues include loss of life, health impacts, human mobility, biodiversity, cultural heritage, and indigenous knowledge. In this context, 18 indigenous peoples and 3 nationalities are at risk from the threats and impacts of climate change[13].

These impacts are expected to increase in intensity over time due to a combination of factors including: increased frequency and/or severity of extreme weather events; changes in temperature, precipitation patterns, sea surface temperatures, variations in sea surface temperatures, changes in spatial and temporal precipitation patterns, expansion of flood-prone areas, intensification of droughts, reduced water quality and quantity, the spread of disease-transmitting vectors (such as dengue fever), and increased coastal erosion. These impacts will be felt across all sectors and regions[14].

### Historical Climate Trends: According to the NextGen Projection Report[15], Fiji has warmed by 0.7 degrees relative to the pre-industrial baseline of 1860–1900, up to the period of 2011–2020. In observed and projected warming shows that Fijis’ average temperature has increased from approximately 24°C in the early 19th century (1750–1849) to around 26°C over recent decades, as shown by commonly used baselines (WMO = 1981 to 1990; AR4 = 1986 to 2005; AR5 = 1995 to 2014 and 2015 to 2020). The strongest increase has been in daily maximum temperatures (around 0.2°C per decade) and a similar increase is projected for the future[16]. In addition, there have been increases in annual mean rainfall and annual rainfall datasets for the entire region of Fiji by including surrounding oceans. Historical observations show large year-to-year variability in rainfall across the country with no significant change in annual rainfall for Fiji between the pre-industrial baseline (1850–1900) to the more recent baselines[17].

### Projected changes in climate: Projections for Fiji are presented for a range of 'global warming levels' (defined as the warming of the global average temperature since the pre-industrial era), including the 2.3°C and 2°C limits from the Paris Agreement. The range of possible change is larger under higher global warming levels compared to lower levels[18].

### Projected changes in mean annual temperatures at temperatures for 1860–1900 are:

- There is a warming by 0.7°C up to 2030.
- By 2050, the warming is 1.2°C regardless of the emissions pathway.
- By 2070, the warming is 1.8°C (RCP6) or 2.5°C (RCP8.5).
- By 2090, the warming is 3.1°C (RCP8.5) to 4.5°C (RCP8.5).

According to the World Bank report, based on the Coupled Model Intercomparison Project Phase 5 (CMIP5) models, the projected warming for Fiji between 2070 and 2099 is 1.3°C under RCP6 and 1.9°C under RCP8.5; between 2040–2069 and 2080-2099 and under the high warming scenario (RCP8.5), the projected warming is 1.3°C and 2.7°C, respectively.

### The Fifth Assessment Report (AR5) of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) projections for rainfall are as follows:

- Under RCP4.5, there will be a decrease in intensity of extreme rainfall events.
- Under RCP8.5, there will be an increase in the intensity for extreme rainfall events[19]. The NextGen Report states that future changes in rainfall are uncertain but could range from wetter to drier, depending on the South Pacific Convergence Zone (SPCZ). The SPCZ is one of the main climate drivers in the south Pacific and is influenced by the presence of El Niño and La Niña.

### This chapter provides an overview of climate change risks to Chad’s food security and livelihoods, drawing on findings from the World Risk Report 2023. The country faces significant impacts from TCs and floods, with annual asset losses exceeding $580 million, reaching over 5% of GDP. In extreme cases—like a 10-year flood event, losses could surpass $2 billion.

### Chad is one of the most vulnerable countries to climate change in Africa. The country has experienced significant droughts since 1973 and has been affected by floods and earthquakes more frequently than any other disaster over the past 37 years with TCs, floods, and earthquakes being the most frequent occurrences.

#### Chad
- **Severe Droughts (1972–2024):** In total, 68 droughts occurred since 2000 (EM-DAT, 2024). Successive droughts have affected the food security and livelihoods of households across large swathes of the country.
- **Population Affected:** The most recent drought in Chad has affected over 1.5 million people in 2018–2019, and 2.4 million people affected in 2009–2010.

#### Climate Change Impacts
- **Floods and Droughts:** These disasters have been associated with outbreaks of disease, and with undernutrition.
- **Health Issues:** The most recent drought has resulted in deaths, of which two-thirds were a result of fever or viral/bacterial disease epidemics such as cholera, meningitis, and measles.

#### IRFC’s Response
- Over the past 10 years, IRFC has launched 3 Disaster Relief Emergency Fund (DREF) operations supporting the national disaster management authority in Chad. These operations have provided humanitarian assistance to respond to disasters, targeting 5.8 million people affected by emergencies.
- Studies show a continuous increase in temperatures, as well as increased variability in rainfall in the Sahel region since the mid-1970s. The interannual variability of precipitation has become more irregular since the early 1960s and very dry years in the 1980s and exceptionally dry years in the 2000s, inter-annual variations have become more irregular since the 1990s.
- The Sahelian climate is characterized by a high degree of variability from year to year. The annual precipitation has changed with the retreat of the 300 mm and the 100 mm isohyets.

#### Climate Change Projections
- **Temperature Increase:** Projected temperature increases are higher in Chad than for global levels, with median temperature increase by climate models at 2.1°C in 2025, 2.6°C in 2030 and 3.5°C in 2050 under RCP8.5.
- **Rainfall Variability:** The number of very hot days per year is also expected to increase across the country with a high degree of certainty. The projected increase ranges from +1 additional day in 2020, to +34 additional days in 2050 and +67 additional days in 2080.
- **Projections for Precipitation:** Projections are varied for rainfall; the models project a decrease of 9% by 2020 and an increase of 10% by 2030, with very high uncertainty. The models project more variability in precipitation than the pre-1850 situation.

#### Chad’s Vulnerability
- **GNI Rank:** Chad has been ranked as one of the most vulnerable countries to climate change, and is ranked 11th/97 of the 125 countries of the global hunger index, with a level of hunger classified as severe. A combination of high poverty, frequent conflicts, and the risk of both droughts and floods have placed Chad at the lowest ranked country overall on the NO-GNI index, with a score of -37.0.

## Chad also faces severe environmental degradation, exemplified by the drying up of Lake Chad, increased desertification, declining fish stocks, the disappearance of certain animal and plant species, and soil degradation (National Adaptation Plan, 2022). These changes have the potential to exacerbate existing conflict factors or contribute to new forms of dispute.

The proposed project will support the development of a national climate change adaptation strategy that is based on the principle that at least one level essential climate services can be delivered on a sustainable basis. Improved data on vulnerability and risk, knowledge management, governance of natural hazards EAWs and disaster preparedness/master management will be improved at national and sub-national levels.

The project will support the development of a National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (NCCAS) in Chad, in the face of changing climate patterns with increased exposure to the impacts of droughts and floods, where they can no longer be managed by traditional methods. The project will also support the implementation of early warning systems, risk education training, and advice on adaptation and preparedness measures and early warning information to reduce both the loss of life and the economic impact of disasters. It will support the piloting, and integration of early action-protection mechanisms in Chad, including the development of a national climate change adaptation strategy in terms of information, preparedness and disaster management to ensure these processes are inclusive, needs-based and people-centred.

## Ethiopia

Ethiopia's climate varies significantly across its regions, classified into three primary zones based on seasonal rainfall patterns. The northern and central western areas experience a single rainy season, with peak precipitation occurring in July and August. The southern areas are characterized by two distinct rainy seasons: a short rainy period from September to November (the Ethiopian rainy season) and the longer rainy season from February to May and a summer rainy season from June to September (Paleo-rainy season). Inland, the climate is influenced by the Indian Ocean monsoon system, with an extended dry season. The temperature ranges between 10°C in the highlands of the Eastern Rift Valley and 35°C in the lowlands of the Southern Orogenic (ENSO) and sea surface temperatures (SSTs) in the Indian and Pacific Oceans. SSTs, with their gradual variations, affect long-term rainfall patterns and are crucial for improving the accuracy of regional-scale climate predictions and seasonal forecasts.

Climate change poses a significant threat to Ethiopia's development, exacerbating existing vulnerabilities. The country faces high impacts from natural hazards and increasing climatic variability, which threaten its development goals. Ethiopia is committed to a green growth path that involves adapting various policies, strategies, and plans to global climate interventions. The legal framework for climate action includes specific sectoral responsibilities and coordination mechanisms at the national level. The government has established a National Climate Change Commission (NCCC) with clear change targets and commitments integrated into public expenditure management, with efforts to mainstream climate action across sectors and levels of governance (World Bank Group, 2015).

Climate change impacts exacerbate existing issues in Ethiopia, including water, agriculture, energy, transport, and urban development. The country faces significant water challenges, with variable and unpredictable rainfall affecting millions of people. Expanding irrigation systems will be necessary to ensure food security for the growing population. Similarly, agricultural sector must become more productive and climate-resilient while, energy, transport, and urban infrastructure need to be adapted to withstand climate impacts. Additionally, diversifying the economy through mining and tourism can enhance resilience (World Bank Group, 2015).

## Ethiopia faces significant economic losses from various climate shocks.

Droughts cause substantial economic damage, with a drought event having a 5–10 year return period leading to losses exceeding US$1 billion and a 1 in 100 year drought causing losses of up to US$2.4 billion. The country is also vulnerable to floods, which have occurred every decade since the 1960s with a 25-year return period causing a 3–3.5% decrease in GDP in development banks, and a 100-year flood leading to a 7% decrease.

### Ethiopia is one of the most climate-vulnerable countries in the world.

Somalia ranked 178 out of 182 countries on the ND-GAIN index (2024), with the least capacity to respond and recover from extreme weather events. Located in the Horn of Africa, Somalia borders Kenya, Djibouti, Ethiopia, and Eritrea. The country is situated between the Middle East and a predominantly arid and semi-arid country with a hot desert climate (1981). It is susceptible to both droughts and floods, which have significant impacts on agriculture production, livestock perishes, reduced agricultural productivity, food insecurity, and ultimately famine and the loss of human life. Somalia's chronic climate-associated shocks, including cycles of droughts, floods, and locust infestation, are compounded by conflict, poverty, and weak governance structures.

### Historical and projected climate change impacts in Somalia are drought and flood, although Somalia is also affected by cyclones and coastal flooding as well as being classified as high risk.

Daily temperature maximums have increased over the last two decades. The northwestern part of the country is expected to experience extreme heat, and the southern and some parts of the northern areas are projected to experience very high heat (19). Climate change projections suggest that the duration, frequency, and intensity of droughts will increase by the end of this century. # Appendix A: Summary of climate hazards across critical sectors and proposed interventions for the participating seven countries

## Cambodia - Water resources
### Climate Hazard
- Increasingly intense precipitation events both within and upstream on the Mekong River as well as upstream on the Tonle Sap Lake.
- Increasingly dry river conditions, especially during heat events.

### Climate Impact
- The likely impacts of increasingly intense precipitation events include severe flooding, which can destroy homes, infrastructure, and livelihoods for millions of vulnerable communities. Upstream, intense rainfall can exacerbate river flooding in Cambodia, disrupting transport operations, causing disruptions downstream.
- Increased dry river conditions will lead to reduced numbers of fish available for fishing, impacting agriculture and reducing crop yields. This can further impact food security, affecting both rural and urban populations. Additionally, increased heat events are likely to increase the incidence of vector-borne diseases and stress on public health systems.

### Proposed Interventions
- Improved water management and storage, both before and after the flood season.
- Enhanced forecasting particularly in terms of timeliness of information for decision-making.
- Country-wide improvements to the speed and quality of early warning systems.
- Strengthened awareness around preparedness actions and updated guidelines on evacuation.
- Systematic coordination, legislation and monitoring across pillars.

## Fiji - Agriculture, water, health
### Climate Hazard
- Droughts, floods, and extreme weather events such as tropical cyclones (rainfall and winds), heat waves, and sea level rise.

### Climate Impact
- Loss of crops, reduced crop yields. Water scarcity. Loss of life through drought and flooding. Increased conflict over natural resources including pasture.
- Reduced livelihoods and food security due to climate change.
- Increased vulnerability and risk, improved data transfer, analysis and sharing, and knowledge management.

### Proposed Interventions
- Standardizing risk assessment methods, establishing a centralized database, and enhancing data collection and analysis capabilities for better understanding of past events and future risks.
- Effective water coverage in certain areas including expanding or upgrading observation networks with AWS and enhancing LSTV systems to improve early warning systems.
- Develop National Emergency Telecommunication Plan (NETP) to strengthen emergency communication infrastructure.
- Telecommunications Cluster (TCT) and create SOPs for data collection, analysis, and sharing of information. Implement cell broadcasting for mass messaging, and improve translation of any messages into local languages.
- Developing communication strategies based on risk perception, including the development of a national campaign to improve community-level preparedness and response.

## Chad - Meteorology and hydrology
### Climate Hazard
- Flash floods, River flooding

### Climate Impact
- Loss of life. Lost livestock. Damages to houses, damages to community and household assets (well, latrine), temporary displacement of households. Displacement. Vector borne and epidemic diseases.
- Loss of crops, reduced crop yields. Water scarcity. Loss of life through drought and flooding. Increased conflict over natural resources including pasture.

### Proposed Interventions
- Development of climate services, multilateral warning systems and disaster risk reduction (DRR) strategies to reduce the impact of climate change and disasters.

## Multi-Hazard Hydro-Meteorological Early Warning System for Pacific Building (MHWES-PB) Phase II

### USD 25 million
**September 2023 – September 2026**

The goal of this initiative is to collaborate on advancing hydro-meteorological early warning systems in the Pacific region, enabling countries to take early action against extreme climate, weather and water related hazards such as floods and flash floods, tropical cyclones, heatwaves, cold temperatures, droughts, climate change impacts, sea level rise and other hazardous disasters. 2018-2023

Project objective: To achieve no new disaster-related deaths and no new significant economic losses yet.

Effective Multi-Hazard EWS (MHEWS) in the Caribbean region will be established through the strong leadership of regional and national meteorological and hydrological services, including National Meteorological Services Offices (NMSOs) which integrate multi-hazard early warning delivery approaches in their service provision. This will lead to improved disaster risk management practices, including stronger warning and dissemination systems; more effective and enhanced response capabilities at all levels of government, communities, business and industry; and a greater focus on reaching and protecting the most vulnerable groups.

The main objective of this project is to strengthen the operational capacity of NMSOs and MHWIs in the region through improved communication infrastructure and improved governance mechanisms that are conducive for users to participate more actively in the design and delivery of risk warning services, drawing upon the CREWS initiative.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 106

## Appendix B: Selection criteria for project Outputs and Activities

The proposals should meet the requirements of the EMARK Initiative (Sendai Framework Target G). They should have a strong rationale describing the circumstances that justify using this programmatic approach. The proposals should also be closely aligned with national strategies and policies based on the national climate investment priorities.

The proposals should be designed to satisfy the following criteria:

1. They should have a clearly defined governance structure for decision-making and ensuring country ownership. This structure should be compatible with the global project structure and organization to ensure smooth coordination and collaboration at the global level and with other participating countries throughout the duration of the project.
2. The outputs of the proposal must be closely aligned with the activities listed under Output 2 of the global funding proposal. This will directly influence the design of the log-frame of the proposal which must be compatible with the logical framework of the global funding proposal.
3. The proposal design process must include comprehensive engagement with government agencies and national, regional as well as global focal points of the four priority areas. This should ensure that there is a clear understanding by all stakeholders on what to expect in terms of weight on the proposed activities. This consultative process must ensure the respective governments retain full ownership of the design process.
4. The support of multilateral agencies and non-governmental agencies should be ensured for countries with challenges emanating from fragility and conflict. Proposals designed for FCAS states should demonstrate appropriate governance arrangements and mechanisms to implement proposed activities.
5. The proposal must meet the reporting requirements for both physical and financial reporting.
6. The proposals need to be fully compliant with the monitoring, evaluation and learning system and knowledge management framework provided in the global funding proposal.
7. The projects should demonstrate sustained impact of investments on a continued basis and should specifically provide for the development of an Operations and Maintenance plan in the initial period of implementation.
8. The proposal should adhere to the legal arrangements and frameworks of the global project and all contractual structures should assure continued operations of the project.
9. The proposal should demonstrate ownership of the respective country government to take onboard and absorb the investments proposed. It should also demonstrate financial and economic sustainability and cost efficiency.
10. The proposal should be compliant with environmental and social safeguards and ensure environmental and social risks are within the GCF risk category "C" or low risk.

**Page 140**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 157

The project must be designed in accordance with the GCF template for funding proposals including all relevant annexes.

### Criteria of Eligibility for Country Proposals are based on the following principles. These are closely aligned with the GCF investment criteria.

#### Impact potential: Following criteria will be used to determine the project's impact potential
- The project must clearly set out the specific risks and vulnerabilities, under current and future conditions of climate change, that the project aims to address.
  - Articulating a clear causal narrative between specific activities and their objective of reducing vulnerability or climate risk. Projects that include arguments that are well evidenced and demonstrate how they will contribute to adaptation and/or resilience will be promoted.
- The project must clearly describe the adaptation benefit that is expected to be generated by the implementation of the project activities.

#### Project alignment with GCF result areas
- Demonstrating the potential for catalyzing impact beyond a one-off private investment by:
  - Clearly describing how the project outcome will be sustainable in the long run through securing additional public and/or private financing.
  - Building the capacity of national institutions to implement similar activities without GCF funding.

#### Demonstrating the potential of being replicated elsewhere in the same country or other countries in a region
- Promoting innovation such as, through the development and/or adoption of new technology or approaches which can contribute to more accurate forecasts, improved dissemination of early warning information on alert systems.
  - Improving sharing of knowledge between national actors with other countries and contributing to the global project knowledge exchange.

#### Sustainable development potential: The proposals should demonstrate sustainable development potential by identifying at least one positive co-benefit from the following with associated indicators
- Economic co-benefits: explicitly aim for poverty reduction.
- Social co-benefits: human health and social development through improved food and nutrition security, reduced loss of life resulting from the impact of extreme weather events.
- Environmental co-benefits: improved ecosystem services and biodiversity.

#### Gender: The project must meet the gender criterion independently of the environmental, social and economic benefits. The project must fall under the UN Gender Equality Marker (GE M) 1.


---
**Page 141**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 158

5. The Recipient must meet the need of the recipient by:
   a. Overcoming barriers related to technical, institutional and/or financial barriers to meeting the objectives of the four pillars of Multi-Hazard Early Warning Systems as described in the Early Warning for All Executive Action Plan.
6. The project must strengthen the capacity of National Meteorological and Hydrological Services and National Disaster Management Agencies Institutions to deliver effective MHEWS, enhance coordination and improve governance.

7. Country Ownership: The project must demonstrate alignment with national priorities climate priorities as reflected in nationally determined contributions (NDCs), national adaptation plans (NAPs) and/or national disaster risk reduction strategies. It must also demonstrate stakeholder engagement in developing the project, including government agencies, civil society organizations, private sector entities and civil society organizations and climate change focal points or relevant climate related hazards. Finally, the project must demonstrate engagement with national designated authorities in developing the project.

8. Efficiency and Effectiveness: To demonstrate efficiency and effectiveness the project should demonstrate that grants from GCF are being used to offset the incremental costs of implementing MHEWS. The project should also demonstrate how it is using blended financing (grants from GCF, additional grants and/or loans) to increase the volume of financing available for strengthening MHEWS, disaster response and adaptation and better tailor concessional to achieve transformative investments which would otherwise not proceed.

In addition to these criteria, the project must have a framework to manage environmental and social risks and impacts of the activities through the design of and implementation of Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF). The ESMF must adhere to compliance benchmarks UNDP as its A2, as per the terms set forth in the AMA.

Secretariat's assessment of FP258

Proposal name: Multi-country Project Advancing Early Warnings for All (EW4All)

Accredited entity: United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

Country(ies): Antigua and Barbuda, Cambodia, Chad, Ecuador, Ethiopia, Fiji, Somalia

Project/programme size: Medium

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The programme, Multi-country Project Advancing Early Warnings for All (EW4All), presents a unique opportunity to scale up and accelerate at scale, the adaptive capacity of communities in the target countries through improved production and dissemination of climate information and multi-hazard early warning systems (MHEWS) among the most vulnerable populations. The proposal contributes towards ensuring that vulnerable communities are better prepared for disasters in a timely fashion and develop the capacity to undertake anticipatory action to mitigate, prepare for, respond to, and recover from hazards and impacts that they face. | As three of the seven target countries, namely Chad, Ethiopia and Somalia are faced with ongoing conflict and/or fragile state status, there are additional risks for a timely and synchronized implementation of the proposed activities. Furthermore, measures have been built into the programme's design, including disaggregated disbursement per country, to ensure the sustainability and provision of a global platform to enable the participating countries to share experiences and learn from each other. |


---
**Page 151**
---

The Multi-country Project Advancing Early Warnings for All focuses specifically on addressing barriers and challenges faced by National Disaster Management Agencies and National Meteorological and Hydrological Services in the least developed countries and small island developing States, including those in fragile conflict-affected states, from operating.

In addition, the programme's implementation through national disaster risk reduction institutions is commendable, because it will facilitate the improvement of the existing capacities, structures, and institutional mechanisms across the global early warning support system and mechanism, while continuing to build the capacity of participating countries at all levels.

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the terms listed on pages 10-12 of the document. The project is an accredited entity (AE), and, if considered appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in annex II to document GCF/BA/02.


## Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

### Programme background

The Multi-country Project Advancing Early Warnings for All is an intervention aimed at promoting climate resilience among vulnerable communities in Antigua and Barbuda, Cambodia, Chad, Ecuador, Ethiopia, Fiji, and Somalia. It helps to institute relevant policies and governance frameworks, establish early warning systems (EWS) infrastructure and setting up systems for its long-term operation, and supporting local capacities through training and capacity building.

The project is designed to strengthen the resilience of vulnerable communities in the participating countries through the development of climate-resilient EWSs, including weather-based forecasts, strengthening of communication and last-mile dissemination channels, and building capacity at all levels. The project aims to reach 80% of the most vulnerable communities with a view to reaching 80 per cent of the most vulnerable communities in the targeted seven countries.

The project's target seven countries face growing disaster risk resulting from increasing prevalence and severity of different types of hazardous events, which pose adverse impacts for both the environment and human development. These include increased vulnerability to climate change and livelihood insecurity, and infrastructure and economic losses. The countries are faced with extremely high levels of vulnerability to climate change, with limited readiness to access knowledge-based information on early warning systems, and a lack of capacity to adapt and climate-proof their social and economic infrastructure.


---
**Page 152**
---

1. The project will overcome these challenges by accelerating the development of EWS4All projects in participating countries, enhancing the enabling conditions for the implementation of EWS4All investment activities in the seven countries based on the Climate Information and Early Warning System (CIES) Investment Framework, and rolling out EWS4All investment activities that meet each participating country's needs, and rolling out EWS4All investment activities in the seven countries based on the Climate Information and Early Warning System (CIES) Investment Framework. It will support the countries to establish and sustain EWS infrastructure, set up systems for early warning dissemination, and build human resources and institutional capacities in the generation and dissemination of early-warning products, and help institute relevant policies and legislation.
2. The project is requesting USD 103.25 million of GCF financing. The total cost of the project, including co-financing, is USD 114.59 million, with co-financing primarily in the form of cash and in-kind contributions from the seven countries, the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) leads, and the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP). The programme will also receive recent disbursements of USD 60.48 million from the Systematic Observations Financing Facility (SOFF).
3. In terms of environmental and social safeguards (ESS), this proposal has been categorized as ESS category 1 (low risk) and ESS category C. The review by the Secretariat confirms the E&S risk category assigned by the AE.
4. **2.2 Component-by-component analysis**
   - **Component 1: Enhancing enabling conditions globally and regionally to promote country-level actions towards the EWS4All goals (total cost USD 13.50 million; GCF cost: USD 6.68 million)**
     - This component will accelerate and scale up national action to global and regional initiatives, including strengthening governance and coordination mechanisms at the global level and respective capabilities. This component will focus on enhancing enabling conditions for the countries to undertake country-level EWS4All investments in a way that will be sustained beyond the project implementation period.
   - **Component 2: Accelerating actions in select sectors towards EWS4All goals (total cost USD 92.50 million; GCF cost: USD 87.63 million)**
     - This component will address gaps in policy, inter-agency coordination and strategies, building technical and scientific capacities; improving capacity for observation; and promoting the development of early warning systems. The project will support the seven countries to develop an executive action plan and will be implemented through the country's national governments with support from UNDP, the World Meteorological Organization (WMO), the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), International Federation of Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies) and their in-country national societies. The project will also strengthen governance and coordination mechanisms; bolster disaster risk knowledge and observation systems; build technical and scientific networks and capacities; and fortify warning dissemination, preparedness and response.
   - **Project management and monitoring and evaluation (M&E) costs (total cost USD 8.58 million; GCF cost: USD 4.39 million)**


---
**Page 153**
---

project implementation and monitoring and evaluation processes will be effectively coordinated.

Gender and environmental and social management framework aspects

A costed environmental and social management framework (ESMF) was developed for the project, outlining specific activities that will sufficiently ensure that ESS safeguards are put in place at all stages of the project. A gender and environment and social management framework has also been developed, laying out costed plans of action that will be undertaken in the seven project countries to ensure that gender equality, equity and safeguards will be upheld throughout the lifespan of the project.

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

Currently, one-third of the world's population, communities in Africa, least developed countries (LDCs), and developing states being particularly affected, lacks comprehensive coverage by early warning systems. At the launch of the EMWF Initiative in March 2022, United Nations Secretary-General António Guterres called on all governments, international organizations, and civil society to support this crucial effort to ensure that every person on Earth is protected by early warning systems by 2030.

The project addresses a robust climate change context and demonstrates how the ambitious EMWF targets can be achieved through an integrated and comprehensive global project that supports the establishment of strengthened and enhanced EWS in the target countries, particularly those that are most vulnerable to the impacts faced due to significant climate variability and change, affecting livelihoods, food security, and economic stability. Since the 1970s, these countries have experienced increased frequency and intensity of droughts, flooding, and environmental degradation. The countries face a wide range of climate risks and hazards:

- **(a)** Hurricanes, extreme rainfall, droughts, and rising sea levels in Antigua and Barbuda.
- **(b)** Floods, extreme heat and longer dry spells in Chad.
- **(c)** Increased intensity and frequency of extreme and slow-onset climatic events such as melting glaciers, increased evaporation rates, surface temperature increase, increased floods and intensified droughts in Ecuador.
- **(d)** More frequent intense temperatures and droughts, increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, including heatwaves and increased floods, flash floods and landslides, sea level rise, and increased storm surges and coastal erosion in Fiji.
- **(e)** More erratic rainfall, prolonged dry spells and worsening drought conditions, wild land and forest fires, desertification, and the spread of malaria and other vector-borne diseases in Ethiopia.
- **(f)** Exacerbated droughts and drought risks resulting from more intense rains during a delayed rainy season and longer dry seasons, unexpected dry periods during the rainy season, and untimely rain in Cambodia; and
- **(g)** Average annual rainfall of 100 mm or less in coastal flooding in Somalia.

The project seeks to support the countries to address these climate challenges by harnessing the crucial role that climate information and early warning play in enabling individuals, communities, and organizations to take appropriate measures to minimize the


---
**Page 154**
---

potential impact of the disasters that the climate hazards would create. The project aims to achieve this by supporting concrete investments in infrastructure and systems needed to observe weather events, forecast climate hazards and provide vulnerable communities and public and private sector decision makers with advance notice of the potential hazards in a way that would allow them to take action to reduce their exposure to the hazards before they occur, and thereby save lives and livelihoods and reduce damage and losses.

The project will support adaptation and resilience-building across seven countries (at least 80 per cent of the population) across the seven countries by providing additional investments in early warning infrastructure and systems (component 1), and for strengthening of climate information services (component 2). These interventions are all vital in ensuring that the countries attain the third EAWII target, which is to provide at least 50 per cent of their populations with sustainable access to supporting communities and businesses to undertake anticipatory action in response to weather-related hazards towards poor to their lives, livelihoods and social and economic systems and infrastructure.

The project will specifically support adaptation and resilience-building across seven core areas of climate-sensitive sectors (agriculture, forestry, water, health, energy efficiency, renewable energies, and climate change information services) in the seven countries (Bolivia, Ethiopia, Ghana, India, Nepal, Papua New Guinea, and Somalia). The grant finance will cover additional costs of public early warning investments.

Within the context of majority of these countries, where a significant percentage of the population is dependent on climate-sensitive sectors for their livelihoods, and where impacts from climate-sensitive sectors such as rain-fed agriculture, access to climate information and early warnings remain a major challenge.

The project will ensure that the GCF programme aims to ensure that it will ultimately benefit 100 per cent of the target countries' population, as everyone can use climate information services. The project will also aim to provide communities with resilience against climate variability and change over a range of decision timelines. The proposal is designed to align with this target.

The benefits and impact potential of this intervention is therefore assessed to be very significant.

## 3.2 Paradigm shift potential

Scale: High

The updated Strategic Plan for the GCF 2014–2017 (GCF 2) notes that GCF can substantially contribute to the EAWII initiative. This is evidenced by strong support from the Board with a clear commitment to work towards its completion. Realizing this target requires the GCF to operationalize the EAWII principles, with an aim prioritizing investments for the most vulnerable, impacting the optimization of investments, coordinating various investments sources and ensuring that they are aligned with the GCF’s strategic objectives.

GCF has a range of tools and approaches that could be harnessed to deliver the USP 2 and EAWII targets. These include the projects and programmematic approaches and ongoing work under the Global Climate Change Information System (GCCIS) initiative, Climate Risk Early Warning Systems Initiative (CREWS)—simplified approval process scaling up framework and fast tracking of investments in early warning systems, and collaboration with partners such as World Meteorological Organization, UNDRR, International Telecommunication Union and International Federation of Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies, International


---
**Page 155**
---

Telecommunication Union) and a broad range of public- and private-sector partners. The ambitious project requires coordinated approval by all the pillar leads and partners. A meeting with all pillars led by SOFF and CREFS was held in Bangkok, 3–5 May 2023 to develop a conceptual framework (EW4AI Global support programme) for the timely delivery of the EW4AI Facility.

The key issues identified to be addressed to deliver the targets include:

- The need to develop a financing plan to accompany the EW4AI implementation strategy. What is the role of GCF in the financing plan? How does it crowd in other investors?
- The need to support pipeline generation, and proposal maturation, in particular via direct access entities.
- The need to provide funding for coordination, fit-for-purpose prioritization, monitoring, lessons learned, knowledge management, and regular meetings; and
- The need to build capacity, early warning, and rapid response capabilities.

The conceptual framework arising from this exercise has led to the development of a GCF project proposal as an "incubator" with a project preparation grant to create the financing plan and pipeline generation.

### Pipeline generation - Prioritizing support for pipeline development particularly by direct access entities, leveraging from frameworks like CREWS and SOFF.

### Coordination - The EW4AI Facility will be established that supports a coordinated approach nationally, regionally and globally. Financial regular meetings to discuss opportunities, pipeline development, and share good practices and lessons learned. A coordination framework will be developed with key milestones, templates, and indicators; and

### Pilot projects - Support for pilot projects to serve as inspiration of what can be achieved.

The EW4AI Facility will be established that supports the preparation of four to five projects (including multi-country programmes) for GCF Board approval annually. This should result in a pipeline of 20–30 projects per year, with a mix of small-scale demonstration projects, local partner-owned and implemented, co-invested by donors. Other projects will be developed through the Facility’s support to national governments. The EW4AI Facility aims at covering 60 countries by 2027. This proposal is the first attempt to operationalize the EW4AI conceptual framework at scale through a programmatic approach.

The project promotes an approach that fosters the development of an integrated and comprehensive MDR risk value chain, which integrates long-term financing strategies to enable the sustainable management of disaster risks in vulnerable communities. The project uses lessons learned from previous work, such as the Afghanistan Community Resilience Project (2015–2019), to develop a framework for integrating MDR systems and processes in a way that has the potential for scaling up or replication across the globe, particularly in climate-sensitive regions.

The project demonstrates a paradigm-shift potential across all the components of the project and ensures the strategic focus is maintained over the long term.


---
**Page 156**
---

3.3 Sustainable development potential

**Scale: High**

According to UNDRR, advances in early warning systems and preparedness have saved many lives and prevented or mitigated economic losses by triggering timely and well-prepared actions. These systems are among the most proven and cost-effective tools for disaster risk reduction (DRR). 3.4 Needs of the recipient

**Scale: High**

The project targets some of the world’s most vulnerable countries, where water, food and livelihood insecurity conditions arise from the various hazards posed by extreme climate variability and change, including droughts, floods, heatwaves, and other weather-related events.

The target communities are facing severe challenges from climate change threatening their current livelihood strategies, and yet, they lack reliable access to usable climate information. The project will provide these countries with the necessary tools to safeguard their livelihoods and natural capital from the impacts of the climate hazards that affect their livelihoods. The project will also help these countries to develop adaptation strategies so that developing countries are not able to withstand the pressure from climate hazards. Through the development of a national early warning system, the project will provide communities with advance information to develop strategies for safeguarding and transforming their livelihood options, based on the latest climate information. This will enable them to better manage climate hazards, thereby building the resilience and adaptive capacity of the vulnerable communities.

The target countries are heavily indebted and do not have sufficient resources to invest in the sectoral infrastructure that is required to build resilient infrastructure and secure livelihoods, water and food insecurity. The countries and beneficiaries are currently struggling with access to usable climate information and early warning systems which would enable them to secure their well-being, livelihood and food, water and health systems. Without access to climate information and early warning, the local communities and economies in the target countries will not be able to adapt effectively to the impacts of climate change and other weather-related events.

The project will fill an important gap through working with key global, national and local partners to develop a national early warning system that provides timely solutions for enhanced access and use of climate information and early warnings for building resilient infrastructure and health, water, food and livelihood systems.


3.5 Country ownership

**Scale: High**

Country ownership is well embedded in the project, which is fully aligned with global climate change adaptation development aspirations, strategies and policies of all seven target countries. The project will promote the development of capacity among local governmental and non-governmental actors in the target countries to disseminate and use climate information and early warning systems for building resilient infrastructure and health, water, food and livelihood systems.

The project also places an important focus on ensuring better synergy with ongoing regional initiatives and national adaptation strategies and programmes. The project will support grass roots, women and youth.


All of the AID’s USP which, along with the four early warning pillar leads that are participating in the project implementing partners, has a strong presence in all the target countries, and a wealth of experience working with stakeholders and dealing with the development challenges faced by these countries. The AID also brings to this initiative as well as experience and partnerships that will facilitate meaningful engagement and involvement of all stakeholders at national level in the successful implementation and scaling up of the programme activities and outputs.

**Page 158**

### Efficiency and effectiveness

#### Scale: Medium to high

1. The overall EWSA action plan proposes an investment of US$ 3.1 billion over five years, which is just USD 620 million per year. This funding will enhance disaster risk knowledge and management, improve observation and forecasting, and strengthen the dissemination of climate information and early warning systems.
2. To achieve these goals, this proposal plans to leverage existing pooled funding mechanisms like CREDI and SOF as well as global climate funds, including development banks.

3. This programme will directly support 10 countries in building their ability to secure, diversify, and strengthen their own adaptive capacity and livelihood systems.
4. It will target 26.8 million direct beneficiaries and 5.2 million indirect beneficiaries by the end of its implementation period.
5. The funding amount is well justified to provide the support needed to safeguard lives, livelihoods, and assets in the participating countries as well as lessons for transforming climate governance in the participating countries and around the globe in general.
6. This programme will also enable access to climate information and early warning systems; it aims to achieve this through the structuring and deployment of a global support mechanism for DWD and of a value-chain approach to ensure that the right actors are involved at each stage, from the development of reliable CIEWS and thereby foster an innovative approach to helping countries to access long-term financial resources.
7. The project also has an innovative exit strategy that will enable scaling up its approach across the globe and will build access and partnership between global actors and governments, communities, and civil society organizations in order to ensure the adoption of locally appropriate and developed solutions based on reliable and science-based climate information.

The upgrades will be designed with a view to ensuring that they are resilient to climate change.
