### Gender policy

The All has provided a gender analysis and action plan and therefore complies with the GCF Gender Policy. The gender assessment identifies barriers to equitable participation and benefit sharing for women in decision-making processes related to project design, implementation, and communication technologies, particularly in rural and marginalized areas, due to lower digital literacy, socioeconomic vulnerability, and limited access to information. Women often face greater responsibilities for unpaid care work reducing opportunities for active participation. Additionally, systemic issues such as the lack of disaggregated gender data, inadequate collection and analysis of gender-sensitive data, and minimal integration of gender-sensitive policies in disaster risk management exacerbate these challenges. Furthermore, women, particularly those with disabilities, indigenous women, and those in rural areas, face compounded challenges in accessing resources, safe evacuation facilities, and targeted interventions during emergencies.


---
**Page 161**
---

10. The gender action plan proposes a comprehensive set of actions to address these challenges and strengthen gender considerations across MDPIW. The gender action plan defines activities, indicators, and targets for women's participation and benefit with corresponding timeframes, budget and gender expertise. Key actions include increasing women’s representation in disaster risk governance structures, strengthening data collection and analysis, and promoting community-based training programmes tailored to women’s needs. It also includes establishing a gender focal person at the national level, prioritizing formats that reach women and marginalized groups effectively. The plan emphasizes the need for a multi-sectoral approach to ensure effective preparedness and response. Furthermore, it underscores the need for gender-sensitive infrastructure development and disaster risk reduction measures addressing women's concerns. Institutional capacity will be strengthened to ensure gender equity in disaster risk governance, with mechanisms to monitor progress through gender-focused indicators. Each country will have to report on its progress towards these goals to the global PMU which will in turn consolidate inputs for annual reporting to the Fund.

### 4.3 Risks

#### 4.3.1 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

The programme aims to implement EWS in seven countries, including small island developing states, least developed countries and fragile and conflict-affected states. The GCF is requested to provide USD 20 million for the project implementation phase, with a share of contributing co-financing of USD 134 million in the form of grants and in-kind contributions.

#### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capable to execute the current programme (low-medium risk)

UNDP will serve as both the EEU and executing entity (EE) for the project. The EE has an extensive portfolio covering global experience in implementing climate adaptation and climate information projects. This funding proposal represents the first multi-country project submitted by UNDP under the GCF, which is expected to be included on the pipeline.

#### 4.3.3 Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

Political risks and coordination challenges: The programme spans multiple countries with diverse political and social dynamics, different coordination capacities and governance structures. These factors may lead to delays in project implementation and other related challenges and expects to address them by focusing on strengthening governance structures in collaboration with national and local entities, non-governmental organizations and other multilateral agencies. UNDP will ensure that the programme is implemented according to the overall scope of the project implementation ensuring progress continues even if delays occur in a single country.

#### 4.3.4 O&M plan: The government commitment for O&M beyond the project implementation is critical for the project's success. However, the O&M expenses for each participating country are grouped under one budget line item and will be covered by the GCF. This is an area of risk that remains unresolved at the time of drafting this assessment.

The economic financial analysis was not available at the time of drafting the assessment. For other countries, the economic financial analysis expected to be submitted by the end of January 2025.

#### 4.3.5 Compliance risks (low risk)

The proposed multi-country Programme will be implemented in Antigua and Barbuda, Cabo Verde, Comoros, Djibouti, Eritrea, Ethiopia, Kenya, Madagascar, Malawi, Mauritius, Mozambique, Namibia, Rwanda, Seychelles, Somalia, Tanzania, Uganda, Zambia, and Zimbabwe. The GCF is subject to restrictions on privileges and immunities with one of these seven host countries, namely Antigua and Barbuda, Cabo Verde, Comoros, Djibouti, Eritrea, Ethiopia, and Kenya. In the case of Djibouti, Somalia, GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst other things, GCF may be subject to restrictions on its ability to undertake certain activities, and/or its role need to be further assessed. Moreover, the ability of GCF to undertake redress activities and/or investigations into grievances can be hindered due to the absence of privileges and immunities for relevant GCF personnel.

76\. Therefore, it is recommended that the Board considers whether disbursements of GCF provided under this Programme should be subject to additional conditions in order to mitigate against litigation and expropriation in the countries, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities for GCF and its personnel.

77\. With regard to the matters set out in this section, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:

(b) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP258

Proposal name: Multi-country Project Advancing Early Warnings for All (EW4All)

Accredited entity: United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

Country/ies: Antigua and Barbuda, Cambodia, Chad, Ecuador, Ethiopia, Fiji, Somalia

Project/programme size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Overview

#### 1.1 Overview
1. This is the first submission of the proposal for the Multi-country Project Advancing Early Warnings for All (EW4All) programme under the GCF standard proposed approval process.
2. The project was announced by the United Nations Secretary General in March 2022 and articulated in the Early Warning System (EWS) Roadmap for Africa, Asia-Pacific, and Latin America and the Caribbean.
3. The multi-country proposal includes a global component as well as prioritized support to seven participating countries across four geographies: Antigua and Barbuda, Cambodia, Chad, Ecuador, Ethiopia, Fiji, and Somalia.

The objective of the programme is to accelerate the design and implementation of EW4All in the seven participating countries by strengthening their capacity within the early warning systems value chain. Additionally, the project will directly support the financial and technical development of seven participating countries to enhance the production of actionable multi-hazard early warnings for all vulnerable communities.

The project will be disseminated to effectively reach the most vulnerable communities in each of the seven countries through a combination of direct funding and capacity building. The dissemination model that this will work in practice, one country funding proposal for Ecuador has been submitted alongside this funding proposal. This is in line with the Inter-Agency Task Force (IATF) and the Inter-Agency Partnership Facility (IPAF) that the remaining six countries proposals are expected to be available by the end of 2023.

The funding proposal responds to several challenges related to MWHV: (i) insufficient financial resources and limited capacities to access financing at country and community levels; (ii) inadequate institutional capacity for early warning system design, implementation, and governance for preparedness and response; (iii) limited technical, scientific and institutional capacities in the seven countries to produce actionable multi-hazard early warnings; (iv) limited sharing of information to climate-vulnerable communities; (v) insufficient national- and local-level disaster preparedness and anticipatory action planning; and (vi) limited opportunities for women and other marginalized groups to engage with and benefit from early warning systems.

The assessment was conducted by the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) in accordance with the terms of reference received by the ITAP on 12 December 2024 and is informed by a set of written questions and answers further discussed during an online meeting held between the ITAP and the Secretariat on 3 January 2025.

<sup>1</sup> Available at <https://digital.library.oecd.org/record/4891086/v/pdf>


---
**Page 166**
---

The Multi-country Project Advancing EWAII is a medium-sized public-sector adaptation investment in environmental and social safeguard risk category C. The total budget of USD 175.4 million comprises a request for GCF grant funds of USD 107.49 million, with expected co-contributions of USD 68 million from the Systematic Observations Financing Facility (SOFF) and the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP). The implementation period is 5 years with an overall benefit stream to 2030.

The project will be delivered through two outputs:

(a) Output 1: Enhancing enabling conditions, both globally and regionally, to promote environmental and social safeguards in EWAII investments.

(b) Output 2: Accelerating actions in the seven participating countries towards EWAII goals, through context-specific activities that address the four key pillars of MHEWS namely (i) early warning systems; (ii) capacity building for disaster risk management, monitoring, analysis and forecasting; (iii) warning dissemination and communication; and (iv) emergency preparedness.

The UNDP Bureau for Policy Programme Support is in the accredited entity (AE). UNDP has been accredited to GCF since March 2015, with a cumulative GCF funding of approximately USD 1.2 billion.

There are two distinct governance and management structures for the global- and national-level components respectively:

(a) Global level: The project will be led by UNDP, supported by four pillar leads - World Meteorological Organization (WMO), United Nations Office for Disaster Risk Reduction, International Telecommunication Union, and International Federation of Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies. UNDP will also play the role of senior partner.

(b) For output 2, UNDP country offices will implement the project as executing entities under a Direct Implementation Modality, in collaboration with the relevant ministries housing the national disaster management authorities or the national meteorological and hydrological services. UNDP will work closely with other key partners in each country; UNDP will also play the role of senior partner.

### 1.2 Impact potential

Scale: Medium

Of the 30 focal countries identified under the global EWAII Initiative, seven countries from across four geographies were selected for participation in this project. All seven countries are highly vulnerable to climate change impacts and have experienced significant losses (leading to heatwaves, damages to agriculture and human health), increases in intensity of rainfall, and decreases in water availability (leading to droughts and/or hydrometeorological droughts) and in some cases decreases in rainfall (leading to agricultural and hydrological droughts), and increased disaster risks from hazardous climate events.

Impact: The project will be implemented in seven countries. UNDP has clarified that country selection was based on exposure to climate variability and change, including from multiple

The proposal outlines how the impacts of climate and climate-related hazards can wreak havoc on the lives and livelihoods of millions of people; impact human health, food and water security, and infrastructure; and undermine development progress and resilience to communities. Within communities, the poor and vulnerable, including women, children, the elderly, and those with disabilities, often lack the means to anticipate and adapt to climate-related disasters.

The funding proposal clearly sets out the rationale for how well-functioning early warning systems can help reduce the impacts of climate hazards and associated disaster hazards.* The Multi-country Project Advancing EWF4ALL programme therefore proposes to strengthen the capacity of national meteorological services, including NMSs, and non-governmental users across sectors, as a foundation for risk-informed development processes.

The project presents two outcomes areas: sustained support for global coordination and cooperation on early warning systems (outcome 1), which is essential if EWF4ALL is to be inclusive and build community resilience to climate hazards (outcome 2). The two associated outputs are: output 1, strengthening the capacity of NMSs in applying global resources and collaboration; and by enhancing the enabling environment, output 1 could help to accelerate efforts towards universal coverage of early warning services by 2027. By helping to improve the effectiveness and reach of EWF4ALL and MHEW, it is seven participating

## Direct beneficiaries are considered to include vulnerable populations that are direct recipients of project activities notably early warning dissemination systems, capacity-building in the use of these systems, and other related activities. Indirect beneficiaries are those whose wider populations located in areas covered by the project and likely to receive measurable benefits from the enhanced MHEWS through improved risk information, forecasting and dissemination.

In the view of the IPF, the scope of both outputs is ambitious, and the extent of impact to be realised will depend on the ability of multiple stakeholders at global, regional, national and local levels to deliver a coherent project.


---
**Page 169**
---

scale to achieve the intended reach to project beneficiaries and to meet overall intended project targets.

The feasibility study for Ecuador prioritizes six focal areas and sectors, and the ITAP recommends that the approach of prioritizing sectors vulnerable to climate impacts is also applied in other countries. The ITAP also recommends a sector-based approach to data collection that could allow for more accurate historical baselines on losses and damages, enabling robust analysis of the impact of climate change on these sectors. This sector-based approach to data collection could also serve as a model for replication in others; whereas a broader national-based approach could enable broader coverage of early warning information and coordinated action across impacts.

Overall, the ITAP assesses the impact potential as medium.


## 1.3 Paradigm shift potential

### Scale: Medium

The framing of the theory of change for the overall funding proposal is aligned with the EWAII Initiative. The two outputs are focused on tackling the barriers to the effective generation, dissemination, communication and use of early warnings for climate change adaptation in developing countries.

#### (a) At the global level, output 1 seeks to achieve a paradigm shift by establishing a long-term financing strategy for an end-to-end MHIWS, strengthening the global monitoring and evaluation framework across all four pillars, segmenting knowledge management, aligning technical assistance with country needs, and providing enhanced capacity building and technical assistance to countries and for early warning actions. This could help to build momentum towards achieving the EWAII target.

#### (b) By extending technical and operational support to the seven countries, output 2 will address barriers related to policy coherence and coordination, technical and scientific capacity building, lack of access to information and data, limited institutional capacity in the least developed countries; lack of forecast-based planning and observation at national level; and lack of preparedness and response. The activities under output are closely aligned to the four pillars of the EWAII Initiative. They will also address the specific needs and contexts of countries as presented in their respective feasibility studies that are yet to be finalized.

The project seeks to create an enabling environment for early warning systems by improving inter-agency coordination at national and local levels; bolster risk knowledge; and strengthen systems to disseminate and communicate early warnings and preparedness information. The use of mobile phones, the internet, and other digital technologies can also support a paradigm shift in countries by employing a more efficient and effective means of communication and dissemination through mobile networks, and ensuring that this information reaches communities in a timely manner.

The potential for creating an enabling environment is high as the project could achieve synergies across global partners supporting the EWAII target. The project's intent to strengthen national capacity building and institutional arrangements will help to achieve the EWAII target in sound. The funding proposal includes a useful appendix that illustrates synergies with the CERF and other initiatives.

The planned development of a longer-term financing strategy will be essential to help unlock much needed finance for sustaining the EWAII architecture to 2027 and beyond. This is a critical element of the project's sustainability and the ITAP recommends that this activity is


---
**Page 170**
---

undertake in consultation with a wide range of partners, including government, non-governmental organizations, the private sector, and civil partners.

### The potential for knowledge-sharing and learning is high, and the funding proposal broadly indicates how the project will develop, disseminate and support the application of detailed information on EWAII best practices and lessons learned to improve national and regional DNS capabilities. There is also provision for knowledge exchange between countries, including through a country expert group, an international advisory board, a global technical advisory expert group, and building shared knowledge and awareness through international events and workshops, although the details have yet to be fully developed.

#### The programme has the potential to bring about a paradigm shift in each of the seven countries. In the view of the ITAP, the project also has the potential to contribute significantly towards achieving the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015-2030 by substantially increasing the availability and/or access to multi-hazard early warning systems and disaster risk information and assessment to the

### Sustainable development potential: Scale: Medium to high

#### The funding proposal is designed to support strategic frameworks and actions at national level, including those that address the impacts of climate change and hazards. In particular, the project will contribute specifically to target G of the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015-2030 by "substantially increasing the availability and/or access to multi-hazard early warning systems and disaster risk information and assessment to


---

people by 2030**, are the four priorities of the “Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction,” and the priorities identified in Article 16 of the Paris Agreement.

The programme could also contribute to the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), specifically SDG 13 (Climate action) and SDG 1 (No poverty). SDG 2 (Zero hunger), SDG 9 (Industry, innovation and infrastructure), SDG 10 (Reduced inequalities), SDG 11 (Sustainable cities and communities), SDG 12 (Sustainable production and consumption), and SDG 15 (Life on land).

### Economic co-benefits: Improved coordination and use of effective end-to-end MWEIS and improved access to information, knowledge and services

The project will support the development of a national MWEIS. The MWEIS infrastructure could enable early and anticipatory action. This enhanced use of MWEIS could help countries to better manage water resources, reduce flood risk, and improve energy. The project could also benefit communities whose livelihoods depend on climate-sensitive natural resources, such as fisheries or agriculture, by improving their ability to adapt and be resilient in the face of climate change.

### Environmental co-benefits: Activities undertaken through country-specific actions

The project will support country-level proposals that could bring positive environmental co-benefits. The project will also address the impact on the environment from climate events such as floods and droughts, which are expected to increase due to climate change. This includes the conservation of natural resources, and the impact on the environment from climate events such as floods and droughts.

The project will support country-level proposals that could bring positive social co-benefits. The project will also address the impact on communities exposed to climate risks, including small islands, coastlines in flood and landslide prone regions, and mountainous areas. This includes the conservation of natural resources, and the impact on the environment from climate events such as floods and droughts.

## 1.4 Needs of the recipient

The funding proposal estimates that globally, between 3.2 billion and 5.6 billion people live in countries that are highly vulnerable to climate change, with the majority living in countries that have gaps in early warning systems. Most of these countries are among the least developed countries, small island developing States and African countries.

The IFAD assessment identifies a total of seven countries out of all seven participating countries. The ITAP notes that all seven countries, spanning a range of geographies, are highly vulnerable to climate change and climate-related hazards, which affect people, livelihoods, assets and economy.

### 1.5 Country ownership

All seven co-beneficiary letters have been received from national designated authorities of participating countries ( Annex I to the funding proposal). Of the total US$60 million expected as co-finance, approximately USD 35 million is envisaged as co-finance contributions from countries but approvals on individual country contributions are only provisional.

The funding proposal classifies Chad, Ethiopia and Somalia as either "in conflict" or "fragile" ( Annex Gb, p.14).

### e. The IEAF has observed that from the funding proposal (table on p. 4), that some countries have proposed national government partners as lead for activity (e.g. Antigua and Barbuda), whereas others lack a national government partner agency (e.g. Chad). In its response to the ITAV, the AE has clarified that governance arrangements vary across countries. The AE


---
**Page 174**
---

has noted that FCAIs often lack government mandated agencies or that existing agencies lack the capacity to implement specific project activities. Consequently, country programs are designed to be implemented by in-country agencies, including non-governmental and multilateral agencies, with the best comparative advantage in terms of capacities. The AF response document notes that the overall design of the country program and implementation will be managed by UNDP or government approved Responsible Parties**13**.

The document also highlights the importance of stakeholder engagement as part of the multi-stakeholder engagement plan. The document spells out that preliminary stakeholder consultations were undertaken during project preparation in 2023 and 2024. The consultations for outputs 1 and 2 seem to have been completed by the end of 2023.

The consultations for global and regional activities (output 1) appear as comprehensive and inclusive, with a wide range of stakeholders being engaged including UNDP, FAO, Microsoft Artificial Intelligence, GCF, and UNDP Regional agencies were also consulted. Including the Caribbean Community Climate Change Centre, Southern African Development Community, United Nations Environment Programme, International Federation for Development Authority on Development Climate Prediction and Applications Centre, Pacific Community, Asian Development Bank, and the World Meteorological Organization as well as the Global Water System for Africa and Asia.

For output 2, national-level consultations were conducted at all seven countries and are summarized in annexes 4 to annex 7 to the funding proposal, with full details to be made available in the country level proposals. The consultations covered topics such as country-specific climate change impacts, vulnerability assessments, adaptation strategies, ways to maximize different technologies, including artificial intelligence, partnerships, potential environmental co-benefits and trade-offs, institutional arrangements for implementation as well as the grievance redress mechanism, and identification of critical actors and implementing partners.

The consultations mainly with national-level stakeholders appear to be on-e-wit, with some limited engagement of local-level stakeholders. The funding proposal clarifies that detailed information about these consultations will be provided in the country level documentation. This will be important in identifying project sites and in informing the implementation of specific activities. However, in the view of the TAP, later consultations could also bring new risks of limited stakeholder engagement or responsiveness to community actions which would stall implementation processes.

The funding proposal notes that there is a need for further consultations with local authorities and community people, including men, women, people with disabilities, the elderly, youth and Indigenous People groups as relevant. In some countries, key groups may also include religious leaders and other traditional leaders. The TAP notes that while the programme will be about undertaking these consultations it is not as well articulated in the overall funding proposal. The extent to which the private sector was involved in consultations is less clear.

Overall, country ownership of the project is assessed as medium.


### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The estimated benefit-cost ratio and socioeconomic benefits in the funding proposal make a compelling case for investing in weather and climate information and disaster risk reduction activities. The TAP notes that the proposed approach to how these activities will be managed or implemented is not clear.

The project would be implemented over a period of 5 years, with a project lifespan of 30 years. The funding proposal notes that total project funding of USD 175,490,730 comprises a request for GFZ grant funding of USD 82,976,730 alongside co-finance of USD 68 million suggesting a co-finance ratio of 1:0.63.

However, the TFAP has observed some variances in co-financing figures presented in the funding proposal and in the letter of commitment received from the AE. The TFAP notes that these are yet to be firmued up. In response to queries from the TFAP, the AE has clarified its written commitments as follows: USD 10 million is expected from the World Bank; USD 25 million is expected from three sources based on letters of commitment received so far. These include:

(a) SOF investments of USD 40.81 million (USD 18 million had already been approved and are subject to a separate letter of commitment from the AE - for countries other than developing countries) – wherein the funding proposal states a figure of USD 30 million. The AE has clarified that this amount is in addition to the USD 40.81 million (USD 25 million co-financing with the SOF and GCF secretariat to ensure complementarity with this project).

(b) Co-financing commitments from governments - letters have so far be received from Ethiopia, Kenya, and Uganda. The AE has clarified that the funding proposal had envisaged a total contribution of USD 15 million and stated that the remaining letters of commitment are expected shortly.

Pillar leads comprising the United Nations Office for Disaster Risk Reduction (indicative USD 12,124,650), IFC (USD 3,751,681), and the International Telecommunication Union (USD 1,973,466), wherein the funding proposal suggested contributions of USD 3 million.

The allocation of the funds from the GCF project are to be aligned with the project’s ambition as set out in annexure 4. ## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### The fundamental premise of the overall Multi-country Project Advancing EWB4H funding proposal is sound and presents an opportunity for GCF resources to be deployed in response to the urgent call from the United Nations Secretary General to advance EWB4H by 2030 in all countries.

The funding proposal presents a programme umbrella approach, and the AE has clarified that details will emerge in separate country activity proposals for the remaining six participating countries. While the TAP review of the 2018-2020 Multi-country proposal serves as a useful model, the TAP review has been somewhat limited due to the lack of detail on the remaining six countries and the focus on the overall programme framework rather than on country-level activities and targeted priority sectors. Information on allocation of financial


---
**Page 178**
---

resources and country-level results is limited, as well as on how the AE will engage with stakeholders across different countries.

### The scale of ambition of the programme, and its reach across diverse country contexts spread over geographies means that there could be some associated risks in implementation. The development of a multi-country proposal for each participating country is complex. Additionally, country readiness to take forward MHEWS, and factors such as political, conflict, and security situations may also impact on the programme.

The role of UNDP is spread across multiple functions including: the AE executing entity, and senior supplier which could bring some risks to coordination and country ownership. The AE will be responsible for overall project management and the Project Implementation Modality, with UNDP country offices effectively responsible for the day-to-day operational delivery and engagement with implementing partners and other stakeholders. This carries some risk that programme implementation may be with the implementing agency rather than with the respective government.

The following actions by the AE will undertake the following actions:

Prior to the AE approving implementation for each individual participating country under output 2, make available to GCF: the full country proposal, including the feasibility study and any other relevant documentation. This should include all project finances with the multi-country funding proposal on which the ITAP assessment is based.

Ensure that future post-disaster needs assessments in all the selected countries are targeted at the most vulnerable groups and communities, and that lessons learned and outcomes of early warning systems. This includes integrating lessons learned from post-disaster needs assessments into the MHEWS framework. The revised MHEWS frameworks are informed by actionable insights to enhance EWS performance and resilience.

During the inception phase, update the MEL plan with further details on the steps and responsibilities for knowledge management and exchange.

Include in each annual progress report an analysis of how each country project contributes to the overall GCF MHEWS system. The ITAP recommends that the Board approve this multi-country funding proposal with the following conditions:

### Conditions for prior to the effectiveness of the Funded Activity Agreement

The AE shall deliver, in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, an updated logframe that is consistent with the theory of change and accurately captures the expected results of the programme. The updated logframe should include all participating countries of the Programme, disaggregated by sex. The updated logframe should also include details on how the AE will ensure effective use of the knowledge systems and Monitoring, Evaluation and Learning (MEL) dashboards (active) in each participating country will link to the overall MHEWS MEL system and to national reporting.

### Condition to be met prior to the first disbursement of funds by GCF under the Funded Activity Agreement

The Accredited Entity shall deliver, in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, a short summary that clearly explains how eligibility criteria will be applied in the context of this multi-country proposal. The summary should also include details on GCF proceeds for each of the seven participating countries. This note should also clarify any


---
**Page 179**
---

changes in approach across particular country contexts and with the overall multi-country scaling proposal.

(d) The Accredited Entity shall deliver, in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, a short note confirming the institutional set-up for output 2 in each participating country, including the identification of country participants in the multilevel governance mechanism under output 1. 

Condition to be met prior to the second disbursement of funds by GCF under the Funded Activity.

The Accredited Entity shall deliver, in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, a short note outlining the overall financial sustainability plan for the Programmes, including how this will be approached in each of the seven participating countries.

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP258)

## Proposal name:
Multi-country Project Advancing Early Warnings for All (EW4All)

## Accredited entity:
United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

## Country(ies):
Antigua and Barbuda, Cambodia, Chad, Ecuador, Ethiopia, Fiji, Somalia

## Project/programme size:
Medium

### Impact potential
The detailed analysis and observations of the ITAP on the Impact Potential rated as Medium are noted and appreciated. We confirm that the estimated target benefiting numbers for each of the seven participating countries have been provided to the GCF. All seven country proposals have been included in the overall funding proposal.

We recognize the ambitious scope of the output and acknowledge that the extent of impact will depend on successfully engaging and working with multiple stakeholders at all levels, from global to local. We assure the ITAP that the Pillar Leads and UNDP are well positioned to do this work effectively.

We confirm that the required pace and scale of implementation will be ensured to reach the intended beneficiaries by stated targets.

### Paradigm shift potential
We note the Medium rating given to the paradigm shift potential and appreciate the careful analysis that has been conducted. We note the critical need for a longer-term financial strategy to sustain the EW4All architecture beyond 2027 and accept the recommendation that this will be undertaken through the GCF’s Financial Strategy Working Group. We also confirm our commitment to a financial sustainability strategy in each participating country to codify sustainability pathways between outputs.

We acknowledge and confirm that mechanisms for knowledge exchange between countries, the establishment of technical expert groups, and sharing of lessons through events and workshops are critical to the success of EW4All. # Introduction

The Gender Analysis and Action Plan (GAAP) has been prepared in support of a project proposal for Multi-Country Project (MCP) for Early Warning Systems (for All EWEAS) to the Green Climate Fund (GCF). This project is supported by the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) in its role as a GCF Accredited Entity. The project has two main outputs:

* **Output 1:** will promote regional and global coordination among various stakeholders, including government agencies, civil society organizations, academia, and the private sector. This Output aims to scale up and accelerate global and regional support for national efforts to increase MWEWS' capacity and coverage. This will be achieved through a comprehensive approach that includes: strengthening monitoring and evaluation (MAI) processes to support EWEAS implementation; and reinforcing institutional capacities at all levels. The activities in this output will focus on coordination activities; developing guidance, tools and training materials; technical assistance; capacity building; knowledge management and dissemination; and peer-to-peer learning. This component will be implemented by UNDP.

* **Output 2:** specifically focuses on seven countries – Antigua and Barbuda, Cambodia, Chad, Eritrea, Ethiopia, Fiji, and Somalia – to test our national level implementation activities to address country and local-level needs to achieve the EWEAS target. This Output addresses barriers that prevent the implementation of a comprehensive early warning system in these countries:

    * Gaps in policies, inter-agency coordination and lack of coherent strategies;
    * Inadequate technical and scientific capacities;
    * Missing institutional structures and processes; and
    * Barriers that prevent effective local actions.

The activities in Output 2 for countries will mirror the four pillars of the EWEAS Executive Action Plan (EAP), and will be implemented through the national governments with support from UNDP. The EWEAS focal points are located at country-level offices and other partner agencies where relevant. The EWEAS pillars and leads are:

* **Pillar 1: Disaster risk knowledge** – led by the United Nations Office for Disaster Risk Reduction (UNDRR);
* **Pillar 2: Detection, observations, monitoring, analysis and forecasting of hazards**, led by the World Meteorological Organization (WMO);
* **Pillar 3: Warning dissemination and communication**, led by the International Telecommunication Union (ITU);
* **Pillar 4: Preparedness to respond, led by the International Federation of Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies (IFRC).

Activities aligned with Pillar 1 will focus on strengthening governance and coordination, establishing clear functions and responsibilities of agencies in the national warning dissemination process. Considerations of differential access to information and resources by women and vulnerable groups will be included in the dissemination strategy. This activity will also establish or strengthen existing local early warning committees or equivalent structures at all levels (national, district, sub-district, village) that are representative of women and vulnerable groups. Capacities of these local institutions, from district down to village level, will be enhanced through regular engagements and training programmes that strengthen institutional structures and procedures.

Activities aligned with Pillar 2 will focus on disaster risk knowledge and observations in order to improve awareness about hazards, exposure populations, services, infrastructure, vulnerabilities of communities,

# Project Institutional Arrangements at the Global Level

UNDP will be the Executing Entity of the EEAW4-MCP and will be responsible for the overall project.

The global implementation arrangement will comprise a Project Board or Project Steering Committee (PSC) comprising representatives from each country participating in the project, the UNDP Country Office Lead, GCI, UNDP and two representatives from each of the country projects, including at least one from the relevant national meteorological service.

The Project Board is responsible for making, by consensus, management decisions when guidance is required by the Project Manager. Project Board decisions will be made in accordance with standards that shall ensure management for development results, best value money, fairness, integrity, transparency and effective internal control systems. The Project Board will meet at least once a year to review progress of the project with the UNDP Executive Coordinator.

A global Project Management Unit (PMU) will also be constituted within UNDP to the overall project coordination, oversight and management of implementation of the consolidated Global GAP that is found in Section 10 of this report. The PMU will also provide technical support help the country PMUs in accessing regional and international resources through networking. The PMU comprises of three full-time staff: a Project Manager, a Monitoring and Evaluation Officer and a Communications Expert. The Project Manager will include a Technical Adviser, a Monitoring Evaluations and Learning Officer, a Communications Expert, a Solicitor and an Administrative Assistant.

The Project Manager will run the project on a day-on-day basis on behalf of the Project Board within the constraints laid down by the Project Board. The Project Manager function will end when the final project terminal evaluation report and other documentation required by the GCF and UNDP has been completed and submitted to the respective bodies for approval.


---
**Page 188**
---

the project. The Project Manager’s prime responsibility is to ensure that the project produces the results specified in the project document, to the required standard of quality and within the specified constraints of time and cost. Safeguards, Gender and Inclusion Expert (SGIE) will work under the supervision of the PM to implement the safeguarding measures. The Project Manager and the SGIE will be in regular contact with country teams and consolidate the result of country-level GAP implementation into the global-GAP.

### 1.2 Project’s Institutional Arrangements at the Country Level

Under Output 2, UNDP will implement the Project under the Direct Implementation Modality (DIM) in all seven countries. At the country level, each country will have a Project Board or Steering Committee made of the implementing partner and the national government. The National Programme Manager (NPM) for GCF as the Senior Beneficiary, the implementing partner will be part of the board. Furthermore, as the Senior Beneficiary, UNDP will provide guidance to the NPM on how to implement the guidelines for the implementation modality. The Project Board will be responsible for making, by consensus, management decisions and approving budgets and work plans. Decisions made at this level will be made per standards to ensure management for development results, best value money, fairness, integrity, transparency and effective international competition. This commitment to fairness and transparency is a key principle of the GCF.

The NPM will run the project on a day-to-day basis. The NPM is responsible for day-to-day planning and decision making in line with the approved work plan and budget. In this regard, SGIE will be supported by a National Safeguards, Gender and Inclusion Expert (NSGE) expert. The NSGE through the NPM, will report to the global PMU on status of the GAP activities at the country level so that they can be consolidated into the global-GAP monitoring.

# General Context for Gender in Early Warnings

Around the world gender inequalities and norms can be found including around household roles, access to resources, gender workloads, livelihoods and education. In most countries there are structural and normative barriers to gender inclusion, and different expectations and access to resources and decision-making between men and women. These factors mean that women’s voices and experiences in relation to disasters and emergencies can make a significant impact when it comes to receiving Early Warnings and in preparing for disasters.

Currently, there is a lack of equitable and meaningful decision-making opportunities and roles for women in the design and management of Multi-Hazard Early Warning Systems (MHEWS) and related areas. There are also gender gaps in mobile phone ownership and access to information technology. The digital divide in mobile user use is most stark in South Asia and Sub-Saharan Africa where, more than half a million women are excluded from the mobile connection and the gender gap scores are 41% and 37%, respectively.

The impacts of hazard events are not gender-neutral. Women are often placed at greater risk through a lack of timely and relevant information about imminent hazards. Women may not have equal access to technology, which can be used for early warning dissemination. This is particularly true in the case of women and marginalized groups living in rural or isolated areas. On the other hand, women are often the drivers, strong networks, leaders, organizers and caretakers in a household, play an important role in production and income. EWS and Disaster Risk Reduction (DRR) at scale without gendered MHEWS is significant and multifaceted, contributing to the effectiveness, inclusivity, and sustainability of these systems. Women can contribute to the development of early warning systems by sharing their knowledge and experiences within communities. Moreover, integrating women’s voices and experiences not only improves the outcomes of MHEWS but also promotes equity and empowerment within disaster-prone communities.

To be effective, early warning systems should not only have a sound scientific basis and reliable technology, but they should also be designed in such a way that they are understood and accessible to all. EWS should consider the unique needs and capacities of different groups, including people who are marginalized or living in vulnerable situations. This includes women and girls with disabilities, older persons, children, and others including the socioeconomic situation, accessibility of basic services and resources, literacy levels, access to communication technologies, and other factors that may affect their ability to receive early warning messages. Not considering gender differences when developing EWS could mean women miss out on benefits but also that opportunities are missed, such as not utilizing women-led community support groups and/or informal dissemination networks.

### Barriers and Challenges that can be found in Early Warning Systems

- **Lack of Gender-Specific Data**: Insufficient data on the unique needs and capacities of different groups, including women.
- **Limited Access to Technology**: Women may have limited access to technology used for early warning dissemination.
- **Rural and Isolated Areas**: Women living in rural or isolated areas often lack timely and relevant information about imminent hazards.
- **Gender Norms and Roles**: Gender norms and roles can limit the participation of women in decision-making processes related to EWS.
- **Limited Capacity Building**: Insufficient capacity building for women and marginalized groups, which can affect their ability to participate effectively in EWS.
- **Inadequate Communication Channels**: Inadequate communication channels that may not be accessible or understandable to all groups, including women.

### Source: PreventionWeb.net

1. Budinovic, M., et al. (2015) Gender in early warning and early action: why do we still need to talk about it?


---
**Page 190**
---

Thus, Multi-Hazard Early Warning Systems can be enhanced by taking into considerations key gender aspects.

The importance of gender considerations is embedded in the MCP for EMWAS. Some general aspects that will guide the project's gender approach include:

- Knowledge and experience: Understanding how women contribute to community dynamics, including vulnerabilities and coping strategies, which are crucial for developing targeted early warning messages and response plans.
- Women's networks: Recognizing the importance of building and strengthening networks accessible to women, such as community networks, women’s groups, and mobile technologies suited to their preferences.
- Women often play key roles in disseminating early warnings in culturally appropriate ways, ensuring messages are understood and acted upon effectively across diverse communities.
- Integrating gender perspectives into climate change and disaster risk assessments helps inform community-level discussions on how best to address these issues.
- Women's active participation in MHEWS activities can enhance trust and credibility within communities, increasing the likelihood that early warnings will be taken seriously and acted upon promptly.
- Women often have extensive local knowledge and networks, facilitating rapid dissemination of early warning messages across diverse communities.
- Women’s involvement contributes to building community resilience by integrating gender-sensitive approaches into disaster risk reduction strategies.
- Women often contribute innovative solutions to enhance MHEWS effectiveness, leveraging their practical experiences and knowledge of community dynamics.
- Women are key in ensuring the inclusion of all vulnerable groups, including children, elderly, and persons with disabilities, ensuring their inclusion in early warning and response efforts.
- There are widespread positive ripple effects from well-supported community-and women-led MHEWS initiatives on gender equality and status of women, and broader community-level engagement and empowerment.

3 Gender Assessment for Ecuador

Ecuador is a middle-income country. Its economy is highly dependent on oil and agricultural exports. In the last decade, Ecuador has increased social spending and investments in infrastructure, hospitals and schools. Nevertheless, income inequality remains a problem, with almost half the rural population living in poverty compared to only 10% of people nationwide. The rates of extreme poverty in rural areas are double that of the total population.

The Human Development Index (HDI) is developed annually by the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP). This indicator not only measures a country's economic development but also analyzes the health, education and standard of living of its citizens to assess the progress of countries in terms of human development and to compare well-being across different nations.

In 2023, Ecuador’s Human Development Index was 0.759, ranking 86th worldwide. This represents an improvement compared to 2020 (the COVID year), when it stood at 0.711.

The Gender Inequality Index (GII) is developed annually by the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) to reflect the disadvantages women face in three key dimensions of human development:

- Reproductive health, measured as the proportion of births attended by skilled personnel and the fertility rate.
- Empowerment, measured by the proportion of parliamentary seats occupied by women and the proportion of economically active men and men aged 25 and older with at least a secondary education.
- Labor Market Participation, measured by the proportion of economically active women and men aged 15 and older.

This index measures potential loss in human development due to inequalities between men and women in the aforementioned dimensions. In 2023, Ecuador’s Gender Inequality Index was 0.385, ranking 91st worldwide. This represents an improvement compared to 2021, when it stood at 0.388.

According to Ecuador government data from June 2023, poverty is concentrated in the South, with urban poverty at 18%, and rural poverty at 47%. Extreme poverty stands at 10%, affecting 5.2% of urban areas and 22.6% in rural areas). Therefore, poverty remains a critical issue, with that 20.90% of women are in a situation of income poverty, compared to only 8.34% for men. This is due to the fact that women earn less than men in economic shocks. As for extreme income poverty, women reach 6.2%, while men register 4.6%.

The youth are especially affected: in 2022, 34.4% of the youth (between 15 and 24 years old) lived in poverty). Although income poverty decreased for both men and women in 2022 (from 28.23% to 24.46% for men and from 37.98% to 34.4% for women), the gender gap increased, reaching 15.5%. This suggests a negative impact of other economic variables, such as inequality in the labour market, wage gaps between men and women, and differences in access to education.

The rate of unemployment is 7.2%, with 8.3% among women and 6.0% among men. The rates of remunerated employment are 15.5% higher than the rate for men. The major causes relate to lack of opportunities in the informal sector, low levels of education, and gender stereotypes.

The poverty rates of indigenous women, mundaños* and girls are above 78%, 57.3% and 45.8%, respectively.* Between 83% and 85% of indigenous boys and girls are in a situation of multidimensional poverty, being the most impoverished population group in the country*. In the case of older adults, the percentage of women


---
**Page 192**
---

Living in multidimensional poverty is 54.4%, 52.3% and 20.6% in the case of Montubias, Indigenous and Afro-Ecuadorian women, while percentages are 50.7%, 42.2% and 18% for men in this same group[1].

Literacy, Education and Access to Information
From 2010 to 2022, the illiteracy rate in Ecuador decreased by 3.1 percentage points, from 6.8% in 2010 to 3.7% in 2022. According to the results of the 2022 Population and Housing Census, out of the total population aged 5 to 94 years old, 1.3 million people are illiterate[2].

By age groups, people aged 65 and older have the highest percentage of illiteracy, with 51.5%, of whom 63.1% are women. The population aged 30 to 64 represents 43.9% of the total illiteracy in the country, with 57.4% of this group being illiterate[2]. Illiteracy among young people is concentrated in the age group of 15 to 29 years, with women representing 43.4% of this group[2].

Ecuador has an estimated population of 16,938,966**, with 48% of them being women. Countrywide 36% of people aged 5 and older are illiterate; in the rural areas, 37% have no formal education at all and 25% have also completed tertiary education[2]. 4.5% have no education. Literacy rates are 96.3%. However, there are significant differences between the genders, areas in the country and ethnic groups; literacy, for example, is higher among women (51%) than men (48%). The indigenous population has a lower literacy rate (4.3% compared to men at 3.1%). There is more parity in the genders around general school and high school education[2]. In urban areas, there are fewer illiterate people (37%), while in rural areas this number increases to 50%. Indigenous groups. Primary attendance is much higher; at 90% or more for all these groups[2].

Meanwhile, access to information varies according to the level of education and age, as well as their ability to utilize digital technologies. According to the 2019 Multisectoral Survey on Information and Communication Technologies (ICT) conducted by INEC, 58.2% of women aged 5 and older own a cell phone, compared to 61.7% for men[3].

In Ecuador, women's access to information varies based on several factors such as age, ethnicity, geographic location or education level. Below are the main ways women access information and the differences observed between men and various groups of women.

Ways of Accessing Information

Traditional Media:
• Radio: It is an important source of information, especially in rural areas. Women often listen to radio programs about their health, education, and government programs; while farmers listen to the radio between 5 and 6 a.m., as they head to the fields (WFP, 2013).
• Television: It is accessible through public service television, private Newz broadcasts and general interest programs that are watched by many women to stay informed (WFP, 2013).
• Print Media: Although less common, some groups of women, especially in urban areas, read free newspapers to obtain information (WFP, 2013).

# Digital Media and Social Networks:

* Internet: Internet access is growing, but significant gaps still exist. Young and urban women are the most likely to use the internet to obtain information. In the lowest income quintile, internet access is present in 43.7% of households, while in the highest income quintile, it is present in 79.4% of households (INEC ENEMODU, 2021).
* Social Networks: Platforms like Facebook, Instagram, and WhatsApp are popular for sharing and receiving information, especially among young women.
* Text Messaging: Many NGOs, government agencies, and health organizations send information directly to mobile phones.

## Community and Personal Sources:

* **Other Women:** Many women obtain information through conversations with friends, family, and neighbors.
* **Community Leaders:** In rural communities, community leaders and local organizations play a crucial role in disseminating information. Community leaders mainly use WhatsApp to coordinate with police and local militias.
* **Women's Groups:** A form of communication that primarily occurs in rural villages.
* **Schools and Educational Institutions:** Young women and mothers often receive information through their children’s schools.

Women and men in Ecuador can access information in similar ways; however, there are some notable differences:

* **Digital Divide:** Men tend to have greater access to digital technologies and the internet compared to women, especially in rural areas. 63.2% of male heads of households have internet access, while only 60.3% of female heads of households do (INEC ENEMODU, 2021).
* **Gender Roles:** Women, due to their traditional roles in the household, may have less time and fewer opportunities to access information through certain media, such as the internet and television.
* There are also differences among groups of women depending on their age, ethnicity, location and socio-economic status.

### Age:

* **Young Women:** Prefer social networks and the internet.
* **Older Women:** Prefer traditional media like radio and television.

### Ethnicity:

* **Indigenous Women:** In rural areas, radio and community leaders are important sources. Language barriers can also influence how they access information. Indigenous people are the ethnic group with the lowest number of active cell phones (30.2%) (INEC ENEMODU, 2021).
* **Afro-Ecuadorian Women:** Similar to indigenous women, but with a greater tendency to use digital media in urban areas.

### Geographic Location:

* **Urban Women:** Greater access to the internet and social networks. At the urban level, 81.1% use the internet (INEC ENEMODU survey), while only 54.5% used the internet (INEC ENEMODU, 2021).
* **Rural Women:** Greater reliance on radio and community leaders. Digital literacy is present in 19.4% of the rural population (persons between 15 and 60 years old who meet the criteria of not having an active cell phone, not using a computer, and not using the internet) (INEC ENEMODU, 2021).

# Socioeconomic Level

* **High and Middle:** Greater access to a variety of media, including the internet, cable television, and print media.
* **Low:** More dependent on radio and free television.

As far as phones are concerned, based on the results of the 2012 Population and Housing Census, a total of 12,485,763 people use cell phones, which represents nearly 100% of people aged 15 and over in the country. Of these, 47.8% are men and 51.3% are women, including a slight majority of female users. Based on data, 55.2% of all cell phone users are between 15-60 years old.

Broken down by age groups:

* Among those aged 15 to 39 years, 5,654,184 people use cell phones, of which 46.2% are men and 44.4% are women. The slight majority of men in this group may be influenced by usage patterns and access to technology at early and young ages.
* Among those aged 30 to 64 years, 5,975,529 people use cell phones, with 46.2% being men and 48.4% women. The higher percentage of women in this age group, which may reflect a higher adoption and use of mobile technology among women in their production years.
* In the 65 and older age group, 877,902 people use cell phones, of which 7.1% are men and 6.9% are women. The difference here is minimal, indicating almost equal adoption of cell phone use among older men and women.

According to the 2012 Population and Housing Census, conducted by INEC, the following results are detailed regarding the access of the population aged 5 years and older to ICT use in the last 3 months:

| Total number of women accessing mobile phones | 6,489,731 |

| Which corresponds to 5.13% of the national total. Of these, (4,735,413) are women in urban areas and 321,508 (2,258,430 women in rural areas) are women in rural areas. |
| Women's access to mobile phones is lower than men's access by a ratio of 6:5, suggesting the need for specific interventions to improve connectivity and access to technology in rural areas to foster more equitable digital inclusion.

Regarding women's use of computers or laptops, 3,216,736 women use these devices, representing 1.54% of the total population. Of this group, 77.2% are in urban areas (2,034,796 women) and 22.8% are in rural areas (1,181,940 women). The disparity in internet access between urban and rural areas can be attributed to several factors such as lower availability of technology and Internet infrastructure, which contributes to fewer opportunities for training in digital technology use or lower purchasing power to buy devices.

A total of 903,876 women use tablets, representing 5.10% of the total population. Of this group, 80.2% are in urban areas (725,009 women) and 19.8% in rural areas (178,867 women). Tablet use in

Employment, Labour Participation and Typical Social Expectations on Gender

Gender inequality in employment is evident in both quantitative and qualitative terms. Women have a higher unemployment rate (4.7% vs 3.2% for men) and are overrepresented in informal jobs, with 67.9% of women in such jobs compared to only 18.5% of men[2]. Women also earn less than men: the median income is USD 402.3 more for men at national level (USD 958 vs. 555.8), and USD 462.8 more in contrast to USD 297.4 for women.

In 2021, the average labor income for men at the national level was USD 496.8, while for women it was USD 430.2. More strictly, the median labor income for men was USD 416.5, in contrast to USD 297.4 for a woman.

This difference is even more evident when considering the median income of those with higher education: men earn 18.2% more than women on average. The difference in the median, which is USD 119, shows an even larger gap when considering the midpoint of incomes. Men earn 40.4% more than women in the median income[3].

In 2021, the average labor income for men at the national level was USD 587.6, while for women it was USD 496.8. More strictly, the median labor income for men was USD 424.2, in contrast to USD 297.4 for a woman.

This difference is even more evident when considering the median income of those with higher education: men earn 24.2% more than women on average. This reveals significant gender disparities in unpaid work. Regarding age group, it is equally important that women are overrepresented among those aged 15 to 39 years (with an index of 32.8%) compared to men[4].

This burden of unpaid work at an early age can interfere with education and development, perpetuating a cycle of gender inequality.


---
**Page 196**
---

In Ecuador, social expectations and cultural beliefs about gender roles are deeply rooted and vary according to region and age group. These expectations are influenced by historical, economic, social, and cultural factors.

Traditionally, men are expected to be the primary economic providers for the family. They are assigned the role of protectors, strength, authority, and independence as valued desirable masculine traits. Men are also expected to be responsible for income generation and financial management while women care of child-rearing and domestic chores. Valued characteristics in women include submission, patience, and dedication.

The activities dominated by men are five times those dominated by women (32% versus 6%), and the latter are linked to care and teaching services. According to data from the Central Bank of Ecuador (2021), the activities with the highest proportion of female employment include animal husbandry, publishing directories and media, food preparation and serving, hairdressing, cleaning, domestic work, child care, social assistance, clothing manufacturing, jewelry making, among others. Meanwhile, the activities with the highest proportion of male employment are agriculture, forestry, fishing, mining, construction, production, wood extraction, wood sheet manufacturing, private security activities, motor vehicle manufacturing, precious mineral product manufacturing, other mineral extraction, machinery manufacturing and repair.

Based on the concept of Unpaid Work, which is the time dedicated to domestic activities, caregiving, and community support without receiving payment or remuneration, women's participation is 75.8% and men’s is 24.2%. This gap is particularly high in rural homes and expectations that assign women the primary responsibility for domestic and caregiving tasks.

In many households, domestic chores and childcare primarily fall on women, regardless of whether they also participate in the labor market. This unequal distribution of domestic responsibilities reflects a cultural expectation that women should be responsible for household duties while men are expected to spend 13 hours a week on congregating and unpaid housework, while men spend an average of 9 hours a week on caregiving and unpaid housework. This gap is particularly high for rural, indigenous, and moutainous women.

The figures are based on data from the ENSO 2014 (INEC, 30 March 2014).

There are regional and generational variations to these gender expectations. In cities, there is a growing trend toward gender equity, with more women participating in the labor market and occupying leadership positions. However, this trend is not reflected in rural areas where women still bear the burden of caregiving responsibilities. In rural areas, traditional gender expectations are more pronounced.

Women have less access to education and job opportunities, reinforcing their traditional role as household caregivers. This lack of access to education and employment contributes to a significant gender gap in land ownership. In the agricultural sector, 13% of women are remunerated, compared to 35% of men; for this reason, women have less access to land and other resources necessary for productive farm work. Rural women perform 23 more hours of unpaid caregiving and domestic work per week (compared with urban women). Women also earn lower wages in lower education levels, fewer hours available for paid work, lower income, and less access to health care, living amid conditions unfavorable environmental factors such as soil productivity, water quantity, and quality.

Meanwhile, younger generations (15-34 years) are increasingly challenging traditional gender expectations. Young women are seeking higher levels of education and labor market participation, more willing to question


---
**Page 197**
---

and challenge traditional gender roles, with greater professional aspirations, seeking more equity in the distribution of domestic tasks, and with high levels of technology and social networks, facilitating access to information, there is an increase in the acceptance of more egalitarian gender roles. On the other hand, older individuals, who have grown up under patriarchal norms, may still hold traditional views on gender roles. Women from rural areas often have less access to education and employment and likely have performed domestic roles for most of their lives. The cultural and social expectations that these generations grew up with strongly influence their perceptions and behaviors regarding gender equality. As women gain more education and information, compared to younger generations, may limit their exposure to new ideas about gender equality.

### Representation and Participation in Decision Making

In Ecuador, women's political participation has shown significant progress in recent years. Following the legal reforms of 2016 that established quotas for political parties to promote gender parity in politics. As of 2024, women hold 43% of the seats in the National Assembly**.

At the local level, women’s representation increased in 2023 compared to the 2019 election. Of the country's 221 cantons, 34 have female mayors, 16 more than in 2019. Regarding provincial councils, 7 of the 24 provinces now have women as presidents.

Regarding the representation of women heading State ministries, in November 2023, the current government appointed 11 women as ministers and secretaries of State, which represents 52% of the total positions in the National Cabinet**.

According to the results of the 2022 Population and Housing Census**, out of a total of 1,386,827 households nationwide, 49.5% are headed by women and 50.5% are headed by men. In urban areas, 40.6% of household representatives are women and 59.4% are men. In rural areas, the percentage is reversed: 53.8% of household representatives are men and 46.2% are women.

According to ethnic self-identification, the gender distribution of household representation is as follows:

* **In indigenous communities:** 35.9% are women and 64.1% are men.
* **Among Afro-Ecuadorians:** 40.8% are women and 59.2% are men.
* **Among Mestizos:** 34.2% are women and 65.8% are men.
* **Among indigenous peoples of the Amazon:** 37.1% are women and 62.9% are men.
* **Among whites:** 38.3% are women and 61.7% are men**.

Despite progress, women's participation in public consultations still faces significant challenges in Ecuador. According to a study by CERES**, although women attend public consultations, their derivative participation remains low due to the lack of information about these processes as well as gender stereotypes that restrict their real influence in decision-making.

In urban areas, women have more opportunities and resources to participate in public consultations due to a greater presence of feminist organizations and inclusion policies, which have increased women's visibility


---
**Page 198**
---

and active roles. According to a UN Women report[1], urban women tend to be more empowered and occupy more leadership spaces in social movements. In rural areas, participation is more limited due to factors such as lack of access to information, lower educational levels, and traditional gender roles. A study by FLACSO Ecuador[2] found that 65% of women in rural areas have experienced at least one incident of some type of violence throughout their lives.

From a cultural and social perspective, indigenous women face significant challenges in speaking openly and freely about the issues that affect them due to deeply rooted gender norms and stereotypes. In indigenous communities, for example, women often have greater restrictions due to patriarchal traditions and social structures and may even be subject to reproductive rights violations and limited access to leadership positions.

Women with greater access to higher education and technology tend to be more empowered and have more influence in their communities. A UNICEF study[3] shows that young people participate more actively in social movements when they are better educated, which is a key factor for women's empowerment. Furthermore, there are examples of female leadership in indigenous organizations, showing a gradual change in this dynamic.

In conclusion, from a sociological point of view, women’s participation in public consultations in Ecuador is influenced by various factors such as socio-economic, cultural, and generational factors. Although there are significant advances, especially in urban areas and among younger generations, structural barriers persist for women to participate fully and equally in decision-making processes. In-country training on gender inclusion policies are crucial to continue advancing gender equality and women's participation in public life.

### Gender-Based Violence

Gender-based violence remains a critical issue in Ecuador. According to the National Survey on Family Relations and Gender Violence Against Women (ENVIWGEM), conducted by the INEC in 2019[4]:

- **65 out of every 100 women** in Ecuador have experienced at least one incident of some type of violence in various contexts throughout their lives including: (i) psychological violence, 56.9%; (ii) physical violence, 38.7%; and (iii) sexual violence, 24.2%.
- **Data from 2019 show that** out of every 100 women in Ecuador have experienced at least one incident of some type of violence in various contexts in the last 12 months. The percentages by type of violence are: (i) psychological violence, 56.9%; (ii) physical violence, 38.7%; (iii) sexual violence, 24.2%; and (iv) patrimonial violence, 6.1%.
- **By ethnic self-identification**, 72% of Afro-Ecuadorian women, 64% of indigenous women, 58% of Montubio women, and 60% of Mestizo women have experienced at least one incident of some type of violence throughout their lives.

- **By age group**, 45% of women between 15 and 17 years; 55% between 18 and 29 years; 69% between 30 and 44 years; 66% between 45 and 64 years, and 65% over 65 years have experienced at least one incident of some type of violence throughout their lives.

### Gender Policies and Legal Framework

Ecuador is a signatory to several international conventions and agreements aimed at promoting gender equality. These include the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action, and the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action. Ecuador has specific legislation on gender equality, gender violence, and national programs and committees dedicated to these issues. These frameworks aim to address gender inequality in all spheres of life; however, there are implementation gaps, but gap remains in their effective implementation, particularly in rural and marginalized communities.

#### Gender Equality Legislation

- Constitutional Law of the Republic of Ecuador (2008): Establishes equal rights and prohibits discrimination based on gender.
- **Gender Violence (legislation)**: Comprehensive Organic Law to Prevent and Eradicate Violence against Women (2018): This law establishes a framework to prevent, punish, and eradicate violence against women in its various forms. Three recent laws aimed at addressing gender inequalities are:
  - The Organic Law on Voluntary Termination of Pregnancy in Cases of Rape (2022).
  - The Organic Law to Promote the Violet Economy (2021), and
  - The National Plan for the Eradication of Gender Violence against Children, Adolescents, and Women (2022-2035): A plan that coordinates efforts to prevent and eradicate gender violence.
- **National Program**: National Program for the Eradication of Gender Violence against Children, Adolescents, and Women (2022-2035): A plan that coordinates efforts to prevent and eradicate gender violence.

#### National Programs

- **National Action Plan on Gender Equality (PAGEN)**: A public policy instrument aimed at recognizing and making visible the participation and work of women and their importance to Family Farming, including from the competencies of the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock to guarantee their rights.
- **Gender-Related Institutional Arrangements**: Ecuador has established several institutional arrangements to support gender equality, including:
  - The National Council for Gender Equality (CNES): An agency responsible for ensuring the implementation of public policies for gender equality.
  - The Ministry of Economic and Social Inclusion (MIESI): Has specific programs aimed at protecting and promoting women's rights.
