The Ministry of Women and Human Rights (MINIDHUR). Ensures and promotes Human Rights through public policies of prevention, care, and reparation to promote gender equality.

These bodies are responsible for developing and enforcing policies that promote women's rights and gender equality.


### Women’s Representation and Participation in DRM Sectors

Women’s participation in disaster risk management (DRM) sectors is essential for building resilient communities. Efforts to include women in DRM decision-making processes and leadership roles are ongoing, but greater representation and targeted support are needed. Programs that train and empower women in DRM can enhance community preparedness and response capabilities, making disaster management more inclusive and effective.

The Organic Law on Risk Management, in its articles 15, 30, and 39, establishes the role of the Council of Communities of Women as a human rights body at the level of central municipalities, such as Guayquil. The Community Committee has already been created, which includes gender organizations, vulnerable groups, and rural communities. Within this framework, training related to early warning systems is provided for women in these areas.

The National Institute of Meteorology and Hydrology (INAMHI) has a quota of 50% of women (NIDAH, 2024), while the core of the National Secretariat for Risk Management (SNDR), there are approximately 13 FTE employees, of which 50% are women (SNDR, 2024).


#### Gender in Early Warnings and Disaster Risk Response

Emergencies and their impacts threaten the rights to human life, endangering livelihoods, food security, and health. Women are more vulnerable than men due to poverty and marginalization, face higher levels of risk and an increased workload due to socially and culturally imposed gender roles. Additionally, they have limited opportunities for decision-making in disaster response.

The impacts of disasters on women include: (i) economic losses; (ii) social exclusion; (iii) increased workloads; (iv) displacement; (v) food insecurity; (vi) water scarcity; (vii) lack of access to health services and sexual violence; (viii) loss of livelihoods due to drought, fires, earthquakes, and volcanic eruptions; (ix) social issues: security crises and other forms of violence.

The impacts of crises on women, both rural and urban, are multidimensional, differentiated, and, in some cases, disproportionate, depending on local contexts as well as the networks and structures of communities. The State must ensure that these impacts are addressed.


#### Differentiated Impact

Depending on the location (rural or urban) of women, the loss of livelihoods varies significantly. In rural areas, a larger percentage of livelihoods depends on agricultural and livestock activities, while in urban areas, it is more related to informal employment. Depending on their type of work, different needs can be identified for the recovery of these livelihoods after an emergency, as well as the implementation of preventive actions to minimize the impacts in the event of a disaster or emergency.

Another differentiated impact relates to the state network for guaranteeing access to rights (institutions and services). The greater the dependence on public institutions and services, the weaker networks during emergencies influence the degree of weakening of public systems. While everyone may experience limitations on their rights during a crisis, the weaker the state structure, the greater


---
**Page 201**
---

the difficulty in restoring it & ensuring those rights. In rural areas, the recovery of these structures that guarantee rights can be more complex.

Additionally, women are particularly vulnerable to climate change, emergencies, and disasters, as social, economic, and political inequalities place them in a position of reduced resilience to adverse effects. A clear example of this is rural women, who, due to limitations in access to or ownership of land, as well as deeply rooted gender norms and practices, face significant barriers when it comes to accessing resources that would enable them to develop enterprises or implement necessary improvements to address challenges.

Among the challenges faced by women are:

- Access to prior information (preparation and warning systems) during and through the emergency, regarding actions they can take to protect their lives.
- Caring for others during the emergency, as women mostly take on the role of caregivers. This role, in addition to influencing the specific situation during the emergency, impacts both the loss and recovery of livelihoods.
- During emergencies, there has been an increase in pre-existing forms of violence, such as domestic violence and sexual abuse. Women are also more likely than men to experience gender-based violence; according to available information, primarily affect women (girls, adolescents, and adults).

Climate change and resource scarcity also have a differentiated impact on women and girls. For example, the decrease in water sources forces them to travel greater distances to access this resource, increasing the daily hours spent fetching water. Women are also more likely than men to be responsible for food production; however, they face additional challenges such as lack of access to land ownership or credit.

The impacts of natural disasters and emergencies are unequal from a gender perspective. Before the COVID-19 health crisis, women were already overrepresented in poverty situations, a situation that worsened after the pandemic. In 2023, for every 100 men in poor households, there were 117.3 women, reflecting their lack of economic opportunities and access to resources.

Inequality, severely affecting women due to the decline in economic activity in key sectors for female employment (agriculture, livestock, fishing), has increased vulnerability during emergencies may further limit their access to land ownership, housing, education, health, and participation in political and decision-making spheres, reducing their adaptive capacities.

In rural areas, barriers to accessing productive assets (land, technical assistance, machinery) and financial instruments (credit and insurance), coupled with their limited mobility and participation in decision-making, restrict women's opportunities to develop their producer skills and place them at a disadvantage in producing, marketing, and expanding the value of their goods. These are other dimensions of women sectors such as agriculture, livestock, forestry, and fishing.

During and after disasters, not all evacuation sites catered by the local authorities provide safety for or disability access for women. Women also face challenges on local capacities and infrastructures. The tide of gender-based violence, including domestic violence, can increase, especially in temporary shelters where a lack of privacy and security is common. In rural areas, gender-based violence may be normalized and less frequently reported by women due to the fear of stigma or retaliation.

Additionally, displaced women lose their livelihoods and face discrimination when attempting to access resources for survival. The loss of livestock, crops, and other productive assets can have devastating impacts on women's lives; losing homes, and crops has a devastating impact, forcing them to migrate in search of resources for survival.


18


---
**Page 202**
---

In summary, natural disasters tend to exacerbate pre-existing gender inequalities, and women often face greater risks and challenges compared to men. This unequal impact highlights the need for a gender-sensitive risk management approach that ensures equitable participation of women in all aspects of disaster planning and response.

Some of the differentiating factors between men and women regarding exposure to hazards and response capacity are as follows:

- **Access to preventive information:** Fewer women have access to this information compared to men. Even when they do have general access to information, due to their participation in work-related activities outside the home, they may not be able to receive it.

- **Access to response information:** Women have less access to this information compared to men.

- **Legal vulnerability:** Women, especially in rural areas, face greater legal vulnerability. The levels of legal protection for women are lower than those for men and do not always cover women living in urban areas, and existing legalizations do not always include women.

However, women also have great potential as agents of change in natural resource management activities; these are important to consider when designing disaster risk reduction programs and distribution services in rural areas, the development of enterprises, and the adoption of innovative decisions to address current problems arising from disasters and emergencies highlight this potential.

In the country, the main hazards affecting the population are landslides, earthquakes, floods, and droughts. Response capacity is affected by a lack of infrastructure, access to production factors, basic services, connectivity, and cultural barriers. In urban areas, the development of response capacities is more complex due to issues associated at the community level, especially affecting vulnerable groups such as girls, boys, and women.

There are global databases, such as DESINVENTAR, that record multi-hazard disasters with a gender perspective. This information can be used to design multi-hazard early warning systems (MHWEWS) and establish mechanisms for the exchange of information between national authorities and international organizations such as the Red Cross, NGOs, state entities, international cooperation, the Church, and volunteers. The database is also used by the Ecuadorian Red Cross in its work on gender equality as a pillar 4 of the project, which focuses on preparedness and response capacity. It is anticipated that, starting in 2025, the Ecuadorian Red Cross will establish mechanisms to consolidate and analyze information, ensuring that actions are more effective.

Public policy through government entities at the central and local levels supports the management of Community Risk Management Committees, providing financing, technical assistance, training, monitoring, follow-up, distribution of goods and services, information systems, response kits, shelters, and humanitarian centers. However, other mechanisms for risk reduction and mitigation policies for risk management are not considered.

New proposals for national and public policies that include a gender perspective have been developed in response to implementing programs and projects aimed at MHWEWS that consider differentiated responses according to the needs and realities of territories and vulnerable groups. Currently, there is no significant difference in the level of awareness of early warning information between men, women, boys, and girls as alerts are delivered through official communication channels and social networks without any specific directionality.

**Recommendations**

In order to enhance inclusiveness and participation it is crucial to consult women about their needs and preferences through:

19


---
**Page 203**
---

Guided interviews with key women in the target communities, which will allow for the collection of detailed testimonies and experiences. Interviewers should preferably be women.

Focus group discussions with women of various ages and backgrounds to gain a deeper understanding of their perspectives and needs.

Develop and administer specific surveys for women that include questions about the project's requirements.

WhatsApp groups for women, where a very specific exchange of information can take place.

Continuous online consultation platforms, where women can provide ongoing feedback on the project.


Proposed activities for the project

1. It is necessary to start with an assessment of needs and preferred communication channels.
2. Both the needs assessment process and the communication of alert messages must be in clear and accessible language, including local languages and adapted to local culture. In short, communication strategies must be tailored to women's realities.
3. For the planning of activities related to the design and dissemination of alert messages, it is important to involve local governments, leaders, civil society organizations and women’s organizations. Support networks for women in the project area can also be used as intermediaries between the community and organizational schemes, which are more developed than in the rest of the population.
4. Capacitating women (leadership, technology) and groups of women who have active roles within their communities, such as women leaders, women entrepreneurs and heads of households, or those actors who want to participate actively in the development of project-related activities. This will allow them to be more effective in disseminating messages to other women response leaders. This should include raising awareness among the families of the women participants about the importance of gender equality, non-violence, and masculinities with young people and husbands, and shared responsibility in household activities.
5. Any measure to disseminate information must include facilitating access to the technology that will be used to receive alerts and messages.
6. According to the type of activities, it is necessary to work on risk management education programs in schools, colleges, and communities, specifically aimed at women and girls.
7. Additionally, training and awareness workshops can be organized on interpreting early warnings and response measures, adapted to the needs and available time of women for their participation.
8. A monitoring and evaluation methodology must be established that includes gender indicators to measure the impact of the implemented measures and make adjustments during the development of the project

# Gender Assessment for Cambodia

Cambodia is ranked "medium" equality on the UNDP Gender Development Index 2023-24*, comparing Human Development Index achievements for women and men*. Women consist of 50.5% of the total population in Cambodia, with a higher proportion living in urban areas (61%) compared to rural areas (49%). Rural women outnumber rural men by about 7%, while urban women are slightly fewer than urban men. The majority of people live in rural areas with 74% of the population living in rural areas* and the majority of them work in the agriculture sector. About 24.4% of the population or 3.4 million people live with disabilities in Cambodia, which is higher than the global average of 15%. The percentage of women living with disabilities (5.7%) is higher than that of men (4.3%).* Cambodia's gender equality index score is 0.628, ranking it as a medium performer on the Gender Equality Index (GEI) compared to other countries in Southeast Asia and East Asian nations (ASEAN region). Cambodia is ranked 122nd out of 193 countries in the Global Gender Gap Report 2023*. Cambodia has made significant progress toward gender equality, particularly through economic change, helped to increase labor earnings and reduce poverty. Living standards have improved, helping Cambodia to narrow urban-rural gaps. However, low-income and rural households still lag in access to basic services and earning opportunities*.

## Education, Vocational Training and Literacy

Cambodia has made significant progress in achieving gender parity in education. The net enrollment in primary school was 98% for male students and 95.5% for female students for the academic year 2022-2023*. The enrollment rate at lower secondary schools is about 86% for female students and 85.4% for male students, with a gender parity index of 1.007. At upper secondary school level, the enrollment rates are 59.5% for female students and 63.5% for men*. However, the proportion of female students in tertiary education and technical vocational training is lower than that of male students, particularly for non-traditional areas of study, such as science, technology, engineering, and mathematics (STEM). In 2019, only 48.7% of female students compared to 56.3% of male students studied in STEM-related subjects at the tertiary level*. The proportion of female students who graduated from a Bachelor's Degree in STEAM-related subjects was 40%, while it was 50% for male students*.

## Gender Gaps

Gender gaps in access to education are particularly evident among ethnic minorities and people with disabilities. Women have fewer opportunities than men, including lower literacy rates and fewer educational opportunities than their male counterparts*. The drop-out rate for both indigenous female and male adolescents aged 14-18 is often higher compared to other groups*.

## Access to Education

The pandemic has widened the education disparity for indigenous groups, women, and people living in poverty. Harmful practices among rural women to ensure children's safety, hold a job outside of the home, understand laws and regulations that relate to them, access technologies, extension services, and finance as well as climate change and disaster risks, including early warning information*.

# Employment and Labor Participation

In 2023, the women’s labor participation rate in Cambodia was 79% compared to men’s labor participation rate of 89%**. The Cambodia Social and Economic Survey (CSS) found that 41% of women aged 15 to 64 years were employed outside their home, while only 23% of men were so engaged. In the formal sector, and services sector (including hospitality and tourism, finance, healthcare and education)**. However, only 40.6% of women in employment are in the formal sector compared with 71.5% for men. The majority work in the informal sector, with insecure working conditions, low pay, and no access to social protection**.

Overall, gender gaps remain large between women and men in Cambodia, across industries and occupations. A United Nations study* found that Cambodian women earn about 80% of men’s wages while owing fewer assets than men. Women are also less likely to have access to land or other productive resources and opportunities. The average gender wage gap in Cambodia is around 19%. This is tied to disparities in education, which can be attributed to factors such as poverty, lack of access to quality education, discrimination, gender-segregated occupations, and social norms associated with women’s primary domestic role**. To address these issues, it is important to create an enabling labor structural barriers for women to take and stay in formal employment, undertake skills training, and pursue opportunities for career development.

## Representation and Participation in Decision Making

Although there is a robust gender equality perspective reflected in national gender equality policy initiatives and legal frameworks, challenges remain for equal representation and participation of women and men in decision-making positions at all levels of government and public administration**. Women account for about 14% of total parliament members in the current mandate**, a decrease from 21% in 2013. In the National Assembly, women hold only 9 out of 57 seats (16%). At the sub-national level, state positions are held by women in the executive body. As of 2022, women hold around 27% of leadership positions at the provincial and district levels, including deputy chiefs of offices to director general. Overall, women make up about 42% of the civil service workforce**.

At the sub-national level, the proportion of women remains low in the positions of governor at the capital city/province (8%) and municipality/khan/district (3.4%). The proportion is also low for the positions of chief of Community/Sangkat (10%), deputy governor at the municipality/khan/district (9%), and deputy governor numbers for the position of deputy governor at the municipality/khan/district levels (20.3%) and members of councils of Community/Sangkat with 22%**.

## Gender-Based Violence and Social Norms

Gender-based violence is a serious problem in Cambodia and remains a concern. An estimated 21% of women aged 15–49 years in Cambodia have experienced physical, sexual or emotional violence from intimate partners in their lifetime, and 43% of them report experiencing severe violence with sustained psychological effects**. Emotional violence is the most common form of violence against women (60%), followed by physical abuse (28%) and sexual abuse (19%). Rural women face higher rates of intimate partner violence than women in urban areas (25% vs 17%, respectively). Gender norms and social attitudes also contribute to gender-based violence, such as the idea that men are entitled to sex regardless of consent, directly contributing to gender-based violence**. Women and girls from indigenous groups who are survivors of gender-based violence face more


---
**Page 206**
---

challenges in seeking legal advice, safe shelter and mental support than non-indigenous women[10]. The Cambodian cultural practices and social norms continue to contribute to the unequal division of labor in the household and community. Women are more likely to be involved in unpaid domestic work such as cooking and generating income, women and girls also are primarily responsible for household and childcare duties. Most of the time, women have less access to education, training, credit, land ownership, and other resources; in particular, carry a heavy burden in terms of household chores, including housework, farming, raising animals, and collecting forest foods, especially in times of food shortage and disasters. Women are still expected to be responsible for the care of children and elderly family members, which limits their time and their capacity and potential. This impacts the roles of women and men in decision-making in the household and the community. Women also have less access to information on disaster risk reduction (DRR) and are less involved in managing the household budget, but men are the primary decision-makers for major expenditures. Moreover, a gender analysis report by the Asia Foundation out that social norms and low level of education and literacy among women limit their participation in DRR activities. Women need more access to technical skills, and training, having their voices heard, and enjoying preference in their families and society.

### Gender Policies and Legal Framework

The Royal Government of Cambodia is committed to promoting gender equality and social inclusion in disaster risk reduction (DRR) management, aligning with the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development's goal of "leaving no one behind." Key national legislation and policies support this commitment:

- The Constitution of 1993 guarantees equal rights for women and men, including human rights, equality before the law, and protection from discrimination.
- The Law on the Prevention of Domestic Violence and Protection of Victims addresses domestic violence and promotes gender equality.
- The National Action Plan for Disaster Risk Reduction (NAP DRR) focuses on the needs of women, children, the elderly, and people with disabilities during disasters.
- The One-Stop Center for Women's Empowerment Phase I promotes gender equality and women's leadership in key sectors, aiming to reduce vulnerability to gender-based violence and improve social protection for the most vulnerable.
- Nearly Ratanak VI(2024-2028), a five-year plan by the Ministry of Women’s Affairs, focuses on gender equality and women's empowerment across various sectors, emphasizing climate change, well-being, legal protection, and education.

Cambodia's Updated Nationally Determined Contributions include gender-focused measures for climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction (DRR).

- The National Action Plan for Disaster Risk Reduction (NAP DRR) 2018-2028 emphasizes gender and vulnerable group-responsive approaches in disaster risk reduction and climate resilience.
- The National Strategy on Gender Mainstreaming in Disaster Management (NSGMDM) aims to address the needs of vulnerable women and other groups through policy-making, leadership development, and gender equality.

The Guideline for Gender Mainstreaming in Disaster Management provides practical guidance for integrating gender perspectives in disaster management policies. Cambodia is also committed to international and regional frameworks promoting gender equality and social inclusion in DRR management, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous People (UNDRIP), United Nations Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (CRPD), ASEAN Agreement on Disaster Management and Emergency Response (AASDME) Work Programme 2021-2025, and the Beijing Agenda for Global Action on Gender-Sensitive Disaster Risk Reduction.


---
**Page 207**
---

Despite these efforts, challenges remain such as limited knowledge and skills within ministries for gender analysis, and insufficient resources and funding for gender-focused interventions

# Gender-Related Institutional Arrangement

Cambodia has established mechanisms at both national and sub-national levels to advance gender equality and ensure the protection of women. Led by the Ministry of Women's Affairs (MoWA) in collaboration with sectorial ministries, local governments, and development partners.

MoWA plays a crucial role in promoting gender equality by coordinating efforts across public institutions, civil society, and the private sector through various programs and the Technical Working Group on Gender (TWG). MoWA also leads the National Committee for Women's Affairs (NCWA) to ensure the implementation of policies and ensuring legal protection for women. MoWA has line departments that work at the provincial level.

Sectorial ministries have Gender Mainstreaming Action Groups (GMAGs) responsible for developing, implementing, monitoring, and evaluating gender equality programs within their respective sectors. The National Committee on Disaster Management (NCDM) is forming a gender working group within its structure, and the Ministry of Women's Affairs is one of the key members of NCDM.

At the sub-national level, Women and Children Consultative Committees (WCCC) operate at provincial and district levels, while Women and Children Consultative Groups and Children (ECCG) are at the commune level. These committees advise on gender equality and women's and children's issues.

The sub-committees for disaster management were also established at the sub-national level to handle various aspects of disaster management. The MoWA, in collaboration with the National Committee on Women Affairs and a member of WCCC and ECCG are members of the sub-committees respectively at the province, district, and commune levels, and mostly in the health and hygiene teams. Besides this, no specific provision ensures participation and representation of diverse groups, including women in the planning, response, and recovery phases.

# Women's Representation and Participation in Disaster Management in DRM Sector

Women’s representation in preparation, implementation, disaster resource management, meteorology, hydrology, and disaster management remains low. According to the latest data from 2023*, the Ministry of Water Resources and Meteorology (MoWARM) has a total of 1,401 officials, with 316 being women (22.5%). Nonetheless, only one woman is working as a director in meteorology and hydrology at the national level, while there are no women serving as secretaries of state (16%), one woman as under-secretary of state (8%), and two women as deputy directors (15%). There are no women in the roles of department director or provincial department director. Moreover, there is only one woman working as a meteorologist at the national level, while there are none at the provincial levels. For example, in Kratie province, no women are working as technical staff in meteorology and hydrology, and there is only one woman in this field in Kampong Thom province**.

There is no comprehensive national data on women in Disaster Management (DM) positions; however, it is believed that women represent less than 13% of officials in existing provincial disaster management committees, which is low compared to civil servants at the provincial level and district levels. This is attributed to a lack of gender-sensitive policies and programs, as well as a mindset and belief that water and disaster management work are more appropriate for men than women persons. Women are often perceived to be good with science-oriented technical skills. This contributes to gender gaps in the education sector in the field related to STEM, and gender disparity in government institutions and other sectors.

* Ministry of Water Resource Management and Meteorology (MoWARM) (2021). Annual report on 2020 achievements and direction for 2024.

## Gender in Early Warning Systems and Disaster Risk Reduction

Efforts to improve disaster information and risk knowledge, such as the CamRi database and PROM[36] dashboard, lack of gender-disaggregated data, which hampers understanding the specific vulnerabilities of different groups. Better data collection, analysis, and sharing are crucial for targeted interventions.

NCDM[37] has been working with the support of development partners, national and international organizations, and the private sector. CamRi serves as a platform database for providing historical damage and loss information. NCDM has produced flood risk maps at the sub-national level in Kratie province to provide an overview of flood risks. The data is integrated into the national and sub-national flood contingency plans and publicly used to raise awareness, particularly among vulnerable groups such as women and children. However, there are still challenges with disaggregation of data such as sex, age, disabilities, and other diversities. The lack of this data disaggregation is one of the biggest barriers to comprehensively understanding of vulnerabilities and impacts of hazards and disaster risk reduction.

In partnership with WFP, NCDM also works to enhance the interactive web-based dashboard called Platform for Real-time Impact Situation Monitoring (PROM). PROM assesses the potential risk and impact of climate

<h3>Gender and Access to Early Warning Information and Technologies</h3>

<p>Cambodia’s Early Warning System (EWS) has been implemented by NCDM in partnership with PII for Flood and ActionAids for drought to provide alerts and information about disaster risks for better preparedness. The EWS 1294 is a mobile phone-based system that sends SMS messages on mobile phones with warnings about natural hazards. Early warning systems (EWS) like EWS 1294 are effective, not inclusive, particularly for women, the elderly, and those without mobile phones.</p>

<p>A recent study of PII shows that most community members in Kratie and Stung Treng provinces were aware of the existence of the system but did not subscribe due to a lack of knowledge and/or understanding of how to utilize it. Awareness and usage of EWS are lower among women, who often receive information from their husbands or children.</p>

<p>Generally, awareness of EWS systems and related information remains limited for both women and men. However, men tend overall to have more information than women as they regularly follow local news through radio, TV, and mobile apps. A national survey (2021-2022) shows that women are less likely than men to own a mobile phone or use it daily. Women also watch TV less often than men do, with 38% of women watching TV once per week compared to 54% of men. Women also listen to the radio at least once a week in Kratie. In Kratie, 90% of 10-20 people who come to check the water level measurement are men.</p>

<p>Women also reported that they accessed information on the river water level and weather forecasting through mobile apps less often than men did. Women were more likely to access information from their children or grandchildren (54%) compared with men (38%). In Kratie, 70% of women had searched the Facebook page of MWRM with their children. Based on the national survey, none of the interviewees reported using social media for disaster risk management in their communities.</p>

<p>Most of them said they received flooding alert from local authorities, particularly group/village leaders through home visits or loudspeakers in some communities, while people living near rivers and lakes were more likely to receive information from mobile phones. Thus, channels of communication and dissemination are critically important to ensure everyone and all groups of people have access to EWS.</p>

<h3>Preparation, Planning, and Responses</h3>

<p>Contingency and response plans include no distinction between the needs of women and more vulnerable groups such as persons with disabilities residing in the affected areas. Moreover, despite community engagement efforts by PII, most of the women and men groups in Kratie and Stung Treng provinces are not aware that they can participate in planning for disaster risk management.</p>

<p>The most vulnerable, including persons with disabilities, are not aware of the process and less likely to be engaged in the planning. Most of the women and men groups in the community meetings in Kratie and Stung Treng provinces were not aware of their right to participate in planning or decision-making processes for their commune contingency and emergency plans or commune development plans.</p>

<p>During the focus group discussion with groups of persons with disabilities, representatives of organizations for people with disabilities (OPDs) in Kratie and Kampong Thom provinces pointed out that despite local authorities and the Committee on Disaster Management team being supportive during the emergency response phase, they were not involved in planning or decision-making processes related to persons with disabilities, considering different types of disabilities, including individuals with physical limitations and</p>

The stakeholders in the consultation workshop also emphasized the need to enhance the capacity and skills of all involved in disaster management on gender equality and disability inclusion so that their needs and responses appropriately address the needs and vulnerabilities of the affected groups of people.

# Access to Safe Evacuation Places

Although efforts have been made to improve safe evacuation sites, concerns remain about the adequacy of facilities at these sites. For instance, in Kratie, only 1 out of 30 listed safe evacuation sites meet the national minimum requirements adopted from WFP, which include at least separate toilets for men and women, clean water storage, and sanitation facilities. Similarly, in Stung Treng, only 2 out of 120 sites meet these standards. These sites are often overcrowded, shared sleeping and cooking spaces, and lack of secure storage for valuables. Additionally, community members have reported that the evacuation sites are not adequately equipped with essential items such as tents, blankets, and food. In a focus group discussion in Kampong Speu province, participants mentioned that these sites were not well-equipped and did not provide adequate shelter or sanitation facilities.

Besides the physical facilities, a concern about being safe from other abuses was also pointed out by women who participated in the focus group discussion meetings. Women have reported incidents of physical and emotional violence against women and children in their communities which can worsen during their stay in the evacuation sites. These reports were consistent with findings from previous studies (see for example, Menchay [1956], Stang Teng [32%], Ratanakiri [26%), and Kratie [16%]). Ensuring inclusive, well-equipped evacuation sites and addressing gender-specific needs and safety concerns are critical for effective disaster response.

# Limited Access to Resources and Services

Vulnerable groups in Cambodia, including the poor, rural women, the elderly, persons with disabilities, and Indigenous communities, have limited capacity to prepare for disasters due to restricted access to resources and services. In drought-prone areas, people holding ID cards from and from risk households reported not having extra savings or assets that could be used as a buffer in case of disaster. Many families were also not able to save enough to survive by day 2. Both women and men in drought-prone communities noted that during the dry season, especially with increasing high temperatures and heat in early 2014, their crops, agricultural and livestock assets, and other livelihoods were severely affected. This led to a lack of food for these families and no savings for emergencies. They also experienced a lack of water for household use (cooking, washing, etc.), which was exacerbated by the lack of access to water storage facilities such as wells, pumps, and rainwater storage. Some families had to buy water for drinking and household use, exacerbating their vulnerability in these areas. In drought-prone areas, these already poor households also lacked access to resources for the poor, women, men with disabilities, and those with disabilities; these groups' capacities to better prepare and take early anticipatory actions to mitigate the disaster risks and impacts on them are limited.

The Cambodian government has expanded its social protection policy to include 'shock-responsive social protection' for at-risk households, aiming to build resilience and mitigate disaster impacts. For better preparation of a more inclusive shock-responsive social protection policy and program, there needs to be a

# Community-Led Early Warning and Disaster Risk Reduction Initiatives

## Women’s Champion Networks: Founded by ActionAid in four provinces, these networks promote women's leadership in disaster management and support communities in understanding and preparing for hazard risks.

### Disability Inclusive Climate Action Project: Implemented by Save the Children and partners, this project enhances the inclusion of persons with disabilities in climate actions and disaster risk reduction, ensuring they have access to early warning information and are more resilient.

## Areas for Improvement in Early Warning and Gender Inclusiveness

The following are areas where improvement in gender inclusiveness should be considered, which have been taken into account when preparing the Gender Action Plan (GAP).

### Enhancing GEDS Analysis:

- **Integrate Gender Equality, Disability, and Social Inclusion (GEDS) analysis into disaster risk assessment to better capture the needs of diverse groups.**
- **Improve platforms like PROM and Cambo to include disaggregated data and make them publicly available.**

## Improving Early Warning Dissemination:

- A comprehensive review and action plan for Emergency Warning (EW) systems should provide analysis and strategies to include inclusivity and address the digital gender gap.
- Use participatory methods, such as community mapping, focus groups, and community-based intervention, including outreach activities, targeting women or persons with disabilities who have no access to early warning information.
- Provide clear, actionable information tailored to different groups' needs.
- Transition from awareness campaigns should also specifically target women or ensure gender balance and youth participation.

## Preparedness and Early Action:

- Ensure evacuation sites meet minimum standards and are safe for all, particularly women and girls, and are designed inclusively.
- Train equity community members, including women and persons with disabilities, for early warning actions.
- Building evidence for shock-responsive social protection schemes for preparedness and anticipatory action in the target provinces, especially to include the poor, women, and persons with disabilities.

## Cross-Pillar Coordination:

- Create GEDS-informing guidelines and training for emergency warning systems.
- Increase representation and participation of vulnerable groups in preparedness and response activities.

# Gender Assessment for Fiji

In 2023 the estimated population of Fiji was 934,175**, of which 469,490 are male (50.14 %) and 464,885 are female (49.86%). As of 2023, approximately 43% of Fiji's population lives in rural areas.

Fiji has made strides toward gender equality, yet it faces persistent obstacles rooted in traditional social norms and patriarchal structures. Despite government policies, laws, and non-governmental organizations promoting gender equality in education and employment, challenges remain, including limited leadership representation, pay inequality, and gender-based violence. These structural barriers hinder progress towards achieving gender parity.

For instance, maternal health has improved, with the maternal mortality ratio decreasing from 49 per 100,000 live births in 2000 to 38 in 2020; a rate that falls below the regional average. Adolescent birth rates have also declined, though its births per 1,000 females aged 15–19 were still recorded in 2022. Educational access has improved with 74% of girls and 82% of boys completing lower secondary school, highlighting progress in youth education.

Despite these advances, economic participation gaps remain significant. In 2023, labour force participation was 35.1% for women compared to 69.5% for men. Women's unemployment rate is 7%, which is higher than the national average of 4%. Despite government efforts, additional women remain more likely to work in vulnerable employment, though this rate is lower than the regional average. Unpaid domestic work still disproportionately affects women and girls, with 60% of women spending over two hours per day on unpaid domestic work (GDV). This also remains a serious issue, with 52% of汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾汾

Vocational training programs in Fiji also cater to young people, especially those from rural or disadvantaged backgrounds. The Ministry of Education's focus on improving educational infrastructure in rural areas and expanding vocational training opportunities for girls aims to provide them with marketable skills. Additionally, initiatives like the Girls in STEM camp by Fiji National University and Government of Fiji aim to encourage girls' participation in science, technology, engineering, and mathematics (STEM) career talks[3]. These efforts aim to boost women's economic opportunities and address gender gaps in vocational and technical fields.

Employment and Labor Participation

Traditional roles are a key role for care family members and carry out domestic work. This includes caring for children, the elderly and family members with disabilities in the household. According to the World Bank, women in Fiji spend 2.9 times as much time on unpaid domestic and care work than men[4].

Fiji has a labor force participation rate of 57.6% for total labour force participation and employment for women aged 15 years or older. The female labor force participation rate was about 38%, while the male participation rate was about 39.1%, while male participation was around 77.3%. This difference reflects not only lower participation rates among women but also their concentration in informal, low-paid, and unpaid work in the formal sector[5]. Women hold paid jobs with a higher probability than men, with much of their employment concentrated in lower-paying sectors, adding to financial instability.

In its 2018 report, the Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women highlighted ongoing concerns about the low participation rate of women in Fiji's workforce. These concerns were reiterated in Fiji's 2019 Universal Periodic Review (UPR) and in a direct request from the Committee of Experts on the Application of the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEADAS). Key issues include low female labor force participation, gender pay gap[6].

The largest in the region—as well as the high concentration of women in low-paying, informal sector jobs, where they often lack access to maternity rights, legal protections, secure benefits, and representation. Additionally, women are more likely than men to be employed in agriculture, fishing, forestry, and fish farming (FF) became the second country to ratify ILO Convention C190 in 2020, aiming to address workplace violence and harassment. However, some women continue to face limited employment options, with some compelled to engage in sex work due to economic pressures and challenges with extending obligations**.

Efforts to improve women's economic empowerment are ongoing through the National Action Plan and ILO working groups, but they still target key barriers such as lack of access to markets, limited training opportunities, and restrictive cultural norms. Additionally, Fiji's Women's Economic Empowerment National Action Plan aims to create a more supportive environment for women's equal participation in the economy and reduce gender-based inequalities.

Rural women play a significant role in the economic survival of their families. This is especially relevant in Fiji where agriculture is the main source of income and employment in rural areas. According to the FAO Country Gender Equality Assessment (2018), women are more likely than men to be employed in agriculture, fishing, forestry, and fish farming. Women's limited access to land and credit is a key factor that constrains women's ability to cultivate commercial crops and engage in agribusiness.

Within agricultural households, women make decisions related to their domestic responsibilities and may share decisions about economic activities with their husbands/partners. However, due to patriarchal norms, men are more likely than women to be involved in decision-making processes regarding the community-based management of natural resources, even though they rely on these resources for their livelihoods and subsistence agriculture.

Women have full rights of inheritance and property ownership by law; local authorities often exclude them from decision-making processes on disposition of indigenous communal land. Limiting women's access to land is a central barrier to forming a formal business and puts pressure on them to marry. Women's dependency on men is a critical challenge**.


Roles and Responsibilities of Women in Fiji's Economic Sectors

Women in Fiji play integral roles across various sectors in both rural and urban settings, contributing significantly to the country's economy. Despite their significant contributions to household income and well-being, they are frequently concentrated in sectors that they lack high visibility or tend to be lower income and less productive. This section highlights women's roles in agriculture and food security as professional and technical fields, highlighting the challenges and opportunities they face**.

# Food Security and Nutrition

Women are central to food security and nutrition across Fiji. In rural areas, women actively cultivate food crops, contributing to the community's food supply. In both rural and urban households, women are typically responsible for buying, preparing, and ensuring the availability of nutritious foods for their families. This role is vital to the health and well-being of individuals and communities; as women ensure that family members have consistent access to essential nutrients.

## Market Vendors and Economic Participation

Women account for 80% of the approximately 3,000 market vendors across Fiji's thirteen municipal markets. These vendors contribute significantly to local economies, providing a vital link between agricultural producers and consumers. They also act as informal economic agents within their communities, enabling them to generate income, support their households, and participate in the local economy.

## Farming, Forestry, and Fisheries

Though women represent only about 45% of the workforce in farming, forestry, and fisheries, their involvement remains important. Women in these areas often engage in subsistence agriculture, fisheries, or small-scale farming to supplement household income and contribute to family food security.

## Tourism Sector

The tourism industry employs a considerable portion of the female workforce in Fiji, with women comprising at least 50% of this sector. However, while women are prevalent in tourism, they are underrepresented in higher-paying professional or managerial positions, with only about one-quarter holding such roles.

## Handicrafts and Artisan Value Chains

Women dominate the handicraft and artisan sector, where they often act as informal artisans within family units, or in small collectives. These groups are sometimes self-formed but are also facilitated by civil society organizations (CSOs), international networks, and government agencies. They produce traditional Fijian crafts, which women preserve cultural heritage while also generating income through tourism and local markets.

## Micro, Small, and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs)

Women are highly active in Fiji's informal economy, where most micro, small, and medium enterprises (MSMEs) are owned by women. These businesses are often needed by women, highlighting their entrepreneurial spirit and resilience. However, many of these women-owned businesses are concentrated in crowded, low-productivity areas like cantens, tailoring, beauty services, food processing, and second-hand clothing.

## Professional and Technical Fields

Fijian women have an established and growing presence in professional and technical fields, supported by their strong participation in educational institutions. Increasingly, women are moving into leadership roles within various professional associations, indicating that their presence in professional fields is likely to continue to grow.


---
**Page 217**
---

continue to rise. This trend reflects the impact of education and professional networks on women’s career growth and development.

### Mining Sector

Although women’s participation in the mining sector is minimal, their roles are often in skilled and technical positions. Women make up 12.4% of the workforce in mining, primarily holding scientific and technical roles such as geologists, engineers, and surveyors. The fact that women hold these traditionally male-dominated, skilled positions within mining demonstrates their capacity to succeed in traditionally male-dominated fields, though opportunities remain limited.

### Global Outsourcing Services

Fiji’s global outsourcing industry employs a substantial proportion of women, who make up approximately 65-70% of the workforce. This sector offers women employment opportunities that might not otherwise be locally available, contributing to both personal and national economic development.[1]

### Female Representation in Fiji’s Public Sector and Governance

Women in Fiji are progressively entering traditionally male-dominated fields such as the judiciary, law enforcement, and senior civil service positions. However, only 5 out of 55 parliamentary seats (9.1%) are held by women. Women also hold a small proportion of ministerial-level positions, with only one woman serving on boards. A recent study by the Fiji Women’s Rights Movement found that women comprise only 21% of board members (40 out of 191), with 30 out of 83 boards having less than 30% female participation.[2] While the female representation in parliament and senior civil service workforce was 38.6% as of 2016, representation in ministerial level positions was only 5.3% in 2026.[3]

### Gender Relevant Legal Framework and Institutional Arrangements

Fiji has made significant strides in promoting gender equality both through international commitments and national legislation. As a signatory to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), Fiji’s National Action Plan for Women, and the United Nations’ Sustainable Development Goals, Fiji demonstrates its commitment to gender equity. However, cultural norms and traditional practices continue to challenge the pace of progress.

### National Gender Policy (2014)

In 2014, Fiji adopted the National Gender Policy, establishing a framework to promote gender equality, equity, social justice, and sustainable development. The policy aims to improve the quality of life for both men and women by:

- Promoting gender mainstreaming across government agencies
- Addressing and removing gender inequalities
- Advancing sustainable development through gender equity

Through this policy, Fiji prioritizes the integration of gender perspectives in all government policies, programs, and projects to reduce gender-based discrimination and support social progress.[1]

**Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) and Committee Oversight**

As a CEDAW signatory, Fiji aligns its policies with global standards for gender equality. The CEDAW Committee monitors Fiji's progress and compliance, urging the government to enact measures that ensure women’s rights across all spheres, including education, employment, and personal safety. Fiji's engagement with the CEDAW Committee highlights its ongoing commitment to these international standards.

**National Action Plan to Prevent Violence Against All Women and Girls (VAWG) 2013-2028**

This five-year action plan, launched in 2013, represents a comprehensive approach to tackling violence against women and girls in Fiji. The plan includes:

- Strategies to prevent violence through education and advocacy
- Initiatives addressing social and cultural norms contributing to gender-based violence
- Partnerships with civil society, the private sector, and international organizations

The plan is critical to reducing violence and enhancing support for survivors, underscoring Fiji's dedication to safeguarding their rights and well-being of women and girls.[2]

**Family Law Act (2003)**

The Family Law Act modernized Fiji’s approach to family and domestic relations, introducing laws that promote the best interests of children within families. This includes:

- Rights regarding marriage, divorce, and custody that aim to protect both partners and children.
- Provisions to ensure that family law disputes are resolved fairly, with attention to the rights and needs of women and children.

The act is a step forward in addressing gender biases in family law and supporting equitable relationships within households.

**Employment Regulations Act (2007)**

The Employment Regulations Act introduced important provisions for workplace equality and protection, including:

- Ensuring fair treatment and protection from discrimination in employment
- Establishing standards for workplace safety, conditions, and benefits
- Protecting women’s rights in employment, especially regarding maternity and equal pay

This law supports women's economic empowerment by providing legal recourse for gender discrimination in the workplace.

**National Policy on Sexual Harassment in the Workplace (2007)**


---
**Page 219**
---

To combat workplace harassment, Fiji introduced the National Policy on Sexual Harassment, which includes:

- Clear guidelines for employers and employees to prevent harassment
- Procedures for reporting and addressing complaints of harassment
- Protective measures to foster respectful and safe work environments

This policy is a critical measure for gender equality, ensuring that women feel secure and valued in their workplaces.

Fiji has passed significant legislation and action plans to promote gender equality, protect women's rights, and empower women across all sectors of society. However, despite these advancements, cultural practices and longstanding social norms continue to pose challenges to achieving full gender equality. Nonetheless, Fiji’s legislative framework and policies have paved the way for continued progress towards gender equality, creating pathways for women to thrive in all aspects of life.

### Institutional arrangements

In Fiji, a diverse network of institutions works toward achieving gender equality and empowering women across all facets of society. This section examines key governmental bodies, such as the Ministry of Women, Children, and Social Protection; the Ministry of Health and Medical Services; the Ministry of Disaster Management and Ministry of Housing and Local Government which administer local councils, direct services, and drive policies and programs supporting women’s rights, economic empowerment and social welfare.

Additionally, non-governmental organizations, international agencies, and faith-based groups play crucial roles in addressing issues like gender-based violence, economic empowerment, and inclusive leadership. Fiji Organizations for Women (FOW) is a key player in this area, providing training, capacity building, advocacy, especially for marginalized communities. Faith-based groups, including the House of Sarah and Catholic Women’s League, further strengthen social networks for women through community engagement and support.

Together, these institutions form a comprehensive framework that promotes gender equity and helps build a more inclusive and resilient Fiji.

### Government Ministries and Local Governments

Fiji's governance structure includes four divisions (Central/Western/Northern/Eastern) serving a total of 14 provinces, and various local councils with distinct functions.

#### Ministry of Women, Children, and Social Protection

The Ministry has three departments: Department of Women, Department of Children, and Department of Social Welfare. Each department implements various services and programs to support families, protect children, and empower women.

- **Department of Women:** Focuses on gender equality through initiatives like:
  - REACH Project (Rights, Empowerment, and Education of Rights for Rural and Urban Fijians): A mobile service delivery project prioritizing outreach to remote communities.
  - Women’s Economic Empowerment National Action Plan (WEE NAP) 2021-2029: Supports national development through economic empowerment of women.
  - Women’s Plan of Action: Focuses on gender-based violence.


---
**Page 220**
---

Ministry of iTaukei Affairs

- Responsible for the welfare and governance of the iTaukei people, this Ministry oversees cultural mapping and integrates iTaukei development interests into national plans.
- Programs include: (i) community access roads, footpaths, footbridges; (ii) water programs; (iii) rural outer island programs; (iv) rural disaster insurance; (v) seafaring entrepreneurial assistance; (vi) sanitation programs.

Ministry for Rural Maritime Development and Disaster Management

The Ministry plays a critical role in working with other government agencies to better serve marginalised communities. Its five strategic priority areas are: (i) rural development (ii) maritime development and (iii) disaster management. Under its five strategic priority areas are capital programs which includes:

- Community access roads, footpaths, footbridges
- Self-help projects
- Water programs
- Rural outer island programs
- Rural disaster insurance
- Seafaring entrepreneurial assistance
- Sanitation programs

Projects under these capital programs have benefited women, children, youths, aging people and persons with disabilities.

Ministry for Housing and Local Government

The ministry oversees two portfolios: Department of housing and local government. The department of Local Government supports & monitors the functions of the municipalities and is responsible for overseeing the administration and regulations of the councils, as well as overseeing the functions of the National Fire Authority. This includes registering and licensing informal business vendors in the municipal markets and licensing of informal business sectors such as street hawkers (for example) and ensuring that they comply with all safety requirements and other local government requirements that address the safe keeping and protection of women and children within the market work space.

The National Fire Authority through their community awareness and education program are extending their training programs to build community capacity skills and knowledge by empowering able-bodied men and women as well as youth in fire prevention and fighting disasters. This information was gathered from the communities visited during the field interview.


Provincial Councils (supported by the Ministry of iTaukei Affairs): Empower and protect indigenous Fijians' land and resources.

Municipal Local Government: Local governance bodies that oversee urban areas under the Ministry of Local Government.

Rural Local Authorities: Manage governance in non-municipal rural areas, providing additional support and services to rural populations.


Non-Government Organisations (NGOs)

Soqosoqa Vakamarama i iTaukei


---
**Page 221**
---

Founded in 1924, this is one of Fiji’s oldest Indigenous Women’s Organizations, advocating for iTaukei women’s rights and representation at the provincial level.

**Fiji Women's Crisis Center (FWCC)**

Provides essential services to survivors of gender-based violence, with counseling, legal, and medical support, along with a 24/7 toll-free hotline.

**Fiji Women’s Rights Movement (JWFM)**

A feminist organization that champions women’s human rights, focusing on legislative reform to remove discrimination and promote gender equality.

**We Rise Coalition**

A coalition of feminist organizations working toward gender equality and inclusive rights for diverse gender identities, supporting transformative change through collaboration.

**Women's Fund Fiji**

Established in 2017, the Fund provides financial support and capacity-building for women’s organizations, especially those serving marginalized communities such as women with disabilities and LGBTIQ groups.

**FemLink Pacific**

A feminist media organization with programs like FEMTalks BFMF and Suitcase Radio that gives women a voice in media, disaster preparedness, and gender equality. International and Multilateral Organizations

**UN Women - Fiji Multi-Country Office**

Works with governments and civil society to design and implement laws, policies, and programs aimed at achieving gender equality, in line with global standards.

**UNDP Pacific Office – Fiji Multi-Country Office**

UNDP in MCO is committed to integrating gender equality and women empowerment into every facet of its programming. The Accelerator Lab has contributed significantly to the 2021-2022 Gender Equality Gold Seal. The accelerator lab has contributed immensely to finding innovative solutions to address the root causes of gender inequalities in the Pacific Islands. Furthermore, their work in Fiji has shown support and encouragement for women’s rights organizations. UNDP is committed to supporting the implementation of the 2021-2022 Gender Equality Strategy, partnering with Fiji Police Force to support survivors of gender-based violence through a multi-sectoral approach. This includes providing psychosocial support, strengthening access to justice for women that survive violence, using sports to promote gender equality by establishing non-discriminatory and teamwork values and analysing the laws and policies from a gender perspective.

**Secretariat of the Pacific Community (SPC)**

The Secretariat of the Pacific Community (SPC) has several initiatives to promote gender equality in the Pacific region, including: Pacific Platform for Action (a roadmap to achieve gender equality and improve the well-being of women and girls in the Pacific. It supports the commitments made by Pacific Island countries


---
**Page 222**
---

and territories to gender equality). Pacific Gender Statistics Dashboard (a tool that provides a comprehensive overview of gender-related indicators and metrics in the Pacific. The dashboard organizes data on women's economic empowerment, political representation, education, health, safety), Pacific Partnership to End Violence Against Women is a program that includes working with men and boys to change attitudes towards violence against women, and supporting national policies. The program also partners with young people to build a pool of trainers to deliver community-based interventions, SPC Gender Flagship (a program that charts the pathway for gender equality in the Pacific).

It was highlighted other gender-related initiatives, including: training authorized justices to issue Interim Protection Orders for survivors of domestic violence, breaking barriers for LGBTIQ human rights, International Women's Day and Micronesian Women's Conference.

International Women's Development Agency (IWDA)
IWDA is a feminist organization working towards resourcing women's rights organizations, promote systematic change (change that is systemic in nature), and gender equality. The use of data to drive transformative change and resource empowerment through research to develop tools, frameworks and policies. In Fiji, IWDA works in partnership with FeniLINKpacific and Fiji Women's Rights Movement (FWRM).

Faith-Based Organizations

House of Sarah (Association of Anglican Women)
Launched in 2005, House of Sarah provides counseling and support services for women affected by violence and builds coalitions within the Christian community.

Catholic Women's League, Fiji
Established in 1968, this organization has over 2,000 members across 36 parishes, promoting social and spiritual development for women.

Fiji Muslim Women's League
An organization affiliated with the National Council of Women in Fiji, addressing the needs of Muslim women, especially in areas like housing and youth support.

Methodist Women Fellowship in Fiji
Part of the World Federation of Methodist and Uniting Church Women, promoting leadership and development among Methodist women in Fiji.

Gender-Based Violence
Data from the 2016 census has some of the highest rates of violence against women in the world. It is deemed that 72% of women in Fiji (aged between 18-64) have experienced physical, emotional, or sexual violence by their intimate partner at least once during their lifetime. Additionally, 53% of women who have been married before the age of 15 years*. Director of Public Prosecution reported 94% rape survivors were women with 88% of cases involving girls under the age of 18 years**. Additionally, 71% of children directly or indirectly experience GBV, usually by male family members, 74% reported sexual violence cases of children under age


---
**Page 223**
---

of 18 years. The most common form of sexual violence in Fiji is child sexual abuse, primarily of girls and some boys under the age of 18 years.

The trend for women to be raped by men and girls in Fiji reveal in 2 or more-partnered women in Fiji have experienced physical and/or sexual violence from a male intimate partner in their lifetime. In ever-partnered women, 30% have experienced physical and/or sexual violence from an intimate partner at some point in their life. 1 in 5 women have experienced violence from a man who is not their partner, and 1 in 5 women have experienced sexual harassment in the workplace in the formal sector, noting rates are unknown in the informal sector.

People of diverse sexual orientations, gender identities, and sex characteristics (including lesbian, bisexual and transgender) experience higher rates of physical and/or sexual violence than heterosexual women. Women with disability are particularly at risk for experiencing physical, economic, sexual and emotional violence from family members, partners, caregivers and institutions (health, social services, etc.) with more rigorous research needed to understand the full extent of violence experiences.

Social Norms and Gender Roles

In Fiji, religious norms and cultural traditions, strong shape young women's roles, influencing their participation in decision-making and public life. These norms rooted in Fiji’s diverse ethnic traditions, often limit women’s autonomy, particularly in community settings, where traditional hierarchies shape how women express their needs and concerns.

Women's Participation in Public Consultations

While women in Fiji do participate in public consultations, their involvement is often constrained by cultural hierarchies that reinforce gendered power. They are able to give voice to their interests, preferences and needs, either directly through holding positions of power in the public and private sectors or indirectly through influencing and advocating through diverse individual and collective, formal and informal mechanisms and channels.

In I-Taukei communities, women typically voice concerns indirectly through community women’s groups or leaders, adhering to established protocols. In Indo-Fijian and settlement communities, similar structures exist but are more likely to be influenced by the patriarchal norms and expectations imposed by these social structures, which may limit their ability to openly and freely discuss issues affecting them.

Social Expectations and Empowerment

Fiji’s patriarchal society reinforces a perception of male dominance, especially within households, and expectations around gender roles remain strong. Women are often seen as caregivers and supporters, while men have more decision-making power in the household. These traditional views can vary significantly between rural and urban settings, with rural communities and older generations holding more traditional views. Younger Fijians, especially in urban areas, may be more open to shifting gender roles, though traditional beliefs continue to exert significant influence.

20


---
**Page 224**
---

Regional and Generational Differences

Gender expectations in Fiji vary widely based on ethnicity, region, and age. Rural areas and older generations tend to uphold traditional gender roles, while younger, urban Fijians are more likely to embrace progressive views on women's empowerment.

Social norms in Fiji both shape and restrict women's roles in public life, with traditional beliefs reinforcing male dominance and limiting women's participation. As urbanization and generational change introduce more progressive perspectives, Fijians continue to navigate the balance between tradition and the push for gender equality.


Bridging Fiji’s Digital Divide: Data Insights and Strategic Suggests for Women’s Inclusion

In Fiji, access to digital tools and information reveals notable gender disparities, especially among low-income communities and rural populations. Although 60% of Fijians were reported to have internet access in 2015, women lag behind men in terms of accessing and using technology on an equal footing with men. Digital adoption data is primarily general but available research suggests that men and women in Fiji do not experience technology access in the same way.


Globally, data from GSMA’s Women4Tech project shows that women in low-income communities are 21% less likely to own mobile phones than men, with many women reporting comfort with basic phone functions like making and receiving calls but far less confidence in using advanced features such as texting. Additionally, fewer women use mobile banking services compared to men.

Fiji’s women expressed strong interest in learning about more advanced features, like mobile banking, online bill payment, and e-commerce, which are increasingly important for economic empowerment in developing economies. However, technical literacy and familiarity with these features are often limited, especially in rural areas where access is less reliable due to factors such as gender norms, age, and geographic isolation can impact women’s access and comfort with technology.


Internet access is also uneven between urban and rural areas. A 2012 survey by the United Nations Capital Development Fund (UNCDF) found that while 45% of Fijians aged 15–74 possess a payment card linked to a current bank account, only 38% have internet access at home. The low adoption rate for digital transactions highlights several barriers, including limited e-commerce infrastructure and the cost of mobile devices in rural areas.

Fiji’s dispersed islands and rugged geography complicate the development of consistent network coverage and technology access, and approximately 20% of the population remains outside of the reach of 2G, 3G, or 4G networks. Digital transformation in these rural and remote areas lags significantly behind that of urban centers.


In terms of accessing information, Fijian women rely on multiple sources, including radio, television, SMS, social media, and newspapers. Women are more likely to use mobile phones for communication purposes, especially in rural areas where community leaders, such as village heads, chiefs, and church leaders, play crucial roles in distributing important information. Although men and women generally use similar information sources, there may be differences based on age, education level, ethnicity, or varying age groups—experience these sources differently. For instance, older women and


---
**Page 225**
---

those in more remote communities often depend on local leaders or word-of-mouth rather than digital platforms, which may be more accessible to younger or urban women.

Nevertheless, technology access for Fijian women encompasses a broad spectrum, including smartphones, basic mobile phones, tablets, and the Internet. Digital literacy is increasing worldwide and plays a pivotal role in empowering women's involvement in decision-making. As of 2023, an estimated 65% of Fijians (over 600,000 people) are Facebook users, underscoring the platform’s reach and influence. Social media platforms like Facebook provide a space for women to share information, connect with community initiatives, and participate in decision-making processes. Women in urban areas, particularly those who are younger and more educated, are more likely to engage with digital resources; whereas those in rural or lower socioeconomic backgrounds may lack similar access.

Significant challenges remain, however, in ensuring that women have equitable access to information, especially in remote communities such as the outer islands of Fiji. The outer islands in the Pacific Islands, with extensive submarine cables connecting it to neighbouring islands and major telecommunications hubs, are not covered by the United Nations Development Programme’s Human Development Index. In addition, approximately 50% of Fiji's rural population remains without mobile network coverage. As a result, remote communities often rely on alternative means of communication, such as community radio stations or word-of-mouth.

The Pacific Islands’ Women’s Needs Assessments (PONDA) in the Pacific have shown that these communities predominantly rely on interpersonal networks—such as village leaders, church heads, and women's groups—to receive warnings and critical information(10).

Given these challenges, engaging women effectively in digital communication strategies requires consulting with communities to understand their unique needs and preferences. Across the Pacific, communities tend to trust local women’s groups more than government or international agencies when it comes to receiving information. This reliance underscores the importance of using culturally appropriate channels and trusted messengers for disseminating critical information.

In Fiji, women are increasingly involved in disaster risk reduction (DRR) activities. Women play a vital role in how Fijian communities detect hazards, respond to emergencies, and share information, making these approaches indispensable for engaging women effectively.

Several strategies could enhance women’s access to information, particularly in the context of climate hazard preparedness. For example, community-based disaster risk reduction training, awareness programs, and consultations can play crucial roles in reducing women's rural and urban areas alike. For instance, local women-led organizations such as the Fiji Women’s Crisis Centre (FWCC) and the Fijian Women’s Development Association (FWD) can empower women to take proactive roles in their communities. Programs like FemLifePacific’s Young Women’s Leadership Team exemplify how youth-centered initiatives can amplify young women's voices and strengthen their leadership skills. These programs provide training, mentorship, networking opportunities for participating in community drills, and assembling essential supplies, all of which equip women better for preparing to respond to emergencies.

In sum, while Fiji is advancing in digital access and ICT development, there are still significant gender-based barriers, particularly in rural and remote communities. Addressing these disparities requires concerted efforts to integrate gender-responsive approaches in digital and information strategies. By promoting digital literacy among women, especially those living in remote areas, and ensuring their participation in emergency planning, Fiji can continue to bridge its digital divide and empower women across the nation.

Gender, Climate Change and Early Warning Systems and Disasters Response
