In Fiji, men, women, boys, and girls experience disasters differently even within the same household. Social roles, expectations, and vulnerabilities vary significantly by gender, affecting how each group prepares for, reacts to, and recovers from disasters.

Men are more likely to be employed in high-risk disaster zones such as fishing or logging, which exposes them to increased risks of injury or death. Women often have greater responsibilities within the household, including childcare and domestic duties, leaving them less time to prepare for disasters. Girls face additional vulnerabilities due to their age and lack of social capital.

Women also experience different roles in decision-making processes related to disaster preparedness and response. For example, women may be excluded from community meetings or decision-making forums that are held primarily by men. This can lead to a lack of representation and input for women's perspectives on disaster risk reduction strategies.

LGBTQ+ individuals and low-skilled migrant workers face increased vulnerability across all ethnicities in Fiji due to intersecting social and economic factors. Women often face differing risks during disasters, such as gender-based violence or displacement from their homes. These differences highlight the need for targeted interventions that address the unique needs of women and girls.

Underlying drivers of vulnerability include living in high-risk disaster zones, poor housing conditions, poor health status, and limited access to resources and information.

Social and cultural norms can influence women's access to information and their capacity to act on early warnings. While traditional knowledge and past experiences are valuable, they can also create a false sense of security as recent weather events become more extreme than in previous generations.

### Recommendations

Developing indicators related to women and climate change, particularly concerning natural hazards and emergencies, can provide actionable insights into gender-differentiated impacts. Empowering women through education and training on Early Warning Systems (EWS) is essential; these tools can benefit from gender-sensitive approaches, ensuring that women receive critical information and can respond effectively during crises.

### Opportunities for Women's Participation in Disaster Preparedness

There are opportunities to build capacity among Fiji women to contribute to the development and implementation of disaster preparedness plans. Women's groups, such as women's church groups, active in many villages, have become vital to civil society and community resilience. Originally formed to support women's spiritual and domestic growth, these groups have evolved to raise awareness of gender-specific issues such as domestic violence during disasters. By sharing information on early warning systems, they often face social pushback as they advocate for women's rights and broader community resilience.

These gender initiatives, built on existing structures, present an effective way to leverage local networks for disaster preparedness, thereby ensuring that gender dynamics are recognized and addressed in Fiji's approach to climate resilience.

### Field Work Findings Related to Gender-Specific Vulnerabilities and Needs in Hazard-Prone Communities of Viti Levu

The information collected is a result of interview with forty people living in hazard and disaster-prone communities using structured questions in a data collection tool. Respondents included an equal representation of men and women from nine hazard-prone communities along Viti Levu's Queens Highway. Nine communes are indigenous Takuak communities, and one is informal settlement with different ethnicities. The study found that women were more vulnerable to disasters such as landslides and flooding dams, near the coast and one in drought area. Fieldwork results reveal that gender plays a significant role in shaping access to information, roles in community response, and specific needs in


---
**Page 227**
---

# Leadership Roles

While men dominate leadership positions among gatekeepers and youth leaders in iTaukei communities, women in leadership are generally involved in community or business-oriented women's groups. This division suggests that gender impacts the types of leadership roles accessible, with men more likely to hold political power, safety responsibilities, and women focusing on social and economic aspects within female-oriented groups.

## Awareness and Access to Early Warnings

Gendered access to early warning varies, largely shaped by access to media and communication channels. Women in iTaukei communities have access to TVs, and radios—are well informed about early warnings, regardless of gender. However, aging men and women (particularly those over 70) and people with disabilities often rely on family members or community gatekeepers for information. This highlights the importance of ensuring that all community members are prepared given that it is always too much work putting up shutters and putting it down when the warning is not really serious. The iTaukei communities have a strong tradition of women's groups, which vary in their level of preparedness differently. In the mixed-ethnic settlement of Ikalai and Fijians of Indian descent, social organization varies by group. The iKalai community has a common structure with strong kinship ties organized around extended families. Women are involved in all aspects of life, including leadership roles; however, have a more individualistic approach, while men tend to form social groups, women often stay focused on family and household matters. In contrast, the Fijian Indian community is patriarchal, where most households have access to early warning information through devices like TVs, radios, and mobile phones, allowing them to respond quickly in need of shelter or evacuation centers when needed.

## Gender and Vulnerability-Specific Needs

Respondents unanimously agreed that early and advanced disaster response begins after the government's initial response. Women and girls' vulnerability focuses on essentials for household maintenance, health, and personal hygiene, including cooking food, food storage, water supply, sanitation, and hygiene. Men are more vulnerable to damage from natural disasters such as ropes, torches, and boats, which reflect traditional roles focused on protection and infrastructure. For advanced disaster response, women's vulnerability focuses on the gender-specific needs that often accompany physical development. Boys, meanwhile, primarily need clean clothing. Vulnerable groups, including elderly and disabled people, face challenges that require specialized training.

## Community Engagement and Participation

Women leaders and youth leaders note that vulnerable individuals, particularly the elderly, disabled, and isolated settlers, often depend on family or community members to receive critical information. Additionally, community members mention that the iTaukei communities are not as responsive to disasters which are usually held in urban areas and are typically accessible to all, particularly those who do not work outside the village. In settlements, social and cultural diversity can be a barrier to disaster preparedness focused campaigns to increase attendance and motivate participation across gender and ethnic lines.

## Training and Capacity Building

Community training initiatives aim to build capacity for hazard response and early warning systems. These trainings, organized by provincial offices, the Red Cross, the National Fire Authority, and other organizations, focus on community-based disaster risk reduction, emergency response, first aid, and small business empowerment for women. The training and capacity-building efforts in these communities are limited to those who can attend classes, which excludes people with disabilities and the elderly. Both men and women have access to training sessions, although participation is limited by age and gender. Women's groups often provide training on disaster preparedness among gatekeepers. Women in particular are engaged through initiatives like the South Pacific Business Development (SPBD), which focuses on empowering women in small businesses. However, training access remains uneven; while some youths, primarily males, receive specialized training (e.g., fire warden


---
**Page 228**
---

# Recommendations for Gender and Vulnerability Responsive Actions

## Key Points

1. **Include Training:** Ensure that training sessions include representatives from all community groups, with both men and women participating. Schools should also incorporate hazard awareness to reach children.
2. **Empower Community Committees:** Engage all registered community committees, including youth, women, health, and disaster committees, by equipping members with training on early warning systems and emergency preparedness roles.
3. **Accessible Evacuation Centres:** Improve evacuation centres to be accessible for the elderly, people with disabilities, and those with limited mobility under adverse weather conditions.
4. **Enhance Awareness Programs:** Use women's groups and local sports teams for awareness programs, tapping into popular activities for effective communication about emergency preparedness roles. Utilize mobile phones and radio disaster-prone areas to ensure timely warnings.
5. **Resource Stockpiling:** Establish community kits that include essential items such as clothing, blankets, and food at community halls for emergency relief, which would address the needs of women, children, and vulnerable groups.

## Inclusion

6. **Include Women in Disaster Committees:** Increase women's involvement in disaster committees like the Disaster Management Committee (DMSC), which are often male-dominated, by providing relevant training and mentoring to ensure that women have the skills needed for active roles in early warning and emergency response.
7. **Consistent Engagement:** Maintain regular programs and outreach for women and vulnerable groups to increase their awareness of disaster preparedness and response. This includes youth and women's awareness programs targeting young women and girls.
8. **Visible Leadership:** Appoint female leaders within the settlement or from religious groups to lead community committees, ensuring clear instructions on responding to hazards, maps of evacuation centres, and preparedness guidance.

## Based on the Note

Based on the note provided at the end of this file, it is also suggested:

1. **Partner with Religious Groups:** Use existing networks (e.g., Christian denominations, Muslim Women's Association, etc.) in Kailali to deliver community programs as residents align closely with these groups.
2. **Form Community Clusters within Religions:** Organize smaller clusters within these groups for better engagement and cohesion.
3. **Improve Local Representation:** Appoint leaders from within the settlement or from each religious group to better represent resident needs and liaise with the District Office.


---
**Page 229**
---

4. Conduct Programs at Religious Venues: Hold workshops and empowerment initiatives at religious sites, which are familiar and trusted by the community.

These findings and recommendations have been used to inform the Gender Action Plan for Fiji's EW4All proposal.

# Gender Assessment for Chad

While the second and most recent General Population and Housing Census of 2009 (RGPH2) counted Chad’s total population at 11,039,873, the World Bank estimated this population as 14,278,568 in 2023, with a 3.5% annual growth rate. The difference between these two figures is significant, with 1,200,000 refugees from Sudan, the Central African Republic and Nigeria, plus 244,000 returnees from Chad’s own refugee camps in Cameroon, Niger, and Libya.

The population density of Chad is 3.5 inhabitants per km², with a sex ratio at birth of 1.04:1 (e.g., 8,623,530 men [50.2%] for 8,556,210 women [49.8%]). The sex ratio at birth was 1.042 for the same year.

The average household size is 5.3 and the average age of the population is 17 years old.


According to the EDOSTA survey conducted in 2018, Chad’s poverty rate (15,503,179) is predominantly rural (76%). More than half (52%) of this working population in rural areas is made up of women. Their main activities are agriculture and livestock farming.

Women represent only 4% of the total workforce in Chad. Women’s participation in the labor force has increased from 30% to 48%, but they remain underrepresented in decision-making positions, especially at higher levels.


Chad's gender equality indices are the lowest in the world, in Africa and in the sub-region. In the UNDP Gender Development Index (GDI) 2023-24*, which compares the achievements of women and men in the UNGP indicators, Chad ranks last with a score of -15.8 out of 100. The GDI is based on three dimensions: education (with an index of 0.796), it is also ranked 145th out of 146 countries in the Global Gender Gap Report. In the UNDP Gender Empowerment Measure (GEM) 2023-24*, which measures women's participation and influence in education and political and economic participation, the country is ranked 183rd out of 196 countries, with an index of 0.671. The Gender Gap Global GGI* Index of the World Economic Forum (WEF, 2023), which takes into account women's representation in politics, education, labor force participation, health and survival, and political empowerment*, ranks Chad 149th out of 146 countries, with an index of 0.57.


According to the MICS 2019 survey based on the latest general population census (RGPH8), the disability rate for women is 3% compared with 2.5% in rural areas) than among men, at 3.0% in urban areas compared with 2.5% in rural areas). Disabled women are doubly marginalised, both because of their gender and their disability. They have fewer opportunities to access education, employment, and social inclusion.


Chad’s economy remains largely undiversified and heavily dominated by the primary sector, which accounted for 64.2% of GDP in 2011 (agriculture: 37%, livestock farming: 9%, forestry: 5%, fishing: 8%, hunting: 9% and mining for 2%). As for the secondary sector, whose activities are becoming increasingly diversified, its contribution to GDP was limited to 7.7% in 2011. The tertiary sector contributed 20.7% to GDP in 2011, with commerce and services being the main economic activities. Agriculture is still the most important activity, which is limited to food crops such as cereals, and cash crops such as cotton, sugar cane and gum arabic, is highly vulnerable due to climate change and desertification and also depends on the security situation. The private sector is made up of small and medium-sized enterprises, most of which operate in the informal economy.

The rural sector plays a crucial role in the Chad economy, contributing 42.5% of GDP in 2020[1]. Rural development therefore remains a government priority, with the aim of stimulating economic growth and reducing poverty in rural areas, while addressing cross-cutting issues such as adaptation to climate change and gender.

### Education, vocational training and literacy

Education in Chad shows significant disparities between men and women aged 15 to 24. According to ECOSOC data[2], among those aged 15 and over, it is higher among men than among women (57.6% compared with 28.1%), and more so in urban areas (66.0% compared with 39.5%). There are major disparities between NDjamena, the capital, and other urban centres (75.6% in N'Djamena compared to 48.1% in other urban centres). The literacy rate is also higher among men than women at all levels of education.

The literacy rate for Chad is 39%, which is below the regional average of 42%. It is slightly above the national level (Leak, Salamat, Bahar-Djama, Kamir, Barh El Gazal, Siala, Char-Bagirmi, Guéra, and Mayo-Kebbi). The literacy rate in Chad is higher than in Burkina Faso but lower than in Cameroon. It is also below the regional average of 42%. # Employment and participation in the labour market

Chadian women face significant obstacles to their economic participation, including discriminatory regulations and restrictive social norms. Although they often hold informal and agricultural jobs, they remain underrepresented in formal employment sectors.

In Chad, women's access to financial services is limited, with only 5% having a bank account and 11.8% accessing microfinance. The cost of the insurance for Microfinance Aim to improve access, but challenges remain, particularly in rural areas (ECOSIT).

In Chad, 60% of the population aged 15 and over is economically active (39.3% of women compared with 72.8% of men) with a ratio of 6:7 women for every 100 men (World Bank, 2018)[1]. Although women make up a majority in the agricultural sector, they are underrepresented in management positions and skilled jobs, which contributes to the persistence of gender pay gaps in other words, women earn less than men. This is due to various factors including education differentials between men and women by sector of activity. However, women are in the minority in skills and responsible jobs (i.e., 14% of senior managers, 10% of middle managers, 16% of skilled workers and 11% of employers). This is due to various factors including education differentials between men and women, relegating them to jobs that are less well paid and less valued than those of men (Doudoum and Djambou, 2018).

Estimates show annual income differs by gender. In 2017, a Chadian man earned an average of around $2,088, while a Chadian woman earned an average of $5,412. The agricultural sector employs the most women. Only 27% of working women work in another sector (FAO, 2021)[2].

In 2022, agriculture remains the main economic activity in rural areas, employing almost 69.3% of the working population aged 15 and over. Of these people employed in agricultural activities, 68.8% of them are in employment, but are often confined to tasks that are less well paid than those performed by men (60-64% of men). Access to land is a major challenge for rural women, with only 15% of owning land, compared with 53% of men.

Agriculture is a crucial economic pillar in Chad, contributing 23% of GDP in 2018 (PNA, 2021). Agricultural production in Chad involves both men and women, but the value of women's contributions is not fully recognized. In the agricultural sector, women account for a significant proportion of jobs to be done, but are more


---
**Page 233**

# Representation and participation in decision-making

Women are in the minority in almost all formal economic sectors. This situation is largely due to delays in school enrollment, early marriage, pregnancy, religious and other stereotypes. In addition to these causes, school wastage is another factor, often due to early pregnancy and early marriage. This imbalance in formal education can also be explained by the massive presence of women in the informal agricultural sector[1] in rural areas.

Women's involvement in rural development is marked by traditional production practices and a low level of product processing and conservation techniques, which limits added value. They spend an average of 63 hours a week on household chores compared to men’s 20 hours per week. Women are also underrepresented in decision-making spheres, accounting for just 15% of decision-makers. Female representation in village umbrella organizations is even lower at only 7%. This lack of female participation is often due to the definition of their interests. Decision-making processes take place at different levels, from national to local and involve a combination of formal and informal mechanisms.

At national level, women are mainly represented in three processes by civil society organizations, particularly umbrella organizations such as the Union Nationale des Associations Féminines (UNAF) or the Fédération des Associations Féminines (FAF). However, this participation is often hampered by a lack of accountability and dissemination of information. Rural women’s representation at these levels is also low. Women are often under-represented on provincial, departmental and local action committees, which reduces their ability to influence the choices made at these levels. This underrepresentation limits their access to resources and opportunities for development in rural areas. They are also excluded from natural resource management committees. Their role in sanitation management is also often secondary, focusing more on maintenance than planning.

Women are often excluded from land ownership and land management decision-making, which contributes to their economic marginalization. Land conflicts, fuelled by these demographic and economic pressures, threaten family farms and access to land for the most disadvantaged.

Despite these challenges, women’s organizations play a crucial role in promoting women's interests in the agricultural sector. They are key actors in advocating for greater representation, equitable representation and greater access to resources and economic opportunities. As agents of change, these organisations continue to defend women’s rights and work to improve their situation, helping to build a more equitable and sustainable society.

There have also been positive developments with the inclusion of a female component in bodies such as the Conseil national de concertation des producteurs ruraux via Tchad (CNCPRT). Nevertheless, this integration remains insufficient. The government has taken some steps towards promoting women’s participation but continues efforts to achieve gender-balanced representation in decision-making processes relating to agriculture and rural development.

Over the past few years, the government has implemented a number of policies to increase the participation of women in decision-making, with a clear increase compared to previous years, but still far from equaling that of men.

With regards to the rate of women holding seats at the national parliamentary level, it was only 12.8% in 2017, which represents a considerable improvement (2.4%) on national level, but places the country among the last in terms of gender equality[2].

[1] INAL, National Gender Profile of the Agriculture and Rural Development Sector, Chad
[2] INAL, National Gender Profile of the Agriculture and Rural Development Sector, Chad


---
**Page 234**
---

In 2018, Chad adopted an ordinance-law aimed at introducing parity in nominative and elective functions, with a 30% quota granted to women from the outset. However, gender inequalities persist in access to civil and political rights. The first female president of Chad, President Idriss Déby In 2021, the transitional government appointed on 2 May 2021 included only 9 women out of a total of 35 ministers (25.7%), 4 women out of state (22.3%), and women accounted for only 15% of the members of parliament designated as advisors to the National Transitional Council (CNT). There are no women on the Military Transition Council (CMT).

In the diplomatic and administrative sectors, the Ministry of Interior is the only one with 2 women out of 24 ambassadors, directors general, and secretaries-general. The Economy, Social and Cultural Affairs sector has 1 woman secretary-general out of 30 (3.69%). In the ministries, women hold 29% of department head posts, 14% of division head posts, 12% of director general posts and 13% of technical director posts.

According to data from the Direction de la Statistique, women are in the minority in the civil service, with only 16% of the total. The highest proportion of women is found among contract employees (46%), followed by middle management (22%), senior management (22%) and support staff (5%). In the public sector, women hold 30% of director general posts, 27% of division head posts, but only 18% are in senior management. The highest proportion of women in any grade is in middle management (Grade B), which is the grade for executive assistants. The astonishing discrepancy in the first two grades is due to the very low number of female directors general and division heads.

In the private sector, women hold 20% of director general posts, 15% of division head posts, but only 8% are in senior management. The support staff category, which requires few qualifications, is the only category where the gap between men and women is not significant (47%). Women are underrepresented at all levels of employment, especially when it comes to access to positions of responsibility. The Ministry of Women’s Affairs has 10% female directors general posts, with 48%, compared with 23% for the Ministry of Territorial Administration and 7% for the Ministry of Justice.

At territorial command level, only 2 out of 56 prefects (3.6%) and none out of 23 governors are women. In the 2011 communal elections, 5 women out of 41 were elected mayors (12%), and only 13% were municipal councillors. The Ministry of Territorial Administration has 7 female directors general posts, with 6 female notaries for 64 male notaries; there is only one female doctor specializing in urology, and only one female gynecologist out of 50. Women are underrepresented at all levels of employment, especially when it comes to the provinces, i.e., outside N'Djamena, which accounts for 95% of formal employment at national level. Lastly, 29% of the new government of the 35th Republic of 2 January 2024 (Republic of Chad 2024) is made up of women, compared with 17% in the previous government. The number of women ministers has increased to 11 and secretaries at state, includes 12 women. Despite progress in involving women in grassroots organizations and local councils, their representation remains low: only 35% of women participate in decision-making remains well below the 30% recommended by the Beijing Platform for Action.

Gender-based violence and social norms

In Chad, gender-based violence (GBV) is a major risk for women and girls, not only during conflicts but also in daily life. Women who are displaced persons and humanitarian workers has an impact on the living conditions of local populations in a context of limited natural resources. Moreover, in eastern Chad, GBV accounted for 55% of reports of human rights violations (Sollieff et al., 2010). Domestic or conjugal violence is one of the main causes of displacement among local populations, internally displaced persons and refugees (Chad Global Protection Cluster, 2012).

According to Chad’s latest multiple indicator demographic and health survey (MDHS-MICS), structural inequalities are reflected in the fact that women aged between 15 and 49 years old are more exposed to multiple forms of GBV; the most recurrent and particularly damaging of which are physical violence (29% of women), sexual violence (13.7% of women), physical or sexual violence (12.6% of women) and forced marriage (8.5% of women). The risk is even greater for young women: 40% of women aged between 15 and 19, child marriage before the age of 15 (28.5% of women compared with 4.4% of men), child marriage before the age of 18 (65.9% of women compared with 37.1% of men) and female genital

# Gender equality policies and legal framework

Chad has specific legislation on gender equality and combating gender-based violence, as well as national programmes and committees dedicated to these issues. Here is an exhaustive list of the main legislation, strategies and structures in place.

## Specific legislation

- **Constitution of the 5th Republic (2003):** This constitution reaffirms equal rights between the sexes, prohibiting all forms of discrimination (Articles 14 and 15) and encouraging the political participation of women.
- **Revision of the Penal Code (2017):** Includes provisions on sexual and domestic violence, child marriage and female genital mutilation, taking into account gender-specific issues.
- **Law n°03/2008:** Establishes a quota for women in public administration positions. Law also provides for a 30% quota for women's participation.
- **Law n°07/2009:** Prohibition of child marriage, protecting the rights of underage girls.
- **Law n°007/PN/2007:** Protection of the rights to education, training and socio-economic integration of people with disabilities, including women with disabilities.
- **Law n°13/2014:** Prohibits forced marriage, early marriage and domestic violence.
- **Law n°12/2018:** Ratification of the Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities, which includes provisions for gender equality.
- **Law n°12/2018 PN:** Governs legal aid and legal assistance, including victims of gender-based violence.
- **Law n°639/PN/95:** Adoption of the Declaration on the policy of integrating women into development.
- **Law n°04/2006 PN:** Labour Code guaranteeing gender equality in employment, with specific restrictions on night work for women.
- **Law n°17/2018 PN:** Creation of the Maison de la Femme, a centre dedicated to the promotion and protection of women's rights.
- **Ordinance n°0317/2018:** Institutes parity in nominative and elective functions, ratified by Law n°03/2008.
- **Order n°943/PN/2018:** Orientation of the Agroindustry Halallicum and Hallalistic System, involving women in the governance bodies of development policies and strategies.

## National programmes and strategies

- **National Gender Policy (PNG 2017-2021):** Drafted in 2021 and adopted in 2017; this policy accompanies the implementation of national action plan (PA-PNG 2013-2020) validated in December 2018, aims to promote gender equality and reduce physical and/or sexual violence against women and girls.


---
**Page 236**
---

* National Strategy to Combat Gender-Based Violence (Stratégie Nationale de Lutte Contre les Violences Basées Genre - SNVBG): Drafted in 2015 and revised in 2022, this strategy aims to eliminate all forms of violence against women.
* National Gender and Climate Change Strategy (SNGCCC 2024): Designed to reciprocally integrate the gender dimension into climate policies at national, provincial, and sectoral levels. This strategy strengthens the resilience of women in vulnerable situations.
* National Social Protection Strategy (SNPS 2016-2020): Aims to establish a comprehensive social protection system that is inclusive and sustainable.
* National Strategy for Inclusive Finance in Chad (SNMIT 2017-2030): Promotes the financial inclusion of women, youth, and vulnerable groups.
* Vision 2030 "The Chad we want": A global strategic framework that includes gender equality and social inclusion objectives in its various areas, in particular good governance and improving the quality of life for all.
* National Development Plan (NPD): The 2017-2021 NDP and the future 2022-2026 NDP highlight the importance of gender equality for Chad's economic and social development.

Committees and coordination structures

* National Gender Policy (NGP) Monitoring and Coordination Committee: This committee is responsible for monitoring and coordinating the NGP, in collaboration with the ministry responsible for gender promotion.
* Technical Commission for the National Strategy to Combat Gender-Based Violence or National GBV Coordination: Coordinates efforts to combat gender-based violence at national level. Its mission is to implement and evaluate the national strategy to combat GBV.

International commitments

Chad has ratified several international conventions aimed at promoting gender equality, including :

* The Charter of the United Nations (1945)
* The Universal Declaration of Human Rights (1948)
* The Non-Discrimination Convention (1975-1995)

Mexico City Conference (1975), which launched the "United Nations Decade for Women" by adopting a World Plan of Action focusing on equality, development and peace.

Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), ratified in 1996.

Resolution 48/104 (1993) on the declaration on the elimination of violence against women.

Resolution 52/125 (25 November 1997) on the International Day for the Elimination of Violence against Women.

United Nations Resolution 13/25 on Women, Peace and Security (2000) and the two resolutions that followed it: Resolution 18/16 adopted in 2003, Resolution 19/17 adopted in 2004, Resolution 20/30 adopted in 2005, Resolution 21/6 adopted in 2006, Resolution 22/6 adopted in 2007, Resolution 23/8 adopted in 2008, Resolution 24/9 adopted in 2009 and Resolution 27/18 adopted in 2020): Guide gender policies in conflict and post-conflict situations.


---
**Page 237**
---

* African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights (27 June 1981): Recommends guaranteeing women’s rights in Africa statutorily.
* Maputo Protocol (2003): Relating to women's rights in Africa.
* Solemn Declaration on Gender Equality in Africa (2004): Adopted by the African Union to promote gender equality.

Regulatory texts
- Decree no. 2015/PRS/MFPPPON/2017: Adoption of the National Gender Policy (PNG).
- Decree no. 2013/PRS/MFPPPON/2013: Declaration of October as National Chad Women's Week.
- Decree no. 943/PR/MFPPPON/2015: Declaration of October as National Chad Women's Week.
- Decree no. 1521/PJ/MFPPPON/2015: Implementing rules for law no. 007/PR/2007 on the protection of pregnant women and mothers.

Order no.106/PR/MFP/MMSSN/MFDP/2010: Creation and responsibilities of the national coordination for prevention, protection and response to gender-based violence

These laws and strategies demonstrate Chad's commitment to promoting gender equality and combating gender-based violence in all its forms. However, significant challenges remain in the effective application of these measures, particularly in rural areas and with regard to traditional and religious practices.

Gender-related institutional arrangements
The institutional framework relating to gender issues and the promotion of women’s rights in Chad is made up of several structures, including (i) national mechanisms or public institutions; (ii) specialized services responsible for the promotion of women or gender (gender focal points), the Observatoire de la Promotion des Égalités entre les Femmes et les Hommes (OPEF) and other public authorities; (iii) civil society organizations and technical and financial partners.

The Ministry for the Promotion of Gender Equality, Family and Children (Ministère de la Promotion des Femmes, de l'Enfance et de la Famille - MIPFFE) is a government ministry responsible for issues relating to the promotion of women and gender. Its mandate includes coordinating action on gender equality and equity, women's empowerment, advisory support, monitoring-evaluation and advocacy, as well as mobilizing resources to finance the implementation of national policies in this area.

This ministry is responsible for implementing the National Gender Policy (PNG) and its action plan (PNP), but it is not a focal point. The MIPFFE has little influence on the implementation of gender equality actions to improve women's living conditions rather than helping to change their position in society. This is reflected in the fact that there are no specific indicators for monitoring progress towards achieving gender equality.

There is no monitoring and evaluation unit within the ministry or in partnership with the sectorial ministries, nor does it have a budget line for this purpose (these are the ones who do not understand the gender concept! Final Report Analyse Genre Tchad, 2012/13)

The gender focal point, appointed by each ministry at the request of the ministry responsible for gender issues, is responsible for coordinating and supervising the implementation of national policies on gender equality and projects of their administration. However, these officials often do not have the necessary capacity to really influence decisions within their administrations. Their profile, their generally non-strategic position and their lack of resources are obstacles to their effectiveness in this role.

In addition, they do not always report to the structures responsible for gender issues in their administration, which further limits their action.

Gender focal points generate an awareness about gender equality on specific gender-related issues and do not have the necessary financial means or authority to fully fulfill their role. This limits their capacity to plan, coordinate and advocate for gender mainstreaming. In addition, high staff turnover among gender focal points hampers the continuity of


---
**Page 238**
---

initiatives and compromises communication channels. In addition, there is no mechanism for monitoring and evaluating the activities of these officials by the Ministry for Women or their own administration, which makes it difficult to ensure that they are fulfilling their responsibilities.

The lack of coordination between the different actors involved in gender equality and women's rights points are not even put in touch with the Ministry for Women, which hampers their integration and effectiveness in their role.


To overcome the lack of monitoring and evaluation, an Observatory for the Promotion of Gender Equality and Equal Opportunities was set up in July 2022 in accordance with the provisions of the PNG. The Observatory's mission is to monitor, control and evaluate the progress made in promoting gender equality and women's rights. It will be composed of a steering committee, which will be responsible for defining its objectives, analysis, studies and research on the gender situation at local, national and continental level. It reports to the Prime Minister and is chaired by the Ministry for Women. It is made up of a Steering Committee, an Executive Secretary, technical staff and ad hoc members. The Observatory's mission consists of State subsidies, donations and legacies.


The creation of the "Maison Nationale de la Femme" (National House of Women) in 2018 has been welcomed by women and officially recognized as a non-governmental organization for training, supervision and psychosocial care of victims of violence, which offers low-cost computer and sewing courses to young women and girls to help them reintegrate into society and the workplace, is an effective way of combating violence and discrimination against women.


There is also the Centre d'Etude et de Formation pour le Développement (CEFED), set up in 1968. This research institute has studies and references on the status of women in Chad, as well as a collection of texts concerning women's rights and gender equality. The CEFED's main objective is to promote women's rights; however, it often focuses on women's practical needs rather than promoting their strategic interests. They face challenges such as lack of financial resources, technical skills and coordination, as well as socio-cultural barriers. Despite this, they have been able to establish a network of 100 associations in Chad, which is mainly made up of NGOs and women's associations operating at national level (including women's groups and networks).


Technical and Financial Partners (TFP) support the government's efforts to promote women's rights; but they encounter difficulties with harmonisation and coordination, which sometimes leads to inefficiency and wasted resources. They also identify challenges such as the lack of gender-disaggregated data, the lack of up-to-date information on women's participation in decision-making processes.


Representation and participation of women in disaster risk management sectors


In Chad, women's participation in disaster risk management remains limited, despite their crucial role in community resilience. Specific data on their representation in this sector remains difficult to access due to a lack of institutional mechanisms for collecting such data.

For example, the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) has implemented projects aimed at improving the capacity of local authorities and civil servants involved in disaster risk management to better involve women, to climate risks. In addition, as part of several initiatives, training has been given to women both in the provinces and at central level, to raise their awareness of hydro-meteorological disasters and climate adaptation measures.


The representation of women in disaster risk management, meteorology, hydrology and natural resource management remains very low. According to recent estimates, women account for around 15% of civil servants involved in disaster risk management in Chad. For example, among the civil servants in the provincial and national departments involved in risk management, very few women hold positions of responsibility such as department directors or heads


Lake Chad ecosystem restoration and rehabilitation project
Community-based climate risk management project


---
**Page 239**
---

technical services. According to a study carried out as part of the SWED project, women account for 5% of civil servants at the Ministry of Territorial Administration (MAT) and Decentralisation (MATED), and 16% at the Ministry of Social Affairs, Family, Women's Rights and Humanitarian Affairs (MASAFAR) and both MASAFAR and MATEDM are headed by women ministers.

In provincial and local disaster management committees (CPA, CDA, CLA), women are often under-represented, representing less than 15% of members, and their role is often limited to administrative or financial tasks. The lack of female representation in these committees is partly due to the fact that women have science with male roles, hinder women's access to influential positions in technical fields, particularly meteorology and hydrology. This low representation is also due to persistent disparities in access to education and training opportunities for women, which can be addressed by a lack of awareness and support mechanisms to promote female leadership in these strategic sectors.

Despite these efforts, it is essential to pursue initiatives to increase the representation of women in disaster risk management in Chad.


Gender in early warning and disaster risk response

Faced with increasing risks from floods and droughts, Chad is striving to strengthen its early warning systems and improve preparedness, including through community-based EWS, Food Security and Early Warning Information System (SSESSAT) at national level and the Platform for Real-time Impact Situation Monitoring (PRISM), supported by the World Food Program (WFP). However, a major challenge lies in the lack of data disaggregated by gender and age. This is particularly true when it comes to early warning systems that do not take into account specific vulnerabilities of women, children, the elderly and people with disabilities in the context of climate-related disasters such as floods or droughts.

The PRISM platform, which uses satellite data, early warning systems and socio-economic assessments to prioritize aid, could become a more effective tool by integrating gender-disaggregated data and collaborating with national institutions such as the National Meteorological Agency (ANM), the Water Resources Directorate (DIREC) and the Ministry of Social Affairs. However, these institutions are not yet fully operational, but their systems often lack the capacity to capture and exploit information relating to gender and diversity.

For inclusive risk management, it is essential to strengthen the collection and analysis of data on the specific vulnerabilities of women, children, the elderly and people with disabilities in the context of climate-related disasters. This could be achieved by expanding the capacity of PRISM and national institutions to include gender-sensitive indices. Such an approach would not only improve understanding of the impacts of disasters but also ensure that aid is more effective for vulnerable groups, including women, children, the elderly and people with disabilities, while integrating national and local priorities into climate disaster risk management plans.


Vulnerabilities and findings from Consultations

During consultations with stakeholders in preparation for this project, several groups of people were identified as particularly vulnerable to disasters, including women, the elderly, people with disabilities, children, farmers, nomadic herders, as well as refugees and Internally Displaced Persons (IDPs). These groups are at greater risk due to their limited access to resources such as land, water and food security, as well as their potential exclusion from decision-making processes and efforts. Early warning and risk reduction must take account of these specific vulnerabilities if they are to be truly inclusive and effective.

Although assistance theoretically depends on the targeting of the most vulnerable by the local authorities (administrators and traditional leaders in practice), food and non-food items are still not being distributed to the


---
**Page 240**
---

most vulnerable, even though this category is the most in need. This observation is reinforced by the insignificant amount of assistance, which does not cover the 10% and needs which would therefore require better targeting. The exclusion of women from disaster risk management also means that many vulnerable groups had not received the planned assistance, to the detriment of others. This is the case for displaced people in Baidoa and certain women’s organisations in Addebo and Bulu. The exclusion of this category of people from formal and informal decision-making processes has a significant impact on disaster impacts.

Women and girls are often among the most vulnerable and exposed to disaster risks, due to their socio-economic status and traditional role in society and their limited access to information and resources. In rural communities, women are traditionally responsible for managing water, food and resources for their families. However, they have limited access to information on early warning systems or other means of finding these resources, exposing them to additional risks, particularly in terms of health and safety. In rural areas, they often have limited access to information and early warning systems, due to their limited exposure to the media and lack of access to mobile phones. This makes it difficult for women and girls to stay away from sources of information that are often the main means of disseminating warnings, such as community radio or television. Women’s voices are also often not represented in disaster risk management processes, which means that their specific needs are not always taken into account in the planning of interventions at local and provincial level, or even national level.

The elderly are another vulnerable group in times of disaster, due to their reduced mobility, increased dependency and isolation. In the event of a disaster, the elderly may find it difficult to evacuate quickly or move to safety if they live alone or with family members who have limited mobility themselves. They can also be isolated or left behind. Older people, particularly those living in remote areas, may have limited access to early warning systems and information in general, especially if this information is disseminated via modern channels such as mobile phones or television. The elderly are often dependent on family or community members for access to essential resources such as water and food. In the event of a disaster, these groups can be left without adequate support from their families if they relatives are themselves affected by the disaster and unable to provide the necessary assistance.

Children and young people are particularly at risk during disasters, due to their dependency and vulnerability to physical and psychological impacts. Children are especially vulnerable to the direct impacts of floods and droughts, such as waterborne diseases, malnutrition and water-related diseases, which are particularly widespread after floods. For example, in Nolisa, a village in the Karamoja region of Uganda, 10 children died from cholera during the rainy season this year. Droughts and floods can also disrupt access to education, as schools may be damaged, inaccessible or closed for long periods of time. This has a significant impact on young people, depriving these groups of essential education and increasing their vulnerability in the long term.

The national and local context lends itself to solidarity and stem or extended families rather than individual and nuclear families, which gives rise to the presence of several generations within the same household. This is particularly true for women who are often responsible for managing resources and providing for the response and support of vulnerable people in times of crisis at household and even community level. It is very common that women have to manage their households with limited or no access to a male family member. However, there are more and more exceptions in certain areas affected by armed conflict, the influx of refugees, the displacement of people and drug trafficking, which has a devastating effect on young people. In these cases, young people often leave their families and communities to seek work elsewhere. Households, are often forced to look for work to support their families, exposing them to dangerous environments such as mines or informal settlements in urban areas where they may be exposed to violence and prosperity. Young people used to migrate to Niger and Nigeria (in the Lac region), Libya (in the Kanem region) and Sudan (in the Guzdar region) in search of better opportunities. This trend is set to be reversed with the armed conflicts in these border countries.


---
**Page 241**
---

People with disabilities are often among the most excluded and exposed during disasters. They face physical, social and communication barriers that limit their ability to protect themselves and access warning systems. People with severe visual or hearing impairments may be particularly vulnerable to landslides and erosion. They may need special help to evacuate, but this is not always available, especially in rural areas where people are more isolated from services. The 2013 revision of the legal framework was deployed, in particular law no. 007 of 09 May 2007 on the protection of people with disabilities which is not being applied to subsidise transport for people with reduced mobility.

People with disabilities, particularly those with severe impairments (during or after disasters) have limited access to warning systems and are often unable to evacuate in time. Those who are adapted to their needs, they may benefit positively from early warning systems. During our interviews, people with disabilities and those living with HIV were keen to stress that sometimes they may be excluded by prepositioning and response plans at community level, as they are not always considered or involved in decision-making processes which can lead to solutions that do not meet their specific needs.

Farmers and Nomadic herders are particularly vulnerable to disasters. Their crops have been devastated on natural resources such as water, pasture and grazing lands, due to extreme weather hazards depending on the amount of rainfall, urban development, the vulnerability of ecosystems and the province's hydrographic network, low rainfall leads to drought, while excess rainfall can cause flooding and water erosion. All these hazards are increasing in frequency and intensity.

Farmers mainly grow rainfed crops (maize, sorghum, millet, etc.), market gardening in the ouads (Kanem and Ouaddai) and lowland polders (Lac), as well as off-season crops to a lesser extent. They are directly affected by droughts and floods which can lead to crop failure and loss of livestock. Droughts can also cause soil erosion causing economic losses and exacerbating food insecurity. Farmers often have limited access to forecasts, where they exist, and cannot plan ahead sufficiently to protect their crops or store food.

Nomadic livestock farmers, who depend on water and pasture resources for their herds, are exposed to increased risks of drought and floods which can lead to the loss of livestock and livelihoods, income and increased food insecurity. Very often, animals are exposed to bad weather, parasites and other vectors of disease such as ticks and flies. Farmers also face a lack of access to water features in some flood-prone areas. In the event of drought, they are exposed to a lack of water and grazing.

Movement is essential to the survival of nomadic herders, but in crisis situations they may be forced to move to areas with better water and grazing resources, leading to conflict with local communities living in these areas. Herders also face challenges accessing health services due to their mobility and limited access to remote areas with poor communications coverage. Their mobile lifestyle also makes regular access to weather information and early warning systems difficult.

Refugees and internally displaced people (IDPs), who are present in the Lac and Ouaddai provinces as a result of conflict, are particularly vulnerable groups to disasters. These people often live in precarious conditions, with limited access to safe shelter, drinking water and basic services. Climatic disasters exacerbate their already difficult living conditions by destroying or damaging their shelters, depleting their meager resources and delaying humanitarian assistance when roads are impassable during periods of heavy rain. Refugee camps are usually located on the outskirts of towns and cities in remote areas with limited communication networks or in isolated camps and do not necessarily speak the same language as local communities. Their access to weather information is often restricted, and warning dissemination mechanisms are not always adapted to their specific needs. For example, refugees mainly speak Hausa and English, while in Ouaddai, they speak Arabic and English.

# Gender and access to information and early warning technologies

Although 78% of Chadians live in rural areas, the country’s two main mobile operators, Airtel and Moov, have increased 2G coverage to 88% of the population by 2020[1]. By 2020, 48.6% and 21.8% of the Chadlean population had access to a mobile phone in urban areas and rural areas respectively. In terms of ownership of a telephone, the differences between the proportions of households with a radio are smaller between urban and rural areas.

According to (EGDSTA 2018), more than half of households (58.6%) own at least one mobile phone nationwide. By region, 85.6% of urban households compared with 50.5% of rural households. In the capital, 97.3% of households own at least one mobile phone, compared with an average of 75% in other urban centers. Almost three-quarters (74%) of households have a radio in urban areas and only half (51%) in rural areas. The provinces of Batoul-El-Gazal (Kanem), Karim (62%), and Lac (38.0%) have significant proportions, while Salamat (19.0%) and Goundam (17.0%) are the least equipped.

The penetration rate for mobile phones (Information and communication Technologies) goods breaks down as follows: radio (20.0%), television (5.0%) and computers (1.4%).

Households with televisions are generally those that have access to electricity from various sources, such as the SNE grid, solar panels or generators [generators]. In Chad as a whole, 5% of households own a television set; by area of residence, this proportion is 60.9% in N'Djamena, 8.7% in other urban centers (21 cities), and only 4.3% in rural areas and only 0.4% in rural areas. The average for urban areas as a whole is higher than the capital, N'Djamena.

Nationally, only 1.4% of households have at least one computer, 12.2% in N'Djamena, 5.8% in urban areas as a whole and 0.1% in rural areas. Unlike mobile phones, computer ownership seems to be reserved to some extent, for the most privileged.

Radio, a medium widely used to inform and communicate, is an asset that is used to a greater or lesser extent by household members. It is owned by 20% of households nationally. Compared with household ownership of a triophone, the differences between the proportions of households with a radio are smaller between urban areas (35%) and rural areas (18%). Among urban households classified as wealthy, 46.7% have a radio compared with only 9.1% in rural households. Among households classified as wealthy, 20.7% own a computer.

At 31 March 2020, it is estimated that more than 69-70% of the population[2] i.e., 12,577,109 out of approximately 18,800,000 people, own a mobile phone based on SIM card users. Meanwhile, the fixed-line penetration rate is low with only 1.683 subscribers or 0.518%, mainly public institutions. The number of social media users in Chad is estimated at approximately 24% (4,798,419 people) and its penetration rate, which stood at 18% of the population at the start of 2023. The EGDSTA has taken this into account in particular when developing approaches for disseminating early warnings.

In the provinces visited during the consultations, there are generally marked differences in access to information between men and women, particularly with regard to disaster monitoring, warning and management. These differences are related to factors such as education level (schooling), mobile telephony and internet, the area (urban or rural) and the standard of living (household income).

Men generally have more direct access to information in general; they are often more present in public spaces where information circulates more freely; they are more literate and they have more time and resources to access radio, television, mobile phones and the Internet (social networks, online media, etc.).

39


---
**Page 243**
---

On the other hand, women, particularly in rural areas, have limited access to these information channels because of their heavy involvement in domestic and agricultural tasks, which keep them away from the channels. This limits their ability to receive information about climate change, especially regarding the number of children she has and her ability to look after the household, to the detriment of her free time, unlike a man who can be occupied by his bravery and the assets he owns, including the number and influence of the members of his family.

In addition, women often report that they pay less attention to the information conveyed through these channels, either because the information is not perceived as directly relevant to them, or because it is less suited for their needs. Women also have limited access to information on important events such as weddings, births, etc.) and in collective settings, such as women's associations or groups, where they mainly obtain information through regular meetings of awareness-raising workshops organised by partners. These informal and formal meetings are also a source of information about natural disasters such as floods and blizzards, but this also means that women often receive information with a certain delay, compared with men.

Community radio stations are the main source of information for local people. Unlike the national radio and television stations of the Office National des Médias Audiovisuels (ONMAA), which are not picked up everywhere in rural areas, community radio stations enjoy greater coverage and the trust of local communities. They also play a key role in disseminating information on climate change and other major climate changes. According to the testimonies gathered, the inhabitants of these provinces have more confidence in their community radio station than in national media because they are perceived as more credible in terms of language and content. The issues addressed include climate change and natural disasters, although some topics are not really covered, unlike national television, which broadcast daily gossips and provincial weather bulletins.

Agricultural extension agents and other rural development services (ANDER, SODELAC, DSSAT) also play an important role in disseminating information to local producers. They regularly receive information bulletins on changes in climate conditions from their headquarters or through the internet and then relay this information to communities. Their role is crucial in popularising disaster risk management practices, particularly among women who are often excluded from these practices because of their limited access to the availability of information and the qualifications and involvement of these agents in the areas concerned. Community meetings, word of mouth and town cries are also traditional channels that are still used to share information on climate change and natural disasters. However, this type of communication is not very widespread as it is shared at these meetings through traditional or even religious leaders and chiefs. This process is slower, but it is still used in some communities, where information circulates well through these traditional social networks, whether formal or informal.

Increasingly, virtual platforms such as WhatsApp are being developed as modern social networks, enabling certain institutions, groups and associations to exchange and share information more generally and on climate change. However, their use remains limited because of the lack of access to mobile phones in particular in rural areas where network coverage is poor and access to the internet is technically and financially difficult for many people. This situation is exacerbated by the fact that women are particularly affected by this inaccessibility to new technologies, and their involvement in these platforms remains low compared with urban women and men respectively.

For example, during the discussion groups with the community of Bahou in the Lac, it was observed that only one woman had a mobile phone, which is a smartphone with internet connectivity, whereas the community's basic mobile phone owners are mainly men. This smartphone owner enjoys a certain notoriety as she is the only member of the community who is obliged to go to town for information and to communicate with the outside world.

Local communities in urban areas are not very well informed about weather forecasts, and those in rural areas are virtually unaware of them. Nevertheless, they express a greater need to improve information and


---
**Page 244**
---

communication channels for forecasting and managing natural disasters. Men and women, although having different needs in terms of access to information, share the desire to have more reliable information through more efficient communication between them.

Women, particularly those involved in groups and associations, express a strong need for training and awareness-raising adapted to their constraints and responsibilities. They want information channels that allow them to access information more easily, despite their multiple occupations. This includes community workshops, radio broadcasts, text messages on mobile phones, and simple audio messages on mobile phones that are useful even when they are busy with housework. Training women in communication groups is also a key channel for transmitting information on disaster risk management and disaster resilience.

As far as men are concerned, community radio stations continue to be the most desired source for monitoring and warning, as they are already widely used and perceived as reliable. However, there is a desire to improve early warning systems by integrating them with other communication channels such as SMS or voice telephone notifications, where mobile coverage allows it.

There is also a growing demand for real-time weather and climate information and tools that enable local communication, particularly farmers, to better anticipate periods of drought or flooding. Producers are also expressing the need to acquire work equipment and technological tools to improve their resilience (better production techniques, improved storage facilities). This would enable them to overcome crises linked to natural disasters more quickly as part of anticipatory action.

Despite these expressed wishes, a number of barriers stand in the way of improving access to information, particularly for women. The first barrier is poverty and unequal access to modern technologies. Many rural families cannot afford mobile phones or other appropriate communication work equipment. This makes it difficult to disseminate information via digital technological channels.

Low literacy, particularly among women, is another barrier, as many cannot read or interpret written or technical information. They also have limited access to appropriate communication strategies, such as the use of visual aids or simple audio messages in local languages.

The third barrier relates to access to energy, which is essential if local traditional channels are to be used, especially in rural areas where simply recharging a mobile phone can require a great deal of effort. Access to electricity is also important for using solar panels and other renewable energy sources such as wind generators, opening up a new market for renewable energies, particularly solar power, which is taking on significant proportions even in some of the most remote areas.

At community level, there is a lack of coordination between the various stakeholders (government, communities, NGOs, etc.), especially regarding community radio stations and extension workers, which can create gaps in the dissemination of coherent and reliable information on natural disasters. This results in fragmented communication strategies that do not always provide adequate coverage of risk information and early warnings, and they respond effectively to the needs of local communities, particularly those of women and vulnerable groups.

At national level, the country is facing a number of major challenges in terms of access to climate information and disaster risk management (DRM). These include a lack of integration of DRM issues into school curricula at both primary and secondary levels. The lack of educational content adapted to different school levels, combined with the absence of training for teachers on these topics, results in poor coverage of climate risks and disasters. This shortcoming is exacerbated by the lack of documents and teaching materials dedicated to DRM, despite the potential efforts of technical and Financial Partners (TFPs).


---
**Page 245**
---

Chad must also overcome the challenge of informing and raising awareness among the general public about climate change adaptation and DRR. Although initiatives such as the celebration of International DRR Day and communication campaigns have been initiated by UNDRR, they are still in their early stages due to a lack of coordination and resources. Awareness-raising programmes on climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction (DRR) are also limited in scope, especially among vulnerable groups such as women with low literacy or monolinguals, having limited access to the formal sources of information (radio, SMS, Internet) used to disseminate these messages. They often rely on oral transmission, which reduces their ability to access crucial information and knowledge.

Most awareness campaigns are conducted in French and Arabic, which could be a serious shortcoming for some of the most vulnerable groups who do not speak or understand either language. The campaign initiated by UNHCR, such as "Safe Cities," "Safe Schools" and "Safe Hospitals," remains limited, as does the production and dissemination of communication and information tools adapted through appropriate channels to reach these groups.

The lack of a national strategy for DRR has also hampered efforts in this area. Capacity building for journalists and national NGOs through specific training remains insufficiently developed, which hinders their ability to effectively communicate climate change adaptation messages to the public. However, although efforts have been made in recent years by the country, with the support of these TIFs, to raise awareness, train and inform local communities and national institutions about adaptation to climate change and DRR, there is still a need for further capacity building among journalists and NGOs.

The development and implementation of public policies to combat climate change and DRR concrete efforts are currently limited. The lack of a national strategy for DRR has also hampered efforts in this area. Capacity building for journalists and national NGOs through specific training remains insufficiently developed, which hinders their ability to effectively communicate climate change adaptation messages to the public.

The importance of integrating gender issues, although the number of women generally attending these meetings is very limited (10-30%).

Preparation for early action and response

In Chad, preparation for early action and response to climatic disasters such as floods and droughts are mainly coordinated by national and local institutions, including the Directorate General of Civil Protection (DGPC) and the National Committee for Civil Protection (CPCA), CDA). These structures play a key role in drawing up and implementing contingency plans and emergency preparedness strategies. Although, there is no specific plan addressing the needs of vulnerable groups.

Existing contingency plans do not sufficiently differentiate between the specific needs of vulnerable groups such as women with disabilities, the elderly or children. Disabled people's organisations and community representatives report a low level of involvement of women and vulnerable people in consultations on disaster risk reduction planning processes. Women and disabled people are often not taken into account, particularly for people with functional limitations, who face increased barriers in accessing emergency services and resources.

Disaster management efforts are managed by the DGPC, provincial and local action committees, and partners such as the UNDP (assessing and forecasting) and SSIARP (food security). These institutions are supported by international organisations such as the FAO, WFP and UNICEF to strengthen early warning systems and plan inclusive responses. However, greater efforts are needed to integrate the principles of gender and inclusion into these processes.

Discussions with stakeholders reveal an urgent need to build institutional capacity for gender equality and the inclusion of people with disabilities. This includes raising community awareness, training those involved in disaster risk reduction planning on disability issues, and ensuring their participation in the planning process. The development of contingency plans specific to the needs of vulnerable groups and their inclusive implementation are essential to ensure appropriate responses to climate shocks.

In the provinces visited during the consultations, anticipatory actions to deal with the risks of natural disasters such as floods and droughts are already in place in various forms, although they are often limited in scope


---
**Page 246**
---

and effectiveness. Local communities rely on ancestral knowledge to anticipate the risks of natural disasters, they observe natural indicators such as changes in animal behavior and vegetation, atmospheric signs (stars, winds), the appearance of clouds in the east or west, etc., which are often reliable predictors of impending weather events.

However, despite its effectiveness and reliability for communities, this knowledge, although valuable, is not sufficiently taken into account in modern forecasting processes and even less integrated into warning, preparedness and response systems, which represents a gap in risk management. For example, we were told that a heavy downpour was expected to fall on the village of X, but it did not rain at all because the weather and bad weather than the neighbouring dale built by the human community using modern materials and construction methods with local knowledge could improve the accuracy of weather forecasts, impact predictions and communications.

### Access to safe evacuation areas

In Chad, climatic disasters such as floods and droughts increase the vulnerability of women. Children, the elderly and people with disabilities are particularly in terms of access to safe evacuation sites. Although public buildings such as schools, health centers and community facilities are often used as temporary evacuation sites, these spaces are rarely adapted to meet the specific needs of vulnerable groups. Basic sanitation facilities (e.g., toilets) are not available at all times, and there is a lack of information on evacuation areas, which are often lacking, making life difficult for evacuees, particularly women and people with disabilities.

Overcrowding, lack of privacy and insecurity expose women and children to an increased risk of physical and psychological violence. Women and girls in particular face the risk of sexual exploitation and abuse; those of the elderly and disabled, are often not taken into account in the planning and management of these spaces. To respond to these shortcomings, it is essential to strengthen the capacity of evacuation sites to be inclusive and adapted to meet the specific needs of vulnerable groups, in order to reduce the risks and suffering during climate crises.

### Limited access to resources and services

In Chad, recurrent floods and droughts increase more vulnerable groups, including rural women, the elderly, people with disabilities, children, and those living in poverty and nomadic and transhumant indigenous peoples, to increased difficulties in preparing for climatic disasters. Women and vulnerable groups remain the most affected by disasters, due to aggravating social factors such as gender inequalities, poverty and illiteracy, which reduce their ability to withstand the impacts. In fact, for example, women are more likely than men to be displaced from their homes during floods because they do not have sufficient food stocks to cope with crises, which makes them particularly vulnerable to climatic disasters, especially in terms of access to resources and services.

These populations also face limited access to essential resources, such as drinking water, agricultural equipment, and the financial means to implement preventive measures. During periods of prolonged drought, water scarcity seriously affects agricultural and pastoral activities, reducing household income and limiting their ability to cope with climatic disasters. Many families have to buy water to meet their domestic needs, exacerbating their poverty and vulnerability.

The indigenous nomadic and transhumant groups have an economy based mainly on pastoral livestock farming. They face difficulties in accessing basic socio-economic services, as well as natural resources such as


---
**Page 247**
---

water and grazing land, especially as climate change disrupts their seasonal movements and makes them more vulnerable (NAP 2011). Women are often marginalized in access to and management of land, even though they are the primary users of pastoral resources. In many cases, women have no legal or de facto right to land ownership, often reserving this right for men. As a result, women depend mainly on family or marital relations for their livelihoods and access to resources. Women are particularly vulnerable in case of event of divorce or widowhood. Moreover, nomadic and transhumant communities are facing increasing restrictions on access to their traditional rangelands and pastoral resources. Demographic expansion, agricultural encroachment, and the conversion of grazing lands into cropland for cash crops, as well as transhumance lands are exacerbating tensions between farmers and herders. Conflicts over land frequently result in violence and displacement, which can have a devastating impact on women's livelihoods.

The lack of inclusive policies and targeted support mechanisms, such as social safety nets adapted to climate shocks, is another major challenge to mitigate the impacts of disasters. A thorough understanding of the specific needs of women, people with disabilities and other marginalised groups is essential to designing programmes that are effective in reducing disaster risk and vulnerability. The adoption of more inclusive approaches into disaster risk reduction strategies, Chad could strengthen the resilience of the most vulnerable communities to the growing impacts of climate change.

Recommendations to improve the inclusion of women and vulnerable groups:

The following recommendations are based on the recommendations from the community consultations, which will help to identify areas for improving the inclusion of women and vulnerable groups in the development of the Gender Action Plan for the V4444 initiative:
- Strengthen the participation of women and vulnerable groups: Introduce quotas in local committees directly created by project and organize targeted training to include their perspectives in risk management.
- Integrating local knowledge and disaggregated data. Collecting and using data disaggregated by gender, age and disability to plan appropriate interventions, combining traditional knowledge and modern methodologies.
- Include inclusive infrastructures in SOP, training and contingency plans: To this build adapted evacuation sites with specific facilities for women and vulnerable people (separate toilets, secure access).
- Promote equitable access to warnings: Develop appropriate alert systems (SMS, audio messages) in local languages, prioritising the needs of women and marginalised groups.
- Raising community awareness: Run campaigns on gender inequalities and the different impacts of disaster risk reduction strategies.
- Create feedback mechanisms: Put in place accessible systems to collect complaints and needs of women and vulnerable groups in order to strengthen the relevance of interventions regarding SEP.
