7 Gender Assessment for Ethiopia

As of July 1, 2024, the estimated population of Ethiopia is 132,059,767[1], of which 66,167,281 are male and 65,892,477 (50.1%) are female (49.89%). Ethiopia has a large rural population with 77% of the population living in rural areas[2]. Twenty-two percent (22%) of Ethiopian households are female-headed[3].

According to UN Women, people with disabilities in Ethiopia make up 9.24% of the country's overall population[4], which is higher among women with disabilities compared to men, regardless of where they live.[5]

Ethiopia has made strides toward gender equality but challenges persist, particularly in rural regions. Significant progress has been achieved in areas such as maternal health and gender-responsive policies. However, there are still significant gaps that need to be addressed. These gaps can severely affect women's rights and opportunities. Ethiopia is ranked 176th out of 305 countries on the UNDP Gender Inequality Index (GII) and 97th of 146 countries in the Global Gender Gap Report 2024, with a score of 0.70[6].

Literacy, Education and Vocational Training

Despite numerous efforts to improve access to education and training, Ethiopia continues to face a high literacy rate, with women disproportionately affected. The gender gap is particularly striking, as women's literacy rates are significantly lower than men's.

In Ethiopia, there are gender disparities in education, with more girls than boys dropping out of school due to early marriage and domestic responsibilities. Efforts are being made to close the gap, but rural areas continue to lag behind urban regions in terms of access to education and literacy programs for women.


Pre-primary education

According to the Educational Statistics Annual Abstract (EASA), the National Gross Enrollment Rate (GER) for pre-primary education in 2021 reached 36.4%, a sharp rise from just 9% in 2005. Despite this improvement, enrollment remains low nationwide. Addis Ababa leads with a GER of 93.8%, while other regions vary from 67.4% to Gandamoa to just 3.1% in Somali. Male enrollment continues to surpass female enrollment by about 12 percentage points for boys compared to 50.6% for girls. Nationally, the Net Enrolment of Ethiopia (NIE) is 98.4%, with a Future Projection of 97.8%.

[8] Gross Enrollment Rate (GER) refers to the enrollment at a specific age level or grade of education, irrespective of the student's age. It is generally calculated as the number of students enrolled in a given year divided by the total population aged 6-14 years old and multiplied by 100.

[9] The GER is an indicator that reflects the extent to which children are completing their educational level or cycle. This indicator reflects how effectively the education system covers its target demographics.

**Page 249**
---

### Primary and middle school education

Primary education in Ethiopia covers grades 1-8, with a national Full Gross Enrollment Rate (GER) of 92%. However, access is uneven, particularly in post-war regions like Somali and Afar, where the Gender Parity Index (GPI) is 0.76 and 0.83, respectively. Addis Ababa is the only region where female enrollment exceeds male enrollment. However, completion rates for grades 6-8 are low for both girls and boys in most regions.

### Secondary education

In Ethiopia, secondary education covers grades 9-12, but enrollment remains low—41% as of 2021/2022, compared to 75% in primary education. While this marks an improvement from 26% in 2016/17, access to secondary education is still limited for both girls and boys. However, girls continue to fall behind boys, with only 39.4% of girls enrolled in 2021, compared to 43.8% of boys.

### Tertiary education

According to the Ethiopian Education Strategy Council (EESC), of total students in 2020, women made up 35% of undergraduates, 27% of master's students, and 13% of PhD students in Ethiopian public universities. Though an improvement from 10 years ago, these numbers remain low, limiting women’s access to higher education. Women are also underrepresented in certain fields such as science, technology, engineering, and mathematics (STEM), with fields like engineering having only 28% female undergraduates and just 9% of the PhDs.

### Technical and Vocational training (TVET)

Female participation in TVET in Ethiopia has been comparable to that of males ranging between 51-54%, over the past decade. However, women are more concentrated in lower levels (1st and 2nd) and underrepresented in higher levels (3rd+). Enrolment is particularly low in regions like Afar, Gambella, Somali, and Harari, where access issues are more severe, especially for women. Many women lacked TVET centres or training facilities to enable them to pursue further education and gain employment.

Despite significant government investment and a rise in institutions from 458 in 2013 to over 1,600 in 2020, the quality of TVET remains low. In response, the Ethiopian Government launched a national strategy for TVET in 2020, a policy brief was announced to enhance TVET quality and address the unique needs of various groups, with a particular focus on supporting female participation.


---
**Page 250**
---

Education accessibility for people with Disabilities

The Ethiopian Government has made strides toward equal educational access for students with disabilities, but challenges remain: issues such as transportation difficulties, inaccessible school buildings, inadequate disability-friendly facilities, and insufficient learning materials persist. Consequently, enrollment rates are low; only 1% of children with disabilities attend primary schools compared to 90% for girls and 1.9% for boys. Across regions, enrollment rates for both sexes with disabilities are low, although boys tend to have slightly higher rates than girls. Addis Ababa shows better performance, with enrollment rates at 25%, but this is far from the pre-pandemic level.

Control of Assets and Digital Divide

In Ethiopia, women's control over assets, including land, remains limited despite progressive legal reforms promoting gender equality. While the Ethiopian Constitution and the Revised Family Code guarantee equal rights for men and women in property ownership, these laws are often restrictive when it comes to women's access and control over these resources. Women-headed households, particularly in rural areas, are more likely to face challenges in land registration and tenure security, which directly impacts their economic empowerment.

In 2019, the Ethiopian government reported that only 35% of women had bank accounts compared to men with 51% and 64% of women and men having access respectively (AFRO MARDIOTTE, 2024). Women's limited access to financial services is further compounded by their lower levels of mobile phone ownership or utilizing mobile devices and mobile internet services compared to men; 60% vs 65% as of 2019 (ETHIOPIA STATISTICAL BUREAU, 2023). The digital divide also affects women's access to credit. Some progress in rural phone ownership with approximately two-thirds of rural households owning a mobile phone has been recorded (International Food Policy Research Institute, 2023). Despite this, sex disparities remain significant; for instance, the proportion of men who own mobile phones is higher than women across regions such as Tigray, Oromia, Amhara and Southern Nations Nationalities and Peoples' Region (SNNPR) in per data as of 2019. The gender gap in mobile phone ownership is particularly pronounced in Addis Ababa, Tigray, Oromia and SNNP, with rates of male phones slightly lagging the Amhara region. This digital divide hinders women's ability to access critical information, financial services, and opportunities for entrepreneurship. Women's limited access to these resources, coupled with their lower levels of education and influence their socio-economic conditions—is constrained by social norms that often prioritize male decision-making in household matters (ETHIOPIA STATISTICAL BUREAU, 2023). The government's digital inclusion initiatives aim to enhance women's agency and access to resources; significant gaps persist in bridging these divides.

Employment and Labour Market Participation

The 2021 Ethiopia Labour & Migration survey reveals significant gender disparities in labour market participation, with only 56.8% of women involved compared to 72.6% of men. When employed, women are more likely than men to work part-time (43%) or as unpaid family workers (29%). Women also have limited access to benefits like pensions and insurance(10%). Additionally, a substantial portion of women (45.3%) engage in unpaid farm work compared to 30.4% of men. Women predominantly hold lower-level jobs with less secure employment status than men. The gender pay gap is significant; women earn wages typically requiring minimal skills, training, or education (35.2%). Unemployment rates also highlight gender inequality, with 17.6% of women versus 5% of men unemployed in 2021. The COVID-19 pandemic disproportionately impacted female-dominated sectors.

Representation and Participation in Decision Making

Women’s representation in politics and decision-making processes is improving, with recent efforts to ensure gender-balanced leadership in public offices. However, women still face cultural barriers that limit their participation at both local and national levels and significant leadership responsibilities continue to be dominated by men on both private and public sectors.

Meanwhile, progress in gender equality in Ethiopia’s government has been notable, with women achieving parity in ministerial appointments in 2018 (AGI 1.021). Women's representation in line ministries rose from 13% in 2015 to 50% by 2018, highlighted by the appointment of a woman as head of the Ministry of Health. However, women still hold only 36% of ministerial positions, a decline from 2018, with most now in less influential roles (AGI). Systemic barriers remain, with women holding just over one-third of seats in parliament and only about 29% of state ministerial positions held by women. Regional disparities are significant, with female representation as low as 7% in some areas. Addressing systemic barriers and enhancing women's leadership capacity continue to be areas crucial for fostering gender equality (AGI).

Gender-Based Violence and harmful practices

Ethiopia has implemented various laws and policies to combat sexual violence, domestic abuse, and harmful practices. The Criminal Code addresses crimes like rape and domestic violence, while the country has ratified international instruments such as the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), the Protocol on the African Charter on Human and People’s Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (Maputo Protocol) and Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action.

Despite these efforts, significant gaps remain in legal enforcement. For instance, the Revised Criminal Code does not recognize marital rape, and workplace harassment is inadequately addressed. Additionally, psychological violence in marital settings is not covered (AGI).

The 2016 Ethiopian Demographic and Health Survey (EDHS) found that 33% of women experienced physical violence by an intimate partner within the past year, with a higher prevalence among younger women (45%). The 2012 Humanitarian Needs overview indicated an increase in individuals receiving gender-based violence (GBV) support, ranging from 67% to 89%, and GBV services were reported as being overburdened. Additionally, 63% of women surveyed aged 15-49 believe it’s justifiable to be a victim for a woman violating social roles such as burning food, arguing with her husband, leaving the house without permission, inflicting physical violence on her husband, or refusing sex (AGI). The government has launched interventions to help women understand their rights. Violence in schools is another facet of GBV where children are victims. The 2016 EDHS found that 35% of students experienced sexual harassment at primary and secondary schools in nine regions of Ethiopia that found that 46% of students spoke of sexual


---
**Page 252**
---

# Gender-Based Violence (GBV)

In response, the government has made progress in working on a national policy for the prevention and response to Gender-Based Violence (GBV). This policy seeks to safeguard vulnerable groups and uphold their rights. To achieve this goal, the government will develop and implement various laws, strategies, and guidelines can be developed.

The current development stands at MoWASA working on developing a National Policy on Women's Empowerment (NP-WE) under the Gender Equality and Women Empowerment Policy rather than being endorsed as separate Policies. The timeline for the endorsement of the Gender Equality and Empowerment Policy is yet to be determined.

# Harmful Traditional Practices (HTPs)

Harmful Traditional Practices (HTPs) in Ethiopia mainly result from social, cultural and religious norms. Several national policies recognize Harmful Traditional Practices as detrimental to women's and girls' rights. The 1993 National Policy on Ethiopia Women aims to eliminate these practices, supported by the Federal Government of Ethiopia. In addition, the government has prioritized gender equality in its development efforts, introducing various laws, policies, and reforms such as the National Action Plan for Gender Equality.

Despite the government's commitment to end child marriage by 2015, the 2016 EDHS found that 40.3% of women aged 20-24 were married before 18, with 18.1% married before 15.

# Gender Policies and Legal Framework

Over the years, the government has prioritized gender equality in its development efforts, introducing various laws, policies, and reforms such as the National Action Plan for Gender Equality to support this goal. However, implementation remains a challenge, especially in remote areas due to deep-rooted social norms hinder progress.

Ethiopia has advanced to international and regional gender commitments through national laws and policies:

- **National Policy on Women (NPW):** In 1993, Ethiopia implemented the National Policy on Women (NPW). It promotes gender-sensitive structures, ensuring equal political, economic, and social rights for women. The NPW was replaced by the National Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment (NGEWE), which will eventually replace the NPW.

This updated policy is being developed through a collaborative process led by the Ministry of Women and Social Affairs.

* **National Action Plan for Gender Equality (NAP-GE):** Ethiopia’s NAP-GE serves as a blueprint for integrating gender considerations across sectors, fostering gender-sensitive planning and implementation. The Plan aligns with Ethiopia’s Ten-Year Development Plan and Growth and Transformation Plan (GTP), emphasizing the inclusion of women in economic and leadership roles.
* **Constitution (1994):** Guarantees gender equality, education, and affirmative action for women and girls.
* **Family Code (2006):** Establishes equality between men and women, raises the legal marriage age to 18 years old, and recognizes women’s rights in long-term partnerships. However, some regions (Afar, Somali) have not fully aligned.
* **Crimes Code (2005):** Criminalizes domestic violence, rape, FGM, abduction, and early marriage.
* **Other Legal Reforms:**
    * Prohibition of sexual harassment (2017)
    * Protection for overseas workers (2016)
    * Free maternal health care (2014)
    * Gender budgeting in financial administration (2016)
* **Ten-Year Development Plan:** Promotes women’s participation in education, leadership, and the economy.
* **Education Policies:** Aim to increase access for girls and boys, including free education up to grade 8. The government is also promoting gender equality in teacher recruitment.
* **Health Policies:** Focus on maternal and child health, expanding reproductive health services.
* **Economic Policies:** Support women’s entrepreneurship through microfinance and economic participation.
* **Disability Inclusion:** Ensures equal rights and employment for persons with disabilities, with a focus on women.
* **Growth and Transformation Plan 1 and II (GTP I aimed to mainstream women and youth agenda in all sectors of the economy. GTP II aims to further strengthen gender equality through rebalancing efforts to address challenges around economic and social benefits for women.**
* **Gender Equality Index:** The Gender Equality Index (GEI) is a composite index that measures progress in 2016 as part of broader reforms to ensure that national and local government budgets address gender disparities. GBE integrates gender considerations into the allocation of public resources, including budgeting for social protection programs. It also ensures targeted investments in programs and services that benefit women and marginalized groups.
* **International Commitments:** Ethiopia is a signatory to several international conventions promoting gender equality, including:
    * CEDAW (Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women)
    * Beijing Platform for Action
    * Maputo Protocol (African Charter on Human and People’s Rights on Women’s Rights) with reservations on marriage and inheritance
    * Maya Declaration on economic rights
    * Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) on gender equality

More specific to the context of disasters, Ethiopia has endorsed the Sendai and the Hyogo Frameworks both of which are applicable in disaster situations. These frameworks have addressed the importance of giving due recognition to the gender dimension of disasters and highlights on the need


---
**Page 254**
---

For a gender perspective to be integrated into all disaster risk management policies, plans and decision-making processes, including those related to risk assessment, early warning, information management, and education and training. Ethiopia monitors and reports on its compliance to the principles outlined in this framework. At a national level, the Strategic Investment Framework (SIPF) is a financial planning framework that will drive Ethiopia's Disaster Risk Management (DRM) system. It recognizes the differential impacts of disaster risk on women and men as well as gender-based risks and vulnerabilities to be identified as one of its objectives under disaster risk profile.

Ethiopia has aligned these commitments with its Ten-Year Development Plan (2021-2030), integrating gender equality across all sectors. Additionally, Ethiopia supports equal education rights through international frameworks such as the Convention on the Rights of the Child and the African Charter on the Rights and Welfare of the Child. These frameworks ensure free and equal access to education for boys and girls.

### Gender-Based Institutional Arrangements

Several institutions in Ethiopia work to promote gender equality and women's empowerment. The Ministry of Women and Social Affairs (MoWSA), established in 2014, leads these efforts, coordinating gender policies across various ministries through Gender Directors. These offices operate from the Federal Government level down to the local government level. Additionally, the National Women's League (NWL) oversees the National Action Plan on Gender Equality and advocates for women's rights.

The Annual Women's Conference brings together stakeholders from government, UN agencies, NGOs, and the private sector. It provides a platform for women parliamentarians to address gender priorities and integrate them into national policies.

Various alliances and working groups, such as those focused on ending child marriage, FGM, and violence against women, collaborate to improve the status of women and girls. The Gender Working Group (GWG) is one example that brings together government officials and civil society organizations focusing on aligning national gender initiatives with international frameworks like CEDAW and the Beijing Platform for Action. The Women's Development Agency (WDA) and professional women's associations play a crucial role in advocacy, training, and holding the government accountable for gender equality. Women's Associations and Cooperatives are also important actors that provide access to credit, financial literacy programs, and access to microfinance, enabling women to participate more actively in decision-making and disaster preparedness activities.

Community-Based Organizations (CBOs) play a crucial role in providing services to vulnerable groups such as internally displaced people, refugees, disabled persons, orphans, and other vulnerable women, including survivors of GBV, head-of-household households, and persons with disabilities.


---
**Page 255**
---

Income diversification. Women and girls also bear the burden of collecting firewood, often spending hundreds of hours annually, exacerbating deforestation and limiting their time for education or employment.

In addition, climate-related disasters increase health risks for women, particularly from waterborne diseases due to inadequate sanitation during floods. Traditional gender roles, coupled with the impact of disaster on household income generation activities, further exacerbate these challenges in Ethiopia. Despite these challenges, Ethiopia has made progress with policies like the Climate Resilient Green Economy (CREG) and the National Adaptation Program of Action (NAPA), which aim to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and increase carbon sequestration. However, women's involvement in decision-making remains limited due to persistent gender norms.

Community consultations undertaken within the framework of the EWAKI project design also attest to the fact that women are disproportionately impacted by disasters. The consultations conducted in Tigray, Oromia, Southern Nations, Nationalities, and Peoples' Region (SNNPR), and Amhara, consistently highlighted the vulnerability of women-headed households living in rural settings. Women in these rural settings face heightened vulnerability due to their traditional roles in household management and agriculture. In regions like Tigray, the impacts of climate change—manifested through erratic rainfall patterns, increased frequency of droughts, and floods—are particularly pronounced. These households often engage in income-generating activities, often without adequate support. Their dependence on natural resources for household energy and food production further exacerbates their susceptibility to climate-induced risks.

Climate change also disproportionately affects women-headed households. The economic insecurity faced by these households is intensified by limited access to financial services, land, agricultural inputs, and microfinance schemes. This lack of access makes it difficult for them to diversify income sources or reduce food insecurity. Platforms like the Emergency Coordination Center in Tigray offer some opportunities for gender-responsive early warning systems (EWWS) that can help mitigate these risks.

In Oromia and SNNPR, grassroots organizations like self-help groups (SHGs) and women's development networks provide some support to their members, but their reach and capacity remain constrained by socio-cultural barriers and resource limitations.

In relation to Gender-Responsive Early Warning Systems, across all the regions where consultations for the EWAKI project were planned, the importance of gender-responsive early warning systems was emphasized. Current systems often fail to consider the specific needs of women, persons with disabilities, and other marginalized groups in their design or implementation. For example, in Tigray, the destruction of community-based early warning networks due to conflict has left many women without critical information. Similarly, in Oromia, logistical challenges hinder the inclusion of women's perspectives.

Efforts to integrate gender-sensitive practices into early warning systems have been made in SNNP and Amhara. For example, the revision of woreda risk profiles that includes gender-specific indicators is an aspect of this effort. The Gender-Responsive Early Warning System (GREWS) development roadmap aims to address the unique vulnerabilities of women and marginalized groups by mainstreaming gender considerations into early warning systems. However, limited comprehensive data on gendered impacts of past disasters hampers the development of truly responsive systems.

Disaster response strategies in Ethiopia are gradually incorporating gender-sensitive frameworks. The Ethiopian Red Cross Society (ERCS), a key stakeholder, emphasizes gender diversity and inclusion in its operations, targeting women and children in 50% of its disaster response efforts. ERCS also conducts


---
**Page 256**
---

vulnerability and capacity assessments that include gender considerations, enabling the development of targeted interventions for women and marginalized communities.

However, challenges persist. In Amhara, ongoing conflicts have disrupted traditional response mechanisms, particularly those supporting women and the elderly among internally displaced persons (IDPs). In Hawzen Woreda of Tigray, the absence of reliable early warning and disaster response systems has left women and IDPs vulnerable to floods and landslides. This is attributed to the weakened collective resilience, community networks and limited women's access to resources for livelihoods and food security. The conflict also exacerbates gender-based violence and exploitation. The youth in Tigray also face challenges related to climate change, including reduced agricultural productivity due to land degradation and irregular rainfall. These barriers discourage agricultural activities and limit opportunities for income generation. Women and IDPs are more likely to pursue less sustainable livelihoods, which negatively impacts both men and women. In Oromia, political instability has led to the displacement of over 100,000 people in recent years. Women, including women without adequate support. Additionally, the settlement of some communities in rural locations is often driven by religious or cultural beliefs rather than economic opportunities. These beliefs however demand they stay on their lands because rivers overflowing is a symbol of a burden and prosperity. The government has not yet mapped out such settlements along river lines due to resource constraints.

In SNNP and Amhara, women, elderly individuals, and people with disabilities face particular challenges during recurring hazards like drought and floods. In SNNP, women's caregiving roles and social norms often prevent them from participating in disaster preparedness activities. Women also have high drought exposure, affecting 55% of women. Additionally, women in flood-prone areas are still unaware of early warning information, heightening their risk. Similarly, in Amhara, drought, floods, and irregular rainfall pose significant challenges for women and IDPs. Women face increased vulnerability, especially among nearly one million internally displaced persons (IDPs), who are largely composed of women, children, and the elderly.

Some of the opportunities for gender-responsive and inclusive systems include capacity building programs targeting women and marginalized groups, which can empower them to participate actively in disaster preparedness and response. This can include technical skills training for managing early warning information and integrating it into community-based disaster risk management strategies like self-help groups, women development associations, development agents and extension workers within communities. Women's participation is also crucial in the integration of early warning systems with flood forecasting and economic resilience. Integrating these with formal disaster management frameworks would ensure gender-responsive and inclusive early warning systems and disaster preparedness and response mechanisms.

Women's Representation and Participation in DRM Sectors

Women are disproportionately affected by disasters, and their involvement in disaster risk management (DRM) is increasing. While Ethiopia is working on gender mainstreaming in disaster preparedness and response, capacity-building efforts are still needed to integrate gender into all levels of disaster risk management.

In Ethiopia, women's status within the community has traditionally limited their ability to acquire the necessary skills or resources for them to have a significant effect on disaster risk reduction. Women's participation in community engagement, including disaster preparedness and mitigation plans and response and resilience processes. Women often bear the brunt of caregiving responsibilities, making them more vulnerable during disasters. However, women are increasingly being recognized as equal partners in DRM strategies. Despite this progress, there is still a need to ensure adequate representation, DRM strategies risk overlooking the unique needs and challenges that women face


---

---

during emergencies, such as access to reproductive health services or safe spaces for shelter. However,
some changes have been recorded where women participate in DRM. Institutional reforms have
enabled the participation of women in leadership positions in DRM undertakings through the
multidisciplinary nature of gender departments and through the general increase in the professional
level of project staff. In this effect Pillar lead by WAWAI project have highlighted the importance for
gender to be mainstreamed across the full cycle of DRM and response. While this is a good part,
there are still many challenges that need to be addressed, such as the lack of gender-sensitive data on
gender of the vulnerabilities of women within the overall DRM cycle. This is because gender
responsive planning requires a better understanding of the differentials impact of disasters and the
tailored response to address that.

Women's perspectives are not included in DRM data collection, compilation, and analysis at the kebele or
woreda levels. While there are some efforts in gender equity in data collection and several indicators that measure women's participation in decision-making processes, these do not include
gender-sensitive data in mitigation, action planning, or decision-making at the kebele or woreda levels.
This is further limited by the limited number and voice of women in community planning and
committees (USAD, 2018).

A comparative analysis of the location-specific consultancies for the EWAI/II project indicate that across
all regions, women's representation in leadership roles within DRM structures remains limited. In
Tigray, gender equality is not a priority as it is seen as a political issue rather than an issue related to
women's empowerment. Before the conflict, women in Tigray were active participants in development and
peace-building processes. However, the war has only disrupted these structures but also led to a
disproportionate burden on women, particularly as household heads managing economic and caregiving responsibilities.
The number of female-headed households increased significantly as a result of the war and they are left with
little capacity to engage in decision-making processes.

In Oromia, cultural norms continue to restrict women's participation in leadership roles. Women are often excluded from political and social activities due to their gender and are more likely to be
confined within the home than men. However, there are promising examples of grassroots initiatives,
such as women-led self-help groups (SHGs), which have started advocating for women's inclusion in local DRM committees.
These groups offer a platform for women to voice their concerns and participate in decision-making processes.
In Amhara region, women face significant resource limitations, including lack of access to transportation,
information, and financial resources. This limits their ability to participate fully in disaster risk reduction
efforts. In the case of the 2016 flood in Amhara, women were not adequately represented at the local level.
Amhara region demonstrates a mixed picture. While the regional DRM offices have attempted to integrate gender into their work and have conducted training on gender equality, women continue to be marginalized from decision-making processes.

In SNIP, the engagement of women in DRM structures is relatively stronger in urban areas, where
education and awareness levels are relatively higher. However, in rural areas, women's participation is
generally low as they are more likely to be engaged in agricultural activities or are generally poor and
vulnerable. Women living in these areas often lack access to information on disaster risk reduction,
especially during droughts or flood-prone or prone river zones. Women in some kebeles in SNIP are the
ones who are most vulnerable, as they have limited access to resources such as land, water, and food.
Lack of information women have been swept away by sudden and raging floods while working on their farms.
Cultural norms also play a significant role in limiting women's participation. For example, in droughts,
execute their vulnerability during droughts food shortages also causing malnutrition in children.

Nevertheless, challenges persist in advancing women to leadership roles within DRM sectors. Leadership, coordination, and technical training opportunities are more accessible to men, often leaving women underrepresented at decision-making tables. Limited access to communication tools, such as mobile phones, TV, and radios is the other significant challenge that contribute to the disproportionate representation of women.

The ERCS, for instance, has acknowledged this imbalance and emphasized the need for targeted initiatives to empower women through leadership programs and tailored disaster management training. However, these efforts are often hampered by a lack of resources and inadequate engagement with the environment and natural resources. This puts them at the forefront in disaster preparedness and response activities, which can be challenging given their limited access to information.

Although DRM policies in Ethiopia promote gender equality, real-world execution is inconsistent. This gap suggests that existing frameworks and protocols, while theoretically inclusive and show the annual inclusion of women in disaster management training, do not fully reflect the diverse cultural resource and cultural barriers. Thus, women's representation in decision-making roles, within the broader context of DRM, remains limited despite their critical role in early warning systems and response activities. By including women in community DRM teams, the EWEAWI initiative could tap into women’s central role in networking communities for the attainment of gender responsive early warning dissemination and disaster risk reduction.


Gender in Early Warning Systems and Disaster Response

Ethiopia's DRM policies have evolved over the years, with the inclusion of gender as an important aspect in national frameworks such as the 2013 National Policy and Strategy on Disaster Risk Management (NDPM). The policy acknowledges that women are disproportionately affected by disasters. While it acknowledges the importance of gender sensitivity, it lacks specific measures to address gender-based vulnerabilities and risks.

The policy recognizes gender as a cross-cutting issue, noting that women and other marginalized groups in rural areas face unique challenges during disaster response activities. It also states that the government is committed to addressing these issues and plans to "give special attention to women" and other vulnerable groups. Additionally, it states that disaster response efforts should be accessible for pregnant and lactating women, along with other marginalized groups.

Ethiopian policies emphasize the importance of gender-sensitive approaches to DRM. The government and various international organizations are working towards enhancing women's participation, especially in early warning systems and disaster risk reduction activities. However, there is still a need to ensure that women are actively involved in planning and implementing disaster preparedness and response measures. Although progress has been made in integrating gender into early warning systems, challenges remain, particularly for marginalized groups living in rural areas.

Efforts are underway to ensure that women have equal access to early warning systems, including the provision of information through various channels such as radio, television, and mobile phones. However, these efforts face several factors, including literacy barriers, limited access to technology, and socio-cultural norms. Many women, especially in rural areas, do not have access to mobile phones, radios, or other technological tools that disseminate early warning messages. This limits their ability to receive timely and accurate information about impending disasters.

# Challenges to women's participation in Early Warnings and DRM include:

- Limited access to education and training in DRM
- Cultural and social norms that restrict women’s roles in decision-making
- Lack of opportunities for women’s participation in leadership roles in DRM.
- Illiteracy
- Limited access to technology
- Limited access to financial services e.g. banks and credit

The status of women in early warning systems (EWS) across Ethiopia reveals significant gaps in representation, participation, and access to information. The consultation findings highlight a stark disparity between men and women’s roles in EWS. This is attributed largely to cultural, logistical, and structural barriers. While some progress has been made in specific regions, systemic challenges continue to undermine women's effective inclusion and leadership in early warning systems.

In Tigray with many women overwhelmed by caregiving responsibilities, and many other in displacement, their engagement in early warning activities has dwindled. For instance, in Hazzen Woreda, the EWS is not fully functional at the kebele level due to lack of trained personnel and informal channels or indigenous knowledge systems, which are inconsistent and often exclude their perspectives. Prior to the conflict, women were active participants in community-based early warning structures. However, since then, they have been marginalized from these structures as well as receiving information related to droughts and floods.

In Oromia, early warning dissemination is primarily carried out through government structures at the kebele level with limited involvement of WAWA; although it exists down to the woreda level, the structure has not been operational at kebele level until very recently where its functionality is being tested through piloting. It has its own initiatives on early warning dissemination at community level (Cultural and Social Mobilization). The role of women in these structures is limited as early warning information is shared. The consultations also highlighted that women in pastoralist communities, where mobile pastoralism is the norm, are not involved in early warning systems or in gender-dominated forums. While self-help groups in Oromia have started integrating early warning education into their activities, these efforts are yet to be fully resourced and resourced.

In SNNPR there are initiatives that seek to encourage and enhance women’s engagement in DRM with the support of governments. However, these networks are not as supportive as women's participation is limited because of cultural impositions. In rural areas, the lack of gender-sensitive communication tools—such as radios or mobile phones—limits access to early warning information. The integration of women into woreda-level disaster risk committees has shown potential; however, this is still in its infancy and there are different capacity building projects that are designed to empower women in DRM. But their effectiveness needs to be measured. Similarly, in Amhara, women's participation in early warning systems is limited due to cultural norms.

The Ethiopian Red Cross Society (ERCS) has made strides in incorporating women into community-based DRM teams, where their insights contribute to better resource allocation and community engagement. Women are also involved as key informants for disaster risk reduction, empowering individuals, and their involvement in disaster response and early warning can lead to quicker, more targeted responses. This not only improves the speed and effectiveness of disaster warnings but also builds a sense of ownership and trust among community members.

56


---
**Page 260**
---

Areas for Improvement in Early Warning, DRM and Gender Inclusiveness

Based on the information reviewed, and findings from consultations, the following general recommendations are made with regards to gender inclusion in EWS and DRM. To the extent possible, this will be taken into consideration in the EWWAI initiative based on its activities:

### Enhance Gender-Sensitive Strategies: Develop and implement tailored engagement and training programs for women and other vulnerable groups to ensure their active participation in disaster preparedness and response initiatives.

### Improve Community Engagement: Enhance the role of community organizations in implementing culturally appropriate communication and dissemination strategies, ensuring that all community members are informed about early warning systems. This includes engaging with local civil society, local governments, leaders, and women's organizations in the communities in the design and implementation of early warnings.

### Finalize Legal Frameworks: Advocate for the swift endorsement of the DRM Policy Enforcement Document and other accountability mechanisms across all sectors involved in gender-responsive disaster risk management.

### Policy Review and Reform: Government should review national DRM policies to ensure they are gender-responsive and align with international frameworks such as the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction, the Beijing Platform for Action, and other relevant conventions and regulations.

#### Gender Responsive Communication: Communication channels including media need to refine their approach by incorporating gender-specific needs and stakeholder feedback into their communication strategy. Any measure to disseminate information must include facilitating access to the technology that will be used to receive alerts and end-time updates. This will enhance the capacity to disseminate early warning messages in a manner that is accessible, relevant, and timely.

#### Improve Access to Technologies: Improve access to technologies such as mobile phones, for the most vulnerable and marginalized groups including women, elderly and persons with disabilities by providing subsidies. At the same time, invest in literacy and digital skills training to ensure comprehensive coverage of early warning systems will complement and enhance each other, empowering vulnerable communities more effectively.

#### Revitalize or Strengthen Community Structures: Rebuilding/strengthening women's development groups, women's organizations and civil society organizations is crucial to strengthening social cohesion, information flow, and economic resilience.

### Enhance Access to Financial Services: Providing credit, cash support, and micro-financing options can empower women and youth to re-enter in income-generating activities, from small businesses to livestock enterprises. This will also provide them with the financial resources they need as a better place to translate early warning information and take the necessary preparedness measures.

### Enhance Gender-Responsive Early Warning Systems: Strengthening early warning systems to reach all communities, especially vulnerable groups, is essential. This includes expanding the logistical support for mobile phone-based early warning systems in remote areas. The design of alerts, needs assessments and the communication of alerts must be clear and accessible language, including local languages and sign language. Consider using text messaging as well as the use of verbal messaging in parallel with the written based alerts to overcome literacy barriers.

57


---
**Page 261**
---

Support Mental Health Services: Addressing psychological trauma and social isolation is essential for long-term recovery, especially for women and youth facing displacement, loss of livelihoods, and increased responsibilities.

**Promote Gender-Specific Data Collection:** Assessment of the needs of women, developing gender-specific indicators and reporting mechanisms within early warning systems will provide insights into the distinct vulnerabilities faced by women and girls. Similarly, gender-disaggregated data is crucial for effective planning, response and DRR capacity development.

**Foster Collaboration with Civil Society:** Tap into existing local and organizational capacities to build upon strengths, share information, understand local dynamics and vulnerabilities to hazards to build trust among communities and facilitate ownership of early warning and DRM processes.

**Strengthen Community Feedback Mechanisms:** Creating platforms for community feedback on disaster risk reduction can help authorities adjust their strategies based on real-time challenges and needs, promoting more inclusive and responsive policies.

**Capacity Building for Local Organizations:** Supporting women's associations, disability organizations, and youth groups with training and resources will increase their ability to contribute meaningfully to disaster risk reduction. Empowering women in decision-making roles within their communities, such as heads of households, or those actors who want to participate actively in the development of project-related activities should be empowered.

**Capacity Building for Government Institutions:** Government staff must be provided with targeted capacity building on gender equality and inclusion. Gender-sensitive policies that consider gender and inclusive considerations in every aspect of EWS. Government should prioritize gender-sensitive training, inclusive decision making and strengthening gender equality in leadership.

**Adequate Funding for Gender-Responsive Disaster Management:** Government with the support of its partners must ensure dedicated funding to implement initiatives that promote disaster risk reduction initiatives, including training, research, and program implementation that target both women and men equally.

**Monitoring and Accountability:** Government with the support of its partners need to implement robust mechanisms to track and ensure that allocated funds are being used effectively to promote gender equality in disaster management. Additionally, it is important to ensure the following are taken into account:

- Training and awareness workshops on interpreting early warnings and response measures should be adapted to the needs and available time of women for their participation.
- Comprehensive Early Warning Systems (EWS) systems should provide analysis and strategies to include inclusivity and to address the digital gender gap.
- Provision of clear and actionable information tailored to different groups' needs.

8 Gender Assessment for Antigua and Barbuda

The estimated population of Antigua &Barbuda as of 2014 is 102,634[1], of which 48,111 people are male (47.07%) and 54,523 are female (52.93%). Approximately 70% of Antigua and Barbuda’s population lives in rural areas and 97% lives in the island of Antigua; 60% of its population resides along the coast.

Antigua and Barbuda has made significant strides over the past decades on issues affecting women and girls, including gender equality, particularly in education and health, where women outperform men. Both girls and boys in Antigua and Barbuda have a very high completion rate of lower secondary school[2]. The proportion of women who are employed is 58%, which is higher than the regional average in Antigua and Barbuda has significantly improved, decreasing from 5% in 2000 to 21 in 2010. This rate is notably lower than the regional average, which is 68%.

However, challenges persist, including the prevalence of gender-based violence (GBV)[3] and a significant proportion of women living below the poverty line[4], which reflects underlying economic vulnerabilities. In fact, approximately half of the households in Antigua and Barbuda are led by women[5]. Women also represent 60% of the informal sector workers, including women with disabilities[6]. This reflects a significant demographic where women often assume the role of primary household managers, especially in single-parent families, which are common in the country. The economic vulnerability of female-headed households is further exacerbated when they face accessing economic opportunities; can therefore increase the vulnerability of female-headed households to poverty and food insecurity[7].

The 2014 CARRIACOM Gender Assessment indicated that approximately 6% of households were affected by Hurricane Irma, in which it was estimated that at least half of the affected households were women, who are more likely than men to head single-parent households and may have experienced significant damage due to their particular circumstances[8].

In addition to these challenges, women continue to face inequalities in wages and access to economic opportunities, signaling the need for sustained efforts to address these gaps[9]. These issues are further compounded by the fact that women are disproportionately affected during natural disasters such as the dismissal of pregnant workers. While specific data on time use is missing, unpaid caregiving responsibilities are largely borne by women[10]. Furthermore, a simple decline in Antigua and Barbuda’s adolescent fertility rate (AFR) was 23.2% in 2014 compared to more than the average of 15-20 high-income countries[11].

Turning to partial parity, the 5.6% of males in Antigua and Barbuda are partially married to women in 2011, which is higher than the national average from 11.2% in 2009. The current rate is lower than the averages for high-income economies and the lowest in the country since 2004, when it was 10.5%.

An additional aspect that demands attention is the lack of gender-related data in several key areas, which hampers efforts to bridge the gaps between men and women, as well as boys and girls. For instance, the breakdown by sex of people with disabilities as well as its disability prevalence itself is

### Literary, Educational and Vocational Training

Education plays a crucial role in shaping the gender landscape in Antigua and Barbuda. The country has achieved significant progress in terms of gender parity in education, with both boys and girls having relatively equal access to primary and secondary education. However, disparities exist at higher levels.

In Antigua and Barbuda, the literacy rate is approximately 95%. This high literacy rate applies to both men and women. While there are no data on a scarce basis for tertiary education (ages 18–24) or above), highlights the country's success in providing broad access to basic education<sup>1</sup>. However, literacy gaps between males and females remain at lower levels of education. Despite these educational opportunities, might still exist, though lack of available data makes difficult to ascertain this.

In Antigua and Barbuda, education enrollment data shows distinct trends across the different levels. For primary education (ages 5–12), enrollment rates for both males and females are fairly balanced. In the 2017/2018 school year, 49% of students were male while 51% were female. At secondary level (ages 13–18), the distribution was more gender-balanced with 46% of students being male and 54% female. At tertiary school population, while females represented 49%, indicating balanced participation between the two sexes<sup>2</sup>. To further support this balance, the government has implemented measures to eliminate gender stereotypes in education. Courses that were historically considered as male-dominated, such as Technical Drawing, have now been included in the curriculum of national schools.

When looking at tertiary education, Antigua and Barbuda shows a trend where females outnumber males. The gender parity index (GPI) for tertiary enrollment—measuring the ratio of females to males—is has been above 1, indicating more females are enrolled in higher education institutions compared to males<sup>3</sup>. This is attributed to the country's emphasis on providing equal access to education. In this context, women and girls have benefited from tertiary education access through impartial scholarships, leading to more women receiving financial support despite the absence of affirmative action. Antigua and Barbuda has also prioritized gender equality in its national development plans, as exemplified by The Hilda Foundation's campaign, which underscores the importance of

### Employment and Labor Participation

While women excel in higher education, enrolling and graduating at higher rates than men, this has not translated to greater economic opportunities or leadership roles. Thus, despite progress in education, labour market participation in Antigua and Barbuda reflects clear gender disparities.

Women make up 48% of the workforce but remain underrepresented in high-paying and leadership positions.


#### The 2018 Labor Force Survey

The 2018 Labor Force Survey revealed that women's participation in the labor market is lower than men's (68.9% compared to 76.1%), with the gap widening during reproductive years due to the greater burden of childcare and household responsibilities.

Women also have a higher unemployment rate for both sexes, but this is more pronounced among women: 50.5% versus 42%. The higher unemployment rate for women suggests that a larger share of women within the workforce were unemployed and actively looking for work in 2018; the unemployment rate for women stood at 9.3%, whereas it was only 6.7% for men.

Similarly, the labor force participation rate for young women was higher than for young men. Around 52.8% of males aged 15 to 24 were either working or seeking employment, compared to 45.0% of females in the same age group. This suggests that young women are more likely to be out of work and facing greater unemployment rates.

The survey also indicates that young men had a greater participation rate; young women faced a higher unemployment rate, with 30.5% unemployed compared to 23.6% of young men. The same survey indicates that senior men were more likely than senior women to be in the labor force: 41.8% versus 31.9%. Women aged 65 and older were still engaged in the workforce, with only 10% of women, aged 65 and older, being still employed.

The impact of COVID-19 on women's employment has been particularly significant. Recent labor force data for Antigua and Barbuda show a decline in female employment, highlighting their vulnerability to the closures of hotels and related businesses during the pandemic, potentially also during national disasters. Additionally, when accounting for the broader effects of the downturn on linked sectors, it is estimated that women are more likely than men to be negatively affected by this decline.

This disproportionate impact on women is particularly concerning given that many women in Antigua and Barbuda are the primary breadwinners or sole providers within their households, often bearing the responsibility of supporting larger families compared to men. As a result, any significant drop in employment due to the effects of COVID-19 can have lasting repercussions, resulting in ongoing challenges for the well-being of the children in their care.


#### Gender-Based Violence (GBV)

According to UN Women, nearly one in three women in the Caribbean faces intimate partner violence or sexual violence at some point in their lives. A regional analysis of Women's Health Surveys


---
**Page 265**
---

conducted between 2016 and 2019 in five Caribbean Community (CARICOM) Member States— Grenada, Guyana, Jamaica, Suriname, and Trinidad and Tobago—revealed that men-partnered women aged 15–49 in relationships with men exhibiting attitudes and behaviors that reinforce male dominance and perpetuate gender inequality were more likely to experience both lifetime and current intimate partner violence.

In Antigua and Barbuda, Gender-Based Violence (GBV) disproportionately affects women and girls, reflecting broader regional trends. While there is no evidence of an increase in gender violence post- pandemic, the issue remains a significant concern. National surveys and reports from Non-Governmental Organizations reveal that domestic violence, sexual harassment, and sexual assault are common, though often underreported due to stigma and fear.

The 2021 Antigua and Barbuda Gender-Based Violence (GBV) Prevalence Survey collected statistics from 2021, which indicate that 16% of women (n=379) reported experiencing intimate partner violence in the past year. This highlights ongoing challenges many women face regarding safety and security in their personal relationships.

To address the issue, the Directorate of Gender Affairs (DGA)[1] plays a key role in supporting victims and raising awareness through initiatives like the 5 Days of Action against Gender-Based Violence. The 2023 theme for this campaign was "Ending Gender-Based Violence: A Call to Action," with events such as a candlelight vigil and educational programs aimed at preventing violence, empowering women, and providing support.

The DGA also collaborates with the Sexual Assault Resource Centre (SARC), which offers services such as medical care, crisis counseling, and legal aid for survivors. This initiative aims to provide comprehensive services and advocacy for victims, underscoring the government's commitment to tackling gender-based violence.

These efforts reflect a growing acknowledgment of the need to protect women and girls in Antigua and Barbuda, though more work remains to be done to eliminate GBV entirely. The government has established a National Action Plan on Gender-Based Violence (GBV) that focuses primarily on urban areas, leaving rural women underserved. Training programs for law enforcement officers and healthcare workers are also critical to improving response mechanisms.

The legal framework protecting women and girls from Gender-Based Violence (GBV) in Antigua and Barbuda is robust, with laws such as the Domestic Violence Act 2013 and the Sexual Offences Act 2015. While this legislation offers protection against physical and sexual violence, there is currently no specific legislative provision for emotional or psychological abuse.

In practice, women who experience GBV can seek remedies through common law, relying on tort law (duty of care) and contract law (breach of implied trust or constructive dismissal).

Gender relevant legal framework and institutional arrangements

The summary provided in this section highlights the key laws, policies, and international commitments relevant to the social, environmental, and economic framework guiding projects in Antigua and Barbuda, emphasizing gender considerations and sustainable development.

National Legislation

* **Antigua and Barbuda Labour Code (1975, with significant amendments):** Prevents discrimination in employment based on various attributes, including race and gender. *Labour Code (Amendment) Act 2013:* This act amended the code to protect workers' rights and introduced provisions related to the employment of young women and persons.
* **Constitution of Antigua and Barbuda (1981):** Prohibits discrimination based on sex and outlines that discriminatory treatment cannot occur due to race, origin, political beliefs, and sex.
* **Sexual Offences Act (1995/Amended in 2004):** This act criminalizes various forms of sexual violence and abuse. The amended version came into effect in 2004. It aims to protect the rights and dignity of victims and emphasizes the importance of consent.
* **Disaster Management Act (2002):** Organizes preparedness and response for natural and man-made disasters.

Childcare and Protection Act (2006): Creates a Child Protection Agency and sets standards for child-care facilities emphasizing child safety and protection from abuse.

Domestic Violence Act (2013): This act provides protection to individuals from domestic violence. It establishes legal mechanisms for the prevention and protection of victims, allowing them to seek restraining orders and providing for the care and support of those affected by domestic violence. The Act recognizes that domestic violence is a violation of human rights and a critical gender issue.

Disabilities and Equal Opportunities Bill (2013): Protects the rights of persons with disabilities, aiming to eliminate discrimination and ensure equal participation in society.

Environmental Protection and Management Act (2013): Serves as the principal framework for environmental governance, integrating Multilateral Environmental Agreements and promoting sustainability.

National Policies

* **National Youth Policy (2007):** Aims to empower youth across various domains such as education, health, employment, and gender equality.
* **National Poverty Strategy 2011-2015:** Focuses on building resilience through environmental sustainability and integrating disaster risk reduction into planning processes.

Internal Policies

* **Department of Environment Gender Policy (2018):** The Department of Environment in Antigua and Barbuda established its Gender Policy in 2018. This policy aims to integrate gender considerations into environmental management, development, and conservation activities within the context of sustainable development[34]. It also designates gender officers to support in this work.

[34] Government of Antigua and Barbuda (2018) Department of Environment [DofE](2018): Gender Policy


---
**Page 267**
---

Environmental and Social Safeguard Policy (2018): In this policy the Department of Environment (DoE) in Antigua and Barbuda requires that all projects comply with standards set forth in Multilateral Environmental Agreements (MEAs) and the Environmental Protection Management Act of 2015, addressing issues like biodiversity and climate change. Additionally, the DoE emphasizes the importance of compliance with human rights, social protection, and gender conventions to which the government is a signatory.

### Multilateral Agreements and International Conventions

- **Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) (1979):** Protects women's rights and promotes equal representation and opportunities.
- **Convention on the Rights of the Child (1989):** Ensures children’s civil, political, and social rights.
- **United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) (1992):** Provides a framework for climate action, emphasizing gender balance in climate policy.
- **Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (1995):** Calls for women's active involvement in environmental decision-making.
- **UN Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (CRPD) (2006):** Ensures rights for persons with disabilities, promoting equal access to rights and freedoms.

### Rio+20 Outcome Document (2012)

- Affirms the need for gender-sensitive policies in sustainable development.

### Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) (2015): The 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development, adopted by all United Nations Member States in 2015, created 17 world Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). These goals aim to end poverty and hunger; ensure healthy lives and promote well-being for all at all ages; ensure access to quality education and promote lifelong learning opportunities for all women and girls. The aim of these global goals is “peace and prosperity for people and the planet” while tackling climate change, promoting gender equality, and working to preserve oceans and forests.

### UNFCCC Gender Action Plan (2017): Aims to enhance women’s participation in climate-related processes and decision-making.

### Institutional Arrangements

In Antigua and Barbuda, the Directorate of Gender Affairs (DoGa) within the Ministry of Social Transformation, Human Resource Development, Youth, and Gender Affairs spearheads the development and implementation of gender policies.

Antigua and Barbuda does not have a specific national gender policy that has been formalized as of now. However, there are ongoing efforts to develop a comprehensive National Gender Equality Action Plan and policy. This initiative is being undertaken by the DoGa with the aim of aligning gender considerations in all government programs and projects. The plan will be developed through consultations with civil society and various government levels, ensuring that the proposed plan incorporates monitoring and evaluation frameworks for accountability.


---
**Page 268**
---

Meanwhile, the Department of Environment (DOE) developed in 2018 a gender policy[1] that focuses on the impacts of climate change and environmental policies on gender. This strategy also aims to create specific policies and project designs to address gender disparities. By implementing this approach, the DOE seeks to enhance project effectiveness, reduce risks, and increase the likelihood of secondary benefits for women.

In 2018, the DOE has also developed the Environmental and Social Safeguards Policy of Antigua and Barbuda, establishing guidelines for managing environmental and social risks in development projects, particularly those related to climate change. The policy aims to ensure that all projects are designed with gender responsiveness by integrating gender considerations into all programs, ensuring equal access to resources, justice, and environmental information for both women and men. In this policy, the DOE promotes a participatory approach involving women and men at all levels of project implementation.

Continuous gender sensitivity training is provided to staff, partners, and community groups. Additionally, all partners and contractors must either have a gender policy or collaborate with the Directorate of Gender Affairs to develop one, reinforcing the commitment to gender equality within environmental frameworks[2].

Gender, Climate Change and Early Warning Systems and Disaster Response

In 2020 and 2021, UN Women conducted studies highlighting the increased vulnerability of climate change and disaster response in Antigua and Barbuda. These studies revealed significant gender differences in how men and women experience the effects of natural hazards, with women, particularly those living in households headed by a woman, being disproportionately affected. For instance, as already illustrated, women are more likely to be employed in sectors such as agriculture, fishing, and tourism, on which they heavily rely, are disrupted by disasters. This was evident during the COVID-19 pandemic when women were more likely to lose their jobs or have reduced income.

Men, who dominate sectors like fisheries, also face challenges, but the loss of equipment like boats mainly impacts their financial stability. On the other hand, women's caregiving roles, especially for children and the elderly, also leave them vulnerable when infrastructure, such as roads and healthcare facilities, are compromised by disasters or climate change. Women in rural areas often lack access to clean water due to inadequate access to medical care during emergencies. Women's health, especially maternal health, is at higher risk of deterioration after disaster-likes diseases outbreak, while mental health services remain underutilized by men.

Moreover, female-headed households (that account for nearly half of all households in the country) often struggle to access disaster information and resources due to economic limitations, such as the inability to pay for transportation or communication devices. This lack of access exacerbates their vulnerability during and after disasters. The studies also underscored gaps in policy frameworks and inadequacy in disaster risk and climate change, with a lack of gender-focused policies or plans in place.

Overall, the need for gender-responsive policies and improved infrastructure to assist women, especially those leading households, in tackling climate change and disaster risks is evident[1].

### Women's Representation and Participation in Climate and DRM Sectors

Women in Antigua and Barbuda face ongoing challenges in political leadership, holding just 5.4% of seats in the national parliament, which reflects broader gender disparities in decision-making roles. These gender imbalances are also evident at the local level; women hold only 10% of elected positions and are underrepresented in technical fields but dominate administrative roles. Childcare and household responsibilities further limit women's ability to engage in disaster recovery, particularly for single-parent households, which compromises weaker families' resilience to climate change and delays recovery efforts in key public services[2].

Despite these challenges, women play significant roles in disaster management and climate change. The National Disaster Management Agency (NDMA) has a female director of the Climate Change and disaster coordinator position[3]. Meanwhile, in the Meteorological Services, responsible for issuing warnings and forecasts and managing the early warning system (EWS), out of eight (8) meteorologists and forecasters, four are women; however, only one is a senior officer. Additionally, both the Directorate of Gender Affairs (DGA) and the Department of Environment (DoE) have female directors[4]. The DoE has made some progress in incorporating gender perspectives into disaster risk management (DRM) at the national, sectoral, and community levels; however, despite these efforts, women's participation is still low. Women's representation in leadership positions within DRM-related organizations is also limited.

Following Hurricane Irma in 2017, women played a key role in the creation and distribution of dignity kits—containing essential health and hygiene items—to affected communities, as well as in recovery efforts through their involvement in the Barbuda Community Council. Out of all 13 members, seven are women[5]. While women's representation is increasing despite the lack of women leaders in leadership positions within DRM-related organizations, this has not necessarily resulted in gender-responsive policies or practices within the sector.

### Gender in Early Warning Systems and Disaster Preparedness

In Antigua and Barbuda, men and women reportedly prepare for and respond to climate-related events in similar ways. However, highlighting the role of gender in shaping resilience, as shown by the data and reports in this section[6].

# Early Warning Systems (EWS): As noted in a 2018 report by the World Meteorological Organization (WMO), radio remains a key source of hazard information, though social media is gaining influence in the region. However, for those living in rural areas with limited access to internet services like Facebook, accessed via mobile phones, are becoming increasingly relevant for early warnings.

While the internet serves as a valuable tool for disseminating warnings, it may inadvertently create barriers between women and men in Antigua and Barbuda. UN Women's 2011 policy brief notes that most households in Antigua and Barbuda do not have access to computers or the internet, allowing them to access critical information. Nevertheless, low-income female-headed households often lack access to data plans that are required by mobile phones for receiving disaster alerts.

Additionally, the 2018 WMO assessment which included Antigua and Barbuda's review of EWS systems, found that women and men may respond differently to EWS based on how they spend their time: (i) differences in household decision-making power and family structures; (ii) the type of economic activities they engage in; and (iii) how they perceive disaster risk. This suggests a need for gender-specific risk and vulnerability data, establishing feedback mechanisms to ensure awareness reaches key stakeholders. The policy brief also highlights that women's roles as primary caregivers may influence their ability to address the distinct needs of urban and rural populations, men and women, young and elderly, and people with disabilities. It also suggested greater use of amateur radio and community alerts via social media.

## Disaster Preparedness: UN Women in its 2011 policy brief noted that women in Antigua and Barbuda tend to spend more on disaster preparedness, stocking up on essentials when alerted to an upcoming hurricane or other natural disaster than men. This is supported by the Government of Antigua and Barbuda and Adaptation Fund report (2020), which found that women in the country spend an average of US$800 more than men on disaster preparedness measures, such as purchasing emergency supplies.

This finding warrants further analysis to understand the driving factors and impacts. Global evidence suggests that women's household responsibilities and family roles may influence their disaster

**Page 271**

### Opportunities for the project to support: Effective access to communication on early warnings and hazard management information, as well as the tools to support women in making informed decisions that may reduce their impact to climate-related hazards.

In this regard the project must look to leverage existing infrastructure such as radio stations and community-based organizations, mobile phones. In addition surveys taken in project development demonstrated the relevance of leveraging community-based organizations and other local actors to disseminate hazard management information regarding hazards but also an potential preventive measures that allow for early alerts. Finally increasing the lead time of early alerts to allow for planning and decision making as well as potential preventive actions would allow women headed households to ensure measures to reduce vulnerability.

### DRM Policies and Gender

The Government of Antigua and Barbuda is making progress in incorporating gender considerations into disaster risk reduction (DRR) across national, sectoral, and agency levels. The National Office of Disaster Services (NODS) collaborates with the Caribbean Disaster Emergency Management Agency (CDEM) to integrate gender into disaster risk reduction efforts. However most national disaster policies still lack a gender perspective.

### The Environmental Protection and Management Act of 2019

outlines the Department of Environment's (DoE) role in leading climate resilience efforts. Through the Sustainable Island Resource Framework (SIRF), the DoE is working with communities to identify priority projects, including loans for female-headed households to boost climate resilience. The Act also requires periodic gender impact assessments of the Fund’s projects. Meanwhile the National Strategic Biodiversity Action Plan (NSBAP) 2018-2030 outlines a series of actions that are aligned with international goals like the Aichi Biodiversity Target 14, focusing on restoring ecosystems that benefit women indigenous groups and vulnerable communities.

The DoE also plays an active role in gender equality efforts in environmental management emphasizing the active involvement of both men and women. The 2018 Environmental Social Safeguard Policy reinforces this by mandating environmental social and gender screening of projects and ensuring that all projects are consistent with national policies on gender equality.

However a review of Antigua and Barbuda’s Post Disaster Public Financial Management found that gender equality is not factored into resource allocation for disaster response. A UN Women survey

### Potential Opportunities for the project to support

- In the preparation of climate information ensure that consultations with end users incorporate women-only consultations and Focus groups, or discussions with women leaders and organizations, particularly those who are most vulnerable and those whose communities are most affected. This will allow a better assessment of needs and preferred communication channels. It is also important to ensure that the message is gender-sensitive and that only one gender message as well gender communicator communicating the message.
- For the planning of activities related to the design and dissemination of alert messages, the project should consider involving women in the design process through local cooperation groups. Additionally, the project will look to ensure that training and awareness workshops are organized with a focus on reaching women. The timing for these events is an important consideration and available time of women for their participation, taking into account the double burden placed on women.
- The program will support risk management education programs at a community level including in schools and social structures, specifically aimed at women and girls. This would allow to train and equip community members, including women and persons with disabilities, for early warning.
- Measures to disseminate information must take into account the existing gender based digital gaps that may become a barrier in receiving early warnings alerts and real-time updates. In this regard, it is important to consider the use of SMS systems that are more accessible to both men and women, for example looking to identify SMS systems that do not require data plan nor access to smart phone technology.
- Ensure that gender equality is considered and action plans for Emergency Warning (EW) systems provide analysis and strategies to include inclusivity and to address the digital gender gaps, while working to promote the representation and participation of vulnerable groups in preparedness activities.

### Gender-disaggregated data

Gender-disaggregated data, is crucial for effective planning and response. It is therefore essential to integrate gender-responsive data collection and analysis in DRR capacity development. In this regard the government of Antigua and Barbuda has made important advancement through their NCDs and Climate Change documents that include including gender-disaggregated data. This best practice will be promoted within the DRM space by working with NOOSS.
