# Gender Assessment for Somalia

Somalia is ranked "medium" on the Global Gender Development Index (GGDI)2021-24, comparing human development achievements for women and men*. Women consist of 50.3% of the total population in Somalia** and 31.9% of Somali households are female-headed***. Somalia has a large rural population with 78% of the population living in rural areas, and most of them work in the agricultural sector. The majority of women in Somalia live in poverty, with 40% of women in Somalia, including 20.3% with mild or moderate degrees of disability and 4.1% with a severe disability****.

Of people living with a disability, women are represented more (57%) than men (43%)****. Meanwhile, the country has made progress in reducing gender inequality compared to other countries in the Horn of Africa region. Somalia is ranked 122nd out of 193 countries in the Gender Inequality Index (GII) and 102nd out of 146 countries in the Global Gender Gap Report 2021****.

On the other hand, women have made significant progress in education compared to other countries. Education has helped to increase labour earnings and reduce poverty****. Living standards have improved, helping Somalia to narrow urban-rural gaps****. However, low-income and rural households still lag in access to basic services such as water, sanitation, and electricity****.

Despite these improvements, the maternal mortality rate remains critically inadequate. The country has one of the highest maternal mortality rates globally, with 105 deaths per 100,000 live births****. This is due to factors including ongoing instability, and limited healthcare infrastructure contributing to this dire situation. Efforts by the Somali government and international organizations like UNICEF and UNHCR aim to improve healthcare services, but challenges persist, especially in rural areas****.

## Education, Vocational Training and Literacy

Somalia has made progress in reducing gender parity in education, though enrollments are still generally low. The net enrolment in primary education is around 57% for female students and 58.6% for male students for the academic year 2023-2024****. The enrolment rate at lower secondary schools (grades seven to nine) is 19%, with a gender parity index of 0.98****. The net enrolment ratio for both sexes is lower for upper secondary schools, at 11.5% for women and 14.3% for men****.

However, the proportion of female students in tertiary education and technical vocational training is lower than that of male students. Female students make up only 27% of all students enrolled in science, technology, engineering, and math. The Ministry of Education (MoE) estimates that only 10% of female students, compared to 47% of male students, are enrolled in Science, Technology, Engineering, Agriculture, Livestock, Arts, and Mathematics (STEAM)-related subjects****.

Literacy has improved steadily for both women and men but remains higher for men at 52.3% compared to 45.4% for women****. There is also a literacy gap between urban and rural areas, with urban areas like Mogadishu having higher literacy rates. For

example, in cities like Mbagathi, about 60% of adults (15 years and older) can read and write, but in rural areas, only about 48% of adults are literate[23]. This gap shows how much harder it is for people in rural areas to get an education. Girls and women in rural areas, especially those from ethnic minorities and people with disabilities, face even more challenges. Over 72% of women in rural areas have never been to school[24], compared to only about 30% of girls and women in urban areas[25]. About 30% are enrolled in primary school, but this drops to 22% in rural areas, and even fewer girls attend school[26]. In rural areas, the gender gap in education is wider; with less than half of the 25th percentile of boys completing secondary school, girls in these communities have fewer chances to get an education and improve their lives.

Additionally, gender gaps in access to education are particularly evident among ethnic minorities and people with disabilities[27]. Indigenous women in Somalia have higher literacy rates and fewer educational opportunities compared to their male counterparts[28]. The dropout rate for indigenous adolescents is also higher than that of non-indigenous students. This can be attributed partly to the fact that they perform household chores. This results in a widening educational disparity for indigenous groups, people with disabilities, and those living in poverty[29].

The education-related obstacles faced by rural girls and women in Somalia, such as higher literacy rates and fewer educational opportunities compared to their male counterparts, hamper their ability to access informed decision-making, hold jobs outside the home, understand relevant laws and regulations, and access technologies, extension services, and finance[30]. Access to early warning systems is also a challenge for rural women in Somalia. Women have limited information from formal sources, while women often rely on informal and social sources[31]. This disparity means that women are less likely to receive timely and accurate warnings, impacting their ability to take appropriate action when needed.

Employment and Labor Participation

In Somalia, women's labour participation in 2023 was 32.7%, compared to men’s 52.5%[32]. According to the Somalia Labour Force Survey, 57% of women aged 15 to 64 were employed in agriculture, forestry, and fishing; 8% in industry; 9% in commerce, transport, accommodation, food services, healthcare, and education[33]. However, just 30% of women are wage and salaried workers, while a significant 70% are in vulnerable employment, often in the informal sector with poor working


---
**Page 275**
---

conditions, low pay, and no social protection[1] gender disparities are stark in Somalia. Women earn about 42% of what men earn, own fewer assets, and face more barriers to accessing better paying jobs.[2] The gender wage gap stands at around 38%, influenced by lower educational attainment for women, labour market discrimination, and societal norms assigning women to primary domestic roles[3]. These factors hinder women's ability to participate in formal employment, access skills training, and advance their careers.

### Representation and Participation in Decision-Making

Despite a robust gender equality perspective reflected in national policies and legal frameworks, Somalia still faces significant challenges in achieving equal representation and participation of women and men at all levels of government. Women hold only 10% of seats in the National Parliament and executive bodies. In 2024, women hold 24% of Federal Parliament seats, up from previous years but below the 30% target[4]. In the executive body, women occupy 10% of senior ministerial positions and 18% of deputy ministerial positions.[5] Women hold only 6.7% of seats in local government councils and 29% of leadership roles in civil service and constitute 42% of the total civil service workforce[6]. However, at the sub-national level, representation is low: 8% in governor positions, 3.43% at the district level, 10.56% of chief position seats, local council chairperson positions, and 7.9% of mayoral positions at the regional level[7]. Women make up 20.3% of deputy governor positions at the district level[8].

### Gender-Based Violence, Social Norms and FGM in Somalia

Gender-based violence (GBV) remains a significant concern in Somalia. Over 60% of women face physical abuse, denial of education, forced marriage, rape, and sexual harassment.[9] Harmful gender norms, such as the belief that men are entitled to sex regardless of consent, contribute significantly to GBV. Moreover, for women and girls, there exist more challenges in accessing legal advice, safe shelter, and mental support[10].

Cultural practices and social norms continue to contribute to the unequal division of labour in households and communities. Women and girls are involved in productive activities such as farming and gathering firewood but also bear a heavy burden of unpaid domestic work.[11] Much of their time is devoted to household chores, childcare, cooking, and cleaning. Indigenous women, in particular, carry a heavy burden in terms of household chores, including housework, farming, tending animals, and collecting forest foods, expecting during food shortages and disasters[12].

Women are still expected to follow social norms and beliefs that endorse men’s privilege while undervaluing women’s capacity and potential[1]. This impacts the roles of women and men in decision-making in households and society as a whole[2]. Women are involved in managing household budgets, but men are the primary decision-makers for major expenditures[3]. Additionally, social norms and low levels of education and literacy are major barriers preventing women and girls from accessing information, technical skills, and training, forcing their voices heard, and enjoying preference in their families and society[4].

Female Genital Mutilation (FGM) is a widespread practice in Somalia. According to the Somalia Health and Demographic Survey (SDHS 2020), 99% of women and girls aged 15-49 have undergone some form of FGM[5]. This practice is deeply rooted in cultural and social norms and continues despite efforts to eliminate it[6].

### Gender Policies and Legal Framework

The Federal Government of Somalia is committed to promoting gender equality and social inclusion in disaster risk reduction and management, aligning with the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development. Key national policies include:

- The Provisional Constitution of 2012 guarantees equal rights for women and men, including human rights, equality before the law, and active participation in all aspects of national life[7]. The Sexual Offences Bill addresses sexual violence and promotes gender equality, though it has faced challenges in implementation. It aims to protect the rights of women, children, the elderly, and people with disabilities during disasters[8].
- The National Gender Policy promotes gender equality and women’s leadership, reduces vulnerability to gender-based violence, and ensures equal access to resources for women and men[9]. The National Gender Strategy (2021–2025) emphasizes gender equality and women's empowerment across various sectors. Somalia’s Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) include gender-focused measures for climate change adaptation and mitigation, disaster risk reduction, and emergency response[10].
- The National Gender Mainstreaming Guidelines provide practical guidance for integrating gender perspectives into national policies[11]. The Gender Guide for Gender Mainstreaming in Disaster Management provides detailed instructions for incorporating gender perspectives in disaster risk reduction and management efforts[12].

Additional gender-sensitive policies include the National Climate Change Policy[1], the gender-sensitive National Water Resource Management Policy[2], the National Disaster Management Strategy[3], and the GCF Somalia NAP Climate Change Adaptation Gender Toolkit[4]. These policies aim to integrate gender perspectives into climate change, water resource management, and disaster risk reduction efforts[5].

Somalia is also committed to international frameworks like Convention on the Elimination of all forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples (UNDRIP), United Nations Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (UNCRPD), the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction, and the African Union Agenda 2063[6]. Despite these efforts, challenges remain in ensuring gender analysis within ministries and insufficient funding for gender-focused interventions[7][8].

### Gender-Related Institutional Arrangements

In Somalia, gender-related institutional arrangements have evolved significantly in recent years, reflecting both progress and ongoing challenges in promoting gender equality. The institutional framework is complex and shaped by various policies, legal frameworks, and the roles of key institutions. The Provisional Federal Constitution, adopted in 2012, includes provisions for gender equality[9]. However, implementation has been inconsistent. Efforts are ongoing to integrate a 30% quota for women's representation in political institutions, although this has yet to be fully realized[10]. Somalia has established mechanisms at both federal and regional levels to promote gender equality and women’s empowerment, coordinated by the Ministry of Women and Family (MoWF) in collaboration with sectoral ministries, local governments, and development partners[11].

The Ministry of Women and Families (MoWF) plays a key role in promoting gender equity by coordinating efforts across public institutions, civil society, and the private sector. It works closely with the Federal Government of Somalia (FGS), the National Council for Women and the Somali National Commission for Women (NCNW) in implementingCEDAW and ensuring legal protection for women[12]. MoWF also collaborates with the Gender Action Plan Working Group (GAPWG)[13], which is responsible for developing, implementing, and monitoring gender mainstreaming action plans (GMAPs)[14]. At the federal member state level, each state has its own Ministry of Women and Human Rights Development[15] to promote gender equality. These ministries partner with local governments and development organizations to ensure the inclusion and participation of women in decision-making processes[16]. In addition, the Somali Disaster Management Agency (SoDMA) acts as its Gender Strategy & Road Map.

2014 – 2037**, will establish a Gender-DIM Working Group. This group aims to bring together key stakeholders—including government entities, civil society organizations, and the private sector—to promote sustained innovation and cross-sector collaboration. The focus will be on building capacity through knowledge exchange and gender-focused research, ensuring that DIM policies and programs are informed by gender-disaggregated data.

Meanwhile, the National Gender Commission oversees the implementation of gender policies and ensures their alignment with national development goals and gender equality standards**. Numerous Civil Society Organizations (CSOs) actively advocate for women’s rights and provide essential services, such as legal aid and support for survivors of gender-based violence**.

Disaster management at the sub-national, district, and village levels holds a multi-faceted approach. The Ministry of Interior, Federal Affairs & Reconciliation (MoI/FAR) collaborates with governments to develop disaster risk reduction strategies. District Disaster Risk Management Committees (DDRMCs) coordinate disaster response and prepare emergency activities. Community-based disaster risk management (CBDRM) initiatives build local capacity and resilience**.

Several inter-sectoral organizations are involved in disaster management, working closely with government agencies and other sectors. The National Disaster Management Agency (NDMA) leads emergency planning and response efforts, including establishing early warning systems. The United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) enhances risk management through the use of information technology and communication systems. The World Bank supports early warning systems. The United Nations Office for Disaster Risk Reduction (UNDRR) supports the National Platform for Disaster Risk Reduction, facilitating multi-sectoral and multi-disciplinary mechanisms for resilience-building**.

Additionally, the World Food Programme (WFP) collaborates with NDMA to develop anticipatory action plans for floods and other disasters, while the International Organization for Migration (IOM) leverages data tools for humanitarian response. Organizations such as UN Women, FAO, Danish Refugee Council (DRC), Norwegian Refugee Council (NRC), and African Union Commission (AUC)/African Union Refugee Council (AURC) also collaborate with local governments, NGOs, and other organizations to enhance disaster preparedness and response at all levels**. These institutions ensure gender-responsive approaches in all phases of disaster management activities.

Despite these advancements, several challenges persist. Deep-rooted cultural norms and practices, such as female genital mutilation (FGM) and early marriages, continue to hinder gender equality efforts**. Ongoing conflict and instability in certain regions exacerbate gender-based violence and

# Women's Representation and Participation in DRM Sectors

Women's representation in Somalia's government remains low, especially in sectors like water resource management and disaster risk reduction and management. As of 2018, women hold about 24% of parliamentary seats nationally (35%), with specific fields: 15% of senior positions in water resource management are held by women; 10% in meteorology and hydrology, and around 20% in the National Disaster Management Authority. Women's representation is even lower at the regional level, amounting significantly to about 5% in disaster management committees (36%). These disparities reflect broader societal norms and systemic barriers that limit women’s participation in governance (38). Efforts are needed to improve training and resources for women to enhance their leadership in these critical areas.

# Gender in Early Warnings and Disaster Risk Response

In Somalia, the integration of gender considerations in early warning systems and disaster risk response is essential to effectively address the vulnerabilities faced by different demographic groups. Recent studies on climate change impacts in Somalia have highlighted the need for comprehensive data collection and analysis to inform disaster management strategies.

As part of the Key Platforms and Initiatives, The United Nations Office for Disaster Risk Reduction (UNDRR) supports Somalia in implementing the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction, promoting gender equality and women's empowerment. This framework aims at reducing vulnerabilities through a gender lens[1]. The Somali Disaster Management Agency (SDMA) plays a crucial role in coordinating disaster response efforts, aiming to strengthen community resilience and incorporate gender considerations into early warning systems and disaster risk management, although challenges in data collection and women’s representation in decision-making persist[2].

The United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) has been active in enhancing disaster resilience, focusing on integrating gender into disaster risk management through capacity-building programs for local authorities and communities, highlighting the need for gender-disaggregated data[3]. Similarly, the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) works to address climate-driven shocks, employing gender-sensitive approaches to support women farmers and advocates for inclusive data collection[4].

The Ministry of Energy and Water Resources is involved in disaster risk reduction related to water management, including flood risk map, and integrating gender considerations into water resource management, which is crucial for affected communities[5]. Additionally, the World Meteorological Organization (WMO) collaborates with Somali authorities to enhance weather forecasting and early warning systems, aiming to incorporate gender-sensitive data to ensure effective communication with all community members[6].

Despite the efforts of various organizations, the availability of gender-disaggregated data remains limited, hampering the understanding of specific vulnerabilities and the impacts of disasters on


---
**Page 281**
---

different groups, particularly women, children, and those with disabilities[1]. Furthermore, women’s representation in disaster management roles is often insufficient, limiting their input in planning and decision-making processes, which is essential for inclusive and responsive disaster risk management strategies[2]. Additionally, an improved coordination among various agencies, including UN bodies, local governments, and civil society organizations, is needed to ensure that the voices of different perspectives are integrated across all disaster management initiatives[3]. Establishing robust mechanisms for collecting gender-disaggregated data across all disaster risk management platforms is essential, as this will help in understanding the unique vulnerabilities faced by women and girls. This, in turn, will enhance the capacity of local authorities and communities to understand and address gender issues in disaster risk management can improve resilience; training programs should emphasize gender-sensitive disaster management strategies[4]. Furthermore, integrating gender perspectives into early warning systems (EWS) and disaster preparedness and response planning can provide critical insights into local vulnerabilities, enhancing the effectiveness of interventions[5]. Finally, advocating for policies that mandate the inclusion of gender considerations in disaster risk management at all levels will help create a more equitable approach to risk reduction and response[6].

Gender and Access to Early Warning Information and Technologies

In Somalia, recent efforts by various organizations and governmental bodies have highlighted the need for comprehensive data collection and analysis to inform disaster management strategies and ensure inclusivity. The implementation of EWS in Somalia is primarily managed by the Federal Government of Somalia (FGS) through the National Disaster Risk Management Authority (NDRMA), with support from the Water and Land Information Management Project[7]. These systems are designed to monitor and provide alerts for various natural hazards, including floods and droughts. As of 2021-2024, Somalia has several centralized and decentralized EWS in place.

Many international organizations, including the Federal and Federal Member States governments in implementing and maintaining EWS key partners include United Nations agencies such as the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) and the United Nations Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs (UNOCHA)[8], international NGOs like ActionAid, Danish Refugee Council, Mercy Corps, Oxfam, Save the Children, World Vision, and others[9]. The European Union, the United Kingdom, the Netherlands, the United States, European Union, and various other countries[10]. The United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) has also been involved in supporting EWS implementation. The World Meteorological Organization (WMO) also play critical roles in enhancing disaster risk management and early warning systems in Somalia[11]. For flood and drought alerts, organizations like ActionAid, Danish Refugee Council, Mercy Corps, Oxfam, Save the Children, Norwegian Refugee Council, and World Vision

provide integrated emergency and early recovery support, including the dissemination of alerts and information to improve preparedness and mitigation[15]. Leading telecom companies such as Hormud Telecom, Golis Telecom Group, and Somtel Network also play a crucial role in disseminating these alerts.

Receiving warnings about natural hazards through EWS in Somalia does not necessarily require users to have a registered mobile phone. However, mobile phones are a common medium for disseminating alerts, especially among the urban population[16]. Mobile phone-based systems can be used by both urban and rural communities, including internally displaced persons (IDPs), pastoralists, and agro-pastoralists. These systems help them prepare for and mitigate the impacts of natural disasters by providing timely alerts and information on weather conditions.

Many community members are not aware of the existence of these systems or do not understand how to utilize them effectively[17]. This lack of awareness can lead to a delay in dissemination indirectly from local authorities, which contributes to lower awareness and usage among them[18].

The most preferred media for early warning alerts in Somalia include radio, mobile phones, and community meetings. Radio remains the most popular platform, with over half of the population listening daily or weekly[19]. Mobile phone-based systems are also widely used by both men and women according to the Somalia Integrated Household Budget Survey (SIHBS) 2002: 85.6% of males and 84.5% of females over 15 years old use mobile phones[20]. This data highlights a relatively small gender gap in mobile ownership, although access to mobile services in Somalia shows significant gender disparities. Men are more likely to use all media platforms compared to women for reasons, 64% of men use this network weekly compared to 37% of women[21].

Water level management in Somalia is overseen by the Ministry of Energy and Water Resources and the Somalia Water and Land Information Management (SWALIM). These institutions monitor and manage water levels across the country.

Focus group discussions for the Early Warning for All (EW4All) at the National Stakeholders consultation and national surveys have highlighted the gender disparities in access to early warning information and technologies. Women, especially in rural areas, have less access to information compared to men[22]. This disparity is also reflected in lower levels of awareness and usage of early warning systems (EWS) among women and other marginalized groups[23].

* Increased awareness through targeted campaigns to educate communities, especially marginalized groups, about the existence and usage of EWS.

Preparedness, Early Actions, and Responses

Observations from the Early Warning for All (EW4ALL) project national level consultations have revealed that contingency and emergency plans in Somalia often overlook the specific needs of women and vulnerable groups, such as people with disabilities or children living in affected areas. Despite the importance of including these engagement groups, these are among the most frequent victims of disasters and are less likely to be involved**148**.

In regions like Banadir, Galguduud, Hiranbelle, Southwest, Jubaland, and Somaliland, community meetings have shown that many women and people with disabilities have not been invited to participate in the decision-making processes related to disaster preparedness or response. Consequently, they remain unaware of the specific needs and interventions included in these plans**149**.

Focus group discussions (FGDs) with the Somalia National Disability Agency (DNA) and representatives from organizations working on disability issues revealed that many women and people with disabilities are not aware of the existing disaster preparedness and response interventions. These interventions often fail to address the specific needs of persons with disabilities, particularly women and girls. This includes considerations for different types of disabilities and ensuring access to necessary services.

Stakeholder consultations noted the need to enhance the capacity and skills of individuals involved in disaster management, including training on gender equality and disability inclusion to ensure that interventions effectively address the needs of all affected groups**150**. Consultations for the Gender Strategy in Somalia highlighted the importance of addressing women's specific vulnerabilities during disasters. These include health risks, economic hardships, and psychosocial stress. Their focus encompasses access to reproductive health services, protection from gender-based violence, privacy and safety, economic empowerment, education, and leadership opportunities**151**.

To effectively address the gendered impacts of disasters, it is crucial to incorporate gender considerations into all DRM initiatives. This involves identifying and responding to gender-specific vulnerabilities, ensuring equitable access to resources and decision-making, and promoting women's leadership in disaster preparedness, response, and recovery efforts.[1][2]

Key recommendations from project-level consultations include, but are not limited to, the need for:

- Comprehensive data collection with gender-disaggregated data;
- Gender-sensitive DRM policies, programs, and practices that promote equal opportunities; 
- Integrating gender considerations into all stages of disaster management, including planning, preparedness, response, and recovery;
- Capacity building for women leaders.

Disaster preparedness and response efforts should focus on equipping communities with the knowledge, skills, and resources needed to actively participate in disaster management. This should include training women leaders and empowering them as decision-makers, developing leadership structures and the development of inclusive early warning systems; Promoting women's participation and leadership in decision-making processes related to disaster risk reduction, response, and recovery by ensuring that they have access to adequate information and effective strategies that address their needs and priorities;
- Engaging with local government bodies, civil society organizations, CSOs, and marginalized communities to ensure that their perspectives and needs are included in decision-making processes; 
- Developing and distributing gender-sensitive DRM toolskits and guidelines to standardize gender integration across all sectors in alignment with the UN Women's strategy on gender-responsive disaster risk reduction and supporting SDGs 5, promoting gender equality through inclusive disaster management.

Mainstreaming gender considerations into policies, procedures, and guidelines relating to disaster management and reduction involves integrating gender perspectives at all levels of decision-making and across all phases of disaster preparedness, response, recovery, and risk reduction.


Access to Safe Evacuation Spaces

Efforts to improve access to safe evacuation sites in Somalia have been made through collaboration between the Federal Government, UN agencies, donors, INGOs, and CSOs. However, significant gaps remain in ensuring that women, children, elderly people with disabilities, and other vulnerable groups are able to evacuate safely during natural disasters such as flooding and drought. Across the Federal Member States (FMS), including Baidoa, Bay, Burco, Hiran, Hobyo, Jowhar, Kismayo, Lower Shabelle, Hiraale, and Jubaland, disparities exist in the availability and quality of evacuation sites.


The Federal Government, in collaboration with the UN and humanitarian partners, has set national minimum standards for safe evacuation sites. These standards include the provision of separate sanitation facilities for women and girls, as well as other considerations to ensure safety for vulnerable groups such as women, children, the elderly, and people with disabilities. Despite these standards, many communities continue to face challenges related to access to clean water, food security, and infrastructure challenges. The Federal Government's National Disaster Management Agency (NODMA) has been leading efforts to coordinate with the Federal Member States to ensure preparedness, but challenges remain in implementation and monitoring at the state level[3][4].


---
**Page 285**
---

In Puntland, coastal and flood-prone areas such as Bosaso and Garowe face frequent displacement during flooding and cyclones. However, only 8 out of 70 identified evacuation sites meet the national minimum standards for schools such as polders and mosques are often used as temporary shelters, but overcrowding is a persistent problem in these facilities. Women and girls are particularly vulnerable to gender-based violence (GBV) in these evacuation sites, and reports from local NGOs indicate that GBV cases have increased significantly since the start of the displacement crisis. Many displaced families are forced to rely on unsafe water sources due to insufficient clean water supply.

In Somaliland, the government has made some strides in establishing life evacuation sites, particularly in areas prone to drought and flooding, such as Hargeisa and Borama. However, only 6 out of 50 sites meet the minimum standards. Challenges include limited access to clean water, poor sanitation facilities, and overcrowding. Reports from local NGOs indicate that displaced families have reported concerns about personal safety, especially during periods of extended stay in these sites. Reports also highlight increased cases of GBV against women and girls as they are at heightened risk of physical and emotional abuse in overcrowded shelters[1].

In Galmudug, regions like Galgabo and Hidho frequently experience flooding, leading to displacement. Unfortunately, only 4 out of 60 designated evacuation sites meet the basic needs of displaced families. Communities living near rivers or lakes are particularly vulnerable as these areas often offer little protection from the elements. Women and girls, in particular, lack access to adequate sanitation facilities and face increased risks of GBV due to overcrowding[2]. The elderly and persons with disabilities are also disproportionately affected by displacement sites that are not accessible for those with mobility issues[3].

In Southwest State, areas like Badwe and Kebbe are heavily impacted by seasonal flooding, leading to frequent displacement. Out of 10 identified evacuation sites, only 5 meet the national minimum standards. Key concerns raised by displaced families include overcrowding, lack of clean water, and poor sanitation facilities. Women and girls face increased risks of GBV due to overcrowding[4]. Sexual harassment when using shared sanitation facilities. Additionally, families in rural areas face challenges in accessing evacuation sites due to poor road infrastructure, making it difficult for them to reach safer areas during displacement.

Hirshabelle, particularly in regions such as Beledweyne and Jawhar, is heavily vulnerable to riverine flooding along the Shabelle River. Only 5 out of 80 identified evacuation sites meet the minimum standards for food and water supply. During ground-based project-level consultations with local communities, Beledweyne expressed concerns about the lack of clean water and the need to rely on river water for drinking and cooking, which poses serious health risks. Overcrowding in shelters is also a significant issue[5]. The elderly and persons with disabilities are also disproportionately affected as evacuation sites are often not accessible for those with mobility issues.

The Federal Government, in partnership with the UNHCR, donors, and IGAD, has recognized the need for urgent action to improve the safety and accessibility of evacuation sites across all Federal Member States. Key recommendations from project-level consultations include:

## Limited Access to Resources and Services

Volunteer groups in Somalia, including the poor, rural women, the elderly persons with disabilities, and non-disabled women, face significant challenges in preparing for disasters due to restricted access to resources and services. According to focus group discussions conducted for various projects, communities are often unable to obtain essential items such as food, water, fuel, blankets, tents, boats, or household supplies for emergencies, as they barely earn enough to survive each day.

Both women and men in drought-prone areas noted that during the dry seasons, especially with increasing high temperatures and heat, their crops, agricultural activities, and livelihoods were severely affected. This negatively impacted their income, leaving them with inadequate means to support their families and to purchase essential items such as food, water, fuel, blankets, tents, boats, or household supplies.

Limited access to resources significantly impacts the capacity of the poor women, elderly, and persons with disabilities to prepare for and take action against disasters. This has a direct impact on themselves and their households. The Somali government, supported by various international donors and organizations, has expanded its social protection policy to include "quick responsive social protection" at risk households. This initiative aims to build resilience and mitigate disaster impacts.

Recurring droughts in Somalia have had devastating effects on agriculture, livestock, and water resources, exacerbating food insecurity and malnutrition and pushing vulnerable populations toward famine and disease. Additionally, the lack of access to clean water has further disrupted livelihoods, destroyed infrastructure, and increased the spread of waterborne diseases, further complicating recovery and reconstruction efforts. Environmental degradation, such as desertification and soil erosion, also contributes to the vulnerability of women and girls in Somalia.

The impact of disasters on vulnerable populations—particularly women and girls—is a critical issue requiring urgent attention. The interaction of gender and disaster risk management (GDMR) is increasing awareness about the disproportionate burden that natural hazards place on marginalized groups. Women and girls are often more vulnerable during disasters due to preexisting social, economic, and cultural inequalities. These include limited access to resources, lower socio-economic status, restricted mobility, and a lack of decision-making power. As a result, women and girls


---
**Page 287**
---

are more likely to suffer higher rates of mortality, injury, displacement, and loss of livelihoods compared to men**10**.

Community-Led Early Warning and Disaster Risk Reduction Initiatives

The Federal Government of Somalia has implemented several community-led early warning and disaster risk reduction initiatives in collaboration with international donors and organizations. These include the Community-Based Early Warning System (CB-EWS) implemented by the UNDP, funded by the Norwegian Refugee Council (NRC) that supports Somali communities in developing resilience to shocks and moving out of poverty. The program focuses on integrated programming models, flexible multi-year funding streams, and community returns to generate systemic change**10**.

Meanwhile, the Somalia Recovery and Resilience Framework (RRF), developed by the Federal Government with support from the World Bank, International Finance Corporation, and European Development, in partnership with the Ministry of Humanitarian Affairs & Disaster Management, aims to break the cycle of recurrent disasters and move towards medium-term recovery and long-term resilience**10**.

Additionally, the Federal Member States have implemented various community-led early warning and disaster risk reduction initiatives. For example, following the catastrophic flooding that occurred during the monsoon flood Response Plan was launched in response to severe flooding in 2019. With support from the Federal Government and international partners, the plan provides critical lifesaving assistance and invests in long-term recovery and resilience building for communities affected by floods. The Disaster Preparedness (DPP) and Communities program, implemented by the International Rescue Committee (IRC) and other partners, supports DPP and host communities in regions including Gedo, Lower Shabelle, and Hiran.

International organizations and NGOs play critical roles in supporting these initiatives. UNDP supports various disaster risk reduction initiatives in Somalia, including capacity building and resilience programs**10**. SOMDA (Somali Disaster Management Agency) is the central body for disaster management in Somalia, coordinating efforts at federal and state levels to provide early warning systems and disaster response plans. The Somali Red Crescent Society (SRCS), through its drought food security and resilience to climate shocks, supporting community-based disaster risk reduction and early warning systems, plays a crucial role**10**. Additionally, the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) and Norwegian Refugee Council (NRC) are also involved in providing technical assistance to enhance resilience and disaster preparedness**10**. These initiatives aim to enhance the resilience of vulnerable communities to natural disasters and climate change.

Areas for Improvement in Early Warning and Gender Inclusiveness

In Somalia, several areas require improvement to enhance early warning systems (EWS) and gender inclusiveness. These improvements are crucial for ensuring that EWS effectively serve all community members, particularly women and vulnerable groups. Below are the key areas for improvement and recommendations.

- Enhancing GEIS Analysis: Integrate Gender Equality, Disability, and Social Inclusion (GEIS) analysis into disaster risk assessments to capture the needs of diverse groups. Improving platforms such as Somalia and Camilo to include disaggregated data and make them publicly available is essential[1].

- Improving Early Warning Dissemination: A comprehensive review and action plan for Emergency Warning (EWS) systems should provide analysis and strategies to include inclusivity and gender perspectives in all aspects of EWS. Community radio broadcasting, used for wide reach, Community-based interventions, including outreach activities targeting women and persons with disabilities who have no access to mobile phones or the internet, are crucial[2]. Ensuring that messages are culturally relevant to different groups' needs and conducting training and awareness campaigns specifically targeting women, ensuring gender balance and youth participation, is crucial[3].

- Preparedness and Early Action: Ensure evacuation sites meet minimum standards and are safe for all, particularly women and girls, and are designed inclusively. Train and equip community members in disaster risk reduction (DRR) and early warning systems to ensure preparedness. Building evidence for shock-responsive social protection schemes for preparedness and anticipatory action in the province, especially to include the poor, women, and persons with disabilities is needed[4].

- Cross-Pillar Coordination: Create GEIS-mainstreaming guidelines and training for emergency warning systems. Increase the representation and participation of vulnerable groups in preparedness and response activities. Incorporate GEIS aspects into standard operating procedures and monitoring frameworks, collecting and analyzing disaggregated data[5].

- Enhancing Gender Responsive Strategies: Developing and implementing gender-responsive strategies to ensure that women's needs are met within EWS. Early warning systems should address the specific needs and vulnerabilities of women and girls by incorporating gender perspectives into all aspects of disaster management[6].

- Removing Structural Barriers: Addressing structural barriers that limit women’s economic autonomy and unpaid work is crucial. Aligning public and private finance through integrated national financing frameworks and investing in gender-responsive infrastructure can help[7].

remove these barriers, enabling women to better participate in and benefit from early warning systems.

• Preventing and Responding to Gender-Based Violence: Improving the skills and knowledge of police officers, judges, prosecutors, and other officials to ensure justice for survivors of sexual and gender-based violence (SGBV) is essential. Providing medical, legal, and psychosocial support to SGBV survivors ensures their needs are met in disaster situations[10].

• Promoting Women's Participation and Leadership: Encouraging women's participation and leadership in all forms of decision-making is vital. This includes promoting women's political participation and leadership at the local level for disaster risk reduction, disaster mitigation initiatives. Women's leadership can lead to more inclusive and effective disaster management strategies[10].

• Strengthening Data Collection and Analysis: Improving the collection and analysis on gender-specific impacts of disasters and early warning systems is vital. This data can inform more effective policies and programs that address the unique needs of women and girls[8, 9].

Building Capacity and Awareness: Enhancing the capacity of communities, especially women and persons with disabilities, to understand and respond to early warning information is essential. Conducting awareness campaigns and training programs can help build this capacity, enabling communities to better prepare for and respond to disasters[10].

This GAP is a consolidated document for Outputs 1 and 2, and as such it is considered the global GAP. Output 2 countries have country-specific GAPs as part of their country funding proposals which provide further detail on each output. Each country’s outputs will report to the global SAP PMO via their country-specific GAPs. The overall progress of this GAP will be the reporting of the output MWP to report to GEF on this global GAP, and the other project activities are mainstreamed.

The main cost of the GAP will be the hiring of country-level and global-level gender staff. Nevertheless, an estimate for each country is provided below, which is only based on a proportion of the activity budget. This GAP is considered a flexible document and will be further updated during project implementation.

- % of all relevant project reports include disaggregated data by sex, age, and diversity (target: 100%)
- At least 2 case studies per year per country focus on GEDS aspects in disaster risk reduction.
- % of grievance reports received are sex-disaggregated annually (target: 100%)

## Timeline

- Most of the activities expected in years 3,4.

## Responsibility

- UNDP and implementing agencies in each country

## Estimated Cost/ Resources

- $150,000 per country
